# The Gospel of Barnabas: A Clear English Translation from the Italian

> **DRAFT — NOT FOR PUBLICATION.** Fresh plain-English rendering prepared with AI assistance from the public-domain Italian text printed by Lonsdale and Laura Ragg (Clarendon Press, 1907). The 1907 English is used only as a check, never as the wording base. Status: **AUDITED** (ladder: DRAFTED → AUDITED → REVIEW-READY → HELD FOR ROBERT). No claim of first, oldest, suppressed, or definitive text is made; house wording is "none found after research." Headings marked "Editorial heading" are helpers added for reading, not manuscript titles.

## Method statement

- **Base text.** This rendering is translated from the Italian text of the Vienna manuscript as printed by Lonsdale and Laura Ragg (Clarendon Press, Oxford, 1907), a public-domain edition. The Italian was worked from throughout; nothing was translated out of a modern English version.
- **Checks.** The Raggs' 1907 English was used only as a check on difficult Italian readings, never as the wording base. A Spanish witness — the Bernabé Pons edition of the Sydney manuscript transcript (chapters 1–120) — was consulted at points where the Italian reading was uncertain.
- **Seam passages.** Passages where the book speaks of the Messiah and of Mohammed are translated exactly as the Italian has them and footnoted; they are never smoothed, softened, or decided.
- **What is not claimed.** No claim is made of a Greek or Latin original, of a new discovery, of primacy, or of a definitive text. The house wording is "none found after research."
- **AI assistance.** This draft was prepared with AI assistance under human direction and audit: every slice was self-audited against the Italian base (G1), the seam passages were independently re-checked word-for-word (G2), and terminology was swept for consistency (G3).
- **Status.** DRAFT — not for publication. Held for Robert Tilford's read-through and his separate publication decision.

---

---

## Front matter — the writer's opening (folio 3a–3b)

*[Editorial heading: Barnabas greets his readers and states why he writes.]*

The true good news of Jesus, called Christ, a new prophet sent by God to the world, as told by Barnabas, his apostle.

Barnabas, apostle of Jesus the Nazarene, called Christ, wishes peace and comfort to everyone who lives on earth.

Dear friends, in these recent days the great and wonderful God has visited us through his prophet Jesus Christ, with great mercy in teaching and in miracles. Because of this, many people, deceived by Satan under a show of piety, are preaching a most godless teaching. They call Jesus the son of God, they reject circumcision — God's covenant forever — and they allow every unclean food. Paul, too, has been deceived in this, and I do not speak of it without grief.

That is why I am writing to you the truth that I saw and heard while I was with Jesus: so that you may be saved, and not be deceived by Satan and lost in God's judgment. So beware of anyone who preaches a new teaching to you contrary to what I write, so that you may be saved forever. May the great God be with you and guard you from Satan and from every evil. Amen.

### Notes — front matter
1. **“True good news”** renders the Italian opening claim exactly as written. The claim is the book's own; the apparatus does not endorse it. Scholarship (see Source Audit §0) dates the composition to 14th-century Italy and finds no demonstrable link between this book and the late-antique “Gospel of Barnabas” titles.
2. **Paul named as deceived** is in the Italian from the first page. It is translated as written and not softened. Robert's recorded stance — translate the seam and hard sayings exactly, footnote, never smooth — is followed here and throughout.
3. Folio markers 3a/3b are the Ragg edition's manuscript foliation. Quotations of the Italian are kept short throughout this draft; the rendering is a fresh translation, not a reprint of the 1907 English.

---

## Chapter 1

*[Editorial heading: The angel Gabriel announces Jesus to the Virgin Mary. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “I b”): “In this first chapter is contained the announcement of the angel Gabriel to the Virgin Mary concerning the birth of Jesus.” The rubric wording is the edition's; the heading above is editorial.]*

In these last years a virgin named Mary — of the line of David, of the tribe of Judah — was visited by the angel Gabriel from God. This virgin lived in all holiness, without any offence, blameless, staying in prayer with fasting. One day, when she was alone, the angel Gabriel came into her room and greeted her, saying, “God be with you, O Mary.”

The virgin was frightened at the sight of the angel, but the angel comforted her, saying, “Do not fear, Mary, because you are pleasing to God, who has chosen you to be the mother of a prophet whom he will send to the people of Israel, so that they may walk in his law with truth of heart.”

The virgin answered, “Now how shall I bear children, since I do not know a man?” The angel answered, “O Mary, God, who made man without a man, is able to bring forth man in you without a man, because with him nothing is impossible.” Mary answered, “I know that God is almighty; so let his will be done.”

The angel answered, “Now let the prophet be conceived in you. You shall call him Jesus, and you shall keep him from wine and from strong drink and from every unclean food, because the child is a holy one of God.” Mary bowed down with humility, saying, “Look, I am God's servant; let it be done according to your word.”

The angel left, and the virgin praised God, saying, “Know the greatness of God, O my soul, and rejoice, my spirit, in God my Saviour, because he has looked on the lowliness of his servant, so that I shall be called blessed by all the nations — because he who is mighty has done great things for me, and blessed be his holy name. His mercy reaches from generation to generation to those who fear him. He has shown strength with his hand and has scattered the proud in the thought of their heart. He has put down the powerful from their seats and has lifted up the humble. The hungry he has filled with good things, and the rich he has sent away empty — because he remembers the promises made to Abraham and to his son forever.”

### Notes — Chapter 1
1. **“Keep him from wine … every unclean food”** is already present at the annunciation (folio 4a). It is a dietary rule stated as God's command in this book; compare the same charge repeated to Joseph in chapter 2. No comment is added in the text; the reader sees it as written.
2. **“To Abraham and to his son”** — the Ragg English notes the son as Ishmael (citing the book's later chapters). The Italian here says only “his son.” The identification is therefore footnoted, not put into the rendering.
3. Mary's song closely follows Luke 1:46–55 in its medieval Italian form. Parallels are noted here rather than harmonised away.

---

## Chapter 2

*[Editorial heading: The angel warns Joseph not to leave Mary.]*

Mary, having learned God's will, feared the people — that they might take offence at her being pregnant and stone her as guilty of fornication — so she chose a companion from her own line, a man named Joseph, of blameless life. For, as a righteous man, he feared God and served him with fasting and prayer, living by the work of his hands, because he was a carpenter.

The virgin, knowing such a man, chose him as her companion and told him God's counsel. Joseph, being righteous, when he understood that Mary was pregnant, wanted to leave her, because he feared God. But look: while he slept, he was rebuked by God's angel, who said, “O Joseph, why do you want to leave Mary your wife? Know that everything wrought in her has been done, by God's will. The virgin will bear a son. You shall call him by the name Jesus. You shall keep him from wine and strong drink and from every unclean food, because he is a holy one of God in his mother's womb. He is a prophet of God sent to the people of Israel, so that he may turn Judah to his heart and Israel may walk in the law of the Lord, as it is written in the law of Moses. He will come with great power, which God will give him, and will work great miracles, by which many will be saved.”

Joseph rose from sleep, thanked God, and stayed with Mary all the years of his life, serving God with all sincerity.

### Notes — Chapter 2
1. **“Your wife”** is what the angel calls Mary before any wedding scene is narrated (folio 5a). The word is translated as written.
2. The charge about wine, strong drink, and unclean food is repeated word-for-word from chapter 1. The repetition is the book's own emphasis and is kept.

---

## Chapter 3

*[Editorial heading: The wonderful birth of Jesus.]*

At that time Herod reigned in Judea by decree of Caesar Augustus, and Pilate was governor in the priesthood of Annas and Caiaphas. So, by Augustus's decree, the whole world was enrolled: everyone went to his own country and presented themselves by their own tribes to be enrolled. Joseph therefore left Nazareth, a city of Galilee, with Mary his wife, who was pregnant, to go to Bethlehem — because it was his city, he being of the line of David — so that he might be enrolled according to Caesar's decree.

When Joseph reached Bethlehem — the city being small and the crowd of strangers there great — he found no place, so he took lodging outside the city in a lodging made as a shelter for shepherds. While Joseph stayed there, the days for Mary to give birth were fulfilled. The virgin was surrounded by an immense light and bore her son without pain. She took him in her arms, wrapped him in cloths, and laid him in the manger, because there was no room in the lodging. A great crowd of angels came with joy to the lodging, blessing God and announcing peace to those who fear God. Mary and Joseph praised the Lord over the birth of Jesus and raised him with the greatest joy.

### Notes — Chapter 3
1. **Herod, Pilate, Annas and Caiaphas are placed together** at the birth (folio 5b). Historically these belong to different moments. The anachronism is left standing in the text and noted here, per the witness-first method: the fork is shown, not corrected in the rendering.
2. **“Without pain”** (folio 6a: *senza dolore* in modern spelling) contradicts the Qur'an's birth account (Q 19:23), where Mary suffers pain. The difference is noted because readers are often told the two books agree everywhere; here they do not. See Source Audit §0.
3. Nazareth is called “a city of Galilee” here without the later geographical errors (e.g., Nazareth on the sea-shore) that appear further on. Each place is translated as it stands in its own chapter.

---

## Chapter 4

*[Editorial heading: Angels announce Jesus to the shepherds, who find him and spread the news.]*

At that time the shepherds were watching over their flock, as is their custom. And look: they were surrounded by an immense light, from which an angel appeared to them, blessing God. The shepherds were filled with fear at the sudden light and the angel's appearance, so the angel of the Lord comforted them, saying, “Look, I announce to you a great joy: a child, a prophet of the Lord, is born in the city of David. He brings great salvation to the house of Israel. You will find the child in the manger, with his mother, who blesses God.” And when he had said this, a great crowd of angels came, blessing God and announcing peace to those who have good will.

When the angels had left, the shepherds spoke among themselves, saying, “Let us go as far as Bethlehem and see the word that God has announced to us through his angel.” Many shepherds came to Bethlehem looking for the newborn child, and they found outside the city the child who had been born, lying in the manger, just as the angel had said. They bowed down to him and gave to the mother what they had, telling her what they had heard and seen. Mary kept everything in her heart, and Joseph thanked God.

The shepherds went back to their flock, announcing to everyone what they had seen. So the whole hill country of Judea was filled with fear, and every man laid this word up in his heart, saying, “What do we think this child will be?”

### Notes — Chapter 4
1. **“A child, a prophet of the Lord”** (folio 6b) is the angel's whole title for Jesus here — prophet, not son or Messiah. The Messiah question (chapters 42, 96–97) has not yet arisen; the terms are kept distinct chapter by chapter so the later seam can be seen cleanly when it comes.
2. “Peace to those who have good will” follows the Latin Vulgate form of Luke 2:14, which this book habitually uses. The dependence is noted, not hidden.

---

## Chapter 5

*[Editorial heading: The circumcision of Jesus.]*

When the eight days were fulfilled, according to the law of the Lord as it is written in the book of Moses, they took the child and carried him to the temple to circumcise him. So they circumcised the child and gave him the name Jesus, as the angel of the Lord had said before he was conceived in the womb. Mary and Joseph understood that this child was to be for the salvation and the ruin of many, so they feared God and kept the child with fear of God.

### Notes — Chapter 5
1. Circumcision on the eighth day is presented as obedience to “the law of the Lord … in the book of Moses” (folio 7a). This matters for the book's own argument (front matter: circumcision as God's covenant forever). The practice is translated as written.
2. “For the salvation and the ruin of many” echoes Luke 2:34 (Simeon). No Simeon scene is narrated here; Mary and Joseph themselves perceive it. The difference from Luke is left visible.

---

---

## Chapter 6

*[Editorial heading: Three wise men are led by a star to Judea, and ask for the newborn King. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “VI”): “Three magi are guided by a star from the east into Judea and, finding Jesus, do him reverence and gifts.” The rubric wording is the edition's; the heading above is editorial.]*

While Herod was king of Judea, after Jesus was born, three wise men in the eastern parts were watching the stars of heaven. A star appeared to them with great brightness. After they had talked it over together, they came to Judea, with the star going ahead of them and guiding them. When they reached Jerusalem they asked where the King of the Jews had been born.

When Herod heard this he was afraid, and the whole city was stirred up. Herod called the priests and the scribes together and asked where Christ was to be born. They answered that he was to be born in Bethlehem, because it is written by the prophet: “And you, Bethlehem, are not small among the rulers of Judah, for from you will come a leader who will lead my people Israel.”

Then Herod called the wise men and asked them about their coming. They answered that they had seen a star in the east, and that it had guided them there, and that with gifts they wanted to worship that new King whom his star showed. Then Herod said, “Go to Bethlehem and search with all care for the child. When you have found him, come and tell me, so that I too may go and worship him.” He said this with deceit.

### Notes — Chapter 6
1. **“Christ” asked of Herod's scribes** (folio 7b) is the book's own word here; the later denial that Jesus is the Messiah (ch. 42, 96–97) has not yet entered. The terms are kept chapter by chapter so the seam can be seen when it comes.
2. The Bethlehem saying follows Micah 5:2 in its medieval Italian form, close to Matthew 2:6. The ruler word is *ducha* (“leader/duke”) in the Italian; the rendering says “leader.”
3. Folio markers 7b etc. are the Ragg edition's manuscript foliation. Quotations of the Italian are kept short; the rendering is a fresh translation, not a reprint of the 1907 English.

---

## Chapter 7

*[Editorial heading: The wise men visit Jesus and return home by another road.]*

The wise men left Jerusalem, and look: the star that had appeared to them in the east went ahead of them. Seeing the star, the wise men were filled with joy. When they came to Bethlehem, outside the city, they saw the star standing still over the lodging where Jesus had been born.

The wise men went in there, and entering the room they found the child with his mother. They bowed down and did him reverence, and they presented to him fragrant things with silver and gold. The wise men told the virgin everything they had seen. While they slept they were warned by the child in a vision not to go to Herod. So they left by another road and returned to their home, announcing everything they had seen in Judea.

### Notes — Chapter 7
1. **Silver is named with the gold** (folio 8a). Matthew 2:11 names gold, frankincense, and myrrh; this book names fragrant things “with silver and gold.” The difference is left standing.
2. **The child himself warns the wise men in a vision** as they sleep. In Matthew 2:12 the warning comes in a dream without naming the child as speaker. The book's own agency is translated as written.

---

## Chapter 8

*[Editorial heading: Jesus is taken to Egypt, and Herod kills the young children.]*

When Herod saw that the wise men did not return, he thought he had been mocked by them, and he decided to have the newborn child killed. But look: while Joseph slept, the angel of the Lord appeared to him, saying, “Get up quickly, take the child with his mother, and go into Egypt, because Herod wants to kill him.”

Joseph got up with great fear, took Mary with the child, and they went into Egypt. They stayed there until Herod's death. Herod, thinking he had been tricked by the wise men, sent his soldiers to kill every child newly born in Bethlehem. The soldiers came and killed every child who was there, as Herod had ordered them. So the prophet's words were fulfilled, saying: “Weeping and tears are great in Ramah. Rachel weeps for her children, but no comfort is given her, because they are not found.”

### Notes — Chapter 8
1. The Ramah saying follows Jeremiah 31:15 as quoted in Matthew 2:18, in the book's Italian wording. “They are not found” renders the Italian singular-verb construction noted by the 1907 editors; the sense is kept plain.
2. The flight and the killing are told in the book's own order without added detail. No historical comment is put into the text.

---

## Chapter 9

*[Editorial heading: Jesus returns to Judea; at twelve he disputes with the teachers in the temple. The edition prints this as “IX a,” the pilgrimage chapter.]*

After Herod died, look: the angel of the Lord appeared in a dream to Joseph, saying, “Return to Judea, because those who want the child's death are dead.” Joseph took the child with Mary — Jesus had now reached the age of seven years — and came to Judea. Hearing that Archelaus, Herod's son, was reigning in Judea, he was afraid to stay in Judea and went into Galilee. They went to live in Nazareth.

The child grew in grace and wisdom before God and before people. When Jesus reached the age of twelve years he went up with Mary and Joseph to Jerusalem to worship there according to the law of the Lord written in the book of Moses. When the prayer was finished they left, having lost Jesus, because they thought he had gone home with the relatives. Mary went back with Joseph to Jerusalem, looking for Jesus among relatives and neighbours. On the third day they found the child in the temple, in the middle of the teachers, disputing with them about the law.

Everyone was amazed at his questions and his answers, saying, “How can such teaching be in him, when he is so small and has not learned to read?” Mary rebuked him, saying, “Son, what have you done to us? Look, I and your father have looked for you for three days in sorrow.” Jesus answered, “Do you not know that the service of God must be put before father and mother?” Jesus went down with his mother and Joseph to Nazareth, and he was subject to them with humility and reverence.

### Notes — Chapter 9
1. **Age seven at the return** (folio 8b–9a) sits close before the twelfth year in the same chapter. Both ages are translated as written; the tight chronology is the book's own and is not smoothed.
2. **“Has not learned to read”** is the crowd's wonder in the Italian. Luke 2:46–47 has amazement at understanding and answers without the reading note. The difference is left visible.
3. “Service of God before father and mother” is Jesus' answer here; compare Luke 2:49. The wording is this book's, not imported from the check column.

---

## Chapter 10

*[Editorial heading: At thirty, on the Mount of Olives, Jesus receives the gospel from the angel Gabriel.]*

When Jesus reached the age of thirty years — as he himself told me — he had gone up to the Mount of Olives with his mother to gather olives. Then at midday, while he was praying, when he came to these words, “Lord, with mercy,” Jesus was surrounded by an immense brightness and by an endless crowd of angels who were saying, “Blessed be God.”

The angel Gabriel presented to him, like a shining mirror, a book, which went down into Jesus' heart. In that book he knew what God has done, what God has said, and what God wants, so that everything was bare and open to him. As he told me, saying: “Believe, Barnabas, that I knew every prophet with every prophecy, so that whatever I say, the whole of it has come out of that book.”

When Jesus had received this vision and knew that he was a prophet sent to the house of Israel, he revealed everything to Mary his mother, telling her that he had to suffer great persecution for God's honour, and that he could no longer stay with her continually to serve her. When Mary heard this she answered, “Son, before you were born everything was announced to me. So blessed be the holy name of God.” That day Jesus left his mother to attend to his prophetic work.

### Notes — Chapter 10
1. **“As he himself told me”** (folio 9b) is Barnabas the narrator stepping into the story. The first-person frame is kept, as the witness-first method requires; the apparatus does not endorse the frame.
2. **A book goes down into Jesus' heart** through Gabriel, like a shining mirror. The image is the book's own account of how Jesus receives the gospel. The 1907 editors noted the parallel they saw with revelation language elsewhere; that comparison stays in scholarship, not in the rendering.
3. This chapter closes the childhood and opens the ministry at thirty. The next slice continues at chapter 11 (the healing of a leper on the way down to Jerusalem).

---

---

## Chapter 11

*[Editorial heading: Jesus heals a man with leprosy on the way to Jerusalem. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XI”): “Jesus miraculously heals a leper and goes into Jerusalem.” The rubric wording is the edition's; the heading above is editorial.]*

As Jesus came down from the mountain to go to Jerusalem, he met a man with leprosy. By God's inspiration the man knew that Jesus was a prophet, so with tears he begged him, saying, “Jesus, son of David, have mercy on me.”

Jesus answered, “Brother, what do you want me to do for you?”

The man with leprosy answered, “Lord, give me health.”

Jesus rebuked him, saying, “You are foolish. Pray to God who created you, and he will give you health — for I am a man, just as you are.”

The man answered, “I know that you, Lord, are a man, but a holy one of the Lord. So you pray to God, and he will give me health.”

Then Jesus sighed and said, “Lord God almighty, for the love of your holy prophets, give health to this sick man.” Having said this, he touched the sick man with his hands and said, in the name of God, “Brother, receive your health.”

When he had said this, the leprosy was cleansed, and the man's flesh was left like a child's. Seeing that he was healed, the man cried out loudly, “Come, Israel, receive the prophet whom God sends you.”

Jesus begged him, “Brother, be quiet and say nothing.” But the more Jesus begged him, the more he cried out, “Look — the prophet! Look — the holy one of God!”

At those words many who were leaving Jerusalem ran back and entered Jerusalem with Jesus, telling what God had done through Jesus for the man with leprosy.

### Notes — Chapter 11
1. **“I am a man, just as you are”** (folio 10a–10b) is Jesus' own correction of the man before any healing. It is translated as written; compare the later chapters where the same denial is sharpened. No wording is imported from the check column.
2. **The healed man proclaims Jesus openly after being told to be quiet.** The disobedient proclamation, and the crowd running back into Jerusalem with Jesus, close the chapter in the Italian. The sequence is left as written.
3. Folio markers 10a/10b are the Ragg edition's manuscript foliation. Short Italian phrases are mentioned in these notes only to anchor a reading; the rendering is a fresh translation, not a reprint of the 1907 English.

---

## Chapter 12

*[Editorial heading: Jesus' first sermon to the people — a blessing-litany on the holy name of God. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XII a”): “First sermon of Jesus made to the people, wonderful in teaching, concerning the name of God.” The heading above is editorial.]*

These words stirred the whole city of Jerusalem, and everyone ran together to the temple to see Jesus, who had entered there to pray — so many that they could hardly fit. So the priests begged Jesus, saying, “This people wants to see you and hear you. Go up onto the pinnacle, then, and if God gives you a word, speak it in the name of the Lord.”

Jesus went up to the place where the scribes used to speak. He made a sign with his hand for silence, opened his mouth, and said:

“Blessed be the holy name of God, who in his goodness and mercy willed to create his creatures so that they would glorify him.

“Blessed be the holy name of God, who created the splendour of all the saints and prophets before every other thing, to send him for the salvation of the world — as he spoke through his servant David, saying: ‘Before the morning star, in the splendour of the saints, I created you.’

“Blessed be the holy name of God, who created the angels so that they would serve him. And blessed be God, who punished and rejected Satan and his followers, who would not reverence the one whom God wills to be reverenced.

“Blessed be the holy name of God, who created man from the clay of the earth and set him over his works.

“Blessed be the holy name of God, who drove man out of paradise for breaking his holy command.

“Blessed be the holy name of God, who with mercy looked on the tears of Adam and Eve, the first parents of the human race.

“Blessed be the holy name of God, who justly punished Cain the brother-killer, sent the flood on the earth, burned three wicked cities, scourged Egypt, drowned Pharaoh in the Red Sea, scattered the enemies of his people, chastised the unbelievers, and punished those who would not turn back.

“Blessed be the holy name of God, who with mercy looked on his creatures and so sent them his holy prophets, so that they would walk in truth and uprightness before him; who freed his servants from every evil and gave them this land, as he promised to our father Abraham and to his son, forever. Then through his servant Moses he gave us his holy law, so that Satan would not deceive us, and he raised us above all other peoples.

“But brothers, what are we doing today, so that we are not punished for our sins?”

Then, with the greatest force, Jesus rebuked the people for forgetting the word of God and giving themselves only to empty things. He rebuked the priests for their neglect in God's service and for their greed for earthly things. He rebuked the scribes because they preached empty teaching and left the law of God aside. He rebuked the teachers of the law because they made the law of God of no effect through their traditions.

Jesus spoke to the people in such a way that everyone wept, from the smallest to the greatest, crying for mercy and begging Jesus to pray for them — except only the priests and their leaders, who that day took up hatred against Jesus for speaking so against priests, scribes, and teachers of the law. They thought about his death, but they said no word, for fear of the people, who had received him as a prophet of God.

Jesus raised his hands to the Lord God and prayed, and the people, weeping, said, “So be it, O Lord, so be it.” When the prayer was finished, Jesus went down from the temple, and that day he left Jerusalem with many who followed him. And the priests spoke evil of Jesus among themselves.

### Notes — Chapter 12
1. **The “splendour … before every other thing”** (folio 10b–11a) is an anticipation in the Messiah/Messenger seam zone. The Italian here does not name that splendour in the body of the sermon; the 1907 editors' note identifies the word with the coming Messenger (see their note at this place, citing later folios where the identification is made explicit). The rendering translates only what the sermon says and leaves the identification in this note, so the reader can see the fork when the naming becomes explicit at chapters 42 and 96–97. Nothing is smoothed or pre-named in the text. Robert's recorded fork is carried here, not decided: (a) his suspicion that the named-Messenger / Messiah-denial passages may be later insertions into the book, and (b) their contradiction with the Qur'an, which names Jesus himself the Messiah (Q 3:45; 4:157, 4:171–172). The passage is translated exactly as written — never smoothed, never deleted — and the fork stays open for Robert's ruling (R4).
2. **“To our father Abraham and to his son”** is again left unnamed in the rendering, as in chapters 1 and 9. The 1907 editors identify the son as Ishmael, pointing to the book's later usage; that identification is footnoted, not inserted.
3. The litany moves through creation, the fall, Cain, the flood, Egypt, and the giving of the law through Moses before turning to the present rebuke. Each “Blessed be the holy name of God” beat is kept, because the repetition is the sermon's form in the Italian.

---

## Chapter 13

*[Editorial heading: Jesus prays through the night in fear; the angel Gabriel comforts him and gives him a ram to offer. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XIII e.”): “The notable fear of Jesus, and his prayer, and the wonderful comfort of the angel Gabriel.” The heading above is editorial.]*

Some days later, Jesus knew in the spirit what the priests wanted, so he went up the Mount of Olives to pray. He spent the night in prayer, and in the morning, as he prayed, Jesus said:

“O Lord, I know that the scribes hate me and the priests are thinking about the death of me, your servant. So, Lord God, almighty and merciful, in mercy hear the prayers of your servant and save me from their traps, for you are my salvation. You know, Lord, that I, your servant, seek you alone, Lord, and I speak your word — for your word is truth, which lasts forever.”

When Jesus had said these words, look: the angel Gabriel came to him, saying, “Do not fear, Jesus, for a thousand times a thousand who live above heaven guard your garments, and you will not die until everything is fulfilled and the world is near its end.”

Jesus fell with his face to the ground, saying, “O great Lord God, how great is your mercy on me! And what shall I give you, Lord, for all you have given me?”

The angel Gabriel answered, “Get up, Jesus, and remember Abraham. Wanting to offer God a sacrifice of his only son Ishmael, to fulfil God's word, when the knife could not cut his son, at my word he offered a sheep in sacrifice. So you too shall do, Jesus, servant of God.”

Jesus answered, “Gladly — but where will I find the lamb? I have no money, and it is not lawful to steal it.”

Then the angel Gabriel showed him a ram, and Jesus offered it in sacrifice, praising and blessing God, who is glorious forever.

### Notes — Chapter 13
1. **Abraham's son is named Ishmael here** (folio 12b), in the angel's mouth, not left unnamed as in chapters 1, 9, and 12. The name is translated exactly as written. In Genesis the son bound for sacrifice is Isaac; the Qur'an (37:99–113) does not name the son. The difference is left standing and noted, not harmonised.
2. **“You will not die until everything is fulfilled”** is Gabriel's assurance to Jesus in this book's telling. It is kept as written; the later passion chapters (214–222, single-witness) will carry their own flag when reached.
3. The ram is shown by Gabriel after Jesus refuses theft. The offering that follows closes the night of prayer; no atonement comment is added in the text.

---

## Chapter 14

*[Editorial heading: After a forty-day fast Jesus chooses twelve apostles. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XIV d.”): “After the fast of forty days Jesus chooses twelve apostles.” The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus came down from the mountain and crossed alone, at night, to the other side of the Jordan. He fasted forty days and forty nights, eating nothing day or night, praying continually to the Lord for the salvation of his people, to whom God had sent him. When the forty days had passed, he was hungry.

Then Satan appeared to him there and tempted him with many words, but Jesus drove him away by the power of God's words. When Satan had left, the angels came and gave Jesus what he needed.

Jesus returned to the region of Jerusalem and was found again by the people with the greatest joy. They begged him to stay with them, for his words were not like the scribes' words; they carried power, because they touched the heart.

Seeing that the crowd was great — people turning back in their hearts to walk in God's law — Jesus went up the mountain and stayed all night in prayer. When day came he went down from the mountain and chose twelve, whom he called apostles. Among them is Judas, the one who was killed on the cross.

Their names are: Andrew and his brother Peter, fishermen; Barnabas, who wrote this, with Matthew the tax-collector, who sat at the tax bench; John and James, sons of Zebedee; Thaddaeus and Judas; Bartholomew and Philip; James; and Judas Iscariot the traitor.

To these he always made known the divine secrets. But Judas Iscariot he made the keeper of what was given in alms — and he stole a tenth of everything.

### Notes — Chapter 14
1. **Barnabas is named among the Twelve** (folio 13b), as one of the chosen apostles and as the writer (“who wrote this”). The claim is the book's own frame and is translated as written; the apparatus does not endorse apostolic authorship (see Source Audit §0).
2. **“Judas, the one who was killed on the cross”** is stated here, long before the passion. It foreshadows chapters 214–222, where Judas is crucified in Jesus' place in this book's account. The statement is kept where the Italian puts it, not moved or softened.
3. **The list as written** names two men called Judas (Thaddaeus and Judas are listed as separate names, and Judas Iscariot is listed again at the end) and does not include Thomas or Simon in this form. The names are rendered in the Italian's order; no canonical list is imported to correct it.
4. **Uncertain reading at “secrets.”** The Italian at folio 13b reads, in the 1907 print, a shortened form before “the divine secrets” that the 1907 editors already flagged as defective, suggesting words may have dropped out. The sense “he always made known / revealed the divine secrets” is the check-column sense; the exact verb cannot be settled from the public-domain print alone and is flagged here as uncertain rather than imported from a modern edition. This is the ch. 14 corrigendum spot named in advance in the Source Audit (§2); Spanish collation (Sydney witness, ch. 1–120) has not yet been performed for this line and may be cited as a fact later, with Robert's ruling on R6 governing its depth.

---

## Chapter 15

*[Editorial heading: At a wedding Jesus turns water into wine. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XV”): “Miracle done by Jesus at the wedding, turning water into wine.” The heading above is editorial.]*

As the feast of Tabernacles drew near, a rich man invited Jesus, with his apostles and his mother, to a wedding. Jesus went, and while they were eating, the wine ran out.

His mother came to Jesus and said, “They have no wine.”

Jesus answered, “What is that to me, my mother?”

His mother told the servants to obey whatever Jesus commanded them. There were six water jars there, used in Israel's custom for washing before prayer.

Jesus said, “Fill those jars with water.” The servants did so. Jesus said to them, “In the name of God, give a drink to those who are eating.”

The servants carried it to the master of the feast, who rebuked the servants, saying, “O worthless servants, why have you kept the better wine until now?” — for he knew nothing of what Jesus had done.

The servants answered, “O sir, there is a holy man of God here, for he has made wine out of water.”

The master of the feast thought the servants were drunk. But those who were sitting near Jesus, having seen the whole thing, rose from the table and bowed to him, saying, “Truly you are a holy one of God, a true prophet sent to us by God.”

Then his disciples believed in him, and many turned back in their hearts, saying, “Praised be God, who has mercy on Israel and visits the house of Judah with love, and blessed be his holy name.”

### Notes — Chapter 15
1. **The wedding is placed at the feast of Tabernacles** in this book (folio 14a), not at Cana as in John 2:1–11, and no place-name is given. The setting is translated as written; the Johannine parallel is noted here only.
2. **“What is that to me, my mother?”** keeps the Italian's abrupt address without softening it into a canonical wording. Compare chapter 10, where Jesus' leaving his mother is narrated without rebuke.
3. The six jars are for washing before prayer “according to the custom of Israel,” as the Italian puts it. The servants, not the master of the feast, are the first witnesses named; the master's charge that the better wine was kept back closes the scene in the Italian order.

---

---

## Chapter 16

*[Editorial heading: Jesus teaches the apostles to leave the evil life behind and become new men. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XVI c.”): “Wonderful teaching given by Jesus to his apostles concerning conversion from the evil life.” The heading above is editorial.]*

One day Jesus called his disciples together and went up on the mountain. When he had sat down there, his disciples came near to him, and he opened his mouth and taught them, saying:

“Great are the benefits God has given us, so we must serve him with truth of heart. Just as new wine is put into new vessels, so you must become new men if you are to hold the new teaching that will come from my mouth.

“I tell you in truth: just as a man cannot see heaven and earth with his eyes at one and the same time, so it is impossible to love God and the world.

“No one can serve two masters who are enemies of each other, because if one loves you, the other will hate you. So I tell you in truth, you cannot serve God and the world, because the world lies in falsehood, greed, and evil. You cannot find rest in the world — only persecution and loss. So serve God and despise the world, and from me you will find rest for your souls. Listen to my words, because I speak to you in truth.

“Truly blessed are those who mourn this earthly life, because they will be comforted. Blessed are the poor who truly hate the pleasures of the world, because they will have plenty in the pleasures of God's kingdom. Truly blessed are those who eat at God's table, because the angels will serve them.

“You are on a journey, like pilgrims. Does a pilgrim load himself down on the road with palaces and fields and other earthly things? Certainly not. He carries light things, valued because they are useful and make the journey easier. That should be your pattern. And if you want another pattern, I will give it to you, so that you do everything I tell you.

“Do not weigh your hearts down with earthly desires, saying, ‘Who will clothe us?’ or ‘Who will give us food?’ Look at the flowers and the trees, with the birds: God our Lord clothes them and feeds them with greater glory than all Solomon's glory. He is able to feed you too — God, who created you and called you to his service. For forty years he made manna rain from heaven for his people Israel in the desert, and he did not let their clothes grow old or tear, though they were six hundred and forty thousand men, besides women and children.

“I tell you in truth, heaven and earth will fail, but his mercy will not fail for those who fear him. But the rich of the world, in their happiness, are hungry and perish.

“There was a rich man whose income grew. He said, ‘What shall I do, my soul? I will pull down my barns because they are small, and I will build new, bigger ones, and then you will triumph, my soul!’ O wretched man — that night he died. He should have remembered the poor and made friends for himself by giving alms from the unrighteous wealth of this world, because friends like that carry treasures into the kingdom of heaven.

“Tell me, please: if you gave money to a banker and he gave you back ten or twenty for one, would you not give such a man everything you had? But I tell you in truth, whatever you give and leave behind for love of God, you will receive a hundredfold, and eternal life besides. See then how glad you should be to serve God.”

### Notes — Chapter 16
1. **“New wine … new vessels / new men”** (folio 14b) opens the teaching and sets its form: the change of life is put as a change of vessel. The saying is translated in its place here; no canonical wording is imported to replace it.
2. **Six hundred and forty thousand men** is the number as written for Israel in the desert. The 1907 editors' check note points to the different number in Exodus/Numbers (600,000). The book's number is kept as written and the difference noted, not corrected in the text.
3. The rich man's speech (“you will triumph, my soul”) is left close to the Italian order. The parable is used here as a warning about hoarding, ending with the bank image and the hundredfold promise; no application is added in the text.

---

## Chapter 17

*[Editorial heading: Philip asks to know God; Jesus describes God and speaks of the Splendour to come after him. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XVII b.”): “In this chapter is clearly perceived the unbelief of Christians, and the true faith of Mumin.” The heading above is editorial.]*

When Jesus had said this, Philip answered, “We are content to serve God, but we still want to know God, because Isaiah the prophet said, ‘Truly you are a hidden God,’ and God said to Moses his servant, ‘I am that which I am.’”

Jesus answered, “Philip, God is a good without which there is no good; God is a being without which nothing is; God is a life without which nothing lives. He is so great that he fills everything and is everywhere. He alone has no equal. He had no beginning and will never have an end, but he has given a beginning to everything and will give an end to everything. He has no father or mother; he has no sons, no brothers, and no companions. And because God has no body, he does not eat, does not sleep, does not die, does not walk, does not move, but remains forever without any human likeness — because he is without body, uncompounded, without matter, of the simplest substance. He is so good that he loves only goodness; he is so just that when he punishes or pardons, it cannot be argued against. In short, I tell you, Philip, here on earth you cannot see him or know him perfectly, but in his kingdom you will see him forever, and in that is all our happiness and glory.”

Philip answered, “Master, what are you saying? It is surely written in Isaiah that God is our father. How then does he have no sons?”

Jesus answered, “In all the prophets many parables are written, so you must not hold to the letter but to the sense. For all the prophets — one hundred and forty-four thousand of them, whom God has sent into the world — have spoken darkly. But after me will come the Splendour of all the prophets and holy ones, and he will shed light on the darkness of everything the prophets have said, because he is God's messenger.”

Having said this, Jesus sighed and said, “Have mercy on Israel, O Lord God, and look with pity on Abraham and on his seed, so that they may serve you with truth of heart.”

His disciples answered, “So be it, O Lord our God.”

Jesus said, “I tell you in truth, the scribes and teachers have made God's law empty with their false prophecies, against the prophecies of God's true prophets. So God is angry with the house of Israel and with this faithless generation.”

His disciples wept at these words and said, “Have mercy, O God, have mercy on the temple and on the holy city, and do not give it over to the scorn of the nations, so that they do not despise your holy covenant.”

Jesus answered, “So be it, Lord God of our fathers.”

### Notes — Chapter 17
1. **“God is a good … a being … a life”** (folio 16a) is the book's compact statement about God: no father, mother, sons, brothers, or companions; no body; no eating, sleeping, death, walking, or moving. It is translated as written. The 1907 editors' margin at this place gathers the Arabic glosses (“Chapter of Sincerity”); the rendering follows the Italian body, not the gloss.
2. **“One hundred and forty-four thousand” prophets** is the number as written. It is kept; no canonical number is substituted.
3. **The Splendour / messenger anticipation** (folio 16b): the Italian here says that after Jesus will come the Splendour of all the prophets and holy ones, who will light up the prophets' dark sayings “because he is God's messenger.” The figure is **not named in the body of this chapter**; the 1907 editors' note at this place (“splendore, i. e. Mohammed, cp. i6b, 46b, called commonly nontio … identified with Messiah”) identifies him with the coming Messenger — the Italian's *nontio*, the same figure the editors say is identified with the Messiah in the later chapters. As with chapter 12, the rendering translates only what the speech says here and leaves the identification in this note, so the reader sees the fork when the naming becomes explicit at chapters 42 and 96–97. Nothing is smoothed or pre-named in the text. Robert's recorded fork is carried here, not decided: (a) his suspicion that the named-Messenger / Messiah-denial passages may be later insertions into the book, and (b) their contradiction with the Qur'an, which names Jesus himself the Messiah (Q 3:45; 4:157, 4:171–172). The passage is translated exactly as written — never smoothed, never deleted — and the fork stays open for Robert's ruling (R4).
4. **“Prophets have spoken darkly … attend to the sense, not the letter”** is Jesus' stated rule for reading the “father” language Philip raises. It is kept in place, because later chapters return to the letter/sense distinction at the seam.

---

## Chapter 18

*[Editorial heading: The world will persecute God's servants, but God guards them, hair by hair. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XVIII a.”): “Here is shown forth the persecution of the servants of God by the world, and God's protection saving them.” The heading above is editorial.]*

Having said this, Jesus said, “You have not chosen me; I have chosen you, so that you will be my disciples. If the world hates you, you will truly be my disciples, because the world has always been an enemy of God's servants. Remember the holy prophets who were killed by the world: in Elijah's time ten thousand prophets were killed by Jezebel, so that poor Elijah barely escaped, along with seven thousand sons of prophets whom the captain of Ahab's army had hidden. O unrighteous world, you do not know God!

“Do not be afraid, because the hairs of your head are counted so that they will not perish. Look at the sparrows and other birds: not one feather falls without God's will. Will God then care more for the birds than for man, for whom he created everything? Is there any man who cares more for his shoes than for his own son? Certainly not. So how much less should you think that God would abandon you, when he cares even for the birds? And why speak of birds? A leaf of a tree does not fall without God's will.

“Believe me, because I tell you the truth: the world will fear you greatly if you keep my words. If it did not fear that its evil would be uncovered, it would not hate you; but it fears being uncovered, so it will hate you and persecute you. If you see your words scorned by the world, do not be sad. Consider that God is greater than you, and he is so scorned by the world that his wisdom is counted as madness. If God bears with the world in patience, why would you be sad, O dust and clay of the earth?

“In your patience you will possess your soul. So if someone gives you a blow on one side of the face, offer him the other side so he can strike it. Do not return evil for evil, because that is what all the worst animals do. Return good for evil instead, and pray to God for those who hate you. Fire is not put out with fire, but with water; so I tell you, you will not overcome evil with evil, but with good. Look at God, who makes the sun come on the good and the evil, and the rain likewise. So you must do good to everyone, because it is written in the law: ‘Be holy, because I, your God, am holy; be clean, because I am clean; and be perfect, because I am perfect.’

“I tell you in truth, a servant tries to please his master, so he does not put on anything that displeases his master. Your clothing is your will and your love. Take care, then, not to will or love anything that displeases God our Lord. Be sure of this: God hates the world's show and lusts, so you must hate the world.”

### Notes — Chapter 18
1. **Ten thousand prophets killed in Elijah's time** (folio 17a) is the figure as written. The 1907 editors' check note points to 1 Kings 18 (100 prophets hidden) and 1 Kings 19:18 (7,000). The book's figure is kept as written and the difference noted, not corrected.
2. **“A leaf … does not fall”** extends the sparrows/feather saying beyond its canonical form. The extension is kept where the Italian puts it; the editors' margin cites the gloss tradition for the line.
3. **“Your clothing is your will and your love”** turns the servant/master image inward. The rendering keeps the clothing metaphor rather than replacing it with a general word for conduct.

---

## Chapter 19

*[Editorial heading: Jesus foretells the betrayal, comforts Barnabas, and heals ten men with leprosy; one returns. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XIX f.”): “Jesus foretelleth his betrayal, and, descending from the mountain, healeth ten lepers.” The heading above is editorial.]*

When Jesus had said this, Peter answered, “Teacher, look — we have left everything to follow you. What will become of us?”

Jesus answered, “Truly, on the day of judgement you will sit beside me, giving testimony against the twelve tribes of Israel.”

Having said this, Jesus sighed and said, “O Lord, what is this? I have chosen twelve, and one of them is a devil.”

The disciples were deeply grieved at this word. Then the one who writes this questioned Jesus secretly, weeping, and said, “Master, will Satan deceive me, and will I then become rejected?”

Jesus answered, “Do not grieve, Barnabas, because those whom God chose before the creation of the world will not perish. Rejoice, because your name is written in the book of life.”

Jesus comforted his disciples, saying, “Do not fear, because the one who will hate me is not grieved at my speaking — there is no feeling for God in him.” At his words the chosen ones were comforted.

Jesus made his prayers, and his disciples said, “Amen — so be it, Lord God, almighty and merciful.”

When the prayer was finished, Jesus came down from the mountain with his disciples and met ten men with leprosy. From far off they cried out, “Jesus, son of David, have mercy on us!”

Jesus called them near to him and said to them, “What do you want from me, brothers?”

They all cried out, “Give us health!”

Jesus answered, “O poor men, have you lost your reason, that you say, ‘Give us health’? Do you not see that I am a man like you? Call on our God who created you — he is almighty and merciful, and he will heal you.”

With tears the men answered, “We know you are a man like us, but still a holy one of God and a prophet of the Lord. So you pray to God, and he will heal us.”

Then the disciples begged Jesus, saying, “Lord, have mercy on them.”

Then Jesus groaned and prayed to God, saying, “Lord God, almighty and merciful, have mercy and hear the words of your servant. For love of Abraham our father and for your holy covenant, have mercy on what these men ask, and give them health.”

Having said this, Jesus turned to the men and said, “Go and show yourselves to the priests, according to God's law.”

The men left, and on the way they were cleansed. One of them, seeing he was healed, came back to find Jesus — this one was an Ishmaelite. Having found Jesus, he bowed down and honoured him, saying, “Truly you are a holy one of God.” With thanks he begged Jesus to take him as a servant.

Jesus answered, “Ten were cleansed; where are the nine?” And he said to the cleansed man, “I have not come to be served, but to serve. So go to your home and tell how much God has done in you, so that they may know that the promises made to Abraham and to his son, with the kingdom of God, are drawing near.”

The cleansed man left, and when he reached his own country he told how much God, through Jesus, had done in him.

### Notes — Chapter 19
1. **“One of them is a devil”** (folio 18a) is spoken here, just after the promise to sit in judgement. As in chapter 14 (“Judas, the one who was killed on the cross”), the betrayal is foreshadowed early and left in place, not moved or softened. Chapters 214–222 will carry the single-witness flag when reached.
2. **The secret question of “the one who writes this”** names Barnabas in Jesus' answer (“Do not grieve, Barnabas … your name is written in the book of life”). The self-reference is the book's own frame and is translated as written; the apparatus does not endorse apostolic authorship (see Source Audit §0).
3. **The returned man is named an Ishmaelite** (folio 19b). In Luke 17 the returned man is a Samaritan. The book's identification is kept as written and the difference noted, not harmonised.
4. **“The promises made to Abraham and to his son”** is again unnamed in the body here. The son is not named in this chapter's speech; chapter 13 named Ishmael in the angel's speech. The pattern (sometimes named, sometimes not) is preserved rather than levelled.

---

## Chapter 20

*[Editorial heading: Jesus calms a storm at sea; at Nazareth he is rejected, as no prophet is received at home. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XX b.”): “Miracle on the sea wrought by Jesus, and Jesus declares where the prophet is received.” The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus went to the Sea of Galilee and got into a boat and sailed to Nazareth, his city. A great storm rose on the sea, so that the boat was close to sinking. Jesus was sleeping on the prow of the boat. His disciples came near to him and woke him, saying, “Master, save yourself, because we are perishing!” They were surrounded by great fear because of the strong contrary wind and the roaring of the sea.

Jesus stood up, raised his eyes to heaven, and said, “O Elohim Sabaoth, have mercy on your servants.” When Jesus had said this, the wind stopped at once and the sea became calm. The sailors were afraid and said, “Who is this, that the sea and the wind obey him?”

When they reached the city of Nazareth, the sailors filled the city with what Jesus had done. The house where Jesus was staying was surrounded by everyone who lived in the city. The scribes and teachers came to him and said, “We have heard how much you have done on the sea and in Judaea. Give us some sign here, then, in your own country.”

Jesus answered, “This faithless generation seeks a sign, but it will not be given to them, because no prophet is received in his own country. In Elijah's time there were many widows in Judaea, but he was sent to be fed only by a widow of Sidon. Many men with leprosy were in Judaea in Elisha's time; still, only Naaman the Syrian was cleansed.”

Then the citizens were enraged. They seized him and carried him to the top of a precipice to throw him down. But Jesus walked through the middle of them and left them.

### Notes — Chapter 20
1. **“O Elohim Sabaoth”** (folio 20a) is kept in its Hebrew form as the Italian gives it, rather than translated into a title. The 1907 editors' margin reproduces the gloss explanation of the name; the body text is what is rendered.
2. **The rejection at Nazareth** follows the sea miracle directly in this book's order. The Elijah/Elisha examples (the Sidon widow; Naaman) are the grounds Jesus gives here for “no prophet is received in his own country”; the sequence is left as written (compare Luke 4).
3. The chapter ends with Jesus walking through the crowd and leaving — no rescue or counter-miracle is added in the Italian, and none is added here.

---

---

## Chapter 21

*[Editorial heading: Jesus frees a man possessed by many demons; the demons enter swine and rush into the sea; then he frees the Canaanite woman's daughter. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XXI a.”): “Jesus heals a demoniac, and the swine are cast into the sea. Afterwards he heals the daughter of the Canaanitess.” The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus went up to Capernaum. As he drew near the city, a man came out of the tombs who was possessed, in such a way that no chain could hold him, and he did great harm to people. The demons cried out through his mouth, saying, “O holy one of God, why have you come before the time to trouble us?” And they begged him not to cast them out.

Jesus asked them how many they were. They answered, “Six thousand six hundred and sixty-six.” When the disciples heard this they were frightened, and they begged Jesus to leave. Then Jesus said, “Where is your faith? The demon must leave — not I.”

Then the demons cried, “We will come out, but let us enter those swine.” Near the sea about ten thousand swine were feeding, belonging to Canaanites. Jesus said, “Go out, and enter the swine.” With a great noise the demons entered the swine and drove them headlong into the sea.

Those who fed the swine fled into the city and told all that had happened through Jesus. The people of the city came out and found Jesus and the man who had been healed. The people were filled with fear and begged Jesus to leave their borders.

Jesus left them and went up into the region of Tyre and Sidon. A Canaanite woman with her two sons, who had left her own country to find Jesus, saw him coming with his disciples and cried out, “Jesus, son of David, have mercy on my daughter, who is tormented by the devil!”

Jesus did not answer her even one word, because they were of the uncircumcised people. The disciples were moved with pity and said, “Master, have pity on them — see how much they cry and weep!”

Jesus answered, “I am sent only to the people of Israel.”

Then the woman came before Jesus with her sons, weeping, and said, “Son of David, have mercy on me!” Jesus answered, “It is not good to take the bread from the children's hands and give it to the dogs.” He said this because of their uncleanness, because they were of the uncircumcised people.

The woman answered, “Lord, the dogs eat the crumbs that fall from their masters' table.” Jesus was struck with wonder at the woman's words and said, “Woman, great is your faith.” He raised his hands to heaven and prayed to God, then said, “Woman, your daughter is freed. Go in peace.”

The woman went away, and returning home she found her daughter blessing God. So the woman said, “Truly there is no other God than the God of Israel.” And all her family joined themselves to the law of God, according to the law written in the book of Moses.

### Notes — Chapter 21
1. **Six thousand six hundred and sixty-six** (folio 20b) is the number of demons as written. It is kept as written; no symbolic gloss is added in the text.
2. **About ten thousand swine** is also as written. The scale belongs to the book's telling and is not reduced to a canonical figure.
3. **“I am sent only to the people of Israel”** and the bread/dogs exchange are translated as written. The woman's answer is left to turn the exchange in place; no softening is introduced.

---

## Chapter 22

*[Editorial heading: Jesus explains why he called the uncircumcised “dogs,” and tells David's word against Goliath. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XXII c.”): “Miserable condition of the uncircumcised, in that a dog is better than they.” The heading above is editorial.]*

That day the disciples asked Jesus, “Master, why did you answer the woman that way, saying they were dogs?”

Jesus answered, “I tell you in truth, a dog is better than an uncircumcised man.” The disciples were sorrowful and said, “Hard are these words — who can take them in?”

Jesus answered, “If you consider, foolish ones, what a dog does — a creature without reason — in the service of its master, you will find my saying true. Tell me: does the dog guard its master's house and risk its life against the thief? Yes, surely. But what does it receive? Many blows and insults, with a little bread, and it always shows its master a glad face. Is this true?”

“True, Master,” the disciples answered.

Then Jesus said, “Now consider how much God has given to man, and you will see how unrighteous he is in not keeping the covenant God made with Abraham his servant. Remember what David said to Saul, king of Israel, against Goliath the Philistine: ‘Lord,’ said David, ‘while your servant was keeping the flock, the wolf, the bear, and the lion came and seized the sheep; your servant went and killed them and rescued the sheep. And what is this uncircumcised one but like them? So your servant will go in the name of the Lord God of Israel and kill this unclean one who blasphemes the holy people of God.’”

Then the disciples said, “Tell us, Master, for what reason must a man be circumcised?”

Jesus answered, “Let it be enough for you to know that God commanded it to Abraham, saying, ‘Abraham, circumcise your foreskin and that of all your house, for this is a covenant between you and me forever.’”

### Notes — Chapter 22
1. **The dog comparison** is translated exactly as written, including its harshness. The explanation that follows in the text — the dog's unpaid service against man's unkept covenant — is the book's own reasoning and is kept in place rather than paraphrased away.
2. **David's speech** is retold here from 1 Samuel 17. The rendering follows this book's Italian wording, not the canonical wording. **Uncertain reading:** at “Remember what David said” (folio 22a) the 1907 editors note the manuscript reading is doubtful — “So MS. ? risguardavano.” The rendering follows the printed “said,” flagged here as uncertain rather than settled from a modern edition; Spanish control has not yet been collated for this line.

---

## Chapter 23

*[Editorial heading: The origin of circumcision — Adam's oath, the covenant with Abraham, and why the flesh must be despised. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XXIII b.”): “Origin of circumcision, and covenant of God with Abraham, and damnation of the uncircumcised.” The heading above is editorial.]*

Having said this, Jesus sat near the mountain overlooking Tyre, and his disciples came close to hear his words. Then Jesus said, “Adam, the first man, having eaten — through Satan's deceit — the food God had forbidden in paradise, his flesh rebelled against the spirit. So he swore, saying, ‘By God, I will cut you!’ He broke a stone and took hold of his flesh to cut it with the stone's edge, and he was rebuked by the angel Gabriel. He answered, ‘I have sworn by God to cut it; I will never be a liar!’ Then the angel showed him the excess of his flesh, and that he cut off.

“So, just as every man takes flesh from Adam's flesh, he is bound to keep what Adam promised with an oath. Adam kept this among his sons, and from generation to generation the duty of circumcision came down. But in Abraham's time only a few on earth were circumcised, because idolatry had multiplied on the earth. Then God told Abraham the matter of circumcision and made this covenant, saying, ‘The soul that has not circumcised his flesh, I will scatter him from my people forever.’”

The disciples trembled with fear at these words, because Jesus spoke with force of spirit. Then Jesus said, “Leave fear to the one who has not circumcised his foreskin, for he is cut off from paradise.”

And having said this, Jesus spoke again: “The spirit in many is ready in God's service, but the flesh is weak. So a man who fears God must consider what the flesh is, where it came from, and where it will end. From the clay of the earth God created the flesh, and into it he breathed the breath of life. So when the flesh hinders God's service, it must be despised and trampled like clay — for the one who hates his life in this world keeps it into eternal life.

“What the flesh is, its desires show even now: it is a harsh enemy of all good, for it alone desires sin. Must a man, to please one of his enemies, stop pleasing God his Creator? Consider this. All the saints and prophets have been enemies of their own flesh for God's service; readily and with gladness they went to death rather than break the law God gave through Moses his servant and go serve false and lying gods.

“Remember Elijah, who fled through desert places in the mountains, eating only herbs, clothed in goatskin. How many days he went without supper! How much cold he endured! How many rains soaked him — for seven years, through that fierce persecution by unclean Jezebel!

“Remember Elisha, who ate barley bread and wore the coarsest clothes. I tell you in truth, because they did not fear to despise the flesh, kings and princes feared them with great dread.

“This should be enough for despising the flesh, O men. But if you look into the graves, you will know what the flesh is.”

### Notes — Chapter 23
1. **Adam's oath and the first cutting** (folio 22b) is this book's origin story for circumcision. It is translated as written. (The 1907 editors' uncertain-reading note “So MS. ? risguardavano.” belongs to the David-speech introduction at folio 22a in chapter 22, and is flagged there — not here.) Spanish control, where extant, has not yet been collated for this chapter.
2. **“False and lying gods”** keeps the Italian phrase as written. The 1907 check note compares Dante; that comparison stays in this note, not in the text.
3. **The seven years of Elijah's persecution** is the book's figure as written.

---

## Chapter 24

*[Editorial heading: The rich glutton and Lazarus — a warning against feasting and serving the flesh. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XXIV a.”): “Notable example of how one ought to flee banqueting and feasting.” The heading above is editorial.]*

Having said this, Jesus wept, saying, “Woe to those who are servants of their flesh, for they are sure to have no good in the other life, but only torments for their sins. I tell you, there was a rich glutton who thought of nothing but feasting, and every day he held a splendid banquet. At his gate stood a poor man named Lazarus, full of sores, who longed for the crumbs that fell under the glutton's table — but no one gave him any; everyone mocked him. Only the dogs had pity on him, for they licked his sores.

“It happened that the poor man died, and the angels carried him to the arms of Abraham our father. The rich man also died, and the devils carried him to the arms of Satan. There, suffering the greatest torment, he lifted his eyes and from far off saw Lazarus in Abraham's arms. The rich man cried, ‘Father Abraham, have mercy on me! Send Lazarus to bring me on his fingers a drop of water to cool my tongue, which is tormented in this flame.’

“Abraham answered, ‘Son, remember that you received your good in the other life, and Lazarus his evil. So now you will remain in torment, and Lazarus in comfort.’

“The rich man cried again, ‘Father Abraham, in my house I have three brothers. Send Lazarus to tell them how much I suffer, so they may repent and not come here.’

“Abraham answered, ‘They have Moses and the prophets; let them hear them.’

“The rich man answered, ‘No, father Abraham — but if a dead man rises, they will believe.’

“Abraham answered, ‘Whoever does not believe Moses and the prophets will not believe the dead either, even if they rise.’”

“See then whether the poor are blessed,” said Jesus, “who have patience and desire only what is necessary, hating the flesh. O wretched are those who carry others to the burial, to give the flesh as food for worms, and do not learn the truth! Far from it, they live here as if immortal — they build great houses, buy great revenues, and live in pride.”

### Notes — Chapter 24
1. **The rich man carried “to the arms of Satan”** is as written; the parallel with Lazarus in Abraham's arms is the book's own pairing.
2. **Three brothers** (folio 24a) is the number as written. In Luke the rich man has five brothers. The book's number is kept and the difference noted, not corrected.
3. The chapter closes by returning to the flesh theme of chapters 22–23; no application is added beyond what the Italian states.

---

## Chapter 25

*[Editorial heading: How to hate the flesh without destroying it — keep it like a horse; one robe, no purse, trust God's provision. Manuscript rubric (Ragg “XXV a.”): “How one ought to despise the flesh, and how one ought to live in the world.” The heading above is editorial.]*

Then the one who writes this said, “Master, your words are true, and so we have left everything to follow you. Tell us, then, how we should hate our flesh — for killing oneself is not lawful, and while we live we must give it food.”

Jesus answered, “Keep your flesh like a horse, and you will live safely. For a horse is given food by measure and work without measure; the bridle is put on it so it walks as you will; it is tied up so it troubles no one; it is kept in a poor place and beaten when it does not obey. So do you, Barnabas, and you will live always with God.

“And do not be offended at my words, for David the prophet did the same, as he confesses, saying, ‘I am like a horse before you, and I am always with you.’

“Now tell me: who is poorer — the one who is content with little, or the one who desires much? I tell you in truth, if the world had a sound mind, no one would gather anything for himself; everything would be in common. But its madness is known in this: the more it gathers, the more it desires; and whatever it gathers, it gathers for the fleshly rest of others. So let a single robe be enough for you. Throw away the purse; carry no bag and no sandals on your feet; and do not think, saying, ‘What will become of us?’ Think instead to do God's will, and he will provide for your need, so that nothing will be lacking to you.

“I tell you in truth, gathering much in this life gives sure witness of having nothing to receive in the other. For the one whose homeland is Jerusalem does not build houses in Samaria, because there is enmity between those cities. Do you understand?”

“Yes,” the disciples answered.

### Notes — Chapter 25
1. **“Keep your flesh like a horse”** (folio 25a) is the book's rule for the question the writer asks: measured food, unmeasured work, bridle, tether, poor lodging, correction. It is addressed in the text to Barnabas by name; the self-reference is the book's frame (see chapter 19, note 2) and is translated as written.
2. **“I am like a horse before you”** is David's confession as this book cites it (Psalm 73 in its numbering, compared by the 1907 editors to the Vulgate wording). The citation is kept as the book gives it.
3. **One robe, no purse, no bag, no sandals** is the travelling rule stated here. Nothing is added about later practice.

---

---

## Chapter 26

*[Editorial heading: How to love God — the treasure in the field, and the story of Abraham questioning his idol-making father. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXVI *." — gloss-letter illegible in this OCR): "How one ought to love God. And in this chapter is contained the wonderful contention of Abraham with his father." The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Jesus said, "There was a man on a journey who, while walking, found a treasure in a field that was for sale for five pieces of money. As soon as he realized this, he sold his cloak to buy that field. Is this believable?"

The disciples answered, "Whoever would not believe this is out of his mind."

Then Jesus said, "You will be out of your minds if you do not give your senses to God to buy your soul, where the treasure of love lies — for love is a treasure beyond compare. Whoever loves God has God, and whoever has God has everything."

Peter answered, "Master, how should we love God with true love? Tell us."

Jesus replied, "I tell you in truth: whoever does not hate his father and his mother, and his own life, and his children and wife, for love of God, is not worthy to be loved by God."

Peter answered, "Master, it is written in the law of God in the book of Moses: 'Honor your father, that you may live long on the earth.' And it also says, 'Cursed be the son who does not obey his father and mother,' so that God commanded such a disobedient son be stoned by the angry crowd before the city gate. Now how do you tell us to hate father and mother?"

Jesus replied, "Every word of mine is true, because it is not mine but God's, who sent me to the house of Israel. So I tell you: everything you have, God has given you. Which is more precious, then — the gift or the Giver? When your father and mother, or anything else, becomes a stumbling block to you in God's service, leave them behind as enemies. Did God not say to Abraham, 'Go out from your father's house and from your kindred, and come live in the land I will give to you and to your seed'? Why did God say this, if not because Abraham's father was an image-maker who made and worshiped false gods? So enmity arose between them, to the point that the father wanted to burn his son."

Peter answered, "Your words are true. Please tell us how Abraham mocked his father."

Jesus replied, "Abraham was seven years old when he began to seek God. One day he said to his father, 'Father, what made man?'

The foolish father answered, 'Man — for I made you, and my father made me.'

Abraham answered, 'Father, that is not so. I heard an old man weeping and saying, "O my God, why have you not given me children?"'

His father replied, 'It is true, my son, that God helps man to make man, but He does not lay His hands to it. A man only needs to pray to his God and give Him lambs and sheep, and his God will help him.'

Abraham answered, 'How many gods are there, Father?'

The old man replied, 'They are endless, my son.'

Then Abraham said, 'Father, what will I do if I serve one god and another bears ill will toward me because I do not serve him? There will surely be discord between them, and war will break out among the gods. And if the god who wishes me harm kills my god, what will I do? He will surely kill me too.'

The old man, laughing, answered, 'Son, do not be afraid. No god makes war on another god. In the great temple there are a thousand gods alongside the great god Baal, and I am almost seventy years old and have never seen one god slap another. And people do not all serve the same god — one serves this one, another that one.'

Abraham answered, 'So they are at peace with one another?'

His father said, 'They are.'

Then Abraham said, 'Father, what are the gods like?'

The old man answered, 'Fool, every day I make a god, which I sell to others to buy bread, and you do not know what the gods are like?' And at that moment he was making an idol. 'This one,' he said, 'is of palm wood, that one of olive, that little one of ivory. Look how fine it is! Does it not seem alive? Truly, it lacks only breath!'

Abraham answered, 'So, Father, the gods have no breath? Then how do they give breath? And having no life, how do they give life? Surely, Father, these are not God.'

The old man grew angry at these words and said, 'If you were old enough to understand, I would break your head with this axe. Be quiet — you have no understanding!'

Abraham answered, 'Father, if the gods help to make man, how can man make the gods? And if the gods are made of wood, it is a great sin to burn wood. But tell me, Father: since you have made so many gods, why have the gods not helped you to make just as many children, so you would be the most powerful man in the world?'

The father was beside himself hearing his son speak this way. The son went on: 'Father, was the world without men for some time?'

'Yes,' the old man answered. 'Why do you ask?'

'Because,' said Abraham, 'I would like to know who made the first God.'

'Now get out of my house!' said the old man. 'Leave me to finish this god quickly, and do not talk to me. When you are hungry you want bread, not words.'

Abraham said, 'A fine god, truly — you cut him as you please and he does not defend himself!'

Then the old man was angry and said, 'Everyone says he is a god, and you, madman, say he is not. By my gods, if you were a man I would kill you!' And with that he struck Abraham with fists and kicks and drove him from the house.

### Notes — Chapter 26
1. **"Hate father and mother"** is translated as written (folio 26a). The chapter itself answers the objection by distinguishing honoring parents from letting them block God's service; no softening is introduced.
2. **The treasure for five pieces** opens the chapter as the reason to surrender the senses to God. The figure is kept as written.
3. **Abraham aged seven** beginning to seek God is the book's chronology here; at chapter 27–28 he is twelve at the temple incident. Both ages are kept as written.

---

## Chapter 27

*[Editorial heading: Against laughing at others — Abraham at twelve goes to the feast. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXVII a."): "In this chapter is clearly seen how improper is laughter in men: also the prudence of Abraham." The heading above is editorial.]*

The disciples laughed at the old man's foolishness and stood amazed at Abraham's prudence. Jesus rebuked them, saying, "You have forgotten the words of the prophet, who says, 'Present laughter is a herald of weeping to come,' and further, 'Do not go where there is laughter, but sit where there is weeping, because this life passes in misery.'

"Then in Moses' time, do you not know that for laughing and mocking at others God turned many men of Egypt into brute beasts? Beware: do not laugh at anyone in any way, or you will surely weep for it."

The disciples answered, "We laughed at the old man's madness."

Then Jesus said, "I tell you in truth: every like loves its like and finds pleasure in it. So if you were not mad, you would not laugh at madness."

They answered, "May God have mercy on us."

Jesus said, "So be it."

Then Philip said, "Master, how did it happen that Abraham's father wanted to burn his son?"

Jesus answered, "One day, when Abraham had reached the age of twelve, his father said to him, 'Tomorrow is the feast of all the gods. We will go to the great temple and bring a present to my god, the great Baal. And you shall choose a god for yourself, for you are old enough to have a god.'

Abraham answered with guile, 'Gladly, my father.' Early in the morning they went to the temple before everyone else. But Abraham carried an axe hidden under his tunic.
