Once inside, as the crowd grew, Abraham hid behind an idol in a dark part of the temple. When his father left, he believed Abraham had gone home ahead of him, so he did not stay to look for him.

### Notes — Chapter 27
1. **"Turned into brute beasts"** for mockery in Moses' time is the book's account as written (folio 28a); the 1907 editors' check note is not imported into the text.
2. **Abraham's guile** — agreeing gladly while planning to hide — is kept as written; the text itself names the tactic.

---

## Chapter 28

*[Editorial heading: Abraham breaks the idols and is delivered from the fire. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXVIII «." — gloss-letter illegible in this OCR) is blank in the Italian line: the rubric numeral is present but no chapter title text follows it; the chapter continues the Abraham story. The heading above is editorial.]*

When everyone had left the temple, the priests locked it and went away. Then Abraham took the axe and cut off the feet of all the idols except the great god Baal. He laid the axe at Baal's feet. As the statues fell — being old and made of pieces — they broke apart.

As Abraham was leaving the temple, some men saw him and suspected he had come to steal something. They seized him and, returning to the temple and seeing their gods broken, cried out in lament, "Come quickly, men — let us kill the one who has killed our gods!" About ten thousand men ran together with the priests and questioned Abraham about why he had destroyed their gods.

Abraham answered, "You are fools! Would a man kill God? It is the great god who killed them. Do you not see the axe by his feet? He surely wants no companions."

Then Abraham's father arrived. Remembering Abraham's many speeches against their gods, and recognizing the axe with which Abraham had smashed the idols, he cried, "It was this traitor son of mine who killed our gods — this axe is mine!" And he told them everything that had passed between him and his son.

So the men gathered a great pile of wood, bound Abraham hand and foot, laid him on the wood, and set fire beneath it.

Lo — God, through His angel, commanded the fire not to burn Abraham His servant. The fire blazed up with great fury and burned about two thousand of the men who had condemned Abraham to death. Abraham found himself free, carried by God's angel to near his father's house without seeing who carried him. And so Abraham escaped death.

### Notes — Chapter 28
1. **"About ten thousand"** gathered and **"about two thousand"** burned are the figures as written (folio 29a–b); they are not adjusted.
2. **The axe at Baal's feet** is the book's explanation Abraham offers the crowd; it is translated as his spoken defense, not endorsed or removed.

---

## Chapter 29

*[Editorial heading: How Abraham came to know God — the palm tree, Gabriel, the washing, and the mountain. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXIX »." — gloss-letter illegible in this OCR) continues the Abraham story. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Philip said, "Great is God's mercy on whoever loves Him. Tell us, Master, how Abraham came to know God."

Jesus answered, "When Abraham reached his father's house, he was afraid to go in. So he moved a little way off and sat under a palm tree. Sitting there alone, he said to himself, 'There must be a God who has life and power beyond man, since He makes man — and man without God cannot make man.'

"Then, looking at the stars, the moon, and the sun, he thought they might be God. But considering how they change and move, he said, 'God must not move, and clouds must not hide Him — otherwise men would be destroyed.'

"As he stayed suspended in this thought, he heard himself called by name: 'Abraham!' Turning and seeing no one on any side, he said, 'I surely heard myself called by name — Abraham.' Twice more he heard the same call, 'Abraham!'

He answered, 'Who is calling me?'

Then he heard, 'I am God's angel, Gabriel.'

Abraham was filled with fear, but the angel comforted him, saying, 'Do not fear, Abraham, for you are God's friend. When you smashed men's gods, you were chosen by the God of angels and prophets, so that you are written in the book of life.'

Then Abraham said, 'What must I do to serve the God of angels and holy prophets?'

The angel answered, 'Go to that spring and wash yourself, for God wants to speak with you.'

Abraham answered, 'How should I wash?'

Then the angel showed himself as a beautiful young man and washed himself in the spring, saying, 'Do likewise, Abraham.' After Abraham had washed, the angel said, 'Go up that mountain, for God wants to speak with you there.'

He climbed the mountain as the angel had told him and, sitting on his knees, said to himself, 'When will the God of angels speak to me?' He heard himself called in a gentle voice: 'Abraham!'

Abraham answered, 'Who is calling me?'

The voice answered, 'I am your God, O Abraham.'

Filled with fear, Abraham bowed his face to the earth, saying, 'How shall Your servant listen to You, who is dust and ashes?'

Then God said, 'Do not fear, but rise. I have chosen you as My servant, and I will bless you and make you grow into a great people. So go out from your father's house and from your kindred, and come live in the land I will give to you and to your seed.'

Abraham answered, 'I will do everything, Lord — but guard me, so no other god can harm me.'

Then God spoke, saying, 'I am God alone, and there is no other God besides Me. I strike and I heal; I kill and I give life; I lead down to hell and I bring out, and no one can escape My hands.' Then God gave him the covenant of circumcision. And so our father Abraham knew God.

Having said this, Jesus lifted his hands and said, 'To You be honor and glory, O our God. So be it.'

### Notes — Chapter 29
1. **Stars, moon, and sun considered then rejected as God** — because they move and are hidden by cloud — is Abraham's reasoning as written (folio 30a). The argument is kept in his voice.
2. **The washing at the spring**, modeled by Gabriel appearing as a beautiful youth, precedes the ascent. The sequence is as written and is not merged with later washing instructions elsewhere in the book.
3. **"I strike and I heal; I kill and I give life"** is God's self-declaration here. It is translated as written; the 1907 editors' parallels (Deut. 32:39; Tobit 13:2) stay in this note, not in the text.

---

## Chapter 30

*[Editorial heading: The Good Samaritan — who is my neighbor? Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXX b") continues at the Feast of Tabernacles. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus went to Jerusalem near the Feast of Tabernacles, a feast of our people. When the scribes and priests learned of it, they took counsel to catch him in his speech. A doctor came up to him and said, "Master, what must I do to have eternal life?"

Jesus answered, "How is it written in the law?"

The tempter answered, "Love the Lord your God and your neighbor. You shall love your God above all things, with all your heart and soul, and your neighbor as yourself."

Jesus answered, "You have answered well. Go and do likewise, I tell you, and you will have eternal life."

He said to him, "And who is my neighbor?"

Jesus, lifting his eyes, answered, "A man was going down from Jerusalem to Jericho, a city rebuilt under a curse. On the road he was seized by robbers, wounded and stripped, and left half dead as they went away. By chance a priest passed that way, saw the wounded man, and passed on without greeting him. Likewise a Levite passed without a word. By chance a Samaritan passed, saw the wounded man, and was moved to pity. He dismounted from his horse, washed the man's wounds with wine, anointed them with ointment, bound them up, and comforted him. He set him on his own horse and, arriving at an inn in the evening, gave him into the host's care.

"Rising in the morning, he said, 'Take care of this man, and I will pay you everything.' And giving the sick man four gold pieces for the host, he said, 'Be of good cheer — I will return soon and bring you to my own home.'

"Tell me," said Jesus, "which of these was the neighbor?"

The doctor answered, "The one who showed mercy."

Then Jesus said, "You have answered rightly. Go and do likewise."

The doctor left in confusion.

### Notes — Chapter 30
1. **"Senofegia"** (folio 31a) is the book's transliteration for the Feast of Tabernacles (Scenopegia). The Italian word is rendered by its meaning; the form is noted here.
2. **The Samaritan gives four gold pieces** to the sick man for the host (folio 31b) — beyond the canonical two denarii. The amount is kept as written.
3. **Jericho "rebuilt under a curse"** is the book's aside on the city; the 1907 check references (Josh. 6:26; 1 Kings 16:34) are not inserted into the text.

---

---

## Chapter 31

*[Editorial heading: Tribute to Caesar, and the centurion whose son is healed. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXXI »." — gloss-letter illegible in this OCR) continues the disputes in Jerusalem. The heading above is editorial.]*

Some priests came near to Jesus and said, "Master, is it lawful to give the tribute to Caesar?"

Jesus turned to Judas and said, "Do you have any money?" Taking a coin in his hand, Jesus turned to the priests and said to them, "This coin has an image on it. Tell me, whose image is it?"

They answered, "Caesar's."

Then Jesus said, "So give what is Caesar's to Caesar, and what is God's give to God."

Confused, they went away.

Then a centurion came up, saying, "Lord, my son is sick. Have mercy on my old age."

Jesus answered, "May the Lord God of Israel have mercy on you."

The man started to leave, and Jesus said to him, "Wait for me. I will come to your house to pray over your son."

The centurion answered, "Lord, I am not worthy for you, a prophet of God, to come into my house. The word you have spoken for my son's health is enough for me, because your God has made you lord over every sickness — as his angel told me in my sleep."

Jesus was filled with wonder. Turning to the crowd, he said, "Look at this foreigner. He has more faith than anyone I have found in Israel."

Then, turning to the centurion, he said, "Go in peace. Because of the great faith God has given you, God has granted health to your son."

The centurion went on his way and met his servants on the road. They told him that his son had been healed.

The man asked, "At what hour did the fever leave him?"

They said, "Yesterday, at the sixth hour, the heat left him."

The man knew that his son had received his health at the moment Jesus said, "May the Lord God of Israel have mercy on you."

So the man believed in our God. Going into his house, he broke all his own gods to pieces, saying, "There is only the God of Israel, the true and living God." And he gave this order: "No one shall eat my bread who does not worship the God of Israel."

### Notes — Chapter 31
1. **The coin is taken from Judas** (folio 32a), at Jesus's request, before the answer about Caesar is given. That detail is kept as written.
2. **The sick person is the centurion's son here**, and the centurion explains his confidence by an angel speaking to him **in his sleep**. Both are given as written, not harmonized with other accounts.
3. **"Yesterday at the sixth hour"** is the servants' timing as written, and the centurion connects the healing to that exact moment.

---

## Chapter 32

*[Editorial heading: A meal at a lawyer's house — washing, traditions, and statues of flesh. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXXII c.") opens at the invitation. The heading above is editorial.]*

A man skilled in the law invited Jesus to a meal, to test him. Jesus went there with his disciples, and many scribes were waiting in the house, also to test him.

The disciples sat down at the table without washing their hands. The scribes called out to Jesus, "Why do your disciples not keep the traditions of our elders? They do not wash their hands before they eat bread."

Jesus answered, "And I ask you: why have you cancelled God's command in order to keep your traditions? You tell the sons of poor parents, 'Offer it, make a vow to the temple.' So they vow that little bit of money with which they ought to support their parents. Then, when their parents want to take some of that money, the sons cry out, 'This money is consecrated to God!' And so the parents go without.

"O false scribes, hypocrites! Does God spend that money? Certainly not. God does not eat — as he says through his servant David the prophet: 'Shall I eat the flesh of bulls and drink the blood of goats? Give me back the sacrifice of praise, and offer me your vows. If I were hungry, I would not ask you for anything, because everything is in my hands and the abundance of paradise is with me.'

"Hypocrites! You do this to fill your own purse. That is why you tithe rue and mint. O miserable men! To others you show a very clear way — a way you yourselves do not want to follow. You scribes and teachers lay weights too heavy to bear on other people's shoulders, but you will not move them with one of your fingers.

"I tell you in truth: every evil has entered the world under the cover of 'the elders.' Tell me, who brought idolatry into the world, if not the custom of the elders? There was a king who loved his father very much. His father's name was Baal. When his father died, the son, to comfort himself, had an image made like his father and set it up in the market square of the city. He made a decree: anyone who came within fifteen cubits of that statue would be safe, and no one, for any reason, was to harm him.

"Because of the benefit they got from it, wrongdoers began to offer roses and flowers to the statue. In a short time the offerings turned into money and food. They began to call it a god, to honor it. What began as custom turned into law, until the idol of Baal spread through the whole world.

"How God still laments this, through the prophet Isaiah, saying: 'Truly this people worships me in vain, because they have cancelled my law, which I gave them through Moses my servant, and they follow the traditions of their elders.'

"I tell you in truth: eating bread with dirty hands does not make a man unclean. What goes into a man does not make him unclean. What comes out of a man — that makes him unclean."

Then one of the scribes said, "If I eat pork, or other unclean foods, will they not stain my conscience?"

Jesus answered, "Disobedience does not go into a man. It comes out of a man, from his heart. So a man is stained when he eats a forbidden food, because of the disobedience."

Then one of the teachers said, "Master, you have spoken a lot against idolatry, as if the people of Israel had idols. You are doing us wrong."

Jesus answered, "I know well that in Israel today there are no statues of wood. But there are statues of flesh."

All the scribes answered in anger, "So we are idolaters?"

### Notes — Chapter 32
1. **The clear way that the teachers "do not want"** is an uncertain point in the printed Italian (folio 33b): the 1907 editors note that either a word should be dropped or the verb read differently ("go" rather than "see"). The sense above follows the plain context; the reading itself is flagged uncertain here and has not been settled from a modern edition.
2. **The Baal origin story** — a dead father named Baal, an image set up with a fifteen-cubit safe zone, offerings turning from flowers to money and food, and custom hardening into law — is told exactly as the chapter tells it.
3. **"Statues of flesh"** is the charge that carries into the next chapter; it is translated as written, without explaining it away in the text.

---

## Chapter 33

*[Editorial heading: What a man's god is, and why idolatry is the greatest sin. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXXIII b.") continues at the same meal. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus answered, "I tell you in truth: the command does not say 'You shall worship,' but it says, 'You shall love the Lord your God with all your soul, and with all your heart, and with all your mind.' Is this true?"

"It is true," everyone answered.

Then Jesus said, "Truly, whatever a man loves, and gives up everything else for, that is his god. So the immoral man has the prostitute as his image. The glutton and the drunkard have their own flesh as their image. The greedy man has silver and gold as his image. And so it is with every other sinner."

Then the man who had invited him said, "Master, what is the greatest sin?"

Jesus answered, "What is the greatest ruin of a house?"

Everyone was silent. Jesus pointed with his finger to the foundation and said, "If the foundation gives way, the house falls in ruin at once, and it has to be built again from the start. But if any other part gives way, it can be repaired. So I tell you: idolatry is the greatest sin. It takes a man away from faith completely, and so away from God, and he can have no spiritual fruit from it. But every other sin leaves a man hope of receiving mercy. That is why I say idolatry is the greatest sin."

Everyone was amazed at what Jesus said, because they could see it could not be answered against him.

Then Jesus went on: "Remember what God said, and what Moses and Joshua wrote in the law, and you will see how serious this sin is. God said, speaking to Israel: 'You shall not make for yourself any image of anything in heaven, or under heaven; you shall not make it of anything above the earth, or under the earth; nor of anything above the water, or under the water. Because I am your God, strong and jealous, and I will take vengeance for this sin on the fathers and on their children, to the fourth generation.'

"Remember: when our people made the calf and worshipped it, by God's command Joshua and the tribe of Levi took the sword and killed one hundred and twenty thousand of those who did not ask God for mercy. O, the fearful judgment of God on idolaters!"

### Notes — Chapter 33
1. **"You shall love," not "You shall worship,"** is the exact contrast Jesus draws with the command (folio 34b). It is kept as his argument, as written.
2. **The foundation comparison** is the chapter's reason for calling idolatry the greatest sin: every other sin, as written, still leaves hope of mercy. Nothing is added to soften that claim.
3. **One hundred and twenty thousand killed** after the calf (folio 35a) is the number as written. It is not adjusted to any other account, and Joshua's role is kept as the chapter gives it.

---

## Chapter 34

*[Editorial heading: The withered hand healed at the door; how to take a seat; pride and Satan's fall. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXXIV c.") continues at the same house. The heading above is editorial.]*

A man was standing in front of the door whose right hand was withered, so that he could not use it. Jesus lifted his heart to God and prayed. Then he said, "So that you may know that my words are true, I say — in the name of God: Man, stretch out your sick hand!"

He stretched it out whole, as if it had never been ill.

Then, with the fear of God, they began to eat. When they had eaten a little, Jesus spoke again:

"I tell you in truth: it would be better to burn a city than to leave a bad custom standing in it. God is angry over such things against the princes and kings of the earth, to whom God has given the sword to destroy wickedness.

"When you are invited, remember: do not put yourself in the first place. A greater friend of the host may come, and the host may say to you, 'Get up and sit further down' — and that would be a shame to you. Instead, go and sit in the lowest place. Then the one who invited you, seeing you, will say, 'Get up, friend, and come and sit up here.' Then it will be a great honor for you. For everyone who lifts himself up will be brought down, and everyone who brings himself down will be lifted up.

"I tell you in truth: Satan became an outcast for no other sin than his pride. So says the prophet Isaiah, rebuking him with these words: 'How have you fallen from heaven, O Lucifer! You were the beauty of the angels, and you shone like the dawn. Truly, your pride has fallen to the earth.'

"I tell you in truth: if a man knew his own misery, he would weep all the time here on earth, and count himself the lowest thing of all. For this reason alone the first man and his wife wept for a hundred years without stopping, asking God for mercy — because they truly knew where they had fallen, through their pride."

When Jesus had said this, he gave thanks. That day, everything Jesus had said, and the miracle he had done, was told all through Jerusalem. The people gave thanks to God, blessing his holy name.

But the scribes and priests, hearing that he had spoken against the traditions of the elders, burned with greater hatred. Like Pharaoh, they hardened their hearts. They looked for a chance to kill him, but they did not find one.

### Notes — Chapter 34
1. **The healing at the door comes before the meal resumes** (folio 35a). The order — prayer, the word spoken, the hand stretched out whole, then eating in the fear of God — is as written.
2. **"Better to burn a city"** than to leave a bad custom in it (folio 35b) is translated without softening. Kings and princes are said to hold the sword from God for this reason; that too is as written.
3. **The first man and his wife weeping “a hundred years”** (folio 36a) because of pride is the book's figure here. It is kept as written.

---

## Chapter 35

*[Editorial heading: How Satan fell — the lump of earth, the refusal to bow, and the spittle. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXXV c.") opens across the Jordan. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus left Jerusalem and went to the desert beyond the Jordan. His disciples sat around him and said, "Master, tell us how Satan fell through pride. We have heard that he fell through disobedience, and that he is always tempting man to do evil."

Jesus answered, "God had created a lump of earth, and had left it for twenty-five thousand years without doing anything else with it. Satan — who was like a priest and head of the angels — had great understanding. He knew that God was going to take, from that lump of earth, one hundred and forty-four thousand marked with the mark of prophecy, and the messenger of God, whose soul God had created sixty thousand years before everything else.

"So Satan, filled with indignation, stirred up the angels, saying: 'Look — one day God will want this earth to be revered by us. Think about it: we are spirit. It is not fitting for us to do such a thing.'

"Many angels therefore turned away from God. Then one day, when all the angels were gathered together, God said: 'Let everyone who holds me as his lord, straight away, show reverence to this earth.'

"Those who loved God bowed down. But Satan, with those who agreed with him, said: 'O Lord, we are spirit. It is not right for us to show reverence to this clay.'

"As soon as he had said this, Satan became horrid and fearsome to look at, and his followers became ugly too — because, for their rebellion, God took back the beauty he had given them when he created them. When the holy angels lifted their heads and saw what a terrible monster Satan and his followers had become, they threw their faces to the earth in fear.

"Then Satan said, 'O Lord, you have unjustly made me hideous. But I am content with it, because I want to cancel everything you will do.'

"The other devils said, 'Do not call him Lord, O Lucifer, because you are lord.'

"Then God said to Satan's followers, 'Repent, and recognize me as God, your creator.'

"They answered, 'We repent of ever having shown you any reverence, because you are not just. Satan is just and innocent, and he is our lord.'

"Then God said, 'Depart from me, you cursed ones. I will have no mercy on you.'

"As he was leaving, Satan spat on that lump of earth. The angel Gabriel lifted up that spittle, along with some of the earth. That is why man now has a navel in his belly."

### Notes — Chapter 35
1. **Twenty-five thousand years** the lump of earth is left, **one hundred and forty-four thousand** marked with prophecy, and the messenger's soul created **sixty thousand years before everything else** (folio 36b) — all three figures are kept exactly as written.
2. **The messenger is not named in the Italian narrative here.** He is called "the messenger of God," and his soul is said to have been created before all other things. This is an anticipation of the seam that is named later in the book (ch. 39–44, 96–97); in this chapter the rendering names nothing the Italian does not name, and the anticipation is flagged here rather than pre-empted in the text. The edition's margin-notes are not part of the Italian narrative and are not imported into it.
3. **The navel explained** — Gabriel lifting Satan's spittle with some earth from the lump (folio 37a) — is the book's own explanation, translated as written.

---

---

## Chapter 36

*[Editorial heading: Why the man who does not pray is worse than Satan. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXXVI *." — gloss-letter illegible in this OCR) continues the teaching after Satan's fall. The heading above is editorial.]*

The disciples were standing in great amazement at the rebellion of the angels.

Then Jesus said: "I tell you in truth: the man who does not pray is more wicked than Satan, and he will suffer greater pains. Satan, before his fall, had no example before him to make him afraid, and God did not send him any prophet to call him to repentance. But man — now that all the prophets have come, except the messenger of God who will come after me, because God willed that I should prepare his way — man, I say, though he has countless examples of God's justice, lives carefree, without any fear, as if there were no God. As the prophet David said of such people: 'The fool has said in his heart, There is no God. So they are corrupt and have become abominable, and not one of them does good.'

"Pray without ceasing, my disciples, so that you may receive. Whoever seeks finds. Whoever knocks, it is opened to him. Whoever asks receives. And in your prayer do not watch for much speaking, because God looks at the heart — as he said through Solomon: 'My servant, give me your heart.'

"I tell you in truth — as God lives — the hypocrites pray a great deal in every place in the city, so that they will be seen and held to be saints by the crowd. But their heart is full of wickedness, and so they do not understand what they are asking. It is necessary for you to understand your own prayer, if you want God to receive it.

"Now tell me: who would go to speak to the Roman governor, or to Herod, if he had not first understood in his heart where he was going and what he was going to do? Certainly no one. And if man does this in order to speak with man, what ought man to do in order to speak with God, and ask him for mercy for his sins, while thanking him for everything he has given him?

"I tell you in truth: very few make true prayer, and so Satan has power over them, because God does not want those who honour him with their lips. In the temple their lips ask for mercy, and their heart cries out for justice — as he says through Isaiah the prophet: 'Take away this people, who trouble me, because with their lips they honour me, but their heart is far from me.'

"I tell you in truth: the man who goes to pray without thinking about it is making fun of God. Now who would go to speak to Herod with his back turned towards him, and in front of him speak well of Pilate the governor, whom Herod hates to death? Certainly no one. Yet the man who goes to pray and does not prepare himself does no less. He turns his back to God and his face to Satan, and speaks well of him — because in his heart there is the love of the wrongdoing of which he has not repented.

"If a man who had injured you said with his lips, 'Forgive me,' and with his hands gave you a slap, how would you forgive him? In the same way God will have mercy on those who with their lips say, 'Lord, have mercy on us,' and with their heart love wrongdoing and think about new sins."

### Notes — Chapter 36
1. **The messenger of God "who will come after me"** (folio 37b) is not named in the Italian narrative here. The rendering names nothing the Italian does not name; this is an anticipation of the seam named later in the book, flagged here rather than filled in in the text. God is said to have willed that Jesus prepare his way — translated as written.
2. **David's "The fool has said in his heart"** and **Solomon's "give me your heart"** are quoted as the chapter's own proofs. The heart, not the amount of speaking, is the chapter's measure of prayer.
3. **The back-to-Herod comparison** — speaking well of Pilate in front of Herod — is kept in its sharp form, as written, without softening what it says about unprepared prayer.

---

## Chapter 37

*[Editorial heading: "Teach us to pray" — the prayer Jesus gives. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXXVII" — printed "xxxvnx" in this OCR, gloss-letter illegible) opens with the disciples weeping. The heading above is editorial.]*

The disciples were weeping at the words of Jesus, and they prayed to him, saying: "Lord, teach us to pray."

Jesus answered: "Consider what you would do if the Roman governor seized you to put you to death. Do that same thing when you go to pray. And let your words be these:

"'Lord our God, may your holy name be hallowed. May your kingdom come in us. May your will always be done, and as it is done in heaven, so may it be done on earth. Give us the bread for every day, and forgive us our sins, as we forgive those who sin against us. And do not let us fall into temptation, but free us from evil. For you alone are our God, to whom belong glory and honour for ever.'"

### Notes — Chapter 37
1. **The prayer begins "Lord our God"** and reads "may your kingdom come **in us**" (folio 39a–39b) — both kept as written, not harmonized with other versions of this prayer.
2. **"The bread for every day"** is the daily-bread petition as the Italian gives it. "Do not let us fall into temptation" is the wording here; nothing is imported from another account to smooth it.
3. **The preparation for prayer** in this chapter is fear-facing: consider what you would do if the governor seized you to put you to death, and bring that same seriousness to prayer. That is the chapter's own instruction, kept as written.

---

## Chapter 38

*[Editorial heading: Washing, the law, and the prophets who prayed. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXXVIII e.") opens with John's question. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then John answered: "Master, shall we stop washing ourselves, as God commanded through Moses?"

Jesus said: "Do you think I have come to destroy the law and the prophets? I tell you in truth — as God lives — I have not come to destroy it, but rather to observe it. Every prophet has observed God's law, and everything God has said through the other prophets. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands: no one who breaks one least command can please God. Rather, he will be the least in God's kingdom, because he will have no part there. And I tell you that one syllable of God's law cannot be broken without the gravest sin.

"But I warn you that it is necessary to observe what God says through Isaiah the prophet, in these words: 'Wash yourselves and be clean. Take your thoughts away from my eyes.'

"I tell you in truth: all the water of the sea will not wash the man who, with his heart, loves wrongdoing. And I say to you further: no one will make a prayer pleasing to God if he is not washed, but he will load his soul with a sin like idolatry.

"Believe me for certain: if a man prayed to God as is right, he would obtain everything he asked. Remember Moses, God's servant, who with prayer struck Egypt, opened the Red Sea, and drowned Pharaoh there with his army. Remember Joshua, who made the sun stand still. Samuel, who put fear into the countless army of the Philistines. Elijah, who made fire rain down from heaven. Elisha raised a dead man — and so many other holy prophets, who with prayer obtained everything they asked. But those men truly did not seek their own things. They only sought God and his honour."

### Notes — Chapter 38
1. **"I have not come to destroy the law"** (folio 39b) is the chapter's opening answer about washing, kept in full: not one syllable of the law broken without the gravest sin, and the man who breaks one least command has no part in the kingdom — as written.
2. **Isaiah's "wash and be clean"** is turned inward here: all the sea's water will not wash a heart that loves wrongdoing, and unwashed prayer is loaded with a sin "like idolatry." Both are translated without softening.
3. **The list of praying prophets** — Moses, Joshua, Samuel, Elijah, Elisha, "and so many other holy prophets" — closes with the reason given: they sought not their own things, but only God and his honour.

---

## Chapter 39

*[Editorial heading: How man sinned through pride — Adam, the writing in the air, and Eve. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XXXIX b.") opens with John's question after the washing teaching. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then John said: "Master, you have spoken well. But we still need to know how man sinned through pride."

Jesus answered: "When God had driven Satan out, and the angel Gabriel had purified that mass of earth where Satan had spat, God created everything that lives — both the animals that fly and those that walk and swim — and he adorned the world with everything it has.

"One day Satan came near to the gates of paradise, and, seeing the horses eating grass, he told them that if that mass of earth should receive a soul, it would mean heavy labour for them. So it would be to their advantage to trample that earth, in such a way that it would no longer be good for anything. The horses woke up, and with a rush they set themselves to run over that earth, which lay among lilies and roses.

"At that, God gave spirit to that unclean part of the earth on which Satan's spittle lay, which Gabriel had lifted from the mass. And he raised up the dog, who barked and filled the horses with fear, and they fled.

"Then God gave the soul to man, while all the holy angels sang: 'Blessed be your holy name, O God our Lord.'

"Adam, having stood up on his feet, saw in the air a writing that shone like the sun. It said: 'There is only one God, and Mohammed is the messenger of God.'

"Then Adam opened his mouth and said: 'I thank you, O Lord my God, that you have been pleased to create me. But tell me, I pray you, what do those words mean — "Mohammed is the messenger of God"? Have there been other men before me?'

"Then God said: 'Be welcome, O my servant Adam. I tell you that you are the first man I have created. And the one you have seen is your son, who will come into the world many years from now, and he will be my messenger, for whom I have created everything. When he comes he will give light to the world. His soul was placed in a heavenly splendour sixty thousand years before I made anything at all.'

"Adam prayed to God, saying: 'Lord, grant me this writing on the nails of the fingers of my hands.' Then God gave the first man that writing on his thumbs. On the nail of the right thumb it said: 'There is only one God.' And on the nail of the left thumb it said: 'Mohammed is the messenger of God.' Then, with a father's love, the first man kissed those words, rubbed his eyes, and said: 'Blessed be that day when you will come into the world.'

"Seeing the man alone, God said: 'It is not good for him to stay alone.' So he made him sleep, and took a rib from the place near the heart, filling the place with flesh. From that rib he made Eve, and gave her to Adam as his wife.

"He set them both as lords of paradise, and said to them: 'See, I give you every fruit to eat, except the apples and the wheat.' About these he said: 'Take care that in no way do you eat of these fruits, because you will become unclean, in such a way that I will not allow you to stay here with me, but I will drive you out, and you will suffer great miseries.'"

### Notes — Chapter 39
1. **Seam passage, translated exactly as written.** Here, for the first time in the narrative, the coming messenger is named in the Italian text itself: Adam sees a writing in the air, shining like the sun — "There is only one God, and Mohammed is the messenger of God" (folio 40b–41a). The name stands in the text as the Italian gives it; it is not smoothed, softened, or removed. This belongs to the book's Messiah/Mohammed seam (named in full at ch. 42, 96–97); the scholarly note that this seam is a feature of the medieval Italian book, and Robert's own recorded suspicion that it may be a later insertion, are carried here rather than decided — and the fork's second prong with it: the naming here contradicts the Qur'an, which names Jesus himself the Messiah (Q 3:45; 4:157, 4:171–172). The translation itself gives the reader what the manuscript says, whole.
2. **"Sixty thousand years"** (folio 41a) is the figure here for when the messenger's soul was placed in heavenly splendour before anything else was made. It is kept exactly as written. An earlier chapter (ch. 35) gave the same sixty thousand years; the figure is not harmonized or corrected.
3. **The writing on the thumb-nails** — the right thumb "There is only one God," the left thumb "Mohammed is the messenger of God" — and Adam kissing the words, are the book's own detail, translated as written.
4. **The forbidden fruits are "the apples and the wheat"** (folio 41a–41b), plural and specific, as written. Eating them makes the pair "unclean" and brings expulsion and "great miseries," in the chapter's own terms.

---

## Chapter 40

*[Editorial heading: Satan in the serpent tempts Eve, and Adam eats. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XL*." — gloss-letter illegible in this OCR) opens with Satan enraged. The heading above is editorial.]*

When Satan knew this, he was maddened with indignation. So he drew near to the gate of paradise, where a horrid serpent stood on guard. The serpent had legs like a camel, and the nails of his feet cut like a razor on every side.

The enemy said to him: "Let me enter into paradise."

The serpent answered: "And how shall I let you enter, when God has commanded me to drive you out?"

Satan answered: "You see how much God loves you, since he has set you outside paradise to keep guard over a lump of clay, which is man. So if you carry me into paradise, I will make you so fearsome that everyone will flee from you, and so you will go and stay as you please."

Then the serpent said: "And how shall I put you inside?"

Satan said: "You are large. So open your mouth, and I will enter into your belly. Then, when you go into paradise, you will set me down near those two lumps of clay that are newly walking on the earth."

So the serpent did that. He set Satan down near Eve, because Adam, her husband, was sleeping. Satan showed himself to the woman like a beautiful angel, and said to her: "Why do you not eat those apples, and the wheat?"

Eve answered: "Our God has told us that if we eat of them we will become unclean, and so he will drive us from paradise."

Satan answered: "He does not say the truth. You must know that God is wicked and envious, and so he does not want equals, but keeps everyone as a servant. That is why he has spoken to you in this way: so that you will not become equal to him. But if you and your companion follow my counsel, you will eat those fruits just like the others, and you will not stay subject to others. Like God, you will know good and evil, and you will do whatever pleases you, because you will be equal to God."

Then Eve took some and ate. When her husband woke, she told him everything Satan had said. He took some of them, his wife offering them to him, and he ate.

As the food was going down, he remembered God's words. Wanting to stop the food, he put his hand into his throat — where every man has the mark.

### Notes — Chapter 40
1. **The serpent here has legs like a camel and razor nails** (folio 42a) and stands guard at the gate; Satan enters paradise inside the serpent's belly and is set down near Eve while Adam sleeps. All translated as written, not drawn from any other account of the temptation.
2. **Satan's counsel names God "wicked and envious"** and promises the pair will be "equal to God" (folio 42a). The blasphemy is the serpent-tempter's speech as the book writes it; it is kept whole so the reader sees the temptation as the chapter presents it.
3. **The mark in every man's throat** — Adam putting his hand into his throat to stop the food as it went down (folio 42a) — is the book's explanation of that mark. It is translated as written and closes the chapter.

---

---

## Chapter 41

*[Editorial heading: Adam and Eve know they are naked and are sentenced. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XLI*." — gloss-letter illegible in this OCR) follows the eating of the fruit. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then both of them knew that they were naked. Being ashamed, they took fig leaves and made a covering for their secret parts.

When midday was past, God appeared and called Adam, saying: "Adam, where are you?"

He answered: "Lord, I hid myself from your presence, because I and my wife are naked, and we are ashamed to present ourselves before you."

Then God said: "And who has robbed you of your innocence, unless you have eaten the fruit by reason of which you are unclean and will not be able to stay longer in paradise?"

Adam answered: "O Lord, the wife whom you have given me begged me to eat, and so I ate."

Then God said to the woman: "Why did you give such food to your husband?"

Eve answered: "Satan deceived me, and so I ate."

"And how did that reprobate enter in here?" said God.

Eve answered: "A serpent that stands at the northern gate brought him near to me."

Then God said to Adam: "Because you listened to the voice of your wife and ate the fruit, cursed be the earth in your works. It shall bring forth for you brambles and thorns, and in the sweat of your face you shall eat bread. Remember that you are earth, and to earth you shall return."

And he spoke to Eve, saying: "And you who listened to Satan and gave the food to your husband shall stay under the dominion of man, who shall keep you as a servant, and you shall bear children with travail."

Having called the serpent, God called the angel Michael, who holds the sword of God, and said: "First drive this wicked serpent out of paradise, and once outside cut off his legs. If he wants to walk, he must drag his body upon the earth."

Afterwards God called Satan, who came laughing, and said to him: "Because you, reprobate, have deceived these and made them unclean, I will that every uncleanness of them and of all their children who truly repent and serve me shall, as they go out of their body, enter by your mouth, and so you shall be filled with uncleanness."

Satan gave a horrible roar and said: "Since you want to make me always worse, I will still make myself what I can."

Then God said: "Depart, cursed one, from my presence."

Satan departed. Then God said to Adam and Eve, who were both weeping: "Go out of paradise, and do penance, and do not let your hope fail, for I will send your son in such a way that your seed shall lift the dominion of Satan from the human race. For to him who will come, my messenger, I will give all things."

God hid himself, and the angel Michael drove them out of paradise. Adam, turning round, saw written above the gate: "There is only one God, and Mohammed is the messenger of God." Weeping, he said: "May it please God, O my son, that you come quickly and draw us out of misery."

"And so," said Jesus, "Satan and Adam sinned through pride — the one by despising man, the other by wishing to make himself equal with God."

### Notes — Chapter 41
1. **The fig-leaf covering, the midday call, and the sentences** are given as written. The earth is cursed to bring "brambles and thorns," bread is eaten "in the sweat of your face," and Eve's sentence is dominion and child-bearing "with travail" (folio 42b–43a).
2. **Michael with the sword of God** drives out the serpent and cuts off his legs outside paradise, so that walking becomes dragging the body on the earth. The detail is the book's own and is kept as written.
3. **The punishment laid on Satan through the mouth** — that the uncleanness of the penitent shall enter by his mouth as they leave the body — and Satan's roar in reply, are translated without softening.
4. **Seam passage, translated exactly as written.** Above the gate Adam sees: "There is only one God, and Mohammed is the messenger of God" (folio 43b). The name stands as the Italian gives it; Adam's prayer that the son come quickly follows. This is part of the Messiah/Mohammed seam (see audit §3); it is not smoothed or removed. The promise that the coming messenger will lift Satan's dominion is stated here in full.

---

## Chapter 42

*[Editorial heading: "I am not the Messiah" — the voice in Judaea. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XLIIc.") opens with the disciples weeping. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then the disciples wept after this discourse, and Jesus was weeping, when they saw many who came to find him, because the chiefs of the priests had taken counsel among themselves to catch him in his talk. So they sent the Levites and some of the scribes to question him, saying: "Who are you?"

Jesus confessed and said the truth: "I am not the Messiah."

They said: "Are you Elijah, or Jeremiah, or one of the ancient prophets?"

Jesus answered: "No."

Then they said: "Who are you? Say, so that we may give testimony to those who sent us."

Then Jesus said: "I am a voice that cries through all Judaea, and cries: Prepare the way for the messenger of God, as it is written in Isaiah."

They said: "If you are not the Messiah, nor Elijah, nor any prophet, why do you preach new doctrine and make yourself of more account than the Messiah?"

Jesus answered: "The miracles that God works by my hands show that I speak what God wills. Nor do I make myself to be counted as him of whom you speak. I am not worthy to loose the ties of the hosen or the straps of the shoes of the messenger of God whom you call Messiah, who was made before me and will come after me, and will bring the words of truth, so that his faith shall have no end."

The Levites and scribes departed in confusion and told everything to the chiefs of the priests, who said: "He has the devil on his back, who tells him everything."

Then Jesus said to his disciples: "Truly I say to you, the chiefs and elders of our people seek an occasion against me."

Peter said: "Then do not go any more into Jerusalem."

Jesus answered him: "You are foolish and do not know what you say, for I must suffer many persecutions, because so have suffered all the prophets and holy ones of God. But do not fear, because there are those who are with us and those who are against us."

Having said this, Jesus departed and went to Mount Tabor. Peter and James and John his brother went up with him, with the one who writes this.

A great light shone above him, and his garments became white like snow, and his face shone like the sun. Moses and Elijah came, speaking with Jesus about all that must come upon our race and upon the holy city.

Peter spoke, saying: "Lord, it is good to be here. If you will, we will make three dwellings here — one for you, one for Moses, and one for Elijah."

While he spoke they were covered by a white cloud, and they heard a voice saying: "Behold my servant, in whom I am well pleased. Hear him."

The disciples were filled with fear and fell with their face on the earth as dead. Jesus came down and raised his disciples, saying: "Do not fear, for God loves you, and has done this so that you may believe my words."

### Notes — Chapter 42
1. **Seam passage, translated exactly as written.** This is one of the three named seam chapters. Jesus confesses publicly: "I am not the Messiah" (folio 44a). The denial is given whole, without softening, and is footnoted here rather than explained away in the text. The scholarly note that this contradicts the Qur'an's naming of Jesus as Messiah, and Robert's recorded suspicion about this seam, stand in the audit and checkpoint; the translation gives what the Italian says.
2. **"A voice that cries through all Judaea"** — "Prepare the way for the messenger of God, as it is written in Isaiah" — is the identity Jesus claims instead. The messenger "was made before me and will come after me" and will bring "the words of truth, so that his faith shall have no end" (folio 44a). The phrase "whom you call Messiah" is kept as written.
3. **Not worthy to loose the shoes** — the hosen-ties and shoe-straps of the coming messenger — is the measure Jesus sets for himself here, translated as written.
4. **The Transfiguration on Mount Tabor** follows in the same chapter: garments white like snow, face like the sun, Moses and Elijah speaking of what must come upon "our race and upon the holy city," Peter's three dwellings, the white cloud, and the voice "Behold my servant, in whom I am well pleased. Hear him" (folios 44a–45a). The naming of Tabor and the phrase "with the one who writes this" are kept as the book gives them.

---

## Chapter 43

*[Editorial heading: The messenger created first — prophet to one nation, messenger to all. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XLIIIb.") opens below the mountain. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus went down to the eight disciples who were waiting below. The four told the eight all they had seen, and so that day every doubt about Jesus left their hearts — except Judas Iscariot, who believed nothing.

Jesus sat at the foot of the mountain, and they ate wild fruits, because they had no bread.

Then Andrew said: "You have told us many things about the Messiah. Of your kindness, tell us clearly everything." The other disciples begged him in the same way.

Jesus said: "Everyone who works works for an end in which he finds satisfaction. So I tell you that God, because he is perfect, has no need of satisfaction, seeing that he has satisfaction in himself. Willing to work, he created before all things the soul of his messenger, for whom he determined to create everything, so that the creatures should find joy and blessedness in God, and so that his messenger should take delight in all his creatures, which he has appointed to be his servants. And why is this so, except because he has willed it?

"Truly I say to you: every prophet, when he has come, has borne the mark of God's mercy to one nation only. So their words did not reach beyond the people to whom they were sent. But the messenger of God, when he comes, God will give him as it were the seal of his hand, in such a way that he will carry salvation and mercy to all the nations of the world that will receive his teaching. He will come with power upon the ungodly and will destroy idolatry, so that he will make Satan confounded. For so God promised Abraham, saying: 'Behold, in your seed I will bless all the tribes of the earth. And as you, Abraham, have broken the idols in pieces, so shall your seed do.'"

James answered: "O master, tell us in whom this promise was made. The Jews say 'in Isaac,' and the Ishmaelites say 'in Ishmael.'"

Jesus answered: "David — whose son was he, and of what lineage?"

James answered: "Of Isaac. Isaac was father of Jacob, and Jacob was father of Judah, of whose lineage is David."

Then Jesus said: "And the messenger of God, when he comes, of what lineage will he be?"

The disciples answered: "Of David."

Then Jesus said: "You deceive yourselves. David in the spirit calls him lord, saying: 'God said to my lord, Sit on my right hand until I make your enemies your footstool. God shall send forth your rod, which shall have lordship in the midst of your enemies.' If the messenger of God, whom you call Messiah, were son of David, how would David call him lord? Believe me, for truly I say to you: the promise was made in Ishmael, not in Isaac."

### Notes — Chapter 43
1. **God creates the messenger's soul first.** "Before all things" God created the soul of his messenger, for whom he determined to create everything (folio 45a–45b). The creatures are appointed to be the messenger's servants and to find joy in God through him. This is translated as written; it continues the book's first-created-splendour theme from chapter 39.
2. **Prophet to one nation, messenger to all nations.** Every prophet bore God's mercy to one nation only; the messenger will receive "as it were the seal of his hand" and carry salvation and mercy "to all the nations of the world that will receive his teaching" (folio 45b). He will come with power upon the ungodly and destroy idolatry, making Satan confounded, under the promise to Abraham: "In your seed I will bless all the tribes of the earth."
3. **The lineage question is answered with David's psalm.** If the messenger were David's son, how would David call him "my lord" (folio 46a)? Jesus concludes in the plain of the Italian: "The promise was made in Ishmael, not in Isaac." The statement is translated exactly as written and belongs to the book's seam teaching; it is not smoothed toward any other genealogy.

---

## Chapter 44

*[Editorial heading: The promise in Ishmael — the rebuke of the scribes and the splendour described. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XLIVc.") opens with the disciples' objection from Moses. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then the disciples said: "O master, it is written in the book of Moses that the promise was made in Isaac."

Jesus answered with a groan: "It is written so, but Moses did not write it, nor Joshua. Our rabbis wrote it, who do not fear God. Truly I say to you: if you consider the words of the angel Gabriel, you will discover the malice of our scribes and doctors. The angel said: 'Abraham, all the world shall know how God loves you. But how shall the world know the love you bear to God? Surely it is necessary that you do something for love of God.'"

Abraham answered: "Behold the servant of God, ready to do all that God wills."

"Then God spoke, saying to Abraham: 'Take your son, your firstborn Ishmael, and come up the mountain to sacrifice him.' How is Isaac firstborn, if when Isaac was born Ishmael was seven years old?"

Then the disciples said: "Clear is the lie of our doctors. Therefore tell us the truth, because we know you are sent from God."

Then Jesus answered: "Truly I say to you, Satan always seeks to wipe out the laws of God. So with his followers — hypocrites and evildoers, the first with false teaching and the others with a wicked life — he has today contaminated almost everything, so that the truth is scarcely found. Woe to the hypocrites, for the praises of this world will turn for them into insults and torments in hell.

"So I say to you: the messenger of God is a splendour that will give gladness to nearly all that God has made, for he is adorned with the spirit of understanding and counsel, the spirit of wisdom and might, the spirit of fear and love, the spirit of prudence and temperance. He is adorned with the spirit of charity and mercy, the spirit of justice and piety, the spirit of gentleness and patience — which he has received from God three times more than God has given to all his creatures. O blessed time when he will come to the world! Believe me, I have seen him and have done him reverence, as every prophet has seen him, since God gives them prophecy from his spirit. When I saw him, my soul was filled with consolation, saying: 'O Mohammed, God be with you, and may he make me worthy to loose your shoes. For if I obtain this, I shall be a great prophet and holy one of God.'"

Having said this, Jesus gave thanks to God.

### Notes — Chapter 44
1. **"It is written so, but Moses did not write it."** Jesus charges the rabbis who "do not fear God" with writing the Isaac reading into Moses (folio 46a–46b). The accusation of falsification against the scribes and doctors, discovered by weighing Gabriel's words, is translated as written, without softening what it claims about the text of Moses.
2. **"Your firstborn Ishmael."** The proof offered is Gabriel's command to sacrifice "your son, your firstborn Ishmael," followed by the arithmetic as the book states it: "How is Isaac firstborn, if when Isaac was born Ishmael was seven years old?" (folio 46b). The seven-year figure is kept exactly as written.
3. **Seam passage, translated exactly as written.** The messenger is named here in Jesus' own speech: "O Mohammed, God be with you, and may he make me worthy to loose your shoes" (folio 47a). The name stands as the Italian gives it. The messenger is "a splendour" adorned with the spirits of understanding and counsel, wisdom and might, fear and love, prudence and temperance, charity and mercy, justice and piety, gentleness and patience — received "three times more" than all creatures (folios 46b–47a). Jesus says he has seen him and done him reverence, as every prophet has. None of this is smoothed, reduced to a title, or removed; it belongs to the seam named at chapters 42 and 96–97.

---

## Chapter 45

*[Editorial heading: Hypocrisy rebuked in the temple. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XLV*." — gloss-letter illegible in this OCR) opens with Gabriel's command to go up to Jerusalem. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then the angel Gabriel came to Jesus and spoke to him in such a way that we also heard his voice. He said: "Arise, and go to Jerusalem."

Jesus departed and went up to Jerusalem. On the Sabbath day he entered the temple and began to teach the people. The people ran together to the temple with the high priest and the priests, who came near to Jesus, saying: "O master, it has been told to us that you say evil of us. Beware, lest some evil befall you."

Jesus answered: "Truly I say to you, I speak evil of the hypocrites. So if you are hypocrites, I speak against you."

They answered: "Who is a hypocrite? Speak to us clearly."

Jesus said: "Truly I say to you, the man who does a good thing so that men may see him is a hypocrite, because his work does not enter the heart, which men cannot see, and so it leaves there every unclean thought and every foul desire. Do you know who is a hypocrite? The man who with his tongue serves God, but with his heart serves men. O wretched man — dying, he loses all his reward. On this the prophet David says: 'Do not put your trust in princes, nor in the children of men, in whom is no salvation, for at death their thoughts perish.' Indeed, before death they find themselves deprived of reward, because man, as Job the prophet of God said, is unstable, so that he never stays in one state. If today he praises you, tomorrow he will abuse you, and if today he wills to reward you, tomorrow he will want to despoil you.

"Woe to the hypocrites, because their reward is vain. As God lives, in whose presence I stand, the hypocrite is a robber and commits sacrilege, because he makes use of the law to appear good and steals the honour of God, to whom alone belong praise and honour for ever.

"More: I tell you that the hypocrite has no faith. If he believed that God sees everything and with terrible judgement punishes wickedness, he would purify his heart. Because he has no faith, he keeps it full of iniquity. Truly I say to you, the hypocrite is like a tomb: white outside, but inside full of stench and worms.

"So if you, O priests, do the service of God because God created you and commands it of you, I do not speak against you, for you are servants of God. But if you do everything for gain, and so buy and sell in the temple as in a market-place, not regarding that the temple of God is a house of prayer and not of business, which you turn into a cave of robbers — if you do everything to please men and have put God out of your mind — then I cry against you that you are sons of the devil and not sons of Abraham, who left his father's house for love of God and was willing to slay his own son. Woe to you, priests and doctors, if you are such, for God will take the priesthood away from you."

### Notes — Chapter 45
1. **Gabriel's voice is heard by the disciples too** — "Arise, and go to Jerusalem" (folio 47b) — and the Sabbath temple teaching follows. The charge brought against Jesus is that he "says evil" of the priests; his answer limits the charge to hypocrites.
2. **The definition of the hypocrite** is twofold as written: doing good "so that men may see him," so the work never enters the unseen heart (folio 47b–48a); and serving God with the tongue while serving men with the heart. David's "Do not put your trust in princes" and Job's unstable man — today praise, tomorrow abuse and despoiling — are quoted as the chapter's proofs and kept as written.
3. **"A robber and commits sacrilege."** The hypocrite uses the law to appear good and "steals the honour of God" (folio 48a). He has no faith, keeps the heart full of iniquity, and is "like a tomb: white outside, but inside full of stench and worms" (folio 48b) — all translated without softening.
4. **The temple charge closes the chapter.** Buying and selling in the temple "as in a market-place," turning the house of prayer into "a cave of robbers," doing all to please men with God put out of mind, brings the sentence: "sons of the devil and not sons of Abraham" and "God will take the priesthood away from you" (folio 48b). Abraham is named as the opposite measure — he left his father's house for love of God and was willing to slay his own son — as written.

---

---

## Chapter 46

*[Editorial heading: The vineyard let out, and the bent woman healed on the Sabbath. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XLVIe.") opens with a fresh address. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus spoke again, saying: "I put an example before you. There was a father of a family who planted a vineyard and made a hedge round it so that animals would not trample it, and in the middle he built a wine-press. Then he let it out to farmers. When the time came to gather the wine, he sent his servants. When the farmers saw them, they stoned some, burned some, and cut others open with a knife. And they did this many times.

"Tell me, what will the owner of the vineyard do to those farmers?"

Everyone answered: "He will make them perish miserably, and he will give his vineyard to other farmers."

Then Jesus said: "Do you not know that the vineyard is the house of Israel, and the farmers are the people of Judaea and Jerusalem? Woe to you, for God is angry with you. You have cut open so many prophets of God that at the time of Ahab there was no one to be found to bury God's holy ones."

When he had said this, the chief priests wanted to seize him, but they feared the common people, who praised him.

Then Jesus saw a woman who from her birth had stood with her head bent down toward the ground. He said: "Lift up your head, O woman, in the name of our God, so that these men may know that I speak the truth, and that God wills me to announce it."

Then the woman stood up whole, praising God.

The chief of the priests cried out, saying: "This man is not sent from God, for he does not keep the Sabbath. Today he has healed a sick person."

Jesus answered: "Now tell me, is it not lawful to speak on the Sabbath day, and to pray for the health of others? And who is there among you who, if his ass or his ox fell into a ditch on the Sabbath, would not pull it out on the Sabbath? Certainly none. Shall I then have broken the Sabbath day because I have given health to a daughter of Israel? Here your hypocrisy is plainly known. O how many there are today who fear a straw striking them in another man's eye, while they have a beam that will cut off their own head! O how many who fear an ant, but take no care about an elephant!"

Having said this, he went out of the temple. But the priests ground their teeth among themselves because they could not seize him and do their will on him, as their fathers did against God's holy ones.

### Notes — Chapter 46
1. **The vineyard parable is applied at once.** The owner plants, hedges, and builds a press, then lets the vineyard out; the servants are stoned, burned, and cut open with a knife, "many times" (folio 49a). Jesus names the vineyard as the house of Israel and the farmers as the people of Judaea and Jerusalem, and the woe follows the same charge — prophets cut open, and in Ahab's time no one left to bury God's holy ones. The violence of the Italian verbs is kept as written.
2. **The bent woman.** She has stood with her head bent to the ground "from her birth" and is told to lift her head "in the name of our God" so the hearers may know the speaker speaks truth (folio 49a–49b). She stands whole, praising God. No name is given to her in the Italian.
3. **The Sabbath defence** rests on speech and prayer for another's health being lawful, and on the ass or ox in the ditch that no one would leave till after the Sabbath (folio 49b). The conclusion is drawn against the accusers: giving health to a daughter of Israel is not a breach.
4. **Straw, beam, ant, elephant.** The closing images grow in the Italian from a straw in another's eye with a beam at one's own head, to fearing an ant and not caring for an elephant (folio 49b). They are translated as written, without reducing them to the familiar canonical forms.

---

## Chapter 47

*[Editorial heading: The widow's only son at Nain raised. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XLVII c.") opens in the second year. The heading above is editorial.]*

In the second year of his prophetic work Jesus went down from Jerusalem and went to Nain. As he drew near the gate of the city, the citizens were carrying to the burial place the only son of his mother, who was a widow. Everyone was weeping over her.

When Jesus arrived, the men understood that Jesus the prophet of Galilee had come, and they set themselves to beg him for the dead man, that since he was a prophet he would raise him. His disciples did the same.

Then Jesus was greatly afraid. Turning to God, he said: "Take me from the world, O Lord, for the world is mad, and they will soon call me God."

Having said this, he wept.

Then the angel Gabriel came and said to him: "O Jesus, do not fear, for God has given you power over every sickness, so that everything you grant in God's name shall be fully done."

At this Jesus sighed and said: "Your will be done, Lord God, almighty and merciful."

Having said this, he came near to the mother of the dead man and with pity said to her: "Woman, do not weep."

He took the hand of the dead man and said: "I say to you, young man, in the name of God, rise up well."

Then the boy came back to life. Everyone was filled with fear, saying: "God has raised up a great prophet among us, and he has visited his people."

### Notes — Chapter 47
1. **"In the second year."** The chapter dates itself to the second year of Jesus' prophetic work (folio 50a). The book counts a three-year ministry; the marker is kept where the Italian puts it.
2. **The fear before the miracle.** Before acting, Jesus is "greatly afraid," turns to God, and asks to be taken from the world, "for the world is mad, and they will soon call me God" (folio 50a). Gabriel's answer gives power over every sickness, everything granted in God's name to be fully done. The sequence — fear, protest, reassurance, sigh, "Your will be done" — is translated in order, without shortening.
3. **"Woman, do not weep"** and the taking of the dead man's hand precede the word: "I say to you, young man, in the name of God, rise up well" (folio 50a). The boy revives and the crowd's verdict is kept as written: a great prophet raised up, and God has visited his people.

---

## Chapter 48

*[Editorial heading: Talk of gods at Nain, and the sick healed at Capernaum. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XLVIII a.") opens with the Roman army in Judaea. The heading above is editorial.]*

At that time the Roman army was in Judaea, for our country was subject to them because of the sins of our fathers. The Romans had a custom of calling anyone a god, and worshipping him, who did some new thing for the good of the common people.

Some of these soldiers were at Nain. They rebuked now one man, now another, saying: "One of your gods has visited you, and you take no account of it. Surely if our gods visited us, we would give them everything we have. You can see how much we fear our gods, since we give the best of what we have to their images."

Satan worked so much on this talk that he stirred up no small discord among the people of Nain.

But Jesus did not stay at all in Nain. He turned to go to Capernaum. The discord at Nain was such that some said: "He is our God, who has visited us." Others said: "God is invisible, so that no one has seen him, not even Moses, his friend and servant. So he is not God, but rather his son." Others said: "He is not God, nor the son of God, for God has no body with which to beget. He is a great prophet of God."

Satan worked so much that in the third year of Jesus' prophetic work great ruin was about to arise from this among our people.

Jesus went into Capernaum. When the citizens knew him, they gathered together all the sick people they had and placed them in front of the porch where Jesus was lodging with his disciples. They called Jesus out and begged him for their health.

Then Jesus laid his hands on each one of them, saying: "God of Israel, by your holy name give health to this sick person."

And everyone was healed.

On the Sabbath Jesus entered the synagogue, and all the people ran together there to hear him speak.

### Notes — Chapter 48
1. **The Roman custom as the book states it.** The soldiers' rebuke rests on the claim given here: Romans call a man a god and worship him for any new public benefit, and give the best they have to their gods' images (folio 50b). The statement is the book's own explanation for what follows and is translated as written.
2. **Three verdicts at Nain, kept distinct.** "He is our God"; "God is invisible — not even Moses has seen him — so he is his son"; "not God nor son of God, for God has no body to beget — a great prophet of God" (folios 50b–51a). The three positions are left standing side by side, with Satan named as the worker of the discord and the third year named as the time when ruin nearly arose.
3. **Capernaum follows without a stop at Nain.** The sick are laid before the porch of the lodging, Jesus is called out, and the healing word is the same for each: "God of Israel, by your holy name give health to this sick person" (folio 51a). The chapter ends at the synagogue door on the Sabbath, where the next teaching begins.

---

## Chapter 49

*[Editorial heading: On judging — the first part, in the synagogue at Capernaum. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XLIX c.") opens with the scribe's reading. The heading above is editorial.]*

That day the scribe read the psalm of David where David says: "When I take the time, I will judge justice."

After the reading of the prophets, Jesus stood up, made a sign for silence with his hands, opened his mouth, and spoke like this:

"Brothers, you have heard the words that David, the prophet, our father, speaks: that when he has taken the time, he will judge justice.

"I tell you in truth, many judge, and in that judgment they fall — for no other reason than that they judge what does not belong to them, and what does belong to them they judge before the time. So the God of our fathers cries to us through his prophet David, saying: 'Judge justly, O sons of men.'

"Wretched, then, are those who set themselves at the corners of the streets and do nothing but judge everyone who passes, saying: 'That one is fair, this one is ugly, that one is good, this one is bad.' Woe to them, for they lift the sceptre of his judgment out of God's hand — he who says: 'I am witness and judge, and my honour I will give to no one.'

"I tell you in truth, these men testify to what they have not seen and have not truly heard, and they judge without having been made judges. So they are hateful on the earth before God's eyes, and he will give a dreadful judgment on them on the last day.

"Woe to you, woe to you who say good of evil and call evil good, for you condemn God as a wrongdoer — he who is the author of goodness — and you justify Satan as good, who is the origin of every evil. Think what punishment you will have, and that it is horrible to fall into God's judgment. What will become of those who justify the wicked for money and do not judge the case of orphans and widows? Truly I say to you, the devils will tremble at the judgment of such men, so terrible will it be.

"You, O man, who are set as a judge: look at no other thing — not relatives or friends, not honour or gain. Look only, with fear of God, to the truth, which you shall seek with the greatest diligence, for it will make you safe in God's judgment. But I warn you: he who judges without mercy shall be judged without mercy."

### Notes — Chapter 49
1. **The text of the day.** The scribe reads David's words, "When I take the time, I will judge justice" (folio 51a). Jesus' whole teaching in chapters 49–50 hangs on that reading and on the companion cry, "Judge justly, O sons of men" (folio 51b). Both are kept as the chapter's stated grounds.
2. **Two faults in judging.** Judging what does not belong to the judge, and judging before the time what does belong to him (folio 51a–51b). The street-corner judges — "that one is fair, this one is ugly, that one is good, this one is bad" — are the picture given, and the charge is that they lift God's sceptre from his hand, against his word "I am witness and judge, and my honour I will give to no one" (folio 51b).
3. **Calling evil good.** The double woe falls on those who "say good of evil and call evil good," condemning God the author of goodness as a wrongdoer and justifying Satan the origin of evil as good (folio 52a). The warning about those who justify the wicked for money and neglect orphans and widows, and that the devils will tremble at their judgment, is translated without softening.
4. **The judge's rule closes the chapter.** No relatives, friends, honour, or gain; only the truth, sought with the greatest diligence, under fear of God (folio 52a). "He who judges without mercy shall be judged without mercy" is the chapter's final sentence as written.

---

## Chapter 50

*[Editorial heading: On judging — the second part: false judgment and its examples. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "L c.") continues the same synagogue teaching. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Tell me, O man — you who judge another man — do you not know that all men had their origin from one clay? Do you not know that no one is good except God alone, so that every man is a liar and a sinner? Believe me, man: if you judge another for a fault, your own heart has something to be judged for.

"O how dangerous it is to judge! O how many have perished by their false judgment! Satan judged man to be viler than himself, so he rebelled against God his creator, and so he is without repentance, as I learned by speaking with him. Our first parents judged Satan's speech to be good, so they were driven out of paradise and condemned all their offspring.

"Truly I say to you — as God lives, in whose presence I stand — false judgment is the father of all sins. For no one sins without will, and no one wills what he does not know. Woe, then, to the sinner who by his judgment judges sin to be worthy and good to be unworthy — who for that reason rejects the good and chooses sin. Surely he will bear an unbearable punishment when God comes to judge the world.

"O how many have perished by false judgment, and how many have come near to perishing! Pharaoh judged Moses and the people of Israel to be wicked. Saul judged David worthy of death. Ahab judged Elijah. Nebuchadnezzar judged the three boys who would not worship their lying gods. The two old men judged Susanna. And all the idolatrous princes judged the prophets. O dreadful judgment of God! The judge perishes; the one judged is saved. And why is this, O man, if not because in their heedlessness they falsely judged the innocent?

"How near the good then came to ruin by judging badly is shown by Joseph's brothers, who sold him to the Egyptians; by Aaron and Mary, Moses' sister, who judged their brother; by Job's three friends, who judged Job, God's innocent friend; by David, who judged Mephibosheth and Uriah; by Cyrus, who judged Daniel to be food for the lions; and by many others who were near their ruin for this. So I say to you: Do not judge, and you shall not be judged."

When Jesus had finished his speaking, many were turned to repentance, weeping for their sins, and they wanted to leave everything to go with him. But Jesus said: "Stay in your own homes, leave sin, and serve God with fear, and so you shall be saved. For I have not come to receive service, but to serve."

Having said this, he went out of the synagogue and out of the city, and withdrew into the desert to pray, for he loved solitude greatly.

### Notes — Chapter 50
1. **One clay, one good.** The argument opens from shared origin — all men from one clay — and from the rule that none is good but God alone, so every man is a liar and a sinner (folio 52a). The judge's own heart having matter for judgment follows from that ground, as written.
2. **False judgment as "father of all sins."** The chain given is: no sin without will, no will without knowledge, so the judgment that calls sin worthy and good unworthy fathers the act (folio 52b). The phrase is kept as the book's own summary of the teaching.
3. **The two lists of judges are kept entire.** Those who perished: Pharaoh on Moses and Israel, Saul on David, Ahab on Elijah, Nebuchadnezzar on the three boys, the two elders on Susanna, the idolatrous princes on the prophets — "the judge perishes; the one judged is saved" (folio 52b–53a). Those who came near ruin: Joseph's brothers, Aaron and Mary on Moses, Job's three friends on Job, David on Mephibosheth and Uriah, Cyrus on Daniel "to be food for the lions" (folio 53a). Names and charges stand exactly as the Italian lists them, including Cyrus where other accounts name another king; the reading is not corrected in the text.
4. **"Do not judge, and you shall not be judged"** ends the teaching (folio 53a). The hearers wish to leave everything and follow; Jesus sends them home instead — leave sin, serve God with fear — "for I have not come to receive service, but to serve." He leaves the synagogue and the city for the desert to pray, "for he loved solitude greatly." All as written.

---

---

## Chapter 51

*[Editorial heading: Jesus pities Satan, who will not repent. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LI") opens after the prayer in the desert. The heading above is editorial.]*

When he had prayed to the Lord, his disciples came near to him and said: "O master, two things we want to know. One is how you talked with Satan, since you say he has no repentance. The other is how God will come to judge on the day of judgment."

Jesus answered: "Truly I tell you, I had pity on Satan, knowing his fall, and I had pity on the human race, whom he urges to sin. So I prayed and fasted to our God, who spoke to me through his angel Gabriel: 'What do you seek, O Jesus, and what is your request?'

"I answered: 'Lord, you know of how much evil Satan is the cause, and that through his temptations many perish. He is your creature, Lord, whom you created. So, Lord, have mercy on him.'

"God answered: 'Jesus, look, I will pardon him. Only make him say: "Lord my God, I have sinned; have mercy on me," and I will pardon him and bring him back to his first state.'
