"I was very glad when I heard this," said Jesus, "thinking I had made this peace. So I called Satan, who came and said: 'What must I do for you, O Jesus?'

"I answered: 'You will do it for yourself, O Satan, for I do not love your service, but I have called you for your own good.'

"Satan answered: 'If you do not want my service, I do not want yours either, for I am nobler than you. You are not worthy to serve me — you who are clay, while I am spirit.'

"'Let us leave this,' I said, 'and tell me: would it not be good for you to return to your first beauty and your first state? You must know that on the day of judgment the angel Michael must strike you with the sword of God a hundred thousand times, and every blow will give you the pain of ten hells.'

"Satan answered: 'We shall see on that day who can do more. Surely I shall have on my side many angels and very powerful idolaters, who will trouble God, and he will know what a great mistake he made in driving me out for the sake of a vile piece of clay.'

"Then I said: 'O Satan, your mind is sick, and you do not know what you are saying.'

Then Satan, mocking, shook his head and said: "Come now, let us make this peace between me and God. What must be done? You say it, O Jesus, since your mind is sound."

I answered: "Only two words need to be said."

Satan answered: "What words?"

I answered: "These: 'I have sinned; have mercy on me.'"

Then Satan said: "Now I will gladly make this peace — provided God says these words to me."

"Now get away from me," I said, "O cursed one, for you are the wicked author of all injustice and sin, but God is just and without any sin."

Satan went away shrieking and said: "It is not so, O Jesus. You are telling a lie to please God."

"Now consider," said Jesus to his disciples, "how he will find mercy."

They answered: "Never, Lord, for he is without repentance. Now tell us about the judgment of God."

### Notes — Chapter 51
1. **The two questions open the long judgment teaching (chapters 51–55).** The disciples ask how Jesus spoke with Satan (recalled from chapter 50) and how God will come to judge (folio 53b). The Satan conversation is told here in full, in direct speech, and is translated in order.
2. **The pardon offered and refused.** God will pardon Satan if Satan will say only, "Lord my God, I have sinned; have mercy on me," and be restored to his first state (folio 53b). Satan's refusal turns on rank — "I am nobler than you… you are clay, while I am spirit" — and on the counter-demand that God say the words to him first (folios 54a–54b). The clay/spirit contrast and the counter-demand are kept as written.
3. **The numbers are kept as written.** Michael is to strike Satan with the sword of God "a hundred thousand times," each blow giving "the pain of ten hells" (folio 54a). Satan's boast of angels and powerful idolaters who "will trouble God" is likewise kept; the 1907 editors flag the Italian at this point as possibly to be read "they will trouble God" (their note is a reading note, cited here as a fact, not copied as text).

---

## Chapter 52

*[Editorial heading: The dread of the judgment day. Manuscript rubric continues the same discourse. The heading above is editorial.]*

"The day of God's judgment will be so dreadful — truly I tell you — that the condemned would sooner choose ten hells than go to hear God speak in anger against them; against them every created thing will bear witness. Truly I tell you, not only the condemned will fear, but the saints and the chosen of God will fear so much that Abraham will not trust in his own righteousness, and Job will have no confidence in his innocence.

"And what shall I say? Even the messenger of God will fear, because God, to make known his majesty, will take memory away from his messenger, so that he will not remember how God has given him all things. Truly I tell you, speaking from the heart, I tremble because I shall be called God by the world, and for this I shall have to give an account. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands, I am a mortal man like other men. For although God has set me as a prophet over the house of Israel, for the health of the weak and the correction of sinners, I am the servant of God — and you are witnesses of this — how I speak against those wicked men who, after my departure from the world, will cancel the truth of my gospel by the working of Satan. But I shall return toward the end, and with me will come Enoch and Elijah, and we shall bear witness against the wicked, whose end shall be accursed."

And when Jesus had said this, he wept. His disciples wept aloud and lifted up their voices, saying: "Pardon, O Lord God, and have mercy on your innocent servant." Jesus answered: "Amen, amen."

### Notes — Chapter 52
1. **"Even the messenger of God will fear."** God will remove the messenger's memory "so that he will not remember how God has given him all things" (folio 54b–55a). This is part of the seam-zone teaching: the messenger here is the coming figure of chapters 42/96–97, already named in this book; the passage is translated exactly as written and not harmonised with any other scripture.
2. **"I shall be called God by the world."** Jesus trembles because the world will call him God and he must give an account for it; he insists, "As God lives… I am a mortal man like other men… I am the servant of God" (folio 55a). The protest is kept at full strength, without softening.
3. **The return with Enoch and Elijah.** After his departure, the wicked "will cancel the truth of my gospel by the working of Satan"; Jesus will return toward the end with Enoch and Elijah to witness against them (folio 55a). The charge of a future cancellation is the book's own claim and is presented as such in the text, not endorsed in the apparatus.

---

## Chapter 53

*[Editorial heading: The signs before the judgment — wars, then fifteen days of signs. Manuscript rubric continues the discourse. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Before that day comes," said Jesus, "great ruin will come before it in the world, for there will be wars so cruel and without pity that the father will kill the son and the son will kill the father, because of the factions of peoples. So cities will be left empty and regions turned to desert. Such plagues will come that no one will be found to carry the dead to burial, so they will be left as food for animals. To those who remain on the earth God will send such barrenness that bread will be valued more than gold, and every unclean thing will be eaten. O miserable age, in which almost no one will be heard to say, 'I have sinned; have mercy on me, O God' — but with horrible voices they will blaspheme him who is glorious and blessed forever.

"After this, as that day draws near, for fifteen days a horrible sign will come every day over those who live on the earth. On the first day the sun will run its course in the sky without any brightness, but black like dyed cloth, and it will give groans like a father groaning for a son near death. On the second day the moon will be turned into blood, and blood will come on the earth like dew. On the third day the stars will be seen fighting among themselves like an army of enemies. On the fourth day the stones and rocks will strike one against another like cruel enemies. On the fifth day every plant and herb will weep blood. On the sixth day the sea will rise up, without leaving its place, to the height of a hundred and fifty cubits, and will stand all day like a wall. On the seventh day it will sink down just as much, so that it can hardly be seen. On the eighth day the birds and the animals of the earth and of the water will gather close to one another and will give roars and cries. On the ninth day there will be a hailstorm so horrible that it will kill in such a way that barely a tenth part of living things will escape. On the tenth day there will come lightnings and thunders so horrible that they will split and burn a third part of the mountains. On the eleventh day every river will run backward and will run blood and not water. On the twelfth day every created thing will groan and weep. On the thirteenth day the sky will be rolled up like a book and it will rain fire, so that every living thing will die. On the fourteenth day there will be an earthquake so horrible that the tops of the mountains will fly through the air like birds, and all the earth will become a plain. On the fifteenth day the holy angels will die, and God alone will remain alive, to whom be honour and glory."

And having said this, Jesus struck his face with both his hands, and then struck the ground with his head. And having raised his head, he said: "Cursed be everyone who will put into my words that I am the son of God." At these words the disciples fell down as dead. Jesus lifted them up, saying: "Let us fear God now, if we do not want to be terrified on that day."

### Notes — Chapter 53
1. **The fifteen signs are listed in full and in order** (folios 55b–56b): blackened sun groaning like a father; moon turned to blood and blood like dew; stars fighting; stones striking; plants weeping blood; sea raised 150 cubits like a wall, then sunk as low; birds and animals gathering and crying; hail killing nine-tenths; lightning splitting a third of the mountains; rivers running backward with blood; all creation groaning; sky rolled up like a book raining fire; earthquake flattening the earth; holy angels dying, God alone alive. Numbers (150 cubits; a tenth part; a third part) are kept as written.
2. **The curse on "son of God."** After the signs, Jesus strikes his face and the ground and says, "Cursed be everyone who will put into my words that I am the son of God" (folio 56b). Translated exactly as written; this is the book's repeated denial (compare chapters 42 and 52) and is not softened or removed.

---

## Chapter 54

*[Editorial heading: The resurrection — darkness, the messenger raised first, and the throne. Manuscript rubric continues the discourse. The heading above is editorial.]*

"When these signs have passed, there will be darkness over the world for forty years, with God alone alive, to whom be honour and glory forever. When the forty years have passed, God will give life to his messenger, who will rise again like the sun, but shining like a thousand suns. He will sit and will not speak, for he will be as if beside himself. God will raise again the four angels favoured by God, who will seek the messenger of God and, having seen him, will place themselves on four sides of the place to watch over him. Then God will give life to all the angels, who like bees will come circling round the messenger of God. Then God will give life to all his prophets, who, following Adam, will go one by one to kiss the hand of the messenger of God, entrusting themselves to him. Then God will give life to every chosen one, who will cry out: 'O Mohammed, remember us!' At their cries pity will awaken in the messenger of God, and he will consider what he must do, fearing for their salvation. Then God will give life to every created thing, and they will return to their being, except that every one will also have a voice. Then God will give life to all the condemned, at whose rising, because of their ugliness, every creature of God will be afraid and will cry: 'Do not let your mercy leave us, O Lord our God.' After this God will make Satan rise again, at whose appearance every creature, for fear, will be as dead, because of the horrid form he will show. 'May it please God,' said Jesus, 'that I do not see that monster on that day. Only the messenger of God will not receive fear from such shapes, because he will fear God alone.'

"Then the angel at whose trumpet-blast all will have risen will sound the trumpet again, saying: 'Come to the judgment, O creatures, for your Creator wants to judge you.' Then there will appear in the middle of the sky, over the valley of Jehoshaphat, a shining throne, over which a white cloud will come. The angels will cry out: 'Blessed be you, our God, who created us and saved us from the fall of Satan.' Then the messenger of God will fear, because he will know that no one has loved God as much as he should. For whoever wants to change a gold coin needs sixty small coins; so if he has only one small coin, he cannot change it. But if the messenger of God will fear, what will the wicked do, who are full of wickedness?"

### Notes — Chapter 54
1. **The order of rising is kept as written** (folios 56b–57b): forty years of darkness with God alone alive; the messenger raised first, shining like a thousand suns, silent "as if beside himself"; the four favoured angels set as watchers; all angels circling like bees; the prophets, following Adam, kissing the messenger's hand; the elect crying, "O Mohammed, remember us!"; all created things given voice; the condemned raised in ugliness; Satan raised last in horrid form. The sequence is the book's own and is not rearranged.
2. **The naming in this chapter is seam material and is footnoted as such.** The Italian names the elect's cry with the name "Mohammed" ("ho machometo") while elsewhere calling the figure "the messenger of God" (folio 57a). Both forms are translated exactly as they stand — the name where the Italian gives the name, "messenger of God" where it gives only that — with no substitution in either direction. This is the judgment-day working-out of the seam announced in chapters 42 and 96–97. Robert's standing policy R4 is confirmed for this passage: translate-and-footnote, never smooth — the name stands where the Italian gives the name, and 'messenger of God' stands where the Italian gives only that.
3. **The gold coin and the sixty small coins** (folio 57b): the messenger fears because love owed to God is like a gold coin that needs sixty mites to change, and he has but one. The arithmetic is kept as written.

---

## Chapter 55

*[Editorial heading: The messenger at the throne — intercession and the book of the elect. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LV") continues the discourse. The heading above is editorial.]*

"The messenger of God will go to gather all the prophets, to whom he will speak, asking them to go with him to pray to God for the faithful. And every one will excuse himself for fear. As God lives, I would not go there, knowing what I know. Then, seeing this, God will remind his messenger how he created every thing for love of him. For this reason his fear will leave him, and with love and reverence he will go near the throne, while the angels sing: 'Blessed be your holy name, O our God.' Having come near the throne, God will open himself to his messenger as a friend opens himself to a friend when they have not seen each other for a long time. The messenger of God will speak first, saying: 'I worship and love you, O my God, and with all my heart and soul I thank you, because you saw fit to create me to be your servant, and you made everything for love of me, so that I might love you for every thing and in every thing and above every thing. So let all your creatures thank you, O my God.' Then everything created by God will say: 'We thank you, Lord, and we bless your holy name.' At this time, truly I tell you, the demons and the condemned with Satan will weep so much that more water will come out of the eyes of one of them than there is in the river Jordan. And still they will not see God.

"God will speak to his messenger, saying: 'You are welcome, O my faithful servant. So ask what you want, for you will obtain everything.' The messenger of God will answer: 'O Lord, I remember that when you created me you said that you wanted to make, for love of me, paradise and the world, the angels and men, so that through me, your servant, they would glorify you. O Lord God, merciful and just, I pray you, remember the promise made to me, your servant.' God will answer, like a friend joking with a friend, and will say: 'Do you have witnesses of this, my friend Mohammed?' With reverence he will say: 'Yes, Lord.' God will answer: 'Go, call them, O Gabriel.' The angel Gabriel will come to the messenger of God and will say: 'Lord, who are your witnesses?' The messenger of God will answer: 'They are Adam, Abraham, Ishmael, Moses, David, and Jesus son of Mary.'

"Then the angel will go and will call those named above, who will go there with fear. When they are present, God will say to them: 'Do you remember what my messenger says?' They will answer: 'What thing, Lord?' God will say: 'That I have made everything for love of him, so that all would praise me through him.' Then every one of them will answer: 'With us there are three witnesses better than we are, O Lord.' Then God will answer: 'Who are these three witnesses?' Moses will say: 'The book you gave me is the first.' David will answer: 'The second is the book you gave me.' And the one who is speaking to you will say: 'Lord, the whole world, deceived by Satan, said that I was your son and your companion; but the book you gave me said truly that I am your servant, and that book confesses what your messenger says.' Then the messenger of God will speak and will say: 'So says the book you gave me, O Lord.' And when the messenger of God has said this, God will speak, saying: 'All that I have now done, I have done so that everyone may know how much I love you.' And having said this, God will give his messenger a book in which are written all the elect of God. So every creature will do reverence to God, saying: 'To you alone, O God, be praise and honour, because you have given us to your messenger.'"

### Notes — Chapter 55
1. **The throne dialogue is translated in full** (folios 57b–60a): the prophets excusing themselves; God restoring the messenger's memory of creation "for love of him"; the messenger's thanksgiving; the weeping of the demons and condemned ("more water… than there is in the river Jordan"); God's "ask what you want"; the appeal to the creation promise; the witnesses called. Nothing is abbreviated.
2. **"My friend Mohammed."** God addresses the messenger by name — "my friend Mohammed" (folio 58b) — and the witness list is Adam, Abraham, Ishmael, Moses, David, and Jesus son of Mary (folio 58b). Both are seam passages: translated exactly as written and flagged here, per the standing seam policy. Robert's standing policy R4 is confirmed for this passage: translate-and-footnote, never smooth — the name stands where the Italian gives the name, and 'messenger of God' stands where the Italian gives only that. The name is not removed, and "messenger of God" is not rewritten as the name where the Italian does not give it.
3. **The three books as witnesses.** Moses names the book given to him as first witness, David the book given to him as second, and Jesus the book given to him as third, saying the world deceived by Satan called him God's son and companion while his book said truly he is God's servant (folios 59a–60a). The book given to the messenger then confirms the claim, and God gives the messenger the book in which all the elect are written. The four-book scheme is kept exactly as the Italian presents it.

---

---

## Chapter 56

*[Editorial heading: The book opened — the elect take their places at God's right hand. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LVI") continues the judgment discourse of chapters 51–55. The heading above is editorial.]*

God will open the book in the hand of his messenger. His messenger, reading in it, will call all the angels and the prophets and all the elect, and on the forehead of each one will be written the faith of the messenger of God. And in the book will be written the glory of paradise. Then each one will go to the right hand of God. Next to God will sit his messenger, and the prophets will sit next to him, and the saints will sit next to the prophets, and the blessed will sit next to the saints. Then the angel will sound the trumpet and will call Satan to judgment.

### Notes — Chapter 56
1. **The judgment seating is kept in order as written** (folios 60b–61a): the book opened in the messenger's hand; the angels, prophets, and elect called by name; the glory of paradise written in the book; the company passing to God's right hand in descending order — messenger nearest, then prophets, then saints, then the blessed.
2. **"The faith of the messenger of God" on the forehead.** The Italian reads *la fede del nontio di Dio* — "the faith of the messenger of God" — written on each forehead (folio 60b). The 1907 check rendering gives "the mark of the messenger of God." The Spanish witness reads "la Ley del Nuncio" ("the Law of the Messenger"). The Italian word is translated as it stands here ("faith"); the three-way fork (Italian faith / Spanish Law / check mark) is footnoted rather than harmonised. This is seam-adjacent material: the messenger is the figure of the seam announced in chapters 42 and 96–97, and the wording is not altered in either direction. Robert's standing policy R4 is confirmed for this passage: translate-and-footnote, never smooth — the name stands where the Italian gives the name, and 'messenger of God' stands where the Italian gives only that.
3. **The trumpet call to Satan closes the chapter** and opens the sentencing that follows in chapter 57.

---

## Chapter 57

*[Editorial heading: Satan struck down, his followers with him, and the reprobates condemned. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LVIIa.") continues the discourse. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then that wretched one will come, and he will be accused by every creature with the greatest shame. God will call the angel Michael, who will strike him a hundred thousand times with the sword of God. He will strike Satan, and every blow will be heavy as ten hells. And he will be the first thrown into the abyss. The angel will call his followers, and they too will be shamed and accused in the same way. The angel Michael, by God's commission, will strike some a hundred times, some fifty, some twenty, some ten, and some five times. And then they will go down into the abyss, for God will say to them: "Hell is your dwelling, O cursed ones."

After that, all the unbelievers and the reprobates will be called to judgment. Against them, first, every creature below man will rise up, bearing witness before God how they served these men, and how these men outraged God and his creatures. And every prophet will rise up, bearing witness against them. So they will be condemned by God to the flames of hell. Truly I tell you, not one idle word or idle thought will pass without punishment on that dreadful day. Truly I tell you, the hair-shirt will shine like the sun, and every louse a man has borne for love of God will be turned into a pearl. O, three and four times blessed are the poor, who with true poverty have served God from the heart! In this world they are free of worldly trouble, and so they are free of many sins; and on that day they will not have to give an account of how they spent the riches of the world, but they will be rewarded for their patience and their poverty. Truly I tell you, if the world knew this, it would sooner choose the hair-shirt than purple, lice than gold, and fasts than feasts.

When everything has been examined, God will say to his messenger: "See, O my friend, how great their wickedness has been. I, their creator, put every created thing to work in their service, and in everything they have dishonoured me. It is therefore most just that I have no mercy on them." The messenger of God will answer: "It is true, Lord, our glorious God. Not one of your friends or your servants could ask you to have mercy on them. Rather, I, your servant, before everyone, ask for justice against them." And when he has said these words, all the angels and the prophets, with all God's elect — no, why do I say the elect? Truly I tell you, spiders and flies, stones and sand will cry out against the wicked and will demand justice. Then God will send every living soul below man back to the earth, and he will send the wicked to hell. As they go, they will see that earth again, to which the dogs and the horses and the other low animals will be returned. So they will say: "O Lord God, let us also be returned to that earth." But what they ask will not be granted to them.

### Notes — Chapter 57
1. **The sentencing numbers are kept as written** (folios 60b–61a): Satan struck "a hundred thousand times" with the sword of God, each blow "heavy as ten hells," and cast first into the abyss; his followers struck a hundred, fifty, twenty, ten, or five times, each according to rank, then sent down with the sentence, "Hell is your dwelling, O cursed ones." The figures are the book's own and are not rounded or explained away.
2. **The witnesses against the reprobates rise in two companies** — first every creature below man, testifying how they served and were outraged, then every prophet (folio 61a). No idle word or idle thought escapes punishment; the hair-shirt shines like the sun and the louse borne for God's love becomes a pearl; the poor are blessed three and four times over for having no worldly riches to account for. The comparisons (hair-shirt against purple, lice against gold, fasts against feasts) are kept at full strength.
3. **The messenger asks for justice, not mercy.** God shows the messenger the wickedness of the condemned — every created thing put in their service, dishonour in return — and the messenger answers that no friend or servant could ask mercy for them, and himself asks justice first of all (folio 61a). Even spiders, flies, stones, and sand cry out for justice. God returns the lower living souls to earth and sends the wicked to hell; seeing the earth to which even dogs and horses return, they beg to be reduced to that earth too, and are refused. The refusal is translated as written.

---

## Chapter 58

*[Editorial heading: John's two questions — why the messenger will have no pity, and how one sword can weigh ten hells. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LVIIIe.") continues the discourse. The heading above is editorial.]*

While Jesus was speaking, the disciples wept bitterly, and Jesus wept many tears. After the weeping, John spoke: "O master, there are two things we want to know. The first is: how can the messenger of God, who is full of pity and mercy, have no pity on the reprobates on that day, since they were made from the same clay as himself? The second is: how is it to be understood that the sword of Michael is heavy as ten hells? Is there, then, more than one hell?"

Jesus answered: "Have you not heard what David the prophet says — that the just man will laugh at the ruin of the sinner and will mock him, saying: 'I saw the man who put his hope in his own strength and in his riches, and forgot God.' Truly I tell you, Abraham will mock his own father, and Adam will mock all reprobate men. And this will be because the elect will rise so perfect and so united to God that not the smallest thought against God's justice will enter their minds. So every one of them will demand justice — and the messenger of God more than all the others. As God lives, in whose presence I stand: I weep now for pity of human nature, but on that day I will demand justice without mercy against those who despise my words — and most of all against those who corrupt my gospel."

### Notes — Chapter 58
1. **John's first question goes to the heart of the judgment teaching** (folio 61a–61b): how can the messenger, full of pity and mercy, show no pity to the reprobates, who share his clay? The answer given is not an exception for the messenger but a transformation of all the elect: risen perfect and united to God, they can conceive no thought against his justice, and so each demands justice, the messenger above all.
2. **The mockery texts are kept as written.** David's just man laughing at the sinner's ruin ("I saw the man who put his hope in his strength and his riches, and forgot God"); Abraham mocking his father; Adam mocking all reprobate men (folio 61b). Jesus applies the same to himself: though he weeps now for pity of humankind, on that day he will demand justice without mercy against those who despise his words, "and most of all against those who defile my gospel." The charge of a future corruption of the gospel is the book's own claim (compare chapter 52) and is presented as such, not endorsed in the apparatus.
3. **The second question — whether there is more than one hell — is taken up in the next chapter** and answered there: hell is one, with seven regions.

---

## Chapter 59

*[Editorial heading: Hell is one, with seven regions — and a rebuke of rest. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LIX") continues the discourse. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Hell is one, O my disciples, and in it the damned will suffer their punishment forever. Yet it has seven chambers, or regions, one deeper than the other, and whoever goes to the deepest will suffer the greater punishment. Yet my word about the sword of the angel Michael is entirely true. For whoever commits a single sin deserves hell, and whoever commits two sins deserves two hells. So in one hell the reprobates will feel punishment as if they were divided among ten hells, or a hundred, or a thousand. For almighty God, by his power and for his justice, will make it so that Satan will have as much pain as would be found in ten hundred thousand hells, and the others each according to their wickedness."

Then Peter answered: "O master, truly God's justice is great, and this speaking has made you very sorrowful today. So do us the favour: rest, and tomorrow you will tell us what hell is like."

Jesus answered: "O Peter, you tell me to rest. O Peter, you do not know what you are saying, or you would not have spoken so. Truly I tell you, rest in this present life is the poison of pity and the fire that burns up every good work. Have you forgotten how Solomon, God's prophet, with every prophet, reproaches laziness? It is true that he says: the lazy man, for fear of the cold, would not work the earth, so in the summer he will go begging. And he said: everything your hand can do, do it without rest. And what does Job, God's most innocent friend, say of our life? As the bird is born to fly, so man is born to work. Truly I tell you, I hate rest above everything."

### Notes — Chapter 59
1. **One hell, seven regions, multiplied pain** (folio 61b–62a). Hell is one and eternal, with seven chambers one below the other, the deepest the worst. The sword arithmetic is explained on the book's own terms: one sin earns hell, two sins earn two hells, so the damned feel in a single hell the pain of ten, a hundred, or a thousand hells, and Satan the pain of ten hundred thousand, each measured by wickedness. The numbers are kept as written and not converted into a general doctrine.
2. **Peter's request for rest draws the rebuke that closes the chapter** (folio 62a): rest in this life is "the poison of pity" and the fire that consumes every good work; Solomon's lazy man who will not work for fear of cold and begs in summer; the command to do all the hand can do without rest; Job's bird born to fly and man born to work. The saying "I hate rest above all things" is kept at full strength.

---

## Chapter 60

*[Editorial heading: What hell is like — the contrary of paradise. Manuscript rubric continues the discourse of chapter 59 (the "contrary" teaching, folios 62b–63b). The heading above is editorial.]*

"Hell is one, and it is the contrary of paradise, as winter is contrary to summer and cold is contrary to heat. So whoever wanted to tell the miseries of hell would first need to have seen the paradise of God's delights. O dwelling cursed by God's justice, under the curse of the faithless and the reprobates! Of it Job, God's friend, said: 'There is no order there, but fear forever.' And Isaiah the prophet says against the reprobates: 'Their flame will never be put out, and their worm will never die.' And David our father, weeping, said: 'Lightning will rain on them, with thunderbolts and brimstone and a great storm.' O wretched sinners! How hateful the delicate foods, the costly clothes, the soft beds, and the sweet harmonious singing will seem to them there. How sick the ravenous hunger, the burning flames, the scorching embers, and the cruel torments with bitter weeping will make them!"

And here Jesus gave a sorrowful groan and said: "Truly, it would be better never to have been formed than to suffer such cruel torment. Now set before you a man who suffers torment in every part of his body, and who has no one to show him pity, but everyone mocks him. Tell me, would that not be great pain?" The disciples answered: "The greatest." Then Jesus said: "Now, that is a delight compared with hell. For truly I tell you, if God were to put in a balance all the pains that all men have suffered in this world and will suffer until the day of judgment, and on the other side put a single hour of the pains of hell, the reprobates would without doubt choose the worldly tribulations. For the worldly ones come by the hand of men, but those come by the hand of devils, who are altogether without pity. O, what cruel fire they will give! O, what bitter cold, which yet will not lessen their flames! What gnashing of teeth, what sobs and weeping! The Jordan does not have as much water as the tears that will flow every moment from their eyes. And there their tongues will curse everything created, together with their father and their mother and their creator, who is blessed forever."

Having said this, Jesus washed himself with his disciples, according to God's law written in the book of Moses, and they prayed. And the disciples, seeing him so afflicted, said nothing to him that day, but every one stood frightened at his words.

### Notes — Chapter 60
1. **Hell described by contraries** (folio 62b): one hell set against paradise as winter against summer and cold against heat; to describe the one, the book says, a person would need first to have seen the other. The proof-texts are named in the text itself — Job ("no order there, but everlasting fear"), Isaiah (flame unquenched, worm undying), and David (lightning, thunderbolts, brimstone, and storm) — and are translated where they stand.
2. **The comparison of pains is kept in full** (folios 62b–63b): the delicate foods, costly clothes, soft beds, and sweet singing of this life turning hateful; the hunger, flames, embers, and torments with bitter weeping; the man tormented in every part of his body with no pity and only mockery, called by the disciples "the greatest" pain and by Jesus "a delight" beside hell. The balance image — all worldly pain from the beginning to the judgment weighed against one hour of hell — is the book's own measure and is not softened.
3. **The closing washing marks the end of the discourse for the day** (folio 63b): Jesus washes with the disciples according to the law written in the book of Moses, they pray, and the disciples, seeing him afflicted, say nothing, each frightened at his words. The next teaching — the watchful householder and the merchant who traded with his neighbours' money — begins after the evening prayer in the following chapter and is reserved for the next slice.

---

---

## Chapter 61

*[Editorial heading: The watchful householder, and the man who gave his neighbours money to trade with. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXI") follows the closing washing and evening prayer of chapter 60 (folio 63b). The chapter rubric boundary flagged in the previous slice is confirmed here against the printed "LXII" rubric at folio 64b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Jesus opened his mouth after the evening prayer and said: "What father of a family, if he knew that a thief wanted to break into his house, would sleep? Surely none, for he would keep watch and stand ready to kill the thief. Do you not know, then, that Satan is like a roaring lion going about looking for someone to devour? In the same way he goes looking to make a man sin. Truly I tell you, if a man acted like the merchant he would have no fear on that day, because he would be well prepared.

"There was a man who gave money to his neighbours so that they could trade with it, and the profit would be shared out in fair measure. Some of them traded well, so that they doubled the money. But some spent the money in the service of the enemy of the man who had given it to them, and spoke evil of him. Now tell me: when the neighbour calls the debtors to account, how will the matter go? Surely he will give an honoured reward to those who traded well, but on the others he will pour out his anger in rebukes, and then he will punish them as the law requires.

"As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands: the neighbour is God, who has given to man everything man has, together with life itself, so that by living well in this world God would have the praise and man the glory of paradise. Those who live well double the money by their example, because sinners, seeing such an example, are turned to repentance; so men who live well will be rewarded with a great reward. But the wicked sinners, who in their sinning put everything God has given them, together with their own life, into the service of Satan, the enemy of God — blaspheming God and giving scandal to others — tell me, what will their punishment be?"

"It will be without measure," said the disciples.

### Notes — Chapter 61
1. **The householder and the thief open the chapter** (folio 63b): no householder who knew a thief meant to break in would sleep; he would watch and stand ready to kill the thief. Satan is set beside this as the roaring lion going about seeking someone to devour, looking for a way to make a man sin. The watchfulness called for is readiness on "that day" — the judgment day of chapters 51–60, which this teaching follows directly after the evening washing and prayer.
2. **The merchant-money parable is kept with its own terms** (folios 63b–64a): a man gives money to his neighbours to trade with, the profit to be divided in fair proportion; some double it, some spend it in the service of the giver's enemy and speak evil of him; at the accounting the good traders are rewarded with honour, the others rebuked and punished as the law requires. The application is then stated in the text itself: the neighbour is God; good living doubles the money because sinners who see the example turn to repentance; the wicked put all God gave them, and their own life with it, into Satan's service. The disciples' verdict — punishment "without measure" — closes the chapter and is kept as spoken.
3. **Chapter boundary confirmed.** The previous slice reserved this teaching as chapter 61 on the strength of the narrative break after the chapter 60 washing; the printed rubric "LXII" at folio 64b, opening the shop-and-soul teaching of the next chapter, confirms that division. The boundary is recorded here so the G1 audit does not have to re-open it.

---

## Chapter 62

*[Editorial heading: The soul as a merchant, the body as its shop, and love as its money. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXIIe.") at folio 64b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Jesus said: "Whoever wants to live well should look at the merchant, who locks up his shop and guards it day and night with great care. With everything he buys, he wants to make a profit when he sells it again, because if he sees he will lose on it, he will not sell — not even to his own brother. Now do the same yourselves, for truly your soul is a merchant and the body is the shop. Whatever it receives from outside, through the senses, and whatever it gives out, that is its buying and selling. And the money, in truth, is love. So guard yourselves: do not sell or buy with your love even the smallest thought from which you gain nothing. Instead, let thought, speech, and work all be for the love of God, for in that way you will be safe on that day.

"Truly I tell you, many make their washings and go to prayer; many fast and give alms; many study and preach to others — and their end is hateful before God. Because they wash the body and not the heart; they call with the mouth and not with the heart; they fast from foods and fill themselves with sins; they give to others what is not good for them, so that they will be thought good; they study in order to know how to speak, not in order to do; they preach to others against the very things they themselves do, and so they condemn themselves with their own tongue. As God lives, these people do not know God with their heart — for if they knew him, they would love him; and just as everything a man has, he has received from God, so he would spend everything for the love of God."

### Notes — Chapter 62
1. **The merchant image is carried through in full** (folios 64b–65a): the shop locked and guarded day and night; nothing resold at a loss, not even to a brother; the soul as merchant, the body as shop, the senses as the doorway of trade, and love as the money. The rule drawn from it — not to buy or sell with one's love even the smallest unprofitable thought, and to let thought, speech, and work all run for the love of God — is translated as the text gives it, without turning the image into a general theory of its own.
2. **The catalogue of empty practice is kept entire and at full strength** (folio 65a): washings without the heart washed, prayer with the mouth only, fasting from food while filling up on sins, alms of what is not good for the giver so as to be thought good, study aimed at speaking rather than doing, preaching against one's own practice. The closing charge — that such people condemn themselves with their own tongue and do not know God with the heart, for knowledge would bring love, and love would spend all it has received — is the book's own reasoning and is not softened.

---

## Chapter 63

*[Editorial heading: The Samaritan city refuses Jesus — and Jesus forbids the prayer for fire. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXIII b."). The heading above is editorial.]*

Some days later Jesus passed by a city of the Samaritans. They would not let him enter the city, and they would not sell bread to his disciples. So James and John said: "Master, please — may we pray God to send fire down from heaven on these people?"

Jesus answered: "You do not know by what spirit you are being led, that you speak like this. Remember that God wanted to destroy Nineveh, because not even one person could be found in that city who feared God. The city was so wicked that God called Jonah the prophet to send him into it — and Jonah, for fear of that people, wanted to run away to Tarsus. So God had him thrown into the sea, and a fish took him in and threw him out again near Nineveh. And preaching there, that people turned so fully to repentance that God had mercy on them.

"Woe to those who call down vengeance, for it will come on themselves, since every man has in himself something for God to avenge. Now tell me: did you create this city, and this people with it, you madmen? Surely not. For all creatures joined together could not create one single new fly out of nothing — and that is what creating is. If the blessed God, who created this city and this people, still upholds it now, why do you want to destroy it? Why did you not say instead: 'Please, master, may we pray to the Lord our God that this people be turned to repentance'? Surely this is what belongs to a disciple of mine: to pray to God for those who do him harm. That is what Abel did, when his brother Cain — cursed by God — was killing him. That is what Abraham did for Pharaoh, who had taken his wife away from him: God's angel did not kill Pharaoh, but only struck him with sickness. That is what Zechariah did, when he was killed in the temple by order of the wicked king. That is what Jeremiah, Isaiah, Ezekiel, Daniel, and David did, with all God's friends and his holy prophets.

"Tell me: if one of your brothers fell sick with a madness, would you kill him because he speaks evil and strikes at whoever comes near him? Surely you would not do that. You would try instead to bring him back to health with medicines suited to his sickness."

### Notes — Chapter 63
1. **The Samaritan refusal and the request for fire are kept as written** (folio 65a–b): the city will neither receive Jesus nor sell bread to his disciples, and James and John ask leave to pray fire down from heaven on it. The rebuke that follows — "you do not know by what spirit you are being led" — is the text's own, and no excuse for the request is added.
2. **Jonah and Nineveh carry the argument** (folio 65b): God meant to destroy Nineveh because no God-fearing person could be found in it; Jonah fled toward "tarsso," rendered "Tarsus" as the 1907 check reads it — not "Tarshish," the destination of the Jonah of the canonical telling; the Italian spelling is kept as written, not harmonised. Jonah was thrown into the sea, taken in by the fish, and cast out near Nineveh; his preaching turned the city to repentance and God had mercy. The woe on those who call down vengeance, and the reason given — every man carries in himself something for God to avenge — are translated at full strength.
3. **The roll of examples closes the chapter** (folio 66a): Abel under Cain's hand, Abraham praying for Pharaoh who took his wife (the angel strikes Pharaoh with sickness, not death), Zechariah killed in the temple, Jeremiah, Isaiah, Ezekiel, Daniel, and David, with all God's friends and prophets, praying for those who harmed them. The closing comparison — a brother sick with madness is treated with fitting medicines, not killed for his raving — sets up the teaching on the sinner's sick mind that opens the next chapter.

---

## Chapter 64

*[Editorial heading: The sinner's mind is sick; your true enemy is your own body — and everyone who flatters you. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXIVc."). The heading above is editorial.]*

"As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands: a sinner has a sick mind when he persecutes another man. Tell me, would anyone break his own head just to tear his enemy's cloak? Now, how can the mind be sound in a man who cuts himself off from God, the head of his soul, in order to hurt the body of his enemy?

"Tell me, O man, who is your enemy? Surely it is your own body, and everyone who praises you. So if your mind were sound, you would kiss the hand of those who insult you, and you would give presents to those who persecute you and strike you again and again. For, O man, the more you are persecuted and insulted in this life for your sins, the less you will have of it on the day of judgment.

"But tell me: if God's holy prophets were persecuted and slandered by the world even though they were innocent, what will become of you, O sinner? And if they bore everything with patience, praying for their persecutors, what should you do, O man, who deserve hell?

"Tell me, my disciples, do you not know that Shimei cursed God's servant David the prophet and threw stones after him? Now, what did David say to those who wanted to kill Shimei? 'What is it to you, Joab, that you want to kill Shimei? Let him curse me, because God wills it so — and he will turn this curse into a blessing.' And so it happened: God saw David's patience and freed him from the persecution of his own son Absalom. Surely not a leaf on a tree stirs without God's will. So when you are in trouble, do not fix your thoughts on how much you are suffering, or on the one who is troubling you. Think instead about how much you deserve to receive for your sins, at the hands of the devils of hell.

"You are angry with this city because it would not receive us or sell us bread. Tell me: are these people your slaves? Did you give them this city? Did you give them their grain, or did you help them gather it in? Surely not — you have never even been in these parts, and you are poor men. So why did you speak like that?"

The two disciples answered: "Lord, we have sinned. May God have mercy on us."

And Jesus answered: "So be it."

### Notes — Chapter 64
1. **The "sick mind" teaching is translated as written** (folios 66b–67a): the sinner who persecutes is of unsound intellect; no one breaks his own head to tear an enemy's cloak; a man who cuts himself off from God, the head of his soul, to injure his enemy's body is in the same case. The true enemy is then named in the text itself — "your own body, and everyone who praises you" — and the counsel follows at full strength: kiss the hand of revilers, give gifts to persecutors, because suffering borne here for one's sins lessens what is faced on the day of judgment.
2. **Shimei and David are kept with their names and words** (folio 67a): Shimei cursing David and throwing stones; David refusing Joab's wish to kill him — let him curse, since God wills it, and God will turn the curse into a blessing; David's patience seen by God and his deliverance from Absalom's persecution. The leaf that does not stir without God's will, and the direction of thought in trouble — not to the suffering or the afflicter, but to what one's sins deserve at the hands of hell's devils — are the book's own sentences, unsoftened.
3. **The chapter closes on the two disciples' confession** (folio 67a): Jesus presses the question home on their anger at the Samaritan city — not their slaves, not their city, not their grain, never even visited, and poor men besides — and James and John answer, "Lord, we have sinned; may God have mercy on us," to which Jesus says only, "So be it." The short close is kept short.

---

## Chapter 65

*[Editorial heading: The pool called Probatica — the sick man of thirty-eight years healed on the Sabbath. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXV*."). The heading above is editorial.]*

The Passover was drawing near, so Jesus went up to Jerusalem with his disciples. He went to the pool called Probatica. The pool had that name because God's angel stirred that water every day, and the first sick person who went into the water after the stirring was healed of every kind of sickness. So a great number of sick people stayed by the pool, which had five porches.

There Jesus saw a sick man who had been there thirty-eight years, ill with a heavy sickness. Jesus knew this by divine inspiration, and he had pity on the sick man and said to him: "Do you want to be made well?"

The sick man answered: "Sir, I have no one to put me into the water when the angel stirs it. When I try to go in, someone quicker than me goes down ahead of me and gets in."

Then Jesus lifted his eyes to heaven and said: "Lord our God, God of our fathers, have pity on this sick man." And having said that, Jesus said: "In God's name, brother, receive your health. Get up and carry your bed away."

At once the sick man got up, praising God, and he put his bed on his shoulders and went home praising God. Those who saw him cried out: "It is the Sabbath today — you are not allowed to carry your bed."

He answered them: "The one who made me well said to me: 'Pick up your bed and go home.'"

They said to him: "Who is he?" He answered: "I do not know his name."

Then they said among themselves: "It must have been Jesus of Nazareth." Others said: "No — he is a holy man of God. So whoever did this thing is a wicked man, because he makes people break the Sabbath."

And Jesus went into the temple, and a great crowd gathered around him to hear his words, and the priests were eaten up with envy.

### Notes — Chapter 65
1. **The pool and its rule are translated as the text states them** (folio 67b): the pool called Probatica, its five porches, the angel stirring the water each day, and the first sick person to enter after the stirring healed of every kind of sickness. No harmonising detail from other accounts is imported; the crowd of sick people waiting by the water is the book's own scene-setting for the healing that follows.
2. **Thirty-eight years is kept as written** (folios 67b–68a): the man has been at the pool thirty-eight years with a heavy sickness; Jesus knows it by divine inspiration; the exchange — "Do you want to be made well?" / "I have no one to put me in" — and the healing word, "In God's name, brother, receive your health; get up and carry your bed away," are translated in place, with the man's praise of God on the road home kept.
3. **The Sabbath dispute and the divided verdict close the chapter** (folio 68a–b): the bed-carrying is challenged as unlawful on the Sabbath; the healed man repeats the words spoken to him and cannot name their speaker; opinion divides — some say it must have been Jesus of Nazareth, others answer that he is a holy man of God and the doer of this must be wicked for making the Sabbath be broken. Jesus then enters the temple, the crowd gathers to hear him, and the priests are consumed with envy. The next chapter opens on a priest's question — "Good master" — and is reserved for the next slice.

---

---

## Chapter 66

*[Editorial heading: A priest asks about the reward in paradise — "Only God is good," and the perils of speech. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXVIc.") at folio 68b, following the Probatica healing and the priests' envy that closed chapter 65. The heading above is editorial.]*

One of them came to him, saying: "Good master, you teach well and truly — so tell me, in paradise what reward will God give us?"

Jesus answered: "You call me good, and you don't know that only God is good. As Job, the friend of God, says: 'A child of a day old is not clean.' Indeed, he said the angels are reprehensible before the presence of God." He said too: "The flesh draws sin, and gathers up iniquities as a sponge gathers water."

So the priest, confounded, fell silent. But Jesus said: "Truly I tell you, nothing is more perilous than speech. For Solomon said: 'Life and death are in the hand of the tongue.'"

And turning to his disciples, Jesus said: "Beware of those who bless you, because they deceive you. With the tongue Satan blessed our first parents, but miserable was the outcome of his words. So did the sages of Egypt bless Pharaoh. So did Goliath bless the Philistines. So did four hundred false prophets bless Ahab — but false were their praises, so that the praised one perished with those who praised him. So not without cause did God say by Isaiah the prophet: 'My people, those who bless you deceive you.'

"Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees — woe to you, priests and Levites, because you have corrupted the sacrifice of the Lord, so that those who come to sacrifice believe that God eats cooked flesh like a man."

### Notes — Chapter 66
1. **The priest's question is kept as spoken** (folio 68b): "Good master, you teach well and truly — tell me, in paradise what reward will God give us?" The answer — "You call me good, and you don't know that only God is good" — is translated as written. The Job saying ("a child of a day old is not clean; indeed, the angels are reprehensible before God's presence") follows the Italian; the 1907 editors' rendering of the scribe's Arabic gloss in the margin reads the flesh-and-sponge sentence differently — noted here as their check-only note, not imported into the text.
2. **The flesh-and-sponge sentence is the Italian's own** (folios 68b–69a): the flesh draws sin and gathers iniquity as a sponge gathers water. It is kept plain, with no imported explanation.
3. **The perils-of-speech teaching is kept entire** (folio 69a): Solomon's "life and death are in the hand of the tongue," the counsel to beware of those who bless you, and the four examples — Satan blessing the first parents, Egypt's sages blessing Pharaoh, Goliath blessing the Philistines, four hundred false prophets blessing Ahab, whose praises ended with the praised perishing alongside the praisers. The Isaiah saying ("My people, those who bless you deceive you") and the woe on the corrupted sacrifice — worshippers made to think God eats cooked flesh like a man — are the text's own charges, unsoftened.

---

## Chapter 67

*[Editorial heading: What the priests do not tell the people about sacrifice — and the new covenant promised. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXVIId.") at folio 69a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"For you say to them: 'Bring of your rams and bulls and lambs to the temple of your God, and don't eat everything yourselves, but give a share to your God of what he has given you.' And you don't tell them the origin of the sacrifice: that it is to testify of the life granted to the son of our father Abraham, so that the faith and obedience of our father Abraham, with the promises made to him by God and the blessing given to him, should never be forgotten.

"But by Ezekiel the prophet God says: 'Take away these your sacrifices, for your victims are abomination to me.' For the time draws near when that will be done of which our God spoke by Hosea the prophet, saying: 'I will call chosen the people not chosen.' And as he says in Ezekiel the prophet: 'God will make a new covenant with his people, not according to the covenant which I gave to your fathers, which they did not observe; and he will take from them the heart of stone, giving them a new heart.' And all this will be because now you don't walk in his law. So you have the key and don't open; rather you block the road for those who would walk in it."

The priest was leaving to go where the high priest stood near the sanctuary, to report everything to him, when Jesus said: "Stay, for I will answer your question."

### Notes — Chapter 67
1. **The sacrifice-origin teaching is kept exactly as written** (folios 69a–69b): the priests are charged with hiding from the people why sacrifices exist — to testify of the life granted to the son of Abraham, keeping Abraham's faith, obedience, promises, and blessing in memory. No naming of which son is added; the Italian does not name one here.
2. **The two covenant quotations are kept in place** (folio 69b): Ezekiel's rejection of the sacrifices ("your victims are abomination to me"), Hosea's "I will call chosen the people not chosen," and Ezekiel's new covenant — not according to the broken fathers' covenant, the heart of stone exchanged for a new heart — all assigned in the text to the priests' failure to walk in the law. The "key" image — holding the key, not opening, and blocking the road for those who would walk — is the text's own.
3. **The bridge to the answer is narrative, not a chapter break.** The priest's departure toward the high priest by the sanctuary, and Jesus calling him back — "Stay, for I will answer your question" — carries the "what reward in paradise" question from chapter 66 into the next chapter's parables. The rubric "LXVII" stands where the printed edition puts it; the boundary is recorded so G1 need not re-open it.

---

## Chapter 68

*[Editorial heading: Those who think of the wages do not love the master — the parable of the ungrateful man. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXVIII") at folio 70a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"You ask me to tell you what God will give us in paradise. Truly I tell you that those who think of the wages do not love the master. A shepherd who has a flock of sheep, when he sees the wolf coming, puts himself to defend the sheep. But not so the servant: when he sees the wolf, he leaves the sheep and flees.

"As God lives, in whose presence I stand: if the God of our fathers were your God, you would not think of saying, 'What will God give me?' But you would say, as David his prophet did: 'What shall I give to God for all that he has given me?'

"I will speak to you by parable, so that you may understand. There was a king who found on the road a man stripped by thieves, mortally wounded, and he had compassion on him. So he commanded his servants to carry the man to the city and tend him — which they did with all diligence. And the king conceived great love for the sick man, so much that he gave him his own daughter as wife and made him his heir. Certainly the king was most merciful — but the man beat the servants, despised the medicines, insulted the bride, spoke evil of the king, and made his subjects rebel against him. And when the king wanted a service, he would say: 'What will the king give me as reward?' Hearing this, what did the king do to such an impious man?"

They all answered: "Woe to him, for the king deprived him of everything and punished him cruelly."

Then Jesus said: "O priests, O scribes and Pharisees, and you high priest who hear my voice: I announce to you what God said to you by his prophet Isaiah: 'I have nourished slaves and exalted them, but they have despised me.'

"He is our God, that king, who found Israel in this world full of miseries. So he gave him to his servants — Joseph, Moses, and Aaron — to tend him. Such love did our God conceive for him that for the sake of the people of Israel he smote Egypt, drowned Pharaoh, and scattered a hundred and twenty kings of the Canaanites and Midianites, and gave him his law, making him heir of all that our people inhabits.

"But how does Israel conduct himself? How many prophets has he killed? How many prophecies has he corrupted? How has he violated the law of God? How many, for this cause, have departed from God and gone to serve idols, through your scandal, O priests! And how you dishonour God with your manner of life! And now you ask me what God will give you in paradise. You should have asked me what punishment God will give you in hell, and what you must do for true penitence, so that God may have mercy on you. This I can tell you — and for this I am sent to you."

### Notes — Chapter 68
1. **The answer to the paradise-reward question is kept as the text argues it** (folio 70a): wage-thinking proves lovelessness; the shepherd defends his sheep while the hireling flees the wolf; the God of the fathers would draw out David's question — "what shall I give to God?" — not "what will God give me?"
2. **The king parable is kept entire with its details** (folio 70a): the stripped, mortally wounded man found on the road; the servants' diligent tending; the king's daughter given in marriage and the heirship; then the man's ingratitude item by item — beating the servants, despising the medicines, insulting the bride, speaking evil of the king, stirring rebellion, and still asking what reward the king will give. The hearers' own verdict — deprived of everything, cruelly punished — is the text's hinge into the application.
3. **The application to Israel is kept at full strength** (folios 70a–70b): the Isaiah charge ("I have nourished slaves and exalted them, but they have despised me"); Joseph, Moses, and Aaron as the tending servants; Egypt smitten, Pharaoh drowned, and a hundred and twenty Canaanite and Midianite kings scattered — the number kept as the Italian gives it, without harmonising. The counter-questions — prophets killed, prophecies corrupted, the law violated, idol-worship through the priests' scandal — and the closing re-direction of the question itself ("what punishment in hell," "what you must do for true penitence," "for this I am sent to you") are translated in place.

---

## Chapter 69

*[Editorial heading: You want the honours without the burdens — and the dumb, deaf, and blind demoniac. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXIX «.") at folio 71a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"As God lives, in whose presence I stand: from me you will not receive flattery, but truth. So I say to you: repent and return to God, as our fathers did after sinning, and do not harden your heart."

The priests were consumed with rage at this speech, but for fear of the people they said nothing.

And Jesus went on, saying: "O doctors, O scribes, O Pharisees, O priests — tell me. You want horses like knights, but you don't want to go to war. You want fine clothes like women, but you don't want to spin and nurse children. You want the fruits of the fields, but you don't want to cultivate the earth. You want the fish of the sea, but you don't want to go fishing. You want honour like citizens, but you don't want the burden of the republic. And you want tithes and firstfruits like priests, but you don't want to serve God in truth. What then will God do with you, who here want every good without any evil? Truly I tell you that God will give you a place where you will have every evil without any good."

And when Jesus had said this, a demoniac was brought to him who could neither speak nor see, and was deprived of hearing. So Jesus, seeing their faith, raised his eyes to heaven and said: "Lord God of our fathers, have mercy on this sick man and give him health, so that this people may know that you have sent me." And having said this, Jesus commanded the spirit to depart, saying: "In the power of the name of God our Lord, depart, malignant one, from the man." The spirit departed, and the mute spoke, seeing with his eyes.

So everyone was filled with fear — but the scribes said: "In the power of Beelzebub, prince of the demons, he casts out the demons."

Then Jesus said: "Every kingdom divided against itself is destroyed, and house falls upon house. If in the power of Satan Satan is cast out, how will his kingdom stand? And if your sons cast out Satan with the scripture that Solomon the prophet gave them, they testify that I cast out Satan in the power of God. As God lives: blasphemy against the Holy Spirit is unpardonable, in this world and in the other — because willingly the malignant reprobates himself, knowing the reprobation."

And having said this, Jesus went out of the temple. And the people magnified him; they brought all the sick they could gather, and Jesus, having made prayer, gave health to all. So on that day in Jerusalem the Roman soldiery, by the working of Satan, began to stir up the people, saying that Jesus was the God of Israel, who had come to visit his people.

### Notes — Chapter 69
1. **The "no flattery, but truth" opening and the honours-without-burdens catalogue are kept entire** (folio 71a): repentance as our fathers did, the priests' rage held back by fear of the people, and the sixfold pattern — horses without war, fine clothes without spinning and child-nursing, fruits without cultivating, fish without fishing, civic honour without the republic's burden, tithes and firstfruits without serving God in truth. The closing reversal — every good without any evil now, every evil without any good then — is the text's own sentence.
2. **The demoniac healing is kept with its terms** (folios 71a–71b): neither speech nor sight, and hearing gone; "seeing their faith" — the faith of those who brought him; the prayer that the people may know God sent him; and the command "In the power of the name of God our Lord, depart, malignant one, from the man." The mute speaks and sees with his eyes.
3. **The Beelzebub charge and answer are kept as argued** (folios 71b–72a): the scribes' charge that he casts out by Beelzebub, the divided-kingdom answer, and the point about "your sons" casting out with the scripture Solomon the prophet gave them — kept as written, with no explanatory gloss added. The blasphemy-against-the-Holy-Spirit saying is translated exactly: unpardonable in this world and the other, because the malignant willingly reprobates himself knowing the reprobation.
4. **The closing Roman-soldiery passage is kept as the text explains it** (folio 72a): the people's magnification and the healings stand beside the soldiery's rumor — "that Jesus was the God of Israel, who had come to visit his people" — which the text attributes to Satan's working. Nothing is softened; the book's own explanation of where that saying came from is translated in place.

---

## Chapter 70

*[Editorial heading: At Caesarea Philippi — Peter's "Christ, son of God" is rebuked. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXX».") at folio 72b. The heading above is editorial. **Seam zone:** the denial here belongs with chapters 42 and 96–97; it is translated exactly as written and footnoted, never smoothed.]*

Jesus departed from Jerusalem after the Passover, and entered the borders of Caesarea Philippi. And the angel Gabriel having told him of the sedition that was beginning among the people, he asked his disciples, saying: "What do men say of me?"

They said: "Some say that you are Elijah, others say Jeremiah, and others say one of the ancient prophets."

Jesus answered: "And you — what do you say that I am?"

Peter answered: "You are the Christ, son of God."

Then Jesus grew angry, and with anger rebuked him, saying: "Go and depart from me, for you are the devil, and you seek to make me stumble." And he threatened the eleven, saying: "Woe to you if you believe this, for I have obtained from God a great curse against those who will believe this."

And he was about to cast Peter out; so the eleven interceded for him with Jesus, who did not cast him out but rebuked him again, saying: "See that never again you say such words, for God would reprobate you."

Peter wept and said: "Lord, I have spoken foolishly; pray God that he forgive me."

Then Jesus said: "If our God would not show himself to Moses his servant, nor to Elijah whom he so loved, nor to any prophet — will you think that God should show himself to this unbelieving generation? But don't you know that God created all things from nothing with a single word, and all men had their origin from a piece of clay? Now how shall God have likeness with man? Woe to those who let themselves be deceived by Satan!"

And having said this, Jesus prayed to God for Peter — the eleven and Peter weeping and saying: "So be it, so be it, O Lord our God, blessed." After that Jesus departed and went into Galilee, so that that vain opinion which the people had begun to hold concerning him might be extinguished.

### Notes — Chapter 70
1. **The Caesarea Philippi exchange is kept entire** (folios 72b–73a): the departure after Passover, Gabriel's warning of the sedition, the disciples' three answers (Elijah, Jeremiah, one of the ancient prophets), and Peter's confession — "You are the Christ, son of God" — which the text answers not with blessing but with rebuke.
2. **The rebuke is seam material and is translated exactly as written** (folio 72b): "Go and depart from me, for you are the devil, and you seek to make me stumble." The threat to the eleven — "Woe to you if you believe this, for I have obtained from God a great curse against those who will believe this" — stands as the Italian gives it. The 1907 editors' rendering of the scribe's Arabic gloss at this point ("the chapter of the curse upon the Christians") is their check-only note on the margin, not imported into the text. Nothing is smoothed, softened, or deleted; this denial sits in the same seam as chapter 42 ("I am not the Messiah") and the named zones at 96–97, and Robert's standing policy (translate-and-footnote) is followed.
3. **Peter's restoration is kept as written** (folios 72b–73a): the near-expulsion, the eleven's intercession, the repeated rebuke ("See that never again you say such words, for God would reprobate you"), Peter's weeping confession of foolish speech, and Jesus praying for him while all weep, "So be it, so be it, O Lord our God, blessed."
4. **The God-not-seen argument and the withdrawal close the chapter** (folio 73a): if God would not show himself to Moses, to beloved Elijah, or to any prophet, why think he would show himself to this unbelieving generation; all things made from nothing with a single word, all men from a piece of clay — "how shall God have likeness with man?" The departure into Galilee, aimed at extinguishing the people's "vain opinion," is kept as the text's own reason for the withdrawal. The next chapter opens after the Passover in Nazareth and is reserved for the next slice.

---

---

## Chapter 71

*[Editorial heading: The paralytic let down through the roof — "I cannot forgive sins." Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXI") at the opening of the Italian column. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus arrived in his own country, and it spread through all the region of Galilee that Jesus the prophet had come to Nazareth. So they diligently sought out the sick and brought them to him, begging him to touch them with his hands. And so great was the crowd that a certain rich man sick with paralysis, not being able to be carried in through the door, had himself carried up onto the roof of the house where Jesus was staying; and when the roof was uncovered, he had himself let down on sheets in front of Jesus. Jesus stood a moment in hesitation, then said: "Do not fear, brother, for your sins are forgiven you."

Everyone was scandalized hearing this, and they said: "And who is this who forgives sins?"

Then Jesus said: "As God lives, I cannot forgive sins, nor can any man — God alone forgives. But as God's servant I can pray for the sins of others. So I have prayed for this sick man, and I am sure that God has heard my prayer. So that you may know the truth, I say to this sick man: In the name of the God of our fathers, the God of Abraham and his sons — rise up healed!" And when Jesus had said this, the sick man rose up healed and glorified God.

Then the common people begged Jesus to pray to God for the sick who stood outside. So Jesus went out to them and, lifting his hands, said: "Lord God of hosts, the living God, the true God, the holy God, who will never die — have mercy on these!" And everyone answered: "Amen." And when he had said this, Jesus laid his hands on the sick, and they all received their health. So they magnified God, saying: "God has visited us by his prophet, and a great prophet has God sent to us."

### Notes — Chapter 71
1. **The forgiveness denial is kept exactly as written** (folio 73b): "As God lives, I cannot forgive sins, nor can any man — God alone forgives. But as God's servant I can pray for the sins of others." Nothing is added and nothing softened; the text's own distinction — cannot forgive, can intercede — stands as the Italian gives it.
2. **The healing formula is kept in place**: "In the name of the God of our fathers, the God of Abraham and his sons — rise up healed!" The people's verdict — "God has visited us by his prophet, and a great prophet has God sent to us" — is the text's own Christology, unharmonised.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of 'he forgiveth.'" The superscripts in the Italian column mark the editors' notes: one gives an alternate rendering of the "I cannot forgive sins" line ("I swear by the living God that I cannot forgive any sin; only God can forgive sins"); one marks "by permission of God"; and one renders the scribe's Arabic marginal gloss on the divine names — "God is sovereign, living, the Truth, a friend and persisting." These are recorded as the editors' check-only notes, not imported into the translation. The editors cite Mark 2:1–12 as the parallel.

---

## Chapter 72

*[Editorial heading: The messenger who is to come — the white cloud. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXII") at folio 74a. The heading above is editorial.]*

That night Jesus spoke in secret with his disciples, saying: "Truly I tell you that Satan wanted to sift you like wheat. But I have prayed to God for you — yet none of you will perish except the one who lays snares for me." And Jesus said this of Judas, because the angel Gabriel told him how Judas was in league with the priests and reported to them everything Jesus said.

With tears, he who writes this drew near to Jesus, saying: "O master, tell me, who is it that will betray you?"

Jesus answered, saying: "O Barnabas, this is not the hour for you to know him; but soon the wicked one will reveal himself, because I shall depart from the world."

Then the apostles wept, saying: "O master, why do you want to leave us? It is much better that we die than be abandoned by you!"

Jesus answered: "Let not your heart be troubled, and do not be afraid — for I did not create you, but God our creator who created you will keep you. As for me, I am now come into the world to prepare the way for the messenger of God, who shall bring salvation to the world. But beware that you are not deceived, for many false prophets will come who will take my words and contaminate my gospel."

Then Andrew said: "O master, tell us some sign, that we may know him."

Jesus answered: "He will not come in your time, but will come some years after you, when my gospel shall be annulled, so that there will be scarcely thirty faithful at that time. God will have mercy on the world, and so he will send his messenger, over whose head shall rest a white cloud, so that he shall be known by one elect of God, and shall be manifested to the world by him. He shall come with great power against the ungodly, and shall destroy idolatry upon the earth. And I rejoice, because through him our God shall be known and glorified, and I shall be known to be true; and he will execute vengeance against those who shall say that I am more than man. Truly I tell you that the moon shall minister sleep to him in his boyhood, and when he is grown up he shall take her in his hands. Let the world beware of casting him out, because he shall slay the idolaters — for many more were slain by Moses the servant of God and Joshua, who spared not the cities which they burned, and slew the children; for to an old wound one applies fire.

"He shall come with truth clearer than that of all the prophets, and shall reprove him who uses the world amiss. The towers of the city of our father shall greet one another for joy. And so when idolatry shall be seen to fall to the ground and confess me a man like other men, truly I tell you the messenger of God shall be come."

### Notes — Chapter 72
1. **Seam material — translated exactly as written, never smoothed.** The Italian names no one in this chapter: the coming figure is "the messenger of God" (*il nontio di DIO* — the printed Italian reads DIOC at the first occurrence, a spelling kept on record; the editors' marginal note glosses it "the prophet of God"). The white cloud over his head, the recognition by one elect of God, the destruction of idolatry, the vengeance against those who say Jesus is "more than man," and the closing test — idolatry fallen and confessing Jesus "a man like other men" as the sign that "the messenger of God shall be come" — are all the text's own, rendered without softening. The name Muhammad is given at chapter 97; it is not imported here.
2. **Details kept as written**: "scarcely thirty faithful" at the time of the sending; the moon ministering sleep to him in boyhood and taken in his hands when grown (the editors cite the Qur'an, chapter 54, opening, as the parallel); Moses and Joshua slaying more idolaters, "for to an old wound one applies fire"; "truth clearer than that of all the prophets"; the towers of the city of our father greeting one another for joy. None of these is harmonised or explained away.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the sign of the prophet of God." Their marginal notes gloss "God creates and preserves" and "God sends." The editors cite Luke 22:31 (the sifting), John 14:27 ("let not your heart be troubled"), and Matthew 24:11 (false prophets) as parallels. Barnabas is named in the text as "he who writes this" — kept as the text presents him, with the audit's §0 verdict (no verifiable line to the historical Barnabas) standing outside the translation.

---

## Chapter 73

*[Editorial heading: How the ancient tempter lays wait for man — four ways, and Satan as a chained dog. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXIII"). The heading above is editorial.]*

"Truly I tell you that if Satan tries whether you are friends of God — because no one attacks his own cities — if Satan had his will over you, he would let you glide along at your pleasure.
