But because he knows you are his enemies, he will do everything to make you perish. But do not fear, for he will be against you like a chained dog, because God has heard my prayer."

John answered: "O master, not only for us, but for those who shall believe the gospel, tell us how the ancient tempter lays wait for man."

Jesus answered: "That wicked one tempts in four ways. The first is when he tempts by himself, with thoughts. The second is when he tempts with words and deeds through his servants. The third is when he tempts with false doctrine. The fourth is when he tempts with false visions. Now how cautious ought man to be — and all the more since he has on his side man's flesh, which loves sin as one who has a fever loves water. Truly I tell you that if a man fears God he shall have victory over all, as David his prophet says: 'God shall give his angels charge over you, who shall keep your ways, so that the devil shall not make you stumble; for a thousand shall fall at your left hand and ten thousand at your right hand, so that they shall not come near you.'

"Furthermore, our God with great love promised us by the same David to keep us, saying: 'I will give you understanding, which shall teach you; and in your ways wherein you shall walk I will make my eye rest upon you.'

"But what shall I say? He has said by Isaiah: 'Can a mother forget the child of her womb? But I tell you, when she forgets, I will not forget you.'

"Tell me, then, who shall fear Satan, having the angels for guard and the living God for protection? Nevertheless it is necessary, as the prophet Solomon says: 'You, my son, who art come to serve God, prepare your soul for temptations.' Truly I tell you that a man ought to do as the banker who examines money, examining his thoughts, so that he does not sin against God his creator."

### Notes — Chapter 73
1. **The chained-dog image and the four ways are kept entire** (folios 75b–76a): Satan "like a chained dog" against the faithful; the ancient tempter's four ways — by himself with thoughts, with words and deeds through his servants, with false doctrine, with false visions; the flesh loving sin "as one who has a fever loves water." The banker image — examining thoughts as the banker examines money — is introduced here and developed in chapters 74–75.
2. **The scripture chain is kept in the text's order**: David's psalm of the angels' charge (a thousand at the left hand, ten thousand at the right), the "I will give thee understanding" promise, Isaiah's "can a mother forget the child of her womb," and Solomon's "prepare thy soul for temptations." The editors cite Psalm 91:11–12 and 7, Psalm 32:8, Isaiah 49:15, and Ecclesiasticus 2:1 as parallels.
3. **Apparatus as printed, with one placement left open for audit**: the editors' chapter-title note at this point reads "The Chapter of appointing a vicegerent" — which does not fit a temptation teaching, and is recorded here exactly as printed with its placement flagged for G1/G2 review (it may be misassigned in the printing, or belong to another chapter; the Vienna copy's title-frames are blank for these chapters). The superscripts in the Italian column mark the editors' renderings of the scribe's Arabic marginal glosses (e.g., on the angels' charge, the thousand and ten thousand, "God loves," "God promised," "by the living God," "God creates") — recorded as check-only notes, not imported into the text.

---

## Chapter 74

*[Editorial heading: Thought itself can be sin — the banker and the coin. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXIV") at folio 76b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"There have been and are in the world men who do not count thought as sin; they are in the greatest error. Tell me, how did Satan sin? It is certain that he sinned by thinking himself more worthy than man. Solomon sinned by thinking to invite to a feast all the creatures of God — whereupon a fish corrected him by eating all that he had prepared. So not without cause does David our father say that to ascend in one's heart dispose one in the valley of tears. And why does God cry by Isaiah his prophet, saying: 'Take away your evil thoughts from my eyes'? And to what purpose does Solomon say: 'With all keeping, keep your heart'? As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands: all this is said against the evil thoughts with which sin is committed — for without thinking it is not possible to sin. Now tell me: when the husbandman plants the vineyard, does he set the plants deep? Assuredly yes. Even so does Satan, who in planting sin does not stop at the eye or the ear, but passes into the heart, which is God's dwelling — as he spoke by Moses his servant, saying: 'I will dwell in them, that they may walk in my law.'

"Now tell me: if King Herod gave you a house to keep, in which he desired to dwell, would you let Pilate his enemy enter there, or place his goods in it? Assuredly no. Then how much less ought you to let Satan enter your heart, or place his thoughts there — seeing that our God has given you your heart to keep, which is his dwelling! Observe, then, that the banker considers the coin: whether the image of Caesar is right, whether the silver is good or false, and whether it is of due weight — so he turns it over much in his hand. Ah, mad world! How prudent you are in your business — so that in the last day you will reprove and judge the servants of God for negligence and carelessness, for without doubt your servants are more prudent than the servants of God. Now tell me: who is it that examines a thought as the banker examines a silver coin? Assuredly no one."

### Notes — Chapter 74
1. **The sins-of-thought teaching is kept without softening** (folios 76b–77a): Satan's sin was a thought — counting himself more worthy than man; Solomon's sin was a thought — the feast for all creatures, corrected by a fish eating all he had prepared. The David, Isaiah, and Solomon sayings on the heart and evil thoughts are the text's own chain, leading to the husbandman image — Satan planting sin deep, past eye and ear, into the heart, "which is God's dwelling."
2. **The Herod-and-Pilate analogy and the banker are kept as written**: the house Herod would inhabit, barred to Pilate his enemy — how much less should Satan enter the heart God gave into our keeping; the banker turning the coin in his hand, checking Caesar's image, the silver's goodness, and its weight. The closing rebuke — "Ah, mad world! ... your servants are more prudent than the servants of God" — and the unanswered question — "who examines a thought as the banker examines a silver coin? Assuredly no one" — stand as the Italian gives them.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the thought." The printed Italian carries a line of the scribe's Arabic gloss inside the David quotation (rendered in the djvu text as untranslated script); the English column gives the sentence whole — "to ascend in one's heart disposeth one in the valley of tears" — and the translation follows the sentence, not the gloss. The editors cite Psalm 84:5–6, Isaiah 1:16, Proverbs 4:23, Leviticus 26:11–12, and Luke 16:8 as parallels.

---

## Chapter 75

*[Editorial heading: How to examine a thought — and the parable of the bad payer. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXV") at folio 77b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then James said: "O master, what is the examination of a thought like the examination of a coin?"

Jesus answered: "The good silver in the thought is piety, for every impious thought comes from the devil. The right image is the example of the saints and prophets, whom we must imitate. And the weight of the thought is the love of God, for which all must be done. Then the enemy will bring you impious thoughts against your neighbor — world-conformed, to corrupt the flesh, and of earthly love, to corrupt the love of God."

Bartholomew answered: "O master, what should we do to think little, so that we do not fall into sin?"

Jesus answered: "Two things are necessary for you. The first is to exercise yourselves much, and the second is to talk little — for idleness is a sink where every unclean thought gathers, and too much talking is a sponge that picks up iniquities. So it is necessary that your working should not only keep the body occupied, but the soul too should be occupied with prayer — for one must never cease from prayer.

"I tell you by parable: there was a man who paid ill, so that no one who knew him would go to work his fields. So he, like the wicked man he was, said: 'I will go to the marketplace to find idle ones who do nothing, and they will come to work my vines.' This man went out of his house and found many strangers standing idle who had no money. He spoke to them and led them to his vineyard — but truly, no one who knew him and had work for his hands went there. He is Satan, that one who pays ill — for he gives labour, and man receives for it the eternal fires in his service. So he has gone forth from paradise and goes seeking laborers. Assuredly he sets to his labors those who stand in idleness, whoever they are — but much more those who do not know him. It is not enough in any way to know evil in order to escape it; one must work good in order to overcome it."

### Notes — Chapter 75
1. **The coin-examination answer is kept as the text gives it** (folios 77b–78a): good silver = piety (every impious thought comes from the devil); right image = the example of the saints and prophets, to be imitated; weight = the love of God, for which all must be done. The enemy's counterfeits — impious thoughts against the neighbor, world-conformed to corrupt the flesh, earthly love to corrupt the love of God — follow in the text's order.
2. **The two remedies and the bad-payer parable are kept entire**: exercise much and talk little; idleness "a sink where every unclean thought gathers"; too much talking "a sponge that picks up iniquities"; body occupied with work and soul with prayer, "for one must never cease from prayer." The bad payer — shunned by all who know him, recruiting idle strangers from the marketplace with no money — is named by the text itself: "He is Satan, that one who pays ill." The wage he pays — labour now, "the eternal fires" in his service — and the closing maxim — knowing evil is not enough to escape it; one must work good to overcome it — are translated in place.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the lazy one." The editors cite Matthew 20:3 (the marketplace idlers) as the parallel. The next rubric, "LXXVI," opens the following chapter (the three vinedressers); the boundary is recorded so G1 need not re-open it.

---

---

## Chapter 76

*[Editorial heading: The three vinedressers — knowledge that only talks. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXVI") at folio 78b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"I tell you for an example: there was a man who had three vineyards, which he let out to three husbandmen. One of them, for not knowing how to cultivate the vineyard, the vineyard brought forth only leaves. The second taught the third how the vines ought to be cultivated, who most excellently hearkened to his words; and he cultivated his as he told him, insomuch that the vineyard of the third bore much. But the second left his vineyard uncultivated, spending his time solely in talking. When the time was come for paying the rent to the lord of the vineyard, the first said: 'Lord, I know not how your vineyard ought to be cultivated; I therefore have not received any fruit this year.'

"The lord answered: 'O fool, do you dwell alone in the world, that you have not asked counsel of my second vinedresser, who knows well how to cultivate the land? Certain it is that you shall pay me.' And having said this he condemned him to work in prison until he should pay his lord; who moved with pity at his simplicity liberated him, saying: 'Begone, for I will not that you work longer at my vineyard; it is enough for you that I forgive your debt.'

"The second came, to whom the lord said: 'Welcome, my vinedresser! Where are the fruits that you owe me? Assuredly, since you know well how to prune the vines, the vineyard that I let out to you must needs have borne much fruit.'

"The second answered: 'O lord, your vineyard is still standing — because I have not pruned the wood nor worked up the soil; but the vineyard has not borne fruit, so I cannot pay you.'

"Whereupon the lord called the third and with wonder said: 'You saidst to me that this man, to whom I let out the second vineyard, taught you perfectly to cultivate the vineyard which I let out to you. How then can it be that the vineyard I let out to him should not have borne fruit, seeing it is all one soil?'

"The third answered: 'Lord, the vines are not cultivated by talking only, but he needs must sweat a shirt every day who wills to make it bring forth its fruit. And how shall your vineyard of your vinedresser bear fruit, O lord, if he does naught but waste the time in talking? Sure it is, O lord, that if he had put into practice his own words, while I who cannot talk so much have given you the rent for two years, he would have given you the rent of the vineyard for five years.'

"The lord was wroth, and said with scorn to the vinedresser: 'And so you have wrought a great work in not cutting away the wood and levelling the vineyard, wherefore there is owing to you a great reward!' And having called his servants he had him beaten without any mercy. And then he put him into prison under the keeping of a cruel servant who beat him every day, and never was willing to set him free for prayers of his friends."

### Notes — Chapter 76
1. **The parable is kept entire and its irony kept sharp** (folios 78b–79b): the ignorant first man pleads ignorance and is pitied and released ("it is enough for thee that I give thee thy debt" — the debt cancelled); the knowing second man, who taught the third but only talked, is beaten without mercy and imprisoned under a cruel servant. The third man's maxim — "the vines are not cultivated by talking only, but he needs must sweat a shirt every day" — stands as the Italian gives it.
2. **One reading kept literal and flagged uncertain**: the second man's plea renders the Italian *la tua uigna he im piedi* — literally "thy vineyard is standing / on its feet." The 1907 check renders "thy vineyard is backward" with the marginal note "Or, is alive (?). Text obscure." The translation keeps the literal sense ("still standing") and records the obscurity; Spanish control is pending (R6).
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the one who has knowledge — two parables thereof." Their marginal notes record two manuscript readings as printed: "1 MS. *pimo* (sic)" (the first man's "I said") and one point where the manuscript reads *ti* ("which I let out to thee"). Their footnote calls this "Another apocryphal parable," citing (?) Matthew 21:28 sqq. or Luke 19:11 sqq. as possible parallels. The English page running head reads "THE THREE VINEDRESSERS" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title.

---

## Chapter 77

*[Editorial heading: Knowing the truth and working against it — the camel proverb. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXVII") at folio 80a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Truly I say to you, that on the day of judgement many shall say to God: 'Lord, we have preached and taught by your law.' Against them even the stones shall cry out, saying: 'When you preached to others, with your own tongue you condemned yourselves, O workers of iniquity.'

"'As God lives,' said Jesus, 'he who knows the truth and works the contrary shall be punished with such grievous penalty that Satan shall almost have compassion on him. Tell me, now, has our God given us the law for knowing or for working? Truly I say to you, that all knowledge has for end that wisdom which works all it knows.

"'Tell me, if one were sitting at table and with his eyes beheld delicate meats, but with his hands should choose unclean things and eat those, would not he be mad?' 'Yea, assuredly,' said the disciples.

"Then said Jesus: 'O mad beyond all madmen art you, O man, that with your understanding know heaven, and with your hands choose earth; with your understanding know God, and with your affection desire the world; with your understanding know the delights of paradise, and with your works choose the miseries of hell. Brave soldier, that leaves the sword and carries the scabbard to fight! Now, know you not that he who walks by night desires light, not only to see the light, but rather to see the good road, in order that he may pass safely to the inn? O miserable world, to be a thousand times despised and abhorred! since our God by his holy prophets has ever willed to grant it to know the way to go to his country and his rest; but you, wicked one, not only will not go, but, which is worse, have despised the light! True is the proverb of the camel, that it likes not clear water to drink, because it desires not to see its own ugly face. So does the ungodly who works ill; for he hates the light lest his evil works should be known. But he who receiveth wisdom, and not only works not well, but, which is worse, employs it for evil, is like to him who should use the gifts as instruments to slay the giver.'"

### Notes — Chapter 77
1. **The judgment-day preachers and the "almost" of Satan's pity are kept as written** (folios 80a–80b): the preachers' plea met by the stones' cry — "with your own tongue ye condemned yourselves, O workers of iniquity" — and the penalty "that Satan shall almost have compassion on him." Nothing is softened; "almost" stands.
2. **The images are kept entire in the text's order**: the man at table — eyes on delicate meats, hands on unclean things; the madman who knows heaven with his understanding and chooses earth with his hands; the brave soldier who leaves the sword and carries the scabbard to fight; the night-walker who wants light not to see the light but to see the good road to the inn; the camel that dislikes clear water lest it see its own ugly face — so the evildoer hates the light lest his evil works be known.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the one who has knowledge — an evil-doer." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "By the living God"; a variant rendering of the Satan-pity saying ("By the living God, whoever has learnt the truth and acts contrary to it will have a fearful punishment; perhaps Satan [even] shall have compassion upon him"); and "God gives." A line of the scribe's Arabic gloss stands in the Italian column and is recorded, not translated. Their footnote cites (?) Luke 13:26–27 as the parallel. The English page running head reads "RIGHT USE OF KNOWLEDGE" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title.

---

## Chapter 78

*[Editorial heading: Is it better not to learn? — the light in the heart. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXVIIIa.") at folio 80b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Truly I say to you, that God had not compassion on the fall of Satan but yet [had compassion] on the fall of Adam. And let this suffice you to know the unhappy condition of him who knows good and does evil."

Then said Andrew: "O master, it is a good thing to leave learning aside, so as not to fall into such condition."

Jesus answered: "If the world is good without the sun, man without eyes, and the soul without understanding, then is it good not to know. Truly I say to you, that bread is not so good for the temporal life as is learning for the eternal life. Know you not that it is a precept of God to learn? For thus says God: 'Ask of your elders, and they shall teach you.' And of the law says God: 'See that my precept be before your eyes, and when you sit down, and when you walk, and at all times meditate thereon.' Whether, then, it is good not to learn, you may now know. Oh, unhappy he who despises wisdom, for he is sure to lose eternal life."

James answered: "O master, we know that Job learned not from a master, nor Abraham; nevertheless they became holy ones and prophets."

Jesus answered: "Truly I say to you, that he who is of the bridegroom's house needs not to be invited to the marriage, because he dwells in the house where the marriage is held; but they that are far from the house. Now know you not that the prophets of God are in the house of God's grace and mercy, and so have the law of God manifest in them: as David our father says on this matter: 'The law of his God is in his heart; therefore his path shall not be digged up.' Truly I say to you that our God in creating man not only created him righteous, but inserted in his heart a light that should show to him that it is fitting to serve God. Wherefore, even if this light be darkened after sin, yet is it not extinguished. For every nation has this desire to serve God, though they have lost God and serve false and lying gods. Accordingly it is necessary that a man be taught of the prophets of God, for they have clear the light to teach the way to go to paradise, our country, by serving God well: just as it is necessary that he who has his eyes diseased should be guided and helped."

### Notes — Chapter 78
1. **The answer to Andrew is kept in its irony** (folios 80b–81b): "If the world is good without the sun, man without eyes, and the soul without understanding, then is it good not to know" — the three comparisons answer his "it is a good thing to leave learning aside." The scripture chain for the precept of learning is the text's own: "Ask of thine elders" and the precept "before thine eyes, and when thou sittest down, and when thou walkest, and at all times meditate thereon."
2. **The heart-light teaching is kept as written**: God "inserted in his heart a light that should show to him that it is fitting to serve God"; "even if this light be darkened after sin, yet is it not extinguished"; "every nation hath this desire to serve God, though they have lost God and serve false and lying gods." The prophets answer the Job-and-Abraham objection — they "are in the house of God's grace and mercy, and so have the law of God manifest in them."
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the Light of the hearts." Their marginal note "God is gracious" renders the scribe's Arabic mark on Adam's fall. Their footnotes cite John 3:20, Deuteronomy 32:7, Deuteronomy 6:7–8 and 11:18–19, and Psalm 37:31 as parallels. The English page running head reads "THE VALUE OF LEARNING" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The rubric number reads "LXXVIIIa." in the Italian column position and "LXXVIIIa." (OCR "LXXVIHa.") at the English head — boundary confirmed by content match, not by the rubric alone.

---

## Chapter 79

*[Editorial heading: God shows the law to the nations — the fruitful plant in the desert. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXIX") at folio 81b. The heading above is editorial.]*

James answered: "And how shall the prophets teach us if they are dead; and how shall he be taught who has not knowledge of the prophets?"

Jesus answered: "Their doctrine is written down, so that it ought to be studied, for the writing is to you for a prophet. Truly, truly, I say to you that he who despises the prophecy despises not only the prophet, but despises also God who has sent the prophet. But concerning such as know not the prophet, as are the nations, I tell you that if there shall live in those regions any man who lives as his heart shall show him, not doing to others that which he would not receive from others, and giving to his neighbour that which he would receive from others, such a man shall not be forsaken of the mercy of God. Wherefore at death, if not sooner, God will show him and give him his law with mercy. Perchance you think that God has given the law for love of the law? Assuredly this is not true, but rather has God given his law in order that man might work good for love of God. And so if God shall find a man who for love of him works good, shall he perchance despise him? Nay, surely, but rather will he love him more than those to whom he has given the law.

"I tell you for an example: There was a man who had great possessions; and in his territory he had desert land that only bore unfruitful things. And so, as he was walking out one day through such desert land, he found among such unfruitful plants a plant that had delicate fruits. Whereupon this man said: 'Now how does this plant here bear these so delicate fruits? Assuredly I will not that it be cut down and put on the fire with the rest.' And having called his servants he made them dig it up and set it in his garden. Even so, I tell you, that our God shall reserve from the flames of hell those who work righteousness, wheresoever they be."

### Notes — Chapter 79
1. **The nations teaching is kept without trimming** (folios 81b–82b): the man among the nations who lives "as his heart shall show him" — "not doing to others that which he would not receive from others, and giving to his neighbour that which he would receive from others" — "shall not be forsaken of the mercy of God," and God will "at death, if not sooner" show him and give him his law with mercy. The law's purpose — not "for love of the law" but "that man might work good for love of God" — and the verdict that God will love such a man "more than those to whom he hath given the law" stand as the Italian gives them.
2. **The fruitful-plant parable is kept entire**: the desert land bearing only unfruitful things, the one plant with delicate fruits, the master who will not cut it down with the rest but has it dug up and set in his garden — "Even so... our God shall reserve from the flames of hell those who work righteousness, wheresoever they be."
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the compassion of God." Their marginal notes render the scribe's Arabic marks: "God sends" (on the prophet sent), "God gives" (on giving his law), and a garbled note on "for love of the law" ("Didst thou imagine that God sent the law for the sake of the law. Nay; rather he sent it... service") recorded as printed. Their footnote cites Luke 10:16 as the parallel. The English page running head reads "EVERY NATION DESIRETH GOD" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title.

---

## Chapter 80

*[Editorial heading: God makes no difference — Job, Noah, Abraham, Lot, Daniel. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXbc.") at folio 82b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Tell me, where dwelt Job but in Uz among idolaters? And at the time of the flood, how writes Moses? Tell me, he says: 'Noah truly found grace before God.' Our father Abraham had a father without faith, for he made and worshipped false idols. Lot abode among the most wicked men on earth. Daniel as a child, with Ananias, Azarias, and Misael, were taken captive by Nebuchadnezzar in such wise that they were but two years old when they were taken; and they were nurtured among the multitude of idolatrous servants. As God lives, even as the fire burns dry things and converts them into fire, making no difference between olive and cypress and palm; even so our God has mercy on every one that works righteously, making no difference between Jew, Scythian, Greek, or Ishmaelite. But let not your heart stop there, O James, because where God has sent the prophet it is necessary entirely to deny your own judgement and to follow the prophet, and not to say: 'Why says he thus?' 'Why does he thus forbid and command?' But say: 'Thus God wills. Thus God commands.' Now what said God to Moses when Israel despised Moses? 'They have not despised you, but they have despised me.'

"Truly I say to you, that man ought to spend all the time of his life not in learning how to speak or to read, but in learning how to work well. Now tell me, who is that servant of Herod who would not study to please him by serving him with all diligence? Woe to the world that studies only to please a body that is clay and dung, and studies not but forgets the service of God who has made all things; who is blessed for evermore."

### Notes — Chapter 80
1. **The catalogue is kept as written, with its figures** (folios 82b–83b): Job in Uz among idolaters; Noah finding grace; Abraham's father "without faith, for he made and worshipped false idols"; Lot among the most wicked; Daniel with Ananias, Azarias, and Misael taken captive at "two years old" and "nurtured among the multitude of idolatrous servants" — kept as the Italian gives it, though it clashes with the canonical Daniel as a youth; the clash is recorded, not corrected.
2. **The no-difference saying is kept exactly**: fire "making no difference between olive and cypress and palm" — so God has mercy "on every one that worketh righteously, making no difference between Jew, Scythian, Greek, or Ishmaelite." The "Ishmaelite" stands as written; nothing is read into it.
3. **The unnamed "prophet" passage is an anticipation, not a naming**: "where God hath sent the prophet it is necessary entirely to deny thine own judgement and to follow the prophet" — the Italian names no one (*il proffeta* only). No name is imported; the passage is footnoted as an anticipation of the seam zone, with the naming reserved to the chapters that name (42, 96–97), per the audit's seam handling.
4. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of knowledge." Their marginal notes render the scribe's Arabic marks: "God keeps"; "Mention of Job and Noah and Abraham and Daniel"; "God the living" (on "As God liveth"); "God the compassionate" (on "hath mercy"); "God sends" (on "hath sent the prophet"). Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Their footnotes cite Job 1:1, Genesis 6:8, the Abraham story earlier in this book (ch. 26–28), Genesis 13:13, Daniel 1:6 sqq., and Colossians 3:11 as parallels. The English page running head reads "GOD MAKETH NO DIFFERENCE" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "LXXXI" (the Samaritan woman), is reserved for the next slice — boundary recorded.

---

---

## Chapter 81

*[Editorial heading: The Samaritan woman at the well. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXI") at folio 83b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Tell me, would it have been a great sin of the priests if, when they were carrying the ark of the testimony of God, they had let it fall to the ground?"

The disciples trembled hearing this, for they knew that God slew Uzzah for having wrongly touched the ark of God. And they said: "Most grievous would be such a sin."

Then said Jesus: "As God lives, it is a greater sin to forget the word of God, wherewith he made all things, whereby he offers you eternal life."

And having said this Jesus made prayer; and after his prayer he said: "Tomorrow we needs must pass into Samaria, for so has said to me the holy angel of God."

Early on the morning of a certain day, Jesus arrived near to the well which Jacob made and gave to Joseph his son. Whereupon Jesus, being wearied with the journey, sent his disciples to the city to buy food. And so he sat himself down by the well, upon the stone of the well. And, lo, a woman of Samaria comes to the well to draw water.

Jesus says to the woman: "Give me to drink."

The woman answered: "Now, art you not ashamed that you, being a Hebrew, ask drink of me which am a Samaritan woman?"

Jesus answered: "O woman, if you knew who he is that asks you for drink, perchance you would have asked of him for drink."

The woman answered: "Now how should you give me to drink, seeing you have no vessel to draw the water, nor rope, and the well is deep?"

Jesus answered: "O woman, whoso drinks of the water of this well, thirst comes to him again. But whosoever drinks of the water that I give has thirst no more; but to them that have thirst they give to drink, insomuch that they come to eternal life."

Then said the woman: "O Lord, give me of this your water."

Jesus answered: "Go call your husband, and to both of you I will give to drink."

Said the woman: "I have no husband."

Jesus answered: "Well have you said the truth, for you have had five husbands, and he whom you now have is not your husband."

The woman was confounded hearing this, and said: "Lord, hereby perceive I that you are a prophet; therefore tell me, I pray: the Hebrews make prayer on mount Sion in the temple built by Solomon in Jerusalem, and say that there and nowhere else [men] find grace and mercy of God. And our people worship on these mountains, and say that only on the mountains of Samaria ought worship to be made. Who are the true worshippers?"

### Notes — Chapter 81
1. **The Uzzah opening is kept as the Italian gives it** (folios 83b–84a): the disciples' trembling at the ark question, "for they knew that God slew Uzzah for having wrongly touched the ark of God," and the pivot — "it is a greater sin to forget the word of God, wherewith he made all things, whereby he offereth thee eternal life." The well is the one "which Jacob made and gave to Joseph his son."
2. **The living-water exchange is kept entire, including the text's own turn** (folios 84a–85a): the well-water/thirst-again saying, then the distinctive Barnabas continuation — "but to them that have thirst they give to drink, insomuch that they come to eternal life" — kept as written, not trimmed to the Johannine form. The five-husbands disclosure, the "O Lord" address, and the Sion-versus-Samaria question close the chapter; the answer opens chapter 82.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the Water." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God punishes"; "God created everything in one speech"; and record "God gives" beside the five-husbands disclosure. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Their footnotes cite 1 Samuel 8:7, Exodus 16:8, 2 Samuel 6:7, John 1:3, Psalm 33:6, and John 4:4–20 as parallels. The English page running head reads "THE WOMAN OF SAMARIA" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "LXXXII" (folio 85a), is confirmed in the Italian column and the English head alike.

## Chapter 82

*[Editorial heading: True worship — and "after me shall come the Messiah." Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXIIb.") at folio 85a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Jesus gave a sigh and wept, saying: "Woe to you, Judaea, for you glory, saying: 'The temple of God, the temple of God,' and live as though there were no God; given over wholly to the pleasures and gains of the world; for this woman in the day of judgement shall condemn you to hell; for this woman seeketh to know how to find grace and mercy before God."

And turning to the woman he said: "O woman, you Samaritans worship that which you know not, but we Hebrews worship that which we know. Truly I say to you, that God is spirit and truth, and so in spirit and in truth must he be worshipped. For the promise of God was made in Jerusalem, in the temple of Solomon, and not elsewhere. But believe me, a time will come that God will give his mercy in another city, and in every place it will be possible to worship him in truth. And God in every place will have accepted true prayer with mercy."

The woman answered: "We look for the Messiah; when he comes he will teach us."

Jesus answered: "Know you, woman, that the Messiah must come?"

She answered: "Yea, Lord."

Then Jesus rejoiced, and said: "So far as I see, O woman, you are faithful: know therefore that in the faith of the Messiah shall be saved every one that is elect of God; therefore it is necessary that you know the coming of the Messiah."

Said the woman: "O Lord, perchance you are the Messiah."

Jesus answered: "I am indeed sent to the house of Israel as a prophet of salvation; but after me shall come the Messiah, sent of God to all the world, for whom God has made the world. And then through all the world will God be worshipped, and mercy received, insomuch that the year of jubilee, which now comes every hundred years, shall by the Messiah be reduced to every year in every place."

Then the woman left her waterpot and ran to the city to announce all that she had heard from Jesus.

### Notes — Chapter 82
1. **This chapter carries seam material and is handled per the audit's seam rule** (folios 85a–86a): "I am indeed sent to the house of Israel as a prophet of salvation; **but after me shall come the Messiah, sent of God to all the world, for whom God has made the world**" is translated exactly as the Italian stands (*dapoi di me uenira il messia mandato da DIOC ha tutto il monddo per il quale DIO ha fato il monddo*) — never smoothed. The Italian names no one here; no name is imported into the text. The 1907 check's footnote identifies the figure as Mohammed (their identification, recorded here as the check's, not as the Italian's). The "in the faith of the Messiah shall be saved every one that is elect of God" and the jubilee reduction ("every hundred years" to "every year in every place") are likewise kept as written.
2. **"Temple of God"** follows the Italian manuscript reading (*tempio di DIO*); the 1907 English harmonizes to "temple of the Lord" (Jer. 7:4). The Spanish witness reads "Templo de Dios," confirming the literal Italian. The check's harmonization is recorded, not followed.
3. **The prayer-direction saying is kept as written**: "a time will come that God will give his mercy in another city, and in every place it will be possible to worship him in truth." The editors' marginal note on the scribe's Arabic mark here records their gloss ("He altered the prayer-direction after the Gospel in the time of the 'seal of the prophets'") — recorded as printed, not endorsed as fact.
4. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the prayer-direction and prayer." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is the way of salvation and compassionate"; "Prophet" (on "we look for the Messiah"); "God sends"; "The prophet of God the worshipped"; "God is truth and is worshipped." A "1 MS. *tempi*" note records the manuscript's *tempio di DIO* reading as printed. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Their footnotes cite Jeremiah 7:4 and John 4:21–26 as parallels. The English page running head continues "THE WOMAN OF SAMARIA" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "LXXXIII" (folio 86a), is confirmed.

---

## Chapter 83

*[Editorial heading: The harvest at Samaria — the midnight prayer of a hundred bowings. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXIIIe.") at folio 86a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Whilst the woman was talking with Jesus came his disciples, and marvelled that Jesus was speaking thus with a woman. Yet no one said to him: "Why speak you thus with a Samaritan woman?"

Whereupon, when the woman was departed, they said: "Master, come and eat."

Jesus answered: "I must eat other food."

Then said the disciples one to another: "Perchance some wayfarer has spoken with Jesus, and has gone to find him food." And they questioned him who writes this, saying: "Has there been any one here, O Barnabas, who might have brought food to the master?"

Then answered he who writes: "There has not been here any other than the woman whom you saw, who brought this empty vessel to fill it with water."

Then the disciples stood amazed, awaiting the issue of the words of Jesus. Whereupon Jesus said: "You know not that the true food is to do the will of God; because it is not bread that sustains man and gives him life, but rather the word of God, by his will. And so for this reason the holy angels eat not, but are nourished only by the will of God. And thus we, Moses and Elijah and yet another, have been forty days and forty nights without any food."

And lifting up his eyes, Jesus said: "How far off is the harvest?"

The disciples answered: "Three months."

Jesus said: "Look now, how the mountain is white with corn; truly I say to you, that to-day there is a great harvest to be reaped."

And then he pointed to the multitude who had come to see him. For the woman having entered into the city had moved all the city, saying: "O men, come and see a new prophet sent of God to the house of Israel"; and she recounted to them all that she had heard from Jesus. When they were come thither they besought Jesus to abide with them; and he entered into the city and abode there two days, healing all the sick, and teaching concerning the kingdom of God.

Then said the citizens to the woman: "We believe more in his words and miracles than we do in what you saidst; for he is indeed a holy one of God, a prophet sent for the salvation of those that shall believe on him."

After the prayer of midnight the disciples came near to Jesus, and he said to them: "This night shall be in the time of the Messiah, messenger of God, the jubilee every year — that now comes every hundred years. Therefore I will not that we sleep, but let us make prayer, bowing our head a hundred times, doing reverence to our God, mighty and merciful, who is blessed for evermore; and so each time let us say: 'I confess you our God alone, that have not had beginning, nor shall ever have end; for by your mercy gave you to all things their beginning, and by your justice you shall give to all an end; that have no likeness among men, because in your infinite goodness you are not subject to motion nor to any accident. Have mercy on us, for you have created us, and we are the works of your hands.'"

### Notes — Chapter 83
1. **The Barnabas self-naming is kept as written** (folios 86a–86b): the disciples question "him who writeth this" — "Hath there been any one here, O Barnabas, who might have brought food to the master?" — and "Then answered he who writeth." The narrator is named Barnabas in the Italian itself; nothing is read into it.
2. **Seam-adjacent material is kept exactly**: "This night shall be in the time of the Messiah, messenger of God, the jubilee every year" (*quessta notte sara al tempo del messia nontio di DIO il iubileo ogni hano*) — translated as the Italian stands and footnoted; the Italian names no one. The hundred-bowings prayer ("bowing our head a hundred times") and its creed-form confession — no beginning, no end, no likeness among men, not subject to motion or accident — are kept entire.
3. **The Samaria stay is kept with its figures**: two days in the city, "healing all the sick, and teaching concerning the kingdom of God"; the citizens' verdict — "a holy one of God, a prophet sent for the salvation of those that shall believe on him." The "and yet another" beside Moses and Elijah in the forty-days fast is kept as the Italian gives it (*moisse he hellia hanchora uno altro*), not filled in.
4. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of absolution." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is worshipped"; "Prophet"; "God sent"; "The prophet of God the worshipped." A "1 MS. *mandate mandate* (bis)" note records the manuscript's doubled word as printed. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Their footnotes cite Deuteronomy 8:3, Matthew 4:4, Exodus 24:18, 1 Kings 19:8, and John 4:27–42 as parallels. The English page running head reads "JESUS AND THE SAMARITAN" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "LXXXIV" (folio 87b), is confirmed.
