## Chapter 84

*[Editorial heading: Prayer must be unmixed — the friend at prayer. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXIV") at folio 87b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Having made the prayer, Jesus said: "Let us give thanks to God, because he has given to us this night great mercy; for that he has made to come back the time that needs must pass in this night, in that we have made prayer in union with the messenger of God. And I have heard his voice."

The disciples rejoiced greatly at hearing this, and said: "Master, teach us some precepts this night."

Then said Jesus: "Have you ever seen dung mixed with balsam?"

They answered: "Nay, Lord, for no one is so mad as to do this thing."

"Now I tell you that there be in the world greater madmen," said Jesus, "because with the service of God they mingle the service of the world. So much so that many of blameless life have been deceived of Satan, and while praying have mingled with their prayer worldly business, whereupon they have become at that time abominable in the sight of God. Tell me, when you wash yourselves for prayer, do you take care that no unclean thing touch you? Yea, assuredly. But what do you when you are making prayer? You wash your soul from sins through the mercy of God. Would you be willing then, while you are making prayer, to speak of worldly things? Take care not to do so, for every worldly word becomes dung of the devil upon the soul of him that speaks."

Then trembled the disciples, because he spake with vehemence of spirit; and they said: "O master, what shall we do if when we are making prayer a friend shall come to speak to us?"

Jesus answered: "Suffer him to wait, and finish the prayer."

Said Bartholomew: "But what if he shall be offended and go his way, when he see that we speak not with him?"

Jesus answered: "If he shall be offended, believe me, he will not be a friend of yours nor a believer, but rather an unbeliever and a companion of Satan. Tell me, if you went to speak with a stable boy of Herod, and found him speaking into Herod's ears, would you be offended if he made you to wait? No, assuredly; but you would be comforted at seeing your friend in favour with the king. Is this true?" said Jesus.

The disciples answered: "It is most true."

Then said Jesus: "Truly I say to you, that everyone when he prays speaks with God. Is it then right that you should leave speaking with God in order to speak with man? Is it right that your friend should for this cause be offended, because you have more reverence for God than for him? Believe me, that if he shall be offended when you make him wait, he is a good servant of the devil. For this desires the devil, that God should be forsaken for man. As God lives, in every good work he that fears God ought to separate himself from the works of the world, so as not to corrupt the good work."

### Notes — Chapter 84
1. **The midnight-prayer sequel is kept as written** (folios 87b–89a): thanksgiving for the night's mercy — "in that we have made prayer in union with the messenger of God. And I have heard his voice" — kept exactly as the Italian stands (*habiamo fato oratione insieme chon il nontio di DIO. he ho sentito la sua uoce*); the Italian names no one, and no name is imported.
2. **The dung-and-balsam teaching is kept sharp**: "every worldly word becometh dung of the devil upon the soul of him that speaketh." The Bartholomew exchange runs its full course — the offended friend is "not a friend of yours nor a believer, but rather an unbeliever and a companion of Satan"; the Herod stable-boy comparison is kept as the Italian gives it.
3. **Two readings flagged uncertain at the garbled points**: the OCR at the "speaketh with God" sentence reads *olgi parlla chon mo* and the closing *chi teme mo* similarly obscures the noun; the 1907 check reads both as God ("speaketh with God"; "he that feareth God"), which the surrounding sense confirms ("leave speaking with God in order to speak with man"). The translation follows the check here and records the debt — Spanish control is pending (R6).
4. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter of the sincere." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is one and of old and for ever"; "God of old and for ever"; "God is greatest, the compassionate and just, and to him be praise"; "God bestows"; "The prophet of God" (on "the messenger of God"); "Prayer is the soul of purity." Their footnote cites ch. 16a and note as the parallel. The English page running head reads "PRAYER MUST BE PURE" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "LXXXV" (folio 89a), follows; its OCR is garbled after the numeral, but the boundary is confirmed by the content match (the friend/enemy teaching begins there).

---

## Chapter 85

*[Editorial heading: The true friend — physician of the soul. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXV") at folio 89a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"When a man works ill or talks ill, if one go to correct him, and hinder such work, what does such an one?" said Jesus.

The disciples answered: "He does well, because he serves God, who always seeketh to hinder evil, even as the sun that always seeketh to chase away the darkness."

Said Jesus: "And I tell you on the contrary that when one works well or speaks well, whosoever seeketh to hinder him, under pretext of aught that is not better, he serves the devil, nay, he even becomes his companion. For the devil attends to nought else but to hinder every good thing. But what shall I say to you now? I will say to you as said Solomon the prophet, holy one, and friend of God: 'Of a thousand whom you know, one be your friend.'"

Then said Matthew: "Then shall we not be able to love any one."

Jesus answered: "Truly I say to you, that it is not lawful for you to hate anything save only sin: insomuch that you cannot hate even Satan as creature of God, but rather as enemy of God. Know you wherefore? I will tell you; because he is a creature of God, and all that God has created is good and perfect. Accordingly whoso hates the creature hates also the creator. But the friend is a singular thing, that is not easily found, but is easily lost. For the friend will not suffer contradiction against him whom he supremely loves. Beware, be you cautious, and choose not for friend one who loves not him whom you love. Know you what friend means? Friend means naught but physician of the soul. And so, just as one rarely finds a good physician who knows the sicknesses and understands to apply the medicines thereto, so also are friends rare who know the faults and understand how to guide to good. But herein is an evil, that there are many who have friends that feign not to see the faults of their friend; others excuse them; others defend them under earthly pretext; and, what is worse, there are friends who invite and aid their friend to err, whose end shall be like to their villany. Beware that you receive not such men for friends, for in truth they are enemies and slayers of the soul."

### Notes — Chapter 85
1. **The reversal is kept as the Italian gives it** (folios 89a–90b): correcting the evildoer serves God — "even as the sun that always seeketh to chase away the darkness"; hindering the doer of good "serveth the devil, nay, he even becometh his companion." The OCR is garbled through part of this reversal (*che olgi seruo il diauollo anziehe diuenta suo chompagno*); the sense is confirmed by the 1907 check and the translation records the debt — Spanish control is pending (R6).
2. **The Satan-as-creature line is kept exactly**: "ye cannot hate even Satan as creature of God, but rather as enemy of God... because he is a creature of God, and all that God hath created is good and perfect. Accordingly whoso hateth the creature hateth also the creator."
3. **The friend catalogue is kept entire**: the "singular thing" that is not easily found but easily lost; "choose not for friend one who loveth not him whom ye love"; "Friend meaneth naught but physician of the soul"; the three false-friend kinds — who feign not to see the faults, who excuse them, who defend them under earthly pretext — and the worst, "who invite and aid their friend to err, whose end shall be like unto their villany." The closing verdict — "in truth they are enemies and slayers of the soul" — is kept unsoftened.
4. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the difference between a friend and an enemy." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "By the living God"; "The Chapter of the friend." A "1 MS. *che che* (bis)" note records the manuscript's doubled word as printed. Their footnotes cite Proverbs 18:24 ("Of a thousand whom ye know, one be your friend" — rendered as printed with their "Or 'a possession all one's own'" alternative) and Surah 44:39 (on "all that God hath created is good and perfect"). The English page running head reads "OF THE TRUE FRIEND" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "LXXXVI" (folio 90b), is reserved for the next slice — boundary recorded.

---

---

## Chapter 86

*[Editorial heading: The true friend, continued — how to choose him, and the friend that is worse than you. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXVIb.") at folio 90b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Let your friend be such, that even as he wills to correct you, so receive you correction; and even as he wills that you leave all things for love of God, so let it content him too that you forsake him for the service of God. But tell me: if a man know not how to love God, how shall he know how to love himself? And how shall he know how to love others, not knowing how to love himself? Assuredly this is impossible. Wherefore when you wouldst choose you one for friend — for truly supremely poor is he that has no friend at all — see that you first consider not the fineness of his kin, not the fineness of his family, not the fineness of his house, not the fineness of his garments, not the fineness of his body, nor yet his fine words, for you shall be easily deceived. But look how he fears God, how he despises earthly things, how he loves good works, and above all how he hates his own flesh; and so shall you easily find the true friend: if he above all things shall fear God, and shall despise the vanities of the world; if he shall be always occupied in good works, and shall hate his own body as a cruel enemy. Nor yet shall you love such a friend in such wise that your love rest in him, for you shall be an idolater; but love him as a gift which God has given you, for so shall God adorn you with greater favour. Truly I say to you, that he who has found a true friend has found one of the delights of paradise; nay, such is the key of paradise."

Thaddaeus answered: "But if perchance a man shall have a friend who is not such as you have said, O master, what ought he to do? Ought he to forsake him?"

Jesus answered: "He ought to do as the mariner does with the ship, who sails her so long as he perceives gain thereby, but when he sees her to be a loss forsakes her. So shall you do with your friend that is worse than you: in those things wherein he is an offence to you, leave him, if you wouldst not that the mercy of God leave you."

### Notes — Chapter 86
1. **Two renderings follow the Italian against the 1907 check** (folios 90b–91b): "so receive thou correction" (*chossi riceui la choretione* — the imperative is second person, addressed to the hearer), where the check reads "so he may receive correction"; and "for so shall God adorn thee with greater favour" (*ti addornera* — "thee"), where the check brackets "[him]". Both divergences are recorded; the Italian is followed.
2. **The friend-teaching is kept whole**: the not-hating-one's-flesh test ("hate his own body as a cruel enemy"), the idolatry warning on love that rests in the friend, the "delights of paradise" and "key of paradise" sayings, and the mariner-and-ship answer to Thaddaeus — including the hard close, "in those things wherein he is an offence unto thee, leave him, if thou wouldst not that the mercy of God leave thee."
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the friend." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "Account of the true friend"; "God bestows"; and, on the mariner passage, "If your friend intends to divert you from the right way, leave him if you do not wish the grace of God to desert you." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. The English page running head reads "OF THE TRUE FRIEND" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "LXXXVII" (folio 92a), is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 87

*[Editorial heading: Offences — the millstone, the eye and the hand, and the sinning brother. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXVII") at folio 92a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Woe to the world because of offences! It needs must be that the offence come, because all the world is set in wickedness; but yet woe to them through whom the offence comes. It were better for the man that he should have a millstone about his neck and should be sunk in the depths of the sea, than that he should offend his neighbour. If your eye be an offence to you, pluck it out; for it is better that you go with one eye only into paradise than with both of them into hell. If your hand or your foot offend you, do likewise; for it is better that you go into the kingdom of heaven with one foot and with one hand than with two hands and two feet go into hell."

Said Simon, called Peter: "Lord, how must I do this? Certain it is that in a short time I shall be dismembered."

Jesus answered: "O Peter, put off fleshly prudence and straightway you shall find the truth. For he that teaches you is your eye, and he that helps you to work is your foot, and he that ministers aught to you is your hand. Wherefore when such are to you an occasion of sin, leave them; for it is better for you to go into paradise ignorant, with few works, and poor, than to go into hell wise, with great works, and rich. Everything that may hinder you from serving God, cast it from you as a man casts away everything that hinders his sight."

And having said this, Jesus called Peter close to him and said to him: "If your brother shall sin against you, go and correct him. If he amend, rejoice, for you have gained your brother; but if he shall not amend, go and call afresh two witnesses and correct him afresh; and if he shall not amend, go and tell it to the church; and if he shall not then amend, count him for an unbeliever, and therefore you shall not dwell under the same roof whereunder he dwells, you shall not eat at the same table whereat he sits, and you shall not speak with him; insomuch that if you know where he sets his foot in walking, you shall not set your foot there."

"But beware that you hold not thyself for better; rather shall you say thus: 'Peter, Peter, if God helped you not with his grace you wouldst be worse than he.'"

Peter answered: "How must I make the correction?"

### Notes — Chapter 87
1. **The chapter break falls mid-sentence as printed** (folios 92b–93a): the Italian ends chapter 87 with Peter's unfinished question — *chome li debo fare la* ("how must I make the") — and the "LXXXVIII" rubric intervenes before *choretione* ("correction") opens chapter 88. The question is completed at the close of this chapter for readability, and the break is recorded as printed; the 1907 check likewise completes it ("How must I correct him?") ahead of its own break.
2. **The severity is kept as written**: the millstone saying, the plucked eye and severed hand and foot, the "ignorant, with few works, and poor" preferred over "wise, with great works, and rich," and the full shunning rule for the unamended brother — no shared roof, no shared table, no speech, not even treading where his foot has trod.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the prevaricator (evil-doer)." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "Everything that hinders you from serving (God), leave it as you would anything that hinders your vision"; "God helps" (on "if God helped thee not"). A "Translation uncertain" note stands in the printed apparatus at this chapter's head, recorded without assignment. A line of the scribe's Arabic gloss stands in the Italian column and is recorded, not translated. Their footnotes cite Matthew 18:6–9 and 1 John 5:19 as parallels. The English page running head reads "OF PUTTING AWAY OFFENCES" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "LXXXVIII" (folio 93a), is confirmed.

---

## Chapter 88

*[Editorial heading: Correcting with mercy — seventy times seven, and the prince. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXVIII") at folio 93a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"[How must I make the] correction?" Jesus answered: "In the way that you thyself wouldst fain be corrected; and as you wouldst fain be borne with, so bear with others. Believe me, Peter, for truly I say to you that every time you shall correct your brother with mercy you shall receive mercy of God, and your words shall bear some fruit; but if you shall do it with rigour of justice, you shall be rigorously punished by God, and shall bear no fruit. Tell me, Peter: those earthen pots wherein the poor cook their food — do they wash them, perchance, with stones and iron hammers? Nay, certainly; but rather with hot water. Vessels are broken in pieces with iron, things of wood are burned with fire; but man is amended with mercy. Wherefore, when you shall correct your brother you shall say to thyself: 'If God help me not, I shall do to-morrow worse than all that he has done to-day.'"

Peter answered: "How many times must I forgive my brother, O master?"

Jesus answered: "As many times as you wouldst fain be forgiven by him."

Said Peter: "Seven times a day?"

Jesus answered: "Not only seven, but seventy times seven you shall forgive him every day; for he that forgives, to him shall it be forgiven, and he that condemns shall be condemned."

Then said he who writes this: "Woe to princes! for they shall go to hell."

Jesus reproved him, saying: "you are become foolish, O Barnabas, in that you have spoken thus. Truly I say to you, that the bath is not so necessary for the body, the bit for the horse, and the tiller for the ship, as the prince is necessary for the state. And for what cause did God give Moses, Joshua, Samuel, David, and Solomon, and so many others who passed judgement? To such has God given the sword for the extirpation of iniquity."

Then said he who writes this: "Now, how ought judgement to be given, condemning and pardoning?"

Jesus answered: "Not every one is a judge: for to the judge alone it appertaineth to condemn others, O Barnabas. And the judge ought to condemn the guilty, even as the father commands a putrified member to be cut off from his son, in order that the whole body may not become putrified."

### Notes — Chapter 88
1. **The Barnabas self-naming is kept as written** (folios 93a–93b): "he who writeth this" cries "Woe unto princes!" and Jesus answers him by name — "Thou art become foolish, O Barnabas" — and again "O Barnabas" in the judging answer. The narrator is named Barnabas in the Italian itself; nothing is read into it.
2. **The prince passage is kept whole, unsmoothed**: the woe against princes is called foolish to its face, and the defence runs its full course — bath for the body, bit for the horse, tiller for the ship, prince for the state; Moses, Joshua, Samuel, David, Solomon "and so many others who passed judgement"; "to such hath God given the sword for the extirpation of iniquity." The judge's answer closes the chapter with the putrified-member image kept as the Italian gives it.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the just." Their marginal note glosses the scribe's Arabic mark: "Pardon thy brother every day seventy times seven times; if thou forgive, thou shalt be forgiven." A "God gives" gloss stands on the sword passage. Their footnotes cite Matthew 18:15–17, Matthew 18:21–22, and Romans 13:4 as parallels. The English page running head reads "OF FORGIVENESS" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "LXXXIX" (folio 94a), is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 89

*[Editorial heading: How long to wait for the sinner's repentance. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "LXXXIX") at folio 94a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Said Peter: "How long must I wait for my brother to repent?"

Jesus answered: "So long as you wouldst be waited for."

Peter answered: "Not every one will understand this; wherefore speak to us more plainly."

Jesus answered: "Wait for your brother as long as God waits for him."

"Neither will they understand this," said Peter.

Jesus answered: "Wait for him so long as he has time to repent."

Then was Peter sad, and the others also, because they understood not the meaning. Whereupon Jesus answered: "If you had sound understanding, and knew that you yourselves were sinners, you would not think ever to shut your heart from mercy to the sinner. And so I tell you plainly, that the sinner ought to be waited for, that he may repent, so long as he has a soul beneath his teeth to breathe. For so does our God wait for him, the mighty and merciful. God said not: 'In that hour that the sinner shall fast, do alms, make prayer, and go on pilgrimage, I will forgive him.' Wherefore this have many accomplished, and are damned eternally. But he said: 'In that hour that the sinner shall bewail his sins, I for my part will not remember any more his iniquities.' Do you understand?" said Jesus.

The disciples answered: "Part we understand, and part not."

Said Jesus: "Which is the part that you understand not?"

They answered: "That many who have made prayer with fastings are damned."

Then said Jesus: "Truly I say to you, that the hypocrites and the Gentiles make more prayers, more alms, and more fasts than do the friends of God. But because they have not faith, they are not able to repent for love of God; and so they are damned."

Then said John: "Teach us, for love of God, of the faith."

Jesus answered: "It is time that we say the prayer of the dawn." Whereupon they arose, and having washed themselves made prayer to our God, who is blessed for evermore.

### Notes — Chapter 89
1. **The "soul beneath his teeth" saying is kept as the Italian gives it** (folios 94a–94b): the sinner is to be waited for "so long as he hath a soul beneath his teeth to breathe" — the measure of God's own waiting, "the mighty and merciful." The fasting-alms-prayer-pilgrimage catalogue is kept entire, with its verdict: "this have many accomplished, and are damned eternally."
2. **The faith-without-works turn is kept sharp**: "the hypocrites and the Gentiles make more prayers, more alms, and more fasts than do the friends of God. But because they have not faith, they are not able to repent for love of God; and so they are damned." John's request for teaching on the faith is answered by the dawn prayer itself — the chapter's close.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the gracious (one)." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is patient (long-suffering)"; "God is patient and powerful and the compassionate"; "God pardons"; "God the compassionate." Their footnotes read "Or, 'No one'" (on "Not every one will understand"), cite Ezekiel 18:37 (on "I will not remember any more his iniquities"), and cross-refer the dawn-prayer note. The English page running head reads "OF PATIENCE WITH OFFENDERS" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "XC" (folio 95a; OCR "XO."), is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 90

*[Editorial heading: Faith — the seal, and the "Wherefore" that is the gate of hell. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XC") at folio 95a. The heading above is editorial.]*

When the prayer was done, his disciples again drew near to Jesus, and he opened his mouth and said: "Draw near, John, for to-day will I speak to you of all that you have asked. Faith is a seal wherewith God seals his elect; which seal he gave to his messenger, from whose hands every one that is elect has received the faith. For even as God is one, so is the faith one. Wherefore God, having created before all things his messenger, gave to him before aught else the faith, which is as it were a likeness of God and of all that God has done and said. And so the faithful by faith sees all things, better than one sees with his eyes; because the eyes can err, nay they do almost always err; but faith errs never, for it has for foundation God and his word. Believe me that by faith are saved all the elect of God. And it is certain that without faith it is impossible for any one to please God. Wherefore Satan seeketh not to bring to nought fastings and prayer, alms and pilgrimages; nay rather he incites unbelievers thereto, for he takes pleasure in seeing man work without receiving pay. But he takes pains with all diligence to bring faith to nought, wherefore faith ought especially to be guarded with diligence, and the safest course will be to abandon the 'Wherefore,' seeing that the 'Wherefore' drove man out of Paradise, and changed Satan from a most beautiful angel into a horrible devil."

Then said John: "Now, how shall we abandon the 'Wherefore,' seeing that it is the gate of knowledge?"

Jesus answered: "Nay, rather the 'Wherefore' is the gate of hell."

Thereupon John kept silence, when Jesus added: "When you know that God has said a thing, who art you, O man, that you shouldst say, forsooth, 'Wherefore have you so said, O God; wherefore have you so done?' Shall the earthen vessel, perchance, say to its maker: 'Wherefore have you made me to hold water and not to contain balsam?' Truly I say to you, it is necessary against every temptation to strengthen yourself with this word, saying 'God has so said'; 'So has God done'; 'God so wills'; for so doing you shall live safely."

### Notes — Chapter 90
1. **This chapter carries seam-adjacent material and is handled per the audit's seam rule** (folios 95a–95b): "which seal he gave to his messenger, from whose hands every one that is elect hath received the faith" (*il qualle sigilo dono allo nontio suo dalle chui mani ogni elleto ha riceuto la fede*) and "God, having created before all things his messenger" (*hauendo chreato DIG auanti di ogni chossa il nontio suo*) are translated exactly as the Italian stands — never smoothed. The Italian names no one here; no name is imported into the text. It is footnoted as an anticipation of the coming messenger, in the line of the earlier anticipations (ch. 12, 17, 36, 80).
2. **The "Wherefore" teaching is kept whole**: the safest course is to abandon the "Wherefore," which "drove man out of Paradise, and changed Satan from a most beautiful angel into a horrible devil"; John's objection — "it is the gate of knowledge" — is answered, "Nay, rather the 'Wherefore' is the gate of hell"; and the chapter closes on the threefold formula, "God hath so said"; "So hath God done"; "God so willeth."
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of surrendering oneself (Islam) to God." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is one"; "Account of the religion of Islam"; "God's first creation was the prophet of God." Their footnotes read "Or, The faith" (on "so is the faith one") and cite Hebrews 11:6 (on "without faith it is impossible... to please God"). Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. The English page running head reads "OF FAITH" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The next rubric, "XCI" (folio 96a), is confirmed and reserved for the next slice.

---

---

## Chapter 91

*[Editorial heading: The great tumult in Judea — "God come to visit them." Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XCI>.") at folio 96a. The heading above is editorial.]*

At this time there was a great stirring throughout all Judea over Jesus, for the Roman soldiery, by the working of Satan, kept pressing the Hebrews with the saying that Jesus was God come down to visit them. So such a sedition flared up that, close upon the Forty Days, all Judea stood in arms, to the point where son was found set against father and brother against brother. For some were saying that Jesus was God come into the world; others were saying no, but that he is a son of God; and others were saying no, because God has no human likeness, and therefore begets no sons — but Jesus of Nazareth is a prophet of God. And all this sprang from the great miracles that Jesus did.

So, to calm the people, there was no help for it but that the high priest should ride out in procession, dressed in his priestly robes, with the holy name of God — the tetragrammaton — upon his forehead. And in the same manner rode out Pilate the governor, and Herod. Then at Mizpeh there gathered three armies, each of two hundred thousand men bearing the sword. Herod spoke to them, but they would not be calmed. Afterward the governor and the high priest spoke, saying: "Brothers, this war has been stirred up by the work of Satan, for Jesus is alive — to him we ought to turn, and ask him to give testimony of himself, and then believe in him according to his word." At this, at last, every one of them was calmed; and laying down their arms they embraced one another, each saying to the other: "Forgive me, brother!"

That day, then, every man laid it upon his own heart to believe Jesus according as he himself should say. And the governor and the high priest promised great gifts to whoever would come and report where Jesus might be found.

### Notes — Chapter 91
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the greatest tumult." Their marginal note glosses the scribe's Arabic mark "God to whom be praise." The English page running head reads "JESUS HAILED AS GOD" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. An unclear printer's mark ("861"" in the OCR) stands in the Italian column at the tetragrammaton line; it is recorded as printed, not resolved.
2. **"Close upon the Forty Days"** (*apresso alia quadragessima*): the editors' note reads it as Lent, not Ramadan ("which does not extend to forty days"). The rendering keeps the Italian's own term.
3. **The three positions are kept whole and unranked**, exactly as the Italian sets them: God come into the world; a son of God; no — God has no human likeness and begets no sons, but Jesus of Nazareth is a prophet of God. Nothing is smoothed between them.
4. **"Mizpeh"** renders the Italian *misfa*, with the check. The figures stand as written: three armies, two hundred thousand swordsmen each.
5. **MS note recorded**: at the governor's speech the printed apparatus notes "MS. che che (bis)" — the manuscript doubles the word; the printed text is followed.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle (Italian + Spanish control for ch. 1–120); Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "XCII" (folio 97a), is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 92

*[Editorial heading: "Our God comes" — the cry at the Jordan. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XCII a.") at folio 97a. The heading above is editorial.]*

At this time we went with Jesus to Mount Sinai by the word of the holy angel, and there Jesus kept the Forty Days with his disciples. When those days were past, Jesus drew near to the river Jordan to go up to Jerusalem — and he was seen by one of those who believed Jesus to be God. With the greatest joy that man ran on, crying out continually: "Our God is coming!" And when he reached the city he set the whole city in motion, saying: "Our God is coming! O Jerusalem, make ready to receive him!" And he bore witness that he had seen Jesus near the Jordan.

Then out of the city went every one, from the smallest to the greatest, to see Jesus, so that the city was left empty — for the women carried their little children in their arms, and in their haste they forgot to take food to eat. When the governor and the high priest heard of this, they rode out, and sent a messenger to Herod, who likewise rode out to find Jesus, so that the sedition of the people might be calmed. For two days they searched for him in the wilderness near the Jordan, and on the third day they found him, near the hour of midday, while he with his disciples was purifying himself for prayer according to the book of Moses.

Jesus marvelled greatly when he saw the multitude covering the ground with people, and said to his disciples: "Perhaps Satan has raised a sedition in Judea. May it please God to take away from Satan the dominion that he has over sinners." And when he had said this, the crowd drew near; and when they recognized him they began to cry out: "Welcome to you, O our God!" — and as to God they began to do him reverence. At this Jesus gave a great groan and said: "Get you from before me, O madmen! For I fear lest the earth open and swallow me up together with you, for your abominable words!" At this the people were filled with terror and began to weep.

### Notes — Chapter 92
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the Christians." The English page running head ("JESUS HAILED AS GOD") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. A line of the scribe's Arabic gloss stands in the Italian column and is recorded, not translated.
2. **"The Forty Days" and the purification "according to the book of Moses" are kept as written**, without harmonizing comment. The two-days' search and the third-day finding near midday are kept entire.
3. **The crowd's cry and the reverence "as to God," and Jesus' groan and rebuke, are translated exactly as written** — the Italian's *pazzi* ("madmen") and the earth-opening fear are not softened. This is seam context for the confession that follows in ch. 93, and for the named seam at ch. 96–97; no name is imported.
4. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "XCIII" (folio 98a), is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 93

*[Editorial heading: The confession of Jesus — "I am a man." Manuscript rubric (Ragg "XCIIIb.") at folio 98a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Jesus, lifting his hand in token of silence, said: "Truly you have sinned greatly, O Israelites, in calling me — a man — your God. And I fear that for this God may lay a heavy scourge upon the holy city, handing it over into the bondage of strangers. O Satan, accursed a thousand times, who has moved you to this!"

And when he had said this, Jesus struck his face with both his hands, and such an outcry of weeping rose up that no one could hear what Jesus was saying. Then he lifted his hand again in token of silence, and when the people had been quieted from their weeping, he said once more: "I confess before heaven, and I call to witness everything that dwells upon the earth, that I am a stranger to all that you have said — for I am a man, born of woman, mortal, subject to the judgment of God, suffering the miseries of eating and sleeping, of cold and heat, like other men. Therefore, when God shall come to judge, my words like a sword shall pierce every one of those who believe me to be more than man."

And when he had said this, Jesus saw a great company of horsemen, and understood that the governor was coming with Herod and the high priest. Then Jesus said: "Can it be that these too have gone mad?" When the governor arrived there with Herod and the priest, every one dismounted, and they made a ring around Jesus, so that the soldiery could not hold back the people, who were eager to hear Jesus speak with the priest. Jesus drew near to the priest with reverence — but the priest was minded to bow down and worship Jesus, when Jesus cried out: "Beware what you do, O priest of the living God! Sin not against our God!"

The priest answered: "Now is Judea so stirred over your signs and your teaching that they cry out that you are God. Constrained by the people, therefore, I have come here with the Roman governor and King Herod. We pray you from our heart that you be content to take away the sedition that is abroad on your account — for some say you are God, some say you are son of God, and some say you are a prophet."

Jesus answered: "And you, O great priest of God — why did you not quiet this sedition? Can it be that you too have gone out of your mind? Are the prophecies, then, with the law of our God, passed into oblivion? O wretched Judea, deceived by Satan!"

And when he had said this, Jesus said again: "I confess before heaven, and I call to witness everything that dwells upon the earth, that I am a stranger to all that men have said of me — that I am more than man. For I am a man, born of a woman, subject to the judgment of God, who lives here like other men, subject to the common miseries. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — you have committed a great sin, O priest, in saying what you have said. May it please God that no great vengeance come upon the holy city for this sin."

Then the priest said: "May God pardon us — and do you pray for us." Then the governor and Herod said: "Sir, it is impossible that a man should do what you do; therefore we do not understand what you say."

Jesus answered: "What you say is true — for God works the good in man, even as Satan works the evil in him. For man is like a shop, where whoever enters with his own consent works and trades therein. But tell me, O governor, and you, O king: you say this because you are strangers to our law. For if you read the testament and covenant of our God, you would see that Moses with a rod turned the water into blood, the dust into lice, the dew into tempest, and the light into darkness. He brought the frogs and the mice upon Egypt, which covered the land; he slew the firstborn, and opened the sea, wherein he drowned Pharaoh. Of these things I have done none — and yet of Moses every one confesses that he is a dead man at this present. Joshua made the sun stand still, and opened the Jordan — which I have not yet done; and yet of Joshua every one confesses that he is a dead man at this present. Elijah made fire come down visibly from heaven, and rain — which I have not done; and yet of Elijah every one confesses that he is a man. And so with very many other prophets, holy men, friends of God, who in the power of God have done things which the mind of him who knows not our God — almighty and merciful, who is blessed for ever — cannot grasp."

### Notes — Chapter 93
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of confession." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is living"; "God is wise"; "God the judge"; "I ask God's pardon." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. The English page running head reads "CONFESSION OF JESUS" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their footnotes cite the Moses/Joshua/Elijah parallels (Exod. vii seq.; Josh. x. 12–14; 1 Kings xviii. 38–39, 41 seq.).
2. **The confession is doubled in the Italian and kept doubled**: once before the weeping crowd, once before the priest — "I am a man, born of woman, mortal, subject to the judgment of God" — translated exactly as written, never smoothed. This is direct seam context for ch. 96–97 (next slice); the Italian names no one, and no name is imported.
3. **"My words like a sword"**: the Italian reads *quando uenira DIG ha iudichare le mie parole chome spada perchotera ogniuno che chrederano me essere piu di homo*. The check's footnote offers a variant turn ("when God shall judge on the Day of Judgement then our words shall cut like a sword him that believeth that I am more than man"); the Italian is followed and the variant recorded.
4. **MS readings recorded as printed**: "MS. presise" at the governor's approach (printed *preside*); "MS. deto to" and "MS. peno" at the second confession and the governor's reply (printed *deto*, *pero*). None is resolved silently.
5. **"The dust into lice"** (*la poluere im pullici*): the check reads "fleas"; the Italian word is *pullici* (lice) and is followed, the divergence recorded.
6. **The Moses–Joshua–Elijah catalogue is kept entire**, including the refrain "every one confesses that he is a dead man" / "that he is a man," and the closing doxology "almighty and merciful, who is blessed for ever."
7. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next manuscript rubric is "XCV" (folio 100a) — there is no chapter 94 in the edition; see the slice headnote and the ch. 95 notes.

---

## Chapter 95

*[Editorial heading: The catechism on the stone — "This is my faith." Manuscript rubric printed "XCVb." (no chapter 94 in the edition) at folio 100a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then the governor with the priest and the king begged Jesus that, to quiet the common people, he would go up into a high place and speak to the people. So Jesus went up upon one of the twelve stones that Joshua had the twelve tribes take up out of the midst of the Jordan, when all Israel passed over there dry-shod; and he said with a loud voice: "Let our priest go up into a high place, where he may confirm my words." So the priest went up there, and Jesus said to him plainly, so that every one could understand:

"It is written in the testament and covenant of the living God that our God has had no beginning, nor shall he ever have an end."

The priest answered: "So it is written therein."

Jesus said: "Is it written there that our God with his word alone has created all things?"

"So it is," said the priest.

Jesus said: "Is it written there that God is invisible, and hidden from the understanding of man, because he is incorporeal and uncompounded, without change?"

"So it is, truly," said the priest.

Jesus said: "Is it written there how the heaven of heavens cannot contain him, because our God is immeasurable?"

"So said Solomon the prophet," said the priest, "O Jesus."

Jesus said: "Is it written there that God has no need — for he does not eat, does not sleep, and suffers no want of any kind?"

"So it is," said the priest.

Jesus said: "Is it written there that our God is everywhere, and that there is no other God but he — who strikes down and heals, and does whatever pleases him?"

"So it is written," answered the priest.

Then Jesus, lifting up his hands, said: "Lord our God, this is my faith, with which I shall come to your judgment — in testimony against every one who shall believe the contrary." And turning to the people he said: "Repent! For from all that the priest has said is written in the book of Moses — the covenant of God for ever — you can perceive your sin: for I am a visible man, and a morsel of clay that walks upon the earth, mortal as other men are. I have had a beginning and I shall have an end — and I am such that I cannot create a fly anew."

Then the people lifted up their voices, weeping, and said: "We have sinned, Lord our God, against you — have mercy on us!" And every one of them begged Jesus to pray for the safety of the holy city, so that our God in his anger would not give it over to be trampled by the nations. So Jesus, lifting up his hands, prayed for the holy city and for the people of God, while every one cried out: "So be it — Amen."

### Notes — Chapter 95
1. **The numbering gap is real and recorded**: the printed edition jumps from chapter 93 to chapter 95. The manuscript rubrics skip XCIV (and, later, XCVI); the text itself is continuous — chapter 93 ends with the Elijah doxology at folio 99a and chapter 95 opens at folio 100a with *Pregorno adonque* ("Then ... begged"), which carries the narrative straight on. No lacuna is posited; the gap is in the numbering only, as printed.
2. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title notes read "The Chapter of the faithful" and "The Chapter of 'There is no god but God.'" Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is all-powerful and the compassionate"; "God the living"; "God is ancient"; "God endures"; "God creates" / "God created everything with one word"; "God is not perceived by the eyes"; "God is hidden"; "He has no body"; "God betrays not"; "God is great"; "God is rich"; "God is sovereign"; "I ask God's pardon"; "God is powerful." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. The English page running head ("CONFESSION OF JESUS," continued) is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their footnotes cite Ps. xc. 2; Ps. xxxiii. 6; 1 Kings viii. 27; Deut. xxxii. 39; Josh. iv. 8.
3. **The catechism is kept whole**: no-beginning-no-end; creation by the word alone; invisible, hidden, incorporeal, uncompounded, without change; the heaven of heavens cannot contain him; no need — eats not, sleeps not, suffers no want; everywhere, no other God but he, who strikes down and heals and does whatever pleases him. The priest's confirmations ("So it is written") are kept entire.
4. **"This is my faith" is translated exactly as written** — *quessta he la fede mia chon la quale ueniro al tuo iuditio, in tesstimonio chontra di ogniuno che chredera il chontrario* — and footnoted as the direct lead-in to the named seam at ch. 96–97 (next slice). Nothing is smoothed; no name is imported.
5. **"A morsel of clay"** (*uno pocho di fanggo*) and **"I cannot create a fly anew"** (*io nom posso chreare una mossca di nouo*) are kept literal, with the check. The people's weeping confession and the prayer for the holy city, closing "So be it — Amen," are kept entire.
6. **MS note recorded**: at the people's plea the printed apparatus notes "MS. che che (bis)" — the manuscript doubles the word; the printed text is followed.
7. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next manuscript rubric is "XCVII" (folio 102b) — the named seam zone (ch. 96–97) opens the next slice.

## Chapter 96

*[Editorial heading: "Art you the Messiah?" — "I am not he." No manuscript rubric in the Italian column; the number 96 is the English edition's editorial division (its head reads "XCVIf"). The heading above is editorial.]*

When the prayer was ended, the priest said with a loud voice: "Stay, Jesus — for we need to know who you are, for the quieting of our nation."

Jesus answered: "I am Jesus, son of Mary, of the seed of David — a man that is mortal and fears God, and I seek that to God be given honour and glory."

The priest answered: "In the book of Moses it is written that our God must send us the Messiah, who shall come to announce to us that which God wills, and shall bring to the world the mercy of God. I pray you, therefore, tell us the truth: art you the Messiah of God whom we expect?"

Jesus answered: "It is true that our God has so promised — but indeed I am not he, for he is made before me, and shall come after me."

The priest answered: "By your words and signs, at any rate, we believe you to be a prophet and an holy one of God. I pray you, therefore, in the name of all Judea and Israel, that you for love of God wouldst tell us in what wise the Messiah will come."

Jesus answered: "As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — I am not the Messiah whom all the tribes of the earth expect, even as God promised to our father Abraham, saying: 'In your seed will I bless all the tribes of the earth.' But when God shall take me away from the world, Satan will raise again this accursed sedition, by making the impious believe that I am God and son of God — whence my words and my doctrine shall be contaminated, insomuch that scarcely shall there remain thirty faithful ones. Whereupon God will have mercy upon the world, and will send his messenger, for whom he has made all things — who shall come from the south with power, and shall destroy the idols with the idolaters; for he shall take away the dominion from Satan which he has over men. He shall bring with him the mercy of God for the salvation of them that shall believe in him — and blessed is he who shall believe his words."

### Notes — Chapter 96
1. **The numbering gap is real and recorded**: the Italian column carries no rubric here — the text runs continuously from chapter 95's closing prayer (folio 101b, *Finita la oratione*) into this chapter with no break. The English column supplies the editorial head "XCVI" (printed "XCVIf," the "f" being the 1907 footnote letter for the Arabic gloss "The Chapter of the Evangelist" at "I am Jesus, son of Mary"). The next Italian rubric, "XCVII," opens chapter 97 at folio 102b. As with chapter 94, the gap is in the numbering only; no lacuna is posited.
2. **The seam, translated exactly as written**: "I am not he, for he is made before me, and shall come after me"; "I am not the Messiah whom all the tribes of the earth expect"; the messenger "for whom he hath made all things" who "shall come from the south with power" and destroy the idols; the impious made to believe "that I am God and son of God." Nothing is smoothed, softened, or deleted. The fork is footnoted per the audit: this contradicts the Qur'an's identification of the Messiah with Jesus, and Robert's recorded suspicion that the seam may be a later insertion is carried here rather than decided. (See SOURCE-AUDIT §3.)
3. **"He is made before me"** (*elgie fato auanti di me*) is kept literal — the Italian makes the coming one prior to Jesus in origin and later in arrival. The check reads the same.
4. **"Scarcely thirty faithful"** echoes the messenger-anticipation of ch. 72 (slice 15); the Italian is followed in both places.
5. **Apparatus as printed**: at the priest's question the editors render the scribe's Arabic mark on *messia* as "Prophet."; at *Viue DIG* ("As God liveth") the gloss reads "By the living God." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Their footnote cites Gen. xxii. 18 for the Abraham promise.
6. **MS note recorded**: at "blessed is he" the printed apparatus notes "1 bato (sic)" — the manuscript writes *bato* for *beato*; the printed text is followed.
7. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle (Italian + Spanish control for ch. 1–120); Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next manuscript rubric is "XCVII" (folio 102b).

---

## Chapter 97

*[Editorial heading: The name of the Messiah — "Wait, Mohammed." Manuscript rubric "XCVII" (Italian column) at folio 102b. The heading above is editorial. Subheadings below are editorial topic breaks, added at Robert's order 2026-10-11 to help following; they follow the Italian's own topic shifts, with the 1907 English paragraphing used as check only.]*

*["The rulers answer" — editorial subheading]*

"Unworthy though I am to untie his sandals, I have received grace and mercy from God to see him."

Then answered the priest, with the governor and the king, saying: "Distress not thyself, O Jesus, holy one of God — because in our time shall not this sedition be any more; for we will write to the sacred Roman senate in such wise that by imperial decree none shall any more call you God or son of God."

*["My consolation is the messenger's coming" — editorial subheading]*

Then said Jesus: "With your words I am not consoled — because where you hope for light, darkness shall come. But my consolation is in the coming of the messenger of God, who shall destroy every false opinion of me; and his faith shall spread and shall take hold of the whole world — for so has God promised to Abraham our father. And that which gives me consolation is that his faith shall have no end, but shall be kept inviolate by God."

*["No prophets after him but false ones" — editorial subheading]*

The priest answered: "After the coming of the messenger of God, shall other prophets come?"

Jesus answered: "There shall not come after him true prophets sent by God — but there shall come a great number of false prophets, whereat I sorrow. For Satan shall raise them up by the just judgment of God, and they shall hide themselves under the pretext of my gospel."

Herod answered: "How is it a just judgment of God that such impious men should come?"

Jesus answered: "It is just that he who will not believe the truth to his salvation should believe a lie to his damnation. Wherefore I say to you, that the world has ever despised the true prophets and loved the false — as can be seen in the time of Michaiah and Jeremiah. For every like loves his like."

*["The naming — 'Wait, Mohammed' — and the crowd's cry" — editorial subheading]*

Then said the priest: "How shall the Messiah be called, and what sign shall reveal his coming?"

Jesus answered: "The name of the Messiah is admirable — for God himself gave him the name when he had created his soul, and placed it in a celestial splendour. God said: 'Wait, Mohammed — For your sake I will create paradise, the world, and a great multitude of creatures, whereof I make you a present; insomuch that whoso shall bless you shall be blessed, and whoso shall curse you shall be accursed. When I shall send you into the world I shall send you as my messenger of salvation, and your word shall be true, insomuch that heaven and earth shall fail, but your faith shall never fail. Mohammed is his blessed name.'"

Then the crowd lifted up their voices, saying: "O God, send us your messenger! O Mohammed, come quickly for the salvation of the world!"

### Notes — Chapter 97
1. **The naming passage, translated exactly as written**: *DIG disse asspeta Machometo* ("God said: 'Wait, Mohammed'"); *Machometo he il suo nome benedeto* ("Mohammed is his blessed name"); the crowd's *ho machometo uieni pressto in sallute del monddo* ("O Mohammed, come quickly for the salvation of the world!"). The name stands where the Italian puts it and is not imported where the Italian does not. Nothing is smoothed. The fork is footnoted per the audit: this contradicts the Qur'an's identification of the Messiah with Jesus, and Robert's recorded suspicion that the seam may be a later insertion is carried here rather than decided. (See SOURCE-AUDIT §3.)
2. **Messiah / messenger terminology** (glossary cross-reference): the priest asks for *il messia*; Jesus denies being *il messia* and answers with the coming of *il nontio di DIG* (the messenger of God); the name given is Mohammed. The Italian's own terms are kept distinct throughout — *messia* is never rendered "messenger" nor *nontio* "messiah."
3. **"Sandals"** renders the Italian *chalziamenti* (foot-coverings; the check reads "hosen"); the sense is the coming one's footgear, and the 1907 notes cite the Baptist's parallel (Mark i. 7). The Italian word is followed, the check's turn recorded.
4. **Apparatus as printed — the MS marginal glosses (Arabic, as the 1907 notes render them)**: at the XCVII rubric, "The Chapter of Mohammed, the prophet of God"; at Jesus' reply, "Jesus said: 'The coming of the prophet of God is our delight, for he, when he comes into the world, will remove the false belief in us from the people of the world, and his religion will keep the whole world in obedience.'"; at *Machometo*, "In the Latin tongue 'laudabilis.'" ("worthy of praise" — matching the "admirable" name); at the naming question, a long parallel gloss: "Certain of the Jews came to Jesus asking the name of the prophet who will be sent in the last times, and Jesus said: 'God created the prophet at the end of time, and placed him in a lamp of light and called him Mohammed. He said: "O Mohammed, be patient, on your account (I have created) many creatures, and have bestowed all on you; and whoever is pleased with you I am pleased with him, and whoever hates you I am quit of him, and when you are sent your word will excel all words and your code shall last."'"; smaller marks glossed "Mohammed," "God loves and gives," "God creates," "The religion of the prophet of God is everlasting, for God Almighty shall preserve his religion," "God keeps," "The prophet of God, the seal of the prophets," "God's judgement is just," "And unto mankind," "Kind with kind (like with like)," "God sends," "The prophet of God," "O Mohammed." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. The margins of this chapter are thick with the Mohammed-name glosses — recorded as the manuscript's own furniture, per the audit's note that the glosses are by the same scribe.
5. **The false-prophets passage is kept whole**: no true prophets after him; a great number of false prophets raised by Satan by God's just judgment, hiding "under the pretext of my gospel"; Herod's question and the answer — "he who will not believe the truth to his salvation should believe a lie to his damnation"; Michaiah and Jeremiah (the 1907 note cites Jer. xxvi. 18, recorded as printed); "every like loveth his like."
6. **Printed readings recorded, none resolved silently**: *disstrug-era* (line-break hyphen, read "shall destroy"); *promesso mo ha abraham* (= *m'ha*, "hath promised to Abraham"); *he1* (= *ha* + footnote 1, "Cp. ? Jer. xxvi. 18."); *uocj* (= *uoci*, "voices"); *pressto* (= *presto*, "quickly"); an unmapped gloss mark "8" at *uenuta8* ("his coming"), recorded as printed.
7. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The English edition's "XCVIII" head stands at folio 104a — the loaves-and-fishes chapter opens the next slice — but no Italian-column rubric is recovered in the djvu OCR at this point; the Italian text runs on continuously ("E deto quessto si parti la turba..."). Visual check of the printed page pending (see the slice-21 G1 item).
8. **Topical split (Robert's order, 2026-10-11)**: chapter 97 is now broken by topic with short labeled editorial subheadings, following the Italian's own topic shifts; the 1907 English paragraphing was used as check only, never as wording base. No text altered, added, or removed in the split.

## Chapter 98

*[Editorial heading: The decree of the senate — the five thousand fed. English head "XCVIII a"; no Italian-column rubric recovered at folio 104a. The heading above is editorial.]*

And having said this, the crowd departed — with the priest, and the governor with Herod — holding great discourses concerning Jesus and concerning his doctrine. Whereupon the priest prayed the governor to write the whole matter to Rome, to the senate; which thing the governor did. Wherefore the senate, having compassion on Israel, decreed that, on pain of death, no one should call Jesus the Nazarene, prophet of the Jews, either God or son of God. Which decree was posted up in the temple, engraved upon copper.

When the greater part of the crowd had departed, there remained about five thousand men, without the women and children — who, wearied by the journey, having been two days without bread (for through desire to see Jesus they had forgotten to bring any), whereupon they ate raw herbs — therefore they could not depart like the others. Then Jesus, when he perceived this, had pity on them, and said to Philip: "Where shall we find bread for these, that they perish not of hunger?"

Philip answered: "Lord, two hundred gold denarii could not buy so much bread that each one should taste even a little."

Then said Andrew: "There is here a child that has five loaves and two fishes — but what will it be among so many?"

Jesus answered: "Make the crowd sit down." And they sat down upon the grass, by fifties and by forties. Thereupon said Jesus: "In the name of God." And he took the bread, and prayed to God, and then broke the bread, which he gave to the disciples, and the disciples gave it to the crowd; and so he did with the fishes. Every one ate, and every one was satisfied.

Then said Jesus: "Gather up that which is left over." So the disciples gathered those fragments, and filled twelve baskets. Whereupon every one touched his eyes with his hands, saying: "Do I see, or do I dream?" And they remained, every one, for the space of an hour, as it were beside themselves, by reason of the great miracle. Afterwards Jesus, when he had rendered thanks to God, took leave of them — but seventy-two men would not leave him. Wherefore Jesus, perceiving their faith, chose them as disciples.

### Notes — Chapter 98
1. **Boundaries**: English head "XCVIII a"; Italian 104a–104b (the 104b folio break falls mid-chapter at *chomprare tanto pane*), ending "...li ellese in dissepoli." ("chose them as disciples"). No Italian-column rubric recovered at 104a in the djvu text; flagged for G1 visual check.
2. **"Two hundred gold denarii"** renders *ducento danari di horo*; the check reads "two hundred pieces of gold." The Italian is followed.
3. **"What will it be among so many"** — the printed *"f ra tanti"* is read as *fra tanti* (line-break/OCR split), as the check reads it.
4. **"In the name of God"** renders the Italian *in nome di DIG* as written; the scribe's Arabic marginal mark here is rendered by the 1907 notes as "By permission of God" — recorded as the edition's apparatus, not followed as text.
5. **"Wearied by the journey"** renders *lassi per il uiaggio*; the check agrees.
6. **The decree, kept as written**: "on pain of death" (*ha pena della uita*); "posted up in the temple, engraved upon copper" (*possto nel tempio in scritura di rame*); none is to call Jesus "either God or son of God."
7. **"Seventy-two men"** (*setanta dui homeni*) kept as written; the 1907 note cites Luke x. 1, recorded as printed.
8. **Apparatus as printed**: at the XCVIII head the editors render the scribe's Arabic gloss as "The Chapter of food." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
9. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle (Italian + Spanish control for ch. 1–120); Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only).

## Chapter 99

*[Editorial heading: God is jealous for his honour. Manuscript rubric "XCIX»." (Italian column) at folio 105a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus, having withdrawn into a hollow place of the desert, in Tiro, near the Jordan, called together the seventy-two with the twelve; and, having seated himself upon a stone, he made them sit near him. And he opened his mouth with a sigh, and said: "Today we have seen a great wickedness in Judea and in Israel — and such a one that my heart still trembles in my breast for fear of God. Truly I say to you, that God is jealous for his honour, and that, as a lover, he loves Israel. You know that when a young man loves a lady, and she loves not him but another, he is moved to anger and kills his rival. Even so, I tell you, does God: for when Israel has loved anything by reason whereof he forgets God, God has brought such thing to nought."

"Now what thing is more dear to God here on earth than the priesthood and the holy temple? Nevertheless, in the time of Jeremiah the prophet, when the people had forgotten God and boasted only of the temple — for there was none like it in all the world — God raised up his wrath by Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, and with an army caused him to take the holy city and burn it with the sacred temple; insomuch that the sacred things, which the prophets of God trembled to touch, were trodden under foot by infidels full of wickedness."

"Abraham loved his son Ishmael a little more than was fitting; wherefore God commanded, to kill that evil love out of the heart of Abraham, that he should slay his son — which he would have done, had the knife cut.

"David loved Absalom vehemently; and therefore God brought it to pass that the son rebelled against his father, and was hanged by his hair and slain by Joab. O fearful judgment of God — that Absalom loved his hair above all things, and it was turned into a rope to hang him!

"As for innocent Job — he came near to loving his seven sons and his three daughters overmuch, when God gave him into the hand of Satan, who not only deprived him of his sons and his riches in one day, but smote him also with grievous sickness; insomuch that for seven continuous years worms came out of his flesh.

"Our father Jacob loved Joseph more than his other sons; wherefore God caused him to be sold, and caused Jacob to be deceived by these same sons — insomuch that he believed that the beasts had devoured his son, and so abode ten years in weeping."

### Notes — Chapter 99
1. **Rubric confirmed**: "XCIX»." in the Italian column at folio 105a; chapter runs 105a–106a, ending "...stete dieci hanni chom pianto." ("abode ten years in weeping"). The next folio marker (106b) falls inside chapter 100, not at this chapter's end.
2. **"A hollow place of the desert, in Tiro"** renders *im parte del disserto chaua in tiro apresso il giordano*; the 1907 note flags "Text obscure" at this point — recorded, and the check's reading ("a hollow part of the desert in Tiro") is followed with that flag carried. The Italian *chaua* ("hollow") stands in the print; the Spanish witness reads "una parte de desierto que avía en Tyro" ("a part of desert that was in Tyro") with no "hollow" equivalent. The editor (after Cirillo-Frémaux) argues "in tiro"/"en Tyro" renders Latin "ad tyram" ("alongside"), not the city Tyre. The Italian is followed as printed; the fork is recorded here, not resolved.
3. **MS notes recorded as printed, none resolved silently**: at *sentatosi* the note reads "MS. sentato"; at *he che* the note reads "MS. &e."; at *uno pocho* the note reads "MS. uno pocho umpocho (sic)." The printed text is followed throughout.
4. **"Seven sons"** — the printed Italian reads *sette noli*; the check reads "seven sons." The printed form is recorded here; "sons" is kept in the rendering.
5. **Figures kept as written**: seven continuous years of Job's affliction; ten years of Jacob's weeping; the knife that did not cut; Absalom hanged by his hair.
6. **Ishmael named as written** ("il suo filgiolo issmaelle"), consistent with ch. 13/15/17 — translated exactly as it stands, never smoothed.
7. **Apparatus as printed — the scribe's Arabic marginal glosses as the 1907 notes render them**: at the rubric, "Chapter of the jealousy of God"; at "as a lover he loveth Israel," "God is jealous and loving"; at "such thing," "God is victorious"; at Abraham's name, "Account of the sacrifice of Ishmael"; at the Job paragraph, "Account of the history of Job"; at the Joseph paragraph, "Account of the history of Joseph." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
8. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only).

---

## Chapter 100

*[Editorial heading: The seventy-two sent out — the lost purse. No Italian-column rubric at folio 106b; the English column's head is recovered in the djvu OCR only as "CX" (the edition's chapter-100 head, gloss letter misread — flagged for G1 visual check). The heading above is editorial.]*

"As God lives, brethren — I fear lest God be angered against me. Therefore it is necessary that you go through Judea and Israel, preaching to the twelve tribes of Israel the truth, that they may be undeceived."

The disciples answered with fear, weeping: "We will do whatsoever you shall bid us."

Then said Jesus: "Let us make prayer and fasting for three days; and from henceforth, every evening when the first star shall appear — when prayer is made to God — we will make prayer three times, asking three times for mercy; because the sin of Israel is three times more grievous than other sins."

"So be it," answered the disciples.

When the third day was ended, on the morning of the fourth day Jesus called together all the disciples and apostles, and said to them: "Suffice it that Barnabas and John abide with me; do you others go through all the region of Samaria and Judea and Israel, preaching penitence — because the axe is laid nigh to the tree, to cut it down. And make prayer over the sick, because God has given me authority over every sickness."

Then said he who writes: "O Master, if your disciples be asked of the manner in which they ought to show penitence, what shall they answer?"

Jesus answered: "When a man loses a purse, does he turn back only his eye, to see it? Or his hand, to take it? Or his tongue, to ask? No, assuredly — but he turns back his whole body, and employs every power of his soul to find it. Is this true?"

He who writes answered: "It is most true."

### Notes — Chapter 100
1. **Boundaries**: Italian 106a–107a, from *Viue DIG...* ("As God liveth...") through *...helgie uerissimo.* ("It is most true," end of the purse parable). The chapter opens on 106a with no folio marker at its opening; the 106b marker falls mid-chapter at *Finite il terzo... giorno*. No Italian-column rubric; the English head is recovered only as "CX" in the OCR — flagged for G1 visual check against the printed page. The division follows the edition's chaptering (the next chapter opens with the Italian rubric "CI b.").
2. **Apparatus as printed — the scribe's Arabic marginal glosses as the 1907 notes render them**: at *Viue DIG*, "By the living God, God the victorious"; at "when prayer is made to God" (the first-star evening prayer), "The Chapter of the evening prayer"; at "God hath given me authority," "God gives"; at the writer's question, "Setting forth (showing) of repentance." A further mark printed as "f" after *Viue DIG* is unmapped in the recovered text and recorded as printed.
3. **"Barnabas and John abide with me"** — *bassti che con me ressti barnaba he ioane*; the rest go through Samaria, Judea, and Israel. The writer names himself Barnabas, as before.
4. **The purse parable kept whole**: eye, hand, tongue — then the whole body and every power of the soul.
5. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only).

---

## Chapter 101

*[Editorial heading: Penitence is a reversing of the evil life. Manuscript rubric "CI b." (Italian column) at folio 107a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then said Jesus: "Penitence is a reversing of the evil life; for every sense must be turned to the contrary of that which it wrought while sinning. For instead of delight must be put sorrow; for laughter, weeping; for revellings, fasts; for sleep, vigils; for idleness, activity; for lust, chastity. Let story-telling be turned into prayer, and avarice into almsgiving."

He who writes answered: "But if they be asked: How ought we to sorrow? How ought we to weep? How ought we to fast? How ought we to be active? How ought we to remain chaste? How ought we to make prayer and give alms? — what shall they answer? And how shall they do true penitence, if they know not how to repent?"

Jesus answered: "Well have you asked, O Barnabas — and I wish to satisfy you fully, if it please God. So today I will speak to you of penitence generally; and that which I say to one, I say to all."

"Know then that penitence, more than anything else, must be done for pure love of God; otherwise it will be vain to repent. For I will speak to you by a similitude: every building, if its foundation be removed, falls into ruin. Is this true?"

"It is true," answered the disciples.

Then said Jesus: "The foundation of our salvation is God, without whom there is no salvation. When man has sinned, he has lost the foundation of his salvation; so it is necessary to begin from the foundation."

"Tell me: if your servants had offended you, and you knew that they grieved not at having offended you, but grieved at having lost their reward — would you forgive them? No, assuredly. Even so, I tell you, will God do to those who repent for having lost paradise."

"Satan, the enemy of all good, has great remorse at having lost paradise and gained hell. But yet he will never find mercy. And know you why? Because he has no love of God at all — nay, he hates his Creator."

### Notes — Chapter 101
1. **Rubric confirmed**: "CI b." in the Italian column at folio 107a; chapter runs 107a–108a, ending *...anzi hodia il suo chreatore.* ("nay, he hateth his Creator" — Satan's remorse).
2. **The 101/102 division**: no Italian rubric recovered at 108a; the English head is recovered in the djvu OCR only as a garbled "PJT a" (the edition's chapter-102 head, "CII a.", misread). The division is placed per the edition's own chaptering, confirmed against an independent transcription of the Ragg English (check material only, not base) — ch. 101 ends with Satan's remorse, ch. 102 opens "Verily I say unto you, that every animal after its own nature..." Flagged for G1 visual check against the printed page.
3. **Printed readings recorded, none resolved silently**: at the "CI b." head, the gloss letter "b" is rendered by the 1907 notes as "The Chapter of repentance"; at *pentirsi* the marker is printed as "0," with the editors' note "How can he repent who knows not repentance?"; at *piazendo ha DIG* the gloss mark "d" is rendered "If it please God"; at *Dioe* the gloss mark "e" is rendered "God is peace."
4. **The reversal catalogue kept entire**: delight→sorrow, laughter→weeping, revellings→fasts, sleep→vigils, idleness→activity, lust→chastity, story-telling→prayer, avarice→almsgiving.
5. **"If it please God"** (*piazendo ha DIG*) — the motive clause for the whole penitence teaching is kept as written.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only).

## Chapter 102

*[Editorial heading: The sinner's weeping — the king and the adopted slave. No Italian-column rubric at folio 108a; division per the edition's chaptering (see ch. 101, note 2). The heading above is editorial.]*

"Truly I say to you, that every animal, according to its nature, when it loses that which it desires, mourns for the lost good. The sinner, then, who wills to do true penitence, must have great desire to punish in himself that which he has wrought against his Creator."

"In such wise that, when he prays, he dare not ask of God paradise, nor that God free him from hell; but, in confusion of mind, prostrate before God, let him say in his prayer: 'Behold the guilty one, O Lord, who has offended you without any cause, at the very time when he ought to have served you. Wherefore here he seeketh that what he has done be punished by your hand, and not by the hand of Satan, your enemy — in order that the ungodly may not rejoice over your creatures. Chastise, punish as it pleases you, Lord; for you will never give me so much torment as this wicked one deserves.'

"Wherefore the sinner, holding to this manner, will find the more mercy with God in proportion as he craves justice."

"Assuredly it is an abominable sacrilege, the laughter of the sinner; for this world is rightly called by our father David a vale of tears."

"There was a king who adopted as son one of his slaves, whom he made lord of all that he possessed. Now it chanced that by the deceit of a wicked man the wretched one fell into the displeasure of the king, so that he suffered great miseries — both in his living and in being despised, and being robbed of all that he won each day by working. Think you that such a man would laugh for any time?"

"No, assuredly," answered the disciples, "for if the king had known it he would have caused him to be slain, seeing him laugh at the king's displeasure. But it is believable that day and night he would weep."

Then Jesus wept, saying: "Woe to the world, for it is sure of eternal torment. O wretched man — for our God had chosen you almost as a son, giving you paradise — whereupon you, O wretched one, by the operation of Satan didst fall into the displeasure of God, and wast cast out of paradise and condemned to the unclean world, where you receive everything with toil, and every good work is stolen from you by continual sinning. And yet the world laughs; and, what is worse, he that is the greatest sinner laughs more than the others. It shall be, therefore, as you have said: God will give the sentence of eternal death upon the sinner who laughs at his sins and weeps not for them."

### Notes — Chapter 102
1. **Boundaries**: Italian 108a–109a, from *lo ui dicho in uerita che ogni animalle...* ("Verily I say unto you, that every animal...") through *...no piange li suoi pechati.* ("weepeth not for them"). No Italian-column rubric; the English head is recovered in the djvu OCR only as a garbled "PJT a" (see ch. 101, note 2). The next rubric, "CIII b.," is confirmed at folio 109b. Division per the edition's chaptering; flagged for G1 visual check.
2. **Printed readings recorded**: at the chapter head (English "CII a.", recovered only as garbled "PJT a"), the gloss letter "a" is rendered by the 1907 notes as "The Chapter of grief in penitence"; at *Dio* the gloss mark "b" is rendered by the 1907 notes as "God the compassionate"; at *dicendo* the mark "*" carries the editors' note "Great lamentation (?)" — recorded as printed. The editors' footnote at the vale-of-tears line cites Ps. lxxxiv. 6 — recorded as printed.
3. **The guilty one's prayer kept whole**: no asking for paradise, no asking to be freed from hell — punishment by God's hand, not Satan's, lest the ungodly rejoice; "thou wilt never give me so much torment as this wicked one deserveth."
4. **The king parable kept whole**: the adopted slave, the deceit, the miseries, the disciples' answer — day and night he would weep.
5. **"Woe to the world"** — Jesus weeping; the sentence of eternal death upon the sinner who laughs at his sins and weeps not for them — kept without softening.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only).
