## Chapter 103

*[Editorial heading: The sinner's weeping — "as a father weeping for his son." Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CIII b.") at folio 109b. The heading above is editorial.]*

The weeping of the sinner ought to be like a father weeping for his son at the point of death. O madman, you weep for the body from which the soul has departed, and you do not weep for the soul from which, through sin, the mercy of God has departed.

Tell me — if a sailor, after his ship was wrecked in a storm, could get back by weeping all that he had lost, what would he do? It is certain he would weep bitterly. But I tell you truly: in everything a man weeps for, he sins — except when he weeps for his sin. For every hardship that comes to man comes to him from God for his salvation, and so he ought to rejoice in it. But sin comes from the devil for man's damnation, and at that man does not grieve. Surely here you can see that man seeks loss and not gain.

Said Bartholomew: "Lord, what shall he do who cannot weep, because his heart is a stranger to weeping?"

Jesus answered: "Not all who shed tears are weeping, O Bartholomew. As God lives, there are men from whose eyes no tear ever fell, and they have wept more than a thousand tear-shedders. The weeping of the sinner is a burning-away of earthly love by the force of sorrow — so that, just as the sun keeps from rotting whatever is laid out in it, so this burning-away keeps the soul from sin. If God gave the true penitent as many tears as the sea has water, he would wish for far more — and so this longing burns up the little drop of moisture that would come out, the way a blazing furnace burns up a drop of water. But those who burst into weeping easily are like the horse that goes faster the lighter his load."

### Notes — Chapter 103
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of weeping in repentance." The English page running head ("OF MOURNING FOR SIN") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The gloss letter "c" at *Viue DIO* is rendered by the 1907 notes as "By the living God"; the gloss mark at *Dio* (*se Dio donassi*) stands as printed. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
2. **"O madman"** renders *ho homo pazo*; **"a burning-away of earthly love"** renders *uno chonsumamento di hafeto terreno* ("a consumption of earthly affection") — kept literal, with the check's sense.
3. **"Whatever is laid out in it"** (*quella chossa che sopra si pone*): the check's sense ("what is placed uppermost," i.e. set out in the sun) is followed; flagged as the editors' reading, not independently verified.
4. **"A thousand tear-shedders"** keeps the Italian's *piu di mille di quelli che lachrimano* whole — men who never shed a tear yet wept more.
5. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next division, chapter 104, opens at folio 110a.

## Chapter 104

*[Editorial heading: Weep for sin alone — the mantle, the hand of God, and the sense of the words. No rubric recovered in the OCR for "CIV"; division per the edition's chaptering, verified by narrative continuity (the edition's English chapter 104 opens with the same "Jeremiah" passage). The heading above is editorial.]*

Truly, there are men who have the inward feeling and the outward tears too — but he who is like that will prove a Jeremiah. In weeping, God measures the sorrow more than the tears.

Then John said: "Master, how does a man lose by weeping for things other than sin?"

Jesus answered: "If Herod gave you a cloak to keep for him, and afterward took it back from you, would you have reason to weep?"

"No," said John.

Then Jesus said: "Then man has even less reason to weep when he loses something, or does not have what he wants. For everything comes from the hand of God. Shall God, then, not have power to do what he pleases with his own things, O foolish man? For you have nothing of your own but sin — and for that you ought to weep, and for nothing else."

Matthew said: "Master, you confessed before all Judea that God has no likeness to man — and now you say that man receives from the hand of God. Then since God has hands, he has likeness with man."

Jesus answered: "You are in error, Matthew, and many have erred in this way, not knowing the sense of the words. For a man ought to consider not the outward shell of the words but the sense — for human speech is like an interpreter between us and God. Do you not know that when God willed to speak to our fathers on Mount Sinai, our fathers cried out: 'Speak to us yourself, Moses, and let not God speak to us, lest we die'? And what did God say through Isaiah the prophet, except that as far as heaven is from earth, so far are God's ways from men's ways, and God's thoughts from men's thoughts?"

### Notes — Chapter 104
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of loss in weeping." The English page running head ("OF MOURNING FOR SIN," continued) is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The gloss letter "c" at *dalla mano di DIG* is rendered "Every thing is from God"; the gloss letter "d" at *delle chosse sui* is rendered "God to whom be praise, God is possessor, every thing is from God" — recorded as printed. The editors' footnote cites Lam. i. 12, &c. — recorded as printed. No canonical parallel is asserted; the editors' citations above are recorded as printed only. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
2. **"Will prove a Jeremiah"** renders *uno hieremia trouerasi* ("will be found a Jeremiah") — kept as written.
3. **"The outward shell of the words"** renders *lo essterno* (the outward part) against *il sensso* (the sense) — the chapter's load-bearing distinction, kept whole.
4. **Matthew's objection and the Sinai/Isaiah answer are kept entire**, exactly as the Italian sets them — no harmonizing comment added.
5. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next division, chapter 105, opens mid-folio 111a at *DIG he tanto inmenso*.

---

## Chapter 105

*[Editorial heading: The immensity of God — nine heavens, and the three things in man. No rubric recovered in the OCR for "CV"; division per the edition's chaptering, verified by narrative continuity (the edition's English chapter 105 opens with "God is so immeasurable" and closes with the "Amen"). The heading above is editorial.]*

God is so immeasurable that I tremble to tell of him. But I must set a reckoning before you. I tell you, then, that the heavens are nine, and they stand as far from one another as the first heaven stands from the earth — and it stands five hundred years' journey from the earth. So the earth stands four thousand five hundred years' journey from the highest heaven. I tell you, then, that the earth stands to the first heaven as the point of a needle — and the first heaven stands to the second in the same way, as a point — and so all the heavens are each smaller than the next. But all the bigness of the earth, together with all the heavens, stands to paradise as a point — no, as a grain of sand. Is this greatness beyond measure?

The disciples answered: "Yes, surely."

Then Jesus said: "As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — everything before God is as small as a grain of sand, and God is as many times greater as the grains of sand it would take to fill all the heavens and paradise — and more. Now think: does God have any proportion with man, who is a little lump of clay standing on the earth? Take care, then, to take the sense and not the bare words, if you want to have eternal life."

The disciples answered: "Only God can know himself — and truly it is as Isaiah the prophet said: he is hidden from human senses."

Jesus answered: "It is true. And so, when we are in paradise, we shall know God the way we here know the sea from a drop of salt water. Returning to my talk, I tell you that only for sin ought one to weep — because by sinning, man forsakes God his Creator. But how shall he weep who sits at drinking-bouts and feasts? He will weep the way ice gives fire! You must turn your feasts into fasts if you want to rule your senses — for that is how our God rules over them."

Thaddaeus said: "Then does God have sense to rule over?"

Jesus answered: "So you go back to saying, 'God has this, God is that.' Tell me — does man have sense?"

"Yes," answered the disciples.

Jesus said: "Can you find a man who has life in him, and yet sense does not work in him?"

"No," said the disciples.

"You deceive yourselves," said Jesus, "for he who is blind, deaf and dumb, and crippled — where is his sense? And when a man lies in a swoon?"

Then the disciples were perplexed — until Jesus said: "Three things make up man: the soul, the sense, and the flesh — each separate in itself. Our God created the soul and the body — that you have heard — but you have not yet heard how he created the sense. So tomorrow, God willing, I will tell you everything."

And having said this, Jesus gave thanks to God and prayed for the safety of our people, every one of us saying: "Amen."

### Notes — Chapter 105
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the excellency of God." The English page running head ("OF THE IMMENSITY OF GOD") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Gloss letters as printed: "a" at *DIG* ("God to whom be praise"), "c" at *Viue DIG* ("By the living God"), "d" at *grano di arena* ("God is greatest"), "e" at *asscoxo* ("God is hidden") — recorded as printed. The editors' footnotes cite Exod. xx. 19; Isa. lv. 9; Isa. xlv. 15; and note the 500-years'-journey tradition as found in Jerus. Talmud, Ber. ix — all recorded as printed. Canonical parallels are asserted: Exod. xx. 19 (the fathers at Sinai — 'let not God speak to us, lest we die'); Isa. lv. 8–9 (God's ways and thoughts far from men's, as heaven from earth); and Isa. xlv. 15 (God hidden — 'he is hidden from human senses'). The editors' remaining citation above, the 500-years'-journey tradition (Jerus. Talmud, Ber. ix), stands as printed record only. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
2. **Numbers kept entire**: nine heavens; five hundred years' journey from earth to the first heaven; four thousand five hundred years to the highest; the point of a needle; the grain of sand — none normalized.
3. **"For that is how our God rules over them"** renders *perche chosi lo signoregio DIO nosstro* — the antecedent of *lo* (the senses) is the editors' reading and is flagged as uncertain rather than smoothed.
4. **"Take the sense and not the bare words"** (*pilgiare il senso he non le parolle nude*) repeats chapter 104's load-bearing distinction — kept in the same wording.
5. **"Tomorrow, God willing, I will tell you everything"** is the bridge into chapter 106's soul-and-sense discourse — kept as written.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "CVIc.," is confirmed in the OCR.

## Chapter 106

*[Editorial heading: The soul and the sense. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CVIc.") confirmed in the OCR; the Italian-column rubric line was dropped by the OCR. Italian folios 112b–113b. The heading above is editorial.]*

When the dawn prayer was done, Jesus sat down under a palm tree, and his disciples drew near to him there. Then Jesus said: "As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — many are deceived about our life. For the soul and the sense and the flesh are joined so closely that most men say the soul and the sense are one and the same thing — dividing it by what it does and not by what it is, calling it the sensing soul, the growing soul, and the thinking soul. But I tell you truly: the soul is one — it thinks and it lives. O fools, where will they find a thinking soul without life? Never, surely. But life without senses is found well enough — as will be seen in the half-alive man when his sense leaves him."

Thaddaeus answered: "Master, when the sense leaves the life, the man has no life."

Jesus answered: "That is not true — for man is deprived of life when the soul departs, because the soul does not come back into the body again except by a miracle. But the sense leaves because of the fear it feels, or because of the great sorrow the soul has. For God created the sense for pleasure, and on that alone it lives — just as the body lives on food and the soul lives on knowledge and love. This sense is now in rebellion against the soul, angry at being deprived of the pleasure of paradise through sin. So there is the greatest need to feed it with spiritual pleasure, for whoever does not want it to live on fleshly pleasure. Do you understand? I tell you truly: God, having created it, condemned it to hell and to unbearable snow and ice — because it said it was God. But when he took away its nourishment, depriving it of its food, it confessed it was God's slave and the work of his hands. And now tell me: in the wicked, how does the sense work? Surely it is as God in them — for they follow the sense, forsaking reason and God's law. And so they turn out abominable, doing no good at all."

### Notes — Chapter 106
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the soul." The English page running head ("THE SOUL AND THE SENSE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Gloss letters as printed: "d" at *Viue DIG* ("By the living God"); "a" and "b" interleaved at *il senso DIG chreato* ("God the Creator," per the 1907 notes — the OCR is garbled here and the letters are recorded as printed, not resolved). The editors' note on the sensing/growing/thinking soul identifies it as the Aristotelian doctrine (Cp. Nic. Eth. i. 13) — recorded as printed. No canonical parallel is asserted; the editors' citations above are recorded as printed only. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
2. **"For God created the sense for pleasure"** renders *perche il senso DIG chreato de dilleto* — the gloss letters interrupt the line in print; the reading follows the editors' sense and is flagged.
3. **Speaker attribution**: the bare *Risspose* ("answered") after *quando il senso lo abanddona* introduces Thaddaeus's reply per narrative continuity — flagged, not silently smoothed.
4. **"It is as God in them"** (*elgie chome DIO in loro*) — translated exactly as written, never softened; this is the chapter's distinctive saying and is kept whole with its reason: they follow the sense, forsaking reason and God's law.
5. **The soul's oneness is kept entire**: one soul that thinks and lives — against the threefold division by operation; the half-alive (*semiuiuo*) man kept as written.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "CVIIc.," is confirmed at folio 114a ("CVII o." also appears in the English-stream OCR and is recorded).

## Chapter 107

*[Editorial heading: Fasting follows sorrow for sin. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CVIIc.") at folio 114a. The heading above is editorial.]*

So the first thing that follows sorrow for sin is fasting. For he who sees that a certain food makes him sick — because he fears death — after sorrowing that he ate it, gives it up so as not to get sick. So the sinner ought to do: seeing that pleasure made him sin against God his Creator by following the sense in these good things of the world, let him sorrow for having done so — because it deprives him of God, his life, and gives him the eternal death of hell. But because man, while he lives, needs to take these good things of the world, fasting is needful here. So let him get to work taming the sense and getting to know God as his Lord. And when he sees the sense hating fasts, let him set before it the state of hell, where no pleasure at all is had but endless sorrow — and let him set before it the delights of paradise, which are so great that one grain of one of paradise's delights is better than all the delights of the world. For then it will be quieted easily — because it is better to be content with little to receive much, than to run wild in little and be deprived of everything, abiding in torment. You must remember the rich feaster, to fast well: for he, wanting every day to feast deliciously here on earth, was deprived forever of a drop of water — while Lazarus, content with crumbs here on earth, shall live forever in the fullest delights of paradise. But let the penitent be on his guard — for Satan tries to wipe out every good work, and more in the penitent than in others, because the penitent has rebelled against him, and from his trusted slave has turned into his rebellious enemy. And so Satan will try to keep him from fasting in any way, on the pretext of sickness — and when that does not work, he will invite him to an extreme fast, so that he falls sick and afterward lives deliciously. And if he does not manage that, he will try to make him set his fast only on bodily food — so that he becomes like Satan himself, who never eats and always sins. As God lives, it is abominable to starve the body of food and fill the soul with pride — despising those who do not fast, and holding oneself better than they. Tell me: will the sick man boast of the diet the doctor makes him keep, and call mad those who are not on a diet? Surely not. But he will sorrow for the sickness that makes the diet needful for him. So I tell you: the penitent ought not to boast of his fasting and despise those who do not fast — but he ought to sorrow for the sin that makes him fast. Nor should the fasting penitent go after dainty foods — let him be content with coarse foods. Now — would a man give dainty food to the dog that bites and the horse that kicks? Surely not — but rather the opposite. And let this be enough for you about fasting.

### Notes — Chapter 107
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note is garbled in the OCR ("The Chapter of" — the title word unrecovered; likely "of fasting," recorded as unrecovered rather than supplied). The English page running head ("OF PENITENCE AND FASTING") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Gloss letters as printed: "d" at *DIO chreatore* and "e" at *DIO uita sua* (rendered "God creates" in the 1907 notes); "f" at *DIO suo signore* (1907 note garbled in OCR); "a" at *Viue Dio* ("By the living God") — recorded as printed. The editors' footnote at the rich feaster cites "above, 23 (p. 50)" — recorded as printed. A canonical parallel is asserted: Luke xvi. 19–31 (the rich man and Lazarus — the daily feasting, the crumbs, and the drop of water refused). The editors' cross-reference above ('above, 23 (p. 50)') stands as printed record only. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
2. **Uncertain reading flagged**: *onde inzio satana* — *inzio* is uncertain in print (possibly "invidioso"); rendered "And so Satan" and flagged, not resolved.
3. **"Let him get to work"** renders *uengi ha mortiffichare* (*uenghi a mortificare* — let him set about mortifying); flagged as the reading, not independently verified.
4. **The rich feaster and Lazarus kept whole**: the drop of water forever denied; the crumbs; the fullest delights of paradise — no softening.
5. **Satan's three moves kept entire**: no fasting at all (pretext of sickness); the extreme fast (sickness, then delicious living); the merely bodily fast (like Satan, who never eats and always sins).
6. **The sick-man and the dog-and-horse sayings kept whole**, exactly as the Italian sets them — including the closing "let this be enough for you about fasting."
7. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "CVIII*. U5a" (asterisk as printed), is confirmed at folio 115a, opening the watching/vigil discourse — outside this slice.

## Chapter 108

*[Editorial heading: Watching — the two sleeps. English rubric "CVIII*" recovered at folio 115a (printed "U5a"); the Italian-column rubric line is not recovered in the djvu OCR — the chapter opens with "Pero asscoltate che io ue diro del uigillare." The heading above is editorial.]*

But hearken to what I shall tell you concerning watching. For just as there are two kinds of sleep — that of the body and that of the soul — so must you be careful in watching, in such wise that while the body watches the soul sleep not. For this would be a most grievous error. Tell me, in a parable: there is a man who while walking strikes himself against a stone, and, so as not to strike it more with his foot, strikes it with his head. What is the state of such a man? The disciples answered: Miserable — for such a man is frenzied. Then said Jesus: Well have you answered, for truly I say to you that he who watches with the body and sleeps with the soul is frenzied. And it is by so much the harder to cure, as the spiritual sickness is more grievous than the bodily. Wherefore shall such a wretch boast of not sleeping with the body, which is the foot of the life, while he perceives not his misery — that he sleeps with the soul, which is the head of the life? The sleep of the soul is the forgetfulness of God and of his fearful judgement. Wherefore the soul that watches is that which in everything and in every place perceives God, and in everything and through everything and above everything gives thanks to his majesty — knowing that always, at every moment, it receiveth grace and mercy from God. Wherefore, in fear of his majesty, there ever sounds in its ear that angelic voice: "Creatures, come to judgement, for your Creator wills to judge you." Therefore it abides continually, ever, in the service of God. Tell me: what do you desire more to see by — the light of a star, or the light of the sun? Andrew answered: By the light of the sun, O master — for by the light of the star we cannot see the neighbouring mountains, and by the light of the sun we see the tiniest grain of sand. Wherefore with fear we walk by the light of the star, but by the light of the sun we go securely.

### Notes — Chapter 108
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of sleep." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "Whoever serves God with his body, without sleeping, must not let his soul sleep with his body"; "God is wise"; "God is guidance and the gracious one." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. The English page running heads ("OF SLEEPING AND WATCHING") are the printers' heads, not manuscript titles.
2. **"The foot of the life" / "the head of the life"** (*piede della uitta* / *chapo della uita*) are kept literal, with the check. The body–foot / soul–head pairing is the Italian's own and is not smoothed.
3. **"That angelic voice"** (*quella uoce angellicha*, "Creatures, come to judgement, for your Creator willeth to judge you") The saying is kept whole. No canonical parallel is asserted; the words are this book's own, recurring from the trumpet call of chapter 54.
4. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle (Italian + Spanish control for ch. 1–120); Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). No rubric line for chapter 109 is recovered in the OCR; the break below is placed where the next chapter-title note falls (see ch. 109, note 1).

## Chapter 109

*[Editorial heading: The neglectful — "How shall we keep God in memory?" No rubric line for this chapter is recovered in the djvu OCR in either column. The break is placed by narrative continuity where the English check's chapter-title note "The Chapter of the neglectful" falls (its p. 251 footnotes): at "Risspose iessu. hora chosi ui dicho..." (folio 116*), Jesus' drawing of the moral from Andrew's star-and-sun answer. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus answered: Even so I tell you that you ought to watch with the soul by the sun of justice, which is our God, and not boast yourselves of the watchings of the body. It is most true, indeed, that bodily sleep is to be fled from as much as possible — but in everything it is impossible, for the sense and the flesh are weighed down with foods, and the mind with business. Wherefore let him that would sleep little flee too much business and too much eating. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — it is lawful to sleep somewhat every night, but it is never lawful to forget God and his fearful judgement; for the sleep of the soul is such oblivion. Then answered he who writes: O master, how shall we be able to have God always in memory? Assuredly it seems to us impossible. Jesus said with a sigh: This is the greatest misery that man can suffer, O Barnabas — that man cannot here on earth have God his creator always in memory, save they that are holy. For they have God always in memory, because they have in them the light of the grace of God, so that they cannot forget God. But tell me: have you seen them that work quarried stones, who by continual practice have so learned to strike that they speak with others and all the while strike the iron that works the stone without looking at the iron, and yet strike not their hands? Now do you likewise: desire to be holy, if you would overcome entirely this misery of forgetfulness. Assured it is that water breaks the hardest stones with a single drop striking thereon a long time. Know you why you have not overcome this misery? Because you perceive not that it is sin. I tell you, then, that it is an error when a prince gives you a present, O man, that you shouldst shut your eyes and turn your back upon him. Even so do they that forget God — for at all times man receiveth from God gifts and mercy.

Now tell me: does our God present you with his bounty at all times? Yea, assuredly — for continually he ministers to you the breath wherewith you live. Truly, truly, I say to you: every time that your body receiveth breath, your heart ought to say, "God be thanked!" Then said John: Most true is your speech, O master; teach us therefore the way to attain to this blessed state. Jesus answered: Truly I say to you, one does not attain to such a state by human powers, but rather by the mercy of God our Lord. It is right true that man ought to desire the good, in order that God may give it him. Tell me: when you are at table, do you take those foods which you would not so much as look at? No, assuredly. Even so I say to you, that you shall not receive that which you will not desire. God is able — if you desire holiness — to make you holy sooner than the eye can wink; but in order that man may know the gift and the giver, our God wills that we should wait and ask. Have you seen them that practise shooting at a mark? Assured it is that they shoot many times in vain; nevertheless they never wish to shoot in vain, but stand always in hope of hitting the mark. Now do you this, you who ever desire to have our God in memory; and when you forget, mourn — for God shall give you grace to attain to all that I have said.

### Notes — Chapter 109
1. **The break is inferred, and flagged as such**: no "CIX" rubric line survives in the djvu OCR. The English check's chapter-title note "The Chapter of the neglectful" stands in its p. 251 footnotes, and its running head changes at p. 253 to "OF KEEPING GOD IN MIND" (the printers' head, not a manuscript title). The Italian text is continuous; the division is placed at Jesus' "Even so" application of the star-and-sun parable, which is where the neglectfulness theme (forgetting God) takes over from the watching theme. Nothing is renumbered.
2. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the neglectful." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "By God the living"; "It is not permitted to forget God and the resurrection"; "The sleep of [the] spirit"; "God is wise"; "God creates"; "The guidance of God." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
3. **"Quarried stones"** renders *pietre uiue* (literally "live stones," i.e. freshly quarried rock); the check reads "quarried stones" and is followed, the literal recorded.
4. **"Doth our God present you with his bounty"** renders the elliptical *ogni tempo DIG nosstro ui presenta* ("our God presents you at all times" — the gift understood); the check supplies "[his bounty]" and the ellipsis is kept visible.
5. **"Sooner than the eye can wink"** renders *piu pressto che non si sera lo hochio* (literally "sooner than the eye shutteth"); the check's turn is followed, the literal recorded.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). No rubric line for chapter 110 is recovered in the OCR; the break below is placed where the next chapter-title note falls (see ch. 110, note 1).

## Chapter 110

*[Editorial heading: The government of fasting — "as he clothes himself." No rubric line for this chapter is recovered in the djvu OCR in either column. The break is placed by narrative continuity where the English check's chapter-title note "The Chapter of government" falls (its p. 253 footnotes): at "il digiuno he uigillia spirituale he tanto unito uno chon laltro..." (folio 117b), the summing-up that turns from remembrance to the ordering of fasting and watching. The heading above is editorial.]*

Fasting and spiritual watching are so united one with the other that, if the watch be broken, straightway the fast is broken. For in sinning, man breaks the fast of the soul, and forgets God. Wherefore watching and fasting are ever necessary to us as regards the soul — and to all men; for to none is it lawful to sin. But the fasting of the body and its watchings — believe me — cannot be done always, nor can all do them: such as the sick, the aged, women with child, travellers, children, and others that are of weak constitution. Wherefore everyone — even as, according to his measure, he clothes himself — so let him choose his fasting. For just as the garments of a child are not fit for a man of thirty years, even so the watchings and fastings of one are not for another.

### Notes — Chapter 110
1. **The break is inferred, and flagged as such**: no "CX" rubric line survives in the djvu OCR. The English check's chapter-title note "The Chapter of government" stands in its p. 253 footnotes, at the paragraph "Fasting and spiritual watching are so united..." The Italian text is continuous; the division is placed there, where the discourse turns to the ordering (*governo*, regulation) of bodily fasting and watching. Nothing is renumbered.
2. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of government." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God bestows and is gracious"; "God bestows"; "Every time a breath is taken the heart must thank God"; "God is sovereign and gives"; "God is gracious"; "God is of old"; "The guidance of God." An editorial conjecture is printed in the apparatus at *propositione* ("Conj. proporzione"); the printed text is followed and the conjecture recorded. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
3. **"Travellers"** renders *uiandati*; the check reads "men that are put upon diet" — the divergence is recorded and the Italian followed.
4. **"According to his measure"** renders *sichome ha sua propositione* ("even as he hath his proportion"); the editors' conjecture *proporzione* is recorded (note 2), the printed *propositione* followed.
5. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next recovered rubric is "CXIb." (folio 118b).

## Chapter 111

*[Editorial heading: Redeeming the time — the best wine for the lord, not the dregs. Manuscript rubric "CXIb." (Italian column) at folio 118b; English rubric "CXIb." recovered. The heading above is editorial.]*

But beware, for Satan will use every effort to bring it to pass that you watch in the night, and afterward — when by God's commandment you ought to be praying and listening to the word of God — you sleep. Tell me: would it please you if a friend of yours should eat the meat and give you the bones? Peter answered: No, master — for such a one ought not to be called friend, but mocker. Jesus answered, sighing: You have well said the truth, O Peter. For truly, everyone that watches with the body more than is necessary — sleeping, or having his head weighed down with slumber, when he should be praying or listening to the word of God — such a wretch mocks God his creator, and so is guilty of such a sin. Moreover, he is a thief, for he steals the time that he ought to give to God, and spends it when and as much as pleases him. In a vessel of the best wine, a man gave his enemies to drink while the wine was at its best; but when the wine was come down to the dregs, he gave his lord to drink. Now what think you the master will do to the servant when he shall know all, and the servant shall stand before him? Assured it is that he will beat him and slay him in righteous indignation, according to the laws of the world. Now what shall God do to the man that spends the best of his time in business, and the worst in prayer and study of the law? Woe to the world, for with this its heart is weighed down with greater sin! Accordingly, when I said to you that laughter should be turned into weeping, feasts into fasting, and sleep into watching, I compassed in three words all that you have heard: that here on earth one ought always to weep, and that the weeping be from the heart, because God our creator is offended; that you ought to fast in order to have lordship over the sense, and to watch in order not to sin; and that bodily weeping, with bodily fasting and watching, be taken according to the constitution of each one.

### Notes — Chapter 111
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter of time." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God creates"; and at the closing, "It is not permitted to anyone to do what is unlawful." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. The English page running head ("FASTING, WATCHING, AND PRAYER") is the printers' head, not a manuscript title.
2. **"By God's commandment"** renders *per chomandamento di mo*; the printed *mo* is kept as printed (the check reads "by commandment of God") and recorded unresolved.
3. **"I compassed in three words"** renders *io ui serai in tre parolle* (literally "I shut up / enclosed for you in three words"); the check's "I compassed" is followed, the literal recorded.
4. **"Laughter into weeping, feasts into fasting, sleep into watching"** (*il risso si chonuerti in pianto, le chrapule in degiuno he il dormire in uigillie*) A canonical parallel is asserted for the opening member only: James iv. 9 ('let your laughter be turned to mourning'). The feasts-into-fasting and sleep-into-watching members are this book's own extension of the triad, and no canonical parallel is asserted for them; the triad stands whole as written.
5. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "CXII b.," is recovered in both columns.

## Chapter 112

*[Editorial heading: The grief of Jesus — the secret told to Barnabas alone. Manuscript rubric "CXII b." (Italian column); English rubric "CXII b." recovered. Folios 119b–120b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Having said this, Jesus said: You needs must seek of the fruits and herbs wherewith to sustain our life, for it is eight days that we have eaten no bread. Wherefore I will pray to our God, and will await you with Barnabas. So all the disciples and apostles departed by fours and by sixes, and went their way according to the word of Jesus. There remained with Jesus he who writes; whereupon Jesus, weeping, said: O Barnabas, it is necessary that I should reveal to you great secrets which, after that I shall be departed from the world, you shall reveal. Then answered he that writes, weeping, and said: Suffer me to weep, O master — and other men also — for we are sinners. And you, that art holy and a prophet of God, it is not fitting for you to weep so much. Jesus answered: Believe me, Barnabas, that I cannot weep as much as I ought. For if men had not called me God, I should have seen God here as he shall be seen in paradise, and should have been safe from fearing the day of judgement. But God knows that I am innocent, for never have I harboured thought to be held more than a poor slave. Nay, I tell you that if I had not been called God, I should have been carried into paradise when I depart from the world — whereas now I shall not go thither until the judgement. Now see you whether I have cause to weep? Know, O Barnabas, that for this I must have great persecution, and shall be sold by one of my disciples for thirty pieces of money. Whereupon — albeit I am sure that he who shall sell me shall be slain in my name, for God shall take me up from the world, and shall so transform the traitor that everyone shall believe him to be me — nevertheless, when he dieth an evil death, I shall abide in that dishonour a long time in the world. But when Mohammed shall come, the sacred messenger of God, this infamy shall be taken away. And this shall God do because I have confessed the truth of the Messiah — who shall give me this reward: that I shall be known to be alive, and a stranger to that infamous death. Then answered he that writes: O master, tell me who is that wretch, for I fain would choke him. "Hold your peace," answered Jesus, "for so God wills, and it cannot be done otherwise. But see you that when my mother shall be afflicted at such an event, you tell her the truth, in order that she may be comforted." Then answered he that writes: All this will I do, O master, if God please.

### Notes — Chapter 112
1. **The seam, translated exactly as written**: *quando uenira machometo sachro nontio di Dio* ("when Mohammed shall come, the sacred nuncio of God"); *ho chonfessato la uerrita del messia* ("I have confessed the truth of the Messiah"); *il quale mi dara quessto premio che io saro chonosiuto per uiuo he allieno da quella morte inffame* ("who shall give me this reward: that I shall be known to be alive, and a stranger to that infamous death"). The name stands where the Italian puts it and is not imported where the Italian does not. The Italian keeps *nontio* (nuncio, messenger) for Mohammed distinct from *messia* (Messiah) — *messia* is never rendered "messenger," nor *nontio* "messiah." Nothing is smoothed, softened, or deleted. The fork is footnoted per the audit: this distinguishes Jesus from "the Messiah" and names Mohammed the "sacred nuncio of God," which contradicts the Qur'an's identification of the Messiah with Jesus — and Robert's recorded suspicion that the seam may be a later insertion is carried here rather than decided.
2. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the grief of Jesus." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God creates"; "Mohammed the prophet of God" (at *machometo*); "God's foreordaining is mighty"; "Please God." Their footnotes cite "Cp. below, 208b and 222b sqq." at the transforming of the traitor, and "Cp. John xiv. 19" at "known to be alive" — recorded as printed. No canonical parallel is asserted; the editors' citations above are recorded as printed only. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. The English page running head ("JESUS CONVERSETH WITH BARNABAS") is the printers' head, not a manuscript title.
3. **"If men had not called me God..."** — the whole "grief" confession (*se li homeni non mi hauessero chiamato DIG, io haueria ueduto DIG quiui chome si uedera im paradisso... non ui andero insino al iuditio*) is translated exactly as written, never softened: Jesus says he would have seen God here as in paradise and been carried into paradise at his departing, but now shall not go thither until the judgement. This is seam context for ch. 96–97 and the ch. 112 seam above; no name is imported.
4. **"Thirty pieces of money"** (*trenta danari*) and **"by fours and by sixes"** (*ha quatro he ha sei*) are kept literal, with the check.
5. **"I fain would choke him"** renders *il uolgio afogare* ("I would choke/drown him"); the check's turn is followed.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle (Italian + Spanish control for ch. 1–120); Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "CXIII," opens the next slice at folio 120b.

## Chapter 113

*[Editorial heading: Beware of idleness — the parable of the barren fig tree. Manuscript rubric "CXIII" (Italian column) at folio 120b. The heading above is editorial.]*

When the disciples came, they brought pine-cones, and by the will of God they found a good quantity of dates. So after the midday prayer they ate with Jesus. Then the apostles and disciples, seeing him that writes of sad countenance, feared that Jesus must soon depart from the world. Whereupon Jesus comforted them, saying: "Fear not, for my hour is not yet come that I should depart from you. I shall abide with you still a little while. But now I must teach you, that you may go — as I have told you — through all Israel to preach penitence, that God may have mercy upon the sin of Israel. Let every one therefore beware of idleness, and much more he that does penance; for every tree that beareth not good fruit shall be cut down and cast into the fire.

"There was a citizen who had a vineyard, and in the midst thereof he had a garden, which had a fine fig tree. For three years, when the owner came, he found no fruit thereon; and seeing every other tree bearing fruit there, he said to his vinedresser: 'Cut down this wretched tree, for it cumbers the ground in vain.' The vinedresser answered: 'Do not, sir, for it is a beautiful tree.' 'Hold your peace,' said the owner, 'for I care not for vain beauties. You should know that the palm and the balsam are nobler than the fig. But I had planted in the courtyard of my house a palm plant and a balsam plant, which I had surrounded with costly walls; but when these bore no fruit, but put forth leaves which heaped themselves up and rotted the ground in front of the house, I had them both removed. And now shall I pardon a fig tree far from the house, which cumbers my garden and vineyard in vain, where every other tree beareth fruit? Assuredly I will suffer it no longer.' Then said the vinedresser: 'Sir, the soil is too rich; wait therefore one year more, for I will prune the fig plant's branches, and take away from it the richness of the soil, putting in poor soil with stones, and so shall it bear fruit.' The owner answered: 'Now go and do so; for I will wait, and the fig plant shall bear fruit. Understand you this parable?" The disciples answered: "No, Lord; therefore explain it to us."

### Notes — Chapter 113
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of penitence." The English page running heads ("JESUS PREDICTETH HIS HUMILIATION," p. 259 — covering the ch. 112 close — and "PARABLE OF THE BARREN FIG-TREE," p. 261) are the printer's heads, not manuscript titles. A line of the scribe's Arabic gloss stands in the Italian column and is recorded, not translated; the marginal gloss at *habbi misserichordia* is rendered "God is gracious."
2. **"Pine-cones"** renders the Italian *pigne*, with the check. **"Of sad countenance"** renders *di malla uolgia* (ill humour), with the check.
3. **"Idleness"** renders *hotio* (ozio); the check reads "sloth." **"Penitence"/"penance"** render *penitenzza*/*penitezza* as written.
4. **The fig-tree figures are kept as written**: three fruitless years; one year more of grace; poor soil with stones. The check's footnotes cite Matt. iii. 10; Luke iii. 9 for the cut-down tree and Luke xiii. 6–9 (expanded) for the parable — recorded as printed. Canonical parallels are asserted: Matt. iii. 10 and Luke iii. 9 ('every tree that beareth not good fruit shall be cut down and cast into the fire'), and Luke xiii. 6–9 (the barren fig tree — three fruitless years and one year more of grace; this book's treatment, poor soil with stones, stands as written where Luke has digging and dunging).
5. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle (Italian + Spanish control for ch. 1–120); Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next division, "CXIV," stands on the English column's head — no Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR at this point; see the slice headnote.

---

## Chapter 114

*[Editorial heading: The parable explained — "Man is born to work." Division per the edition's "CXIV" head (English column); no Italian-column rubric recovered in the djvu OCR at this point — the Italian runs on from ch. 113 unbroken in the text layer. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus answered: "Truly I say to you, the owner is God, and the vinedresser is his law. God, then, had in paradise the palm and the balsam — for Satan is the palm and the first man the balsam — whom he cast out because they bore no fruit of good works, but uttered ungodly words that condemned many angels and many men. Now that God has man in the world, in the midst of his creatures that all serve God according to his precept — and man, I say, bearing no fruit at all — God would cut him down and send him to hell, seeing he pardoned not the angel and the first man, punishing the angel eternally and the man for a time. Wherefore the law of God says that man has too much good in this life, and so it is needful that he be troubled and deprived of earthly goods, that he may do good. Therefore our God waits for man to penitence.

"Truly I say to you, our God has condemned man to work, so that, as said Job, the friend and prophet of God: 'As the bird is born to fly and the fish to swim, even so is man born to work.' So also David our father, a prophet of God, says: 'Eating the labours of our hands we shall be blessed, and it shall be well with us.' Wherefore let every one work according to his quality. Now tell me: if David our father and Solomon his son worked with their hands, what ought the sinner to do?"

Said John: "Master, to work is a fitting thing, but this ought the poor to do."

Jesus answered: "Yea, for they cannot do otherwise. But know you not that good, to be good, must be free from necessity? Thus the sun and the other planets are forced by the precept of God, so that they cannot do otherwise — wherefore they shall have no merit. Tell me: when God gave the precept to work, he said not: 'The poor man shall live by the sweat of his face'; nor did Job say: 'As the bird is born to fly, so the poor man is born to work.' But God said to man: 'In the sweat of your face shall you eat bread,' and Job said that man is born to work. Therefore he who is not man is free from this precept. Assuredly for no other reason is everything costly, but that there is a great multitude of idle folk — who, if they laboured, some in tilling the earth and some in fishing the water, there would be the greatest plenty in the world. And of the lack thereof it will be needful to render account in the dreadful day of judgement."

### Notes — Chapter 114
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the repentance of the slothful." The English page running head ("MAN IS BORN TO LABOUR") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is patient and repenting"; "David said in the Psalms that if a man be content with what he acquire lawfully with his hand, it shall be well for them and the dominion shall be made easy for them"; "The best thing is that which comes of free choice, and what is not of free choice cannot be good"; "God gives and is wise." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Their footnotes cite Job v. 7 (Vulg.) and Ps. cxxviii. 2 — recorded as printed. Canonical parallels are asserted: Job v. 7 (Vulg. — 'man is born to labour, and the bird to flight'; the 'fish to swim' member is this book's addition) and Ps. cxxviii. 2 ('thou shalt eat the labour of thine hands: happy shalt thou be, and it shall be well with thee').
2. **The palm/balsam identification is kept exactly as written**: Satan is the palm, the first man the balsam — cast out of paradise for no fruit of good works and for ungodly words condemning many angels and many men. Nothing is smoothed.
3. **"Are forced"** renders *sono sforzati* (constrained); the check reads "are strengthened" — the divergence is recorded, the Italian followed.
4. **"He who is not man"** is kept without the check's inserted "[only]" — the Italian has no *solo*; the insertion is recorded, not followed.
5. **The Job and David sayings are kept whole**: "As the bird is born to fly and the fish to swim, even so is man born to work"; "Eating the labours of our hands we shall be blessed, and it shall be well with us." The "in the sweat of thy face" precept is kept as addressed to man, not to the poor man only.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next division, "CXV," stands on the English column's head — no Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR at this point; see the slice headnote.

---

## Chapter 115

*[Editorial heading: The evils of lust — the enemy in one's own house. Division per the edition's "CXV" head (English column); no Italian-column rubric recovered in the djvu OCR at this point — the Italian runs on from ch. 114 unbroken in the text layer. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Let man tell me: what has he brought into the world, for which he would live in idleness? Certain it is that he was born naked, incapable of anything; hence of all that he has found he is not the owner, but the dispenser — whereof he will have to render account in that dreadful day.

"The abominable lust that makes man like the brute beasts ought greatly to be feared; for the enemy is of one's own household, so that one cannot go into any place whither your enemy comes not. Ah, how many have perished through lust! Through lust came the deluge, insomuch that the world perished before the mercy of God — insomuch that only Noah was saved, with eighty-three human persons. Through lust God overwhelmed three wicked cities, whence escaped only Lot with his two daughters. Through lust the tribe of Benjamin was all but extinguished. And I tell you truly, that if I should narrate to you how many have perished through lust, the space of five days would not suffice."

James answered: "O master, what is meant by lust?"

Jesus answered: "Lust is an unbridled desire of love, which, not being ruled by reason, bursts the bounds of man's intellect and affection — so that the man, not knowing himself, loves that which he ought to hate. Believe me: when a man loves a thing — not because God has given him such a thing, but as its owner — he is a fornicator; for the soul, which ought to abide united with God its creator, he unites with the creature. Wherefore God laments by Isaiah the prophet, saying: 'You have committed fornication with many lovers; nevertheless return to me, and I will receive you.' As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — were it not for the internal lust of man's heart, he would not fall into the external; for if the root be removed the tree dieth speedily.

"Let man content himself therefore with the wife whom his creator has given him, and let him put every other woman into oblivion."

Andrew answered: "How shall a man forget the women, living in the city where there is so great a quantity of them?"

Jesus answered: "O Andrew, certain it is that he who lives in the city shall do ill; for the city is a sponge that draws in every iniquity."

### Notes — Chapter 115
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the repentance of the sensually impure." The English page running head ("THE EVILS OF LUST") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "O sons of Adam, tell me what ye have brought into the world, whereon ye can rely and do nothing?"; "Account of the family of Noah and the family of Lot"; "Setting forth sensual desire"; "God creates and repents"; "By the living God." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Their footnotes cite Gen. vi. 1 sqq. (deluge); Gen. vi. 18 and 2 Pet. ii. 5 (eight souls — against the Italian's eighty-three, recorded as written); Gen. xix (the three cities); Judges xix–xx (Benjamin); and Jer. iii. 1 for the "many lovers" lament — though the Italian names *Esaia* (Isaiah), recorded exactly as written. Canonical parallels are asserted: Gen. vi. 1 sqq. (the deluge and Noah saved); Gen. xix (Lot escaping with his two daughters — the 'three cities' count stands as this book's wording); Judges xix–xx (Benjamin all but extinguished); and Jer. iii. 1 (the 'many lovers' lament actually quoted — the Italian names Isaiah, recorded exactly as written). The editors' remaining citations above, Gen. vi. 18 and 2 Pet. ii. 5 (eight souls, against this book's eighty-three), stand as printed record only.
2. **The figures are kept as written, never harmonized**: Noah saved with eighty-three persons (not eight); Lot escaping with his two daughters (*filgiole* — the check reads "children," the divergence recorded); the tribe of Benjamin all but extinguished; five days would not suffice the telling.
3. **MS note recorded**: at "the world perished" the printed apparatus notes "1 So MS.; conj. peril" — the manuscript reads *per il monddo*, conjectured as *perì* (perished); the conjectured sense is followed and the note recorded.
4. **The definition of lust is kept whole**: unbridled desire of love, unruled by reason, bursting intellect and affection — the man not knowing himself, loving what he ought to hate; loving a thing as owner rather than as God's gift is fornication, the soul united to the creature instead of the creator.
5. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "CXVI" (folio 124a), is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 116

*[Editorial heading: The lust of the eye — Elijah and the blind man. Manuscript rubric "CXVI" (Italian column) at folio 124a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"It behoves a man to live in the city even as the soldier lives when he has enemies around the fortress — defending himself against every assault, and always fearing treachery on the part of the citizens. Even so I say: let him repel every outward offer of sin, and fear the sense — because it desires supremely things impure. But how shall he defend himself if he bridle not the eye, which is the origin of every carnal sin? As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — he who has not bodily eyes is safe from receiving punishment save only to the third degree, while he that has eyes receiveth it to the seventh degree.

"In the time of Elijah the prophet it came to pass that Elijah, seeing a blind man weeping, who was of good life, asked him, saying: 'Why weepest you, O brother?' The blind man answered: 'I weep because I cannot see Elijah, the holy prophet of God.' Then Elijah rebuked him, saying: 'Cease from weeping, O man, for in weeping you sinnest.' The blind man answered: 'Now tell me: is it a sin to see a holy prophet of God, who raises the dead and makes the fire to come down from heaven?' Elijah answered: 'You speak not the truth, for Elijah is not able to do anything of all that you say, because he is a man as you are. For all men cannot make a fly to be born.' Said the blind man: 'You say this, O man, because Elijah must have rebuked you for some sin of yours, wherefore you hatest him.' Elijah answered: 'Would to God that you wert speaking the truth! For, O brother, if I should hate Elijah I should love God, and the more I should hate Elijah the more I should love God.' Hereupon was the blind man greatly angered, and said: 'As God lives, you are an impious fellow! Can God then be loved while hating the prophets of God? Begone forthwith, for I will not listen to you any longer!' Elijah answered: 'Brother, now may you see with your intellect how evil is bodily seeing! For you desire sight to see Elijah, and hatest Elijah with your soul.' The blind man answered: 'Now begone! for you are the devil, that wouldst make me sin against the holy one of God.' Then Elijah gave a sigh, and said with tears: 'You have spoken the truth, O brother, for my flesh, which you desire to see, separates you from God.' Said the blind man: 'I do not wish to see you; nay, if I had eyes, I would close them so as not to see you.' Then said Elijah: 'Know, brother, that I am Elijah!' The blind man answered: 'You speak not the truth.' Then said the disciples of Elijah: 'Brother, he truly is the prophet of God, Elijah.' 'Tell me,' said the blind man, 'if he be the prophet — of what stock am I, and how became I blind?'"

### Notes — Chapter 116
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on the eye (and) repentance." The English page running head ("THE LUST OF THE EYE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "The eye is the cause of every sensual desire"; "By the living God"; "Dialogue of Elias and the blind man." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
2. **"Fear the sense"** follows the check for the uncertain Italian *che terni il sensso* — flagged as an uncertain reading (the sense "fear" is the check's; the Italian form is unclear in the text layer). Spanish collation (R6) may settle it.
3. **The degrees are kept as written**: the eyeless secure save unto the third degree; the seeing punished unto the seventh.
4. **"All men cannot make a fly to be born"** is kept literal — the Italian reads *tutti li homeni* (the check adds "in the world," recorded, not followed).
5. **The Elijah dialogue is kept entire**, exactly as written — including Elijah's "if I should hate Elijah I should love God" paradox, the blind man's anger, and the closing test question. The story runs on unbroken into ch. 117; the division stands on the recovered "CXVII" rubric.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "CXVII" (at the 125a/125b turn), is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 117

*[Editorial heading: The idol in the heart — "everyone that finds pleasure in the creature." Manuscript rubric "CXVII" (Italian column) at the 125a/125b turn. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Elijah answered: 'you are of the tribe of Levi; and because you, on entering the temple of God, didst look lustfully upon a woman — you being near the sanctuary — our God took away your sight.' Then the blind man weeping said: 'Pardon me, O holy prophet of God, for I have sinned in speaking with you; for if I had seen you I should not have sinned.' Elijah answered: 'May our God pardon you, O brother — for as for me, I know that you have told me the truth — seeing that the more I hate myself the more I love God; and if you sawest me you wouldst quiet your desire, which is not pleasing to God. For Elijah is not your creator, but God. Wherefore, as far as you are concerned, I am the devil,' said Elijah weeping, 'because I turn you aside from your creator. Weep then, O brother, because you have not that light which would make you see the true from the false; for if you hadst had that, you wouldst not have despised my doctrine. Wherefore I say to you, that many desire to see me, and come from far to see me, who despise my words. Wherefore it were better for them, for their salvation, not to have eyes; seeing that everyone that finds pleasure in the creature, be he who he may, and seeketh not to find pleasure in God, has made an idol in his heart, and has forsaken God.'"

Then said Jesus, sighing: "Have you understood all that Elijah has said?"

The disciples answered: "In sooth, we have understood, and we are beside ourselves, knowing that here on earth there are very few that are not idolaters."

### Notes — Chapter 117
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on the body of the idol." The English page running head ("ELIJAH AND THE BLIND MAN") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "By the living God"; "God is the creator." Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated.
2. **"Didst look lustfully"** renders the Italian *uedessti ... una dona mallamente* (didst look upon a woman evilly); the check reads "lookedst lewdly" — the divergence is recorded, the Italian followed.
3. **MS note recorded**: at "as far as thou art concerned, I am the devil" the printed apparatus notes "1 MS., apparently, parte" — the manuscript apparently reads *parte* for the printed *per te*; the printed text is followed and the editors mark the passage "Text obscure." Nothing is resolved silently.
4. **"Hath made an idol in his heart"** (*haffato uno iddolo nel chore suo*) is kept literal — the chapter's own turn on idolatry, matching the editors' chapter-title note. The closing exchange — Jesus' sighing question and the disciples' "very few that are not idolaters" — is kept entire.
5. **Boundary note**: the next rubric, "CXVIII" (folio 126a), is confirmed in the Italian column and opens the next slice ("Allora iessu disse. uoi dite la uerita..."). The English column's head at that point is recovered only as the garbled "CXVIIK" in the OCR — recorded as printed; both columns agree the chapter is 118, which lies outside this slice.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle (Italian + Spanish control for ch. 1–120); Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only).
