## Chapter 118

*[Editorial heading: The two lights — "Mine eye is a thief." Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXVIII") at folio 126a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Jesus said: "You speak the truth — for Israel now desired to establish firmly the idolatry that they have in their hearts, by holding me for God. Many of them have now despised my teaching, saying that I could make myself lord of all Judea by confessing to be God, and that I am mad to want to live in poverty among the desert places, and not abide continually among princes in delicate living. O unhappy man, who prizest the light that is common to flies and ants, and despise the light that is common only to angels and prophets and holy friends of God! If then the eye be not guarded, O Andrew, I tell you that it is impossible not to fall headlong into lust. Wherefore Jeremiah the prophet, weeping, said truly: 'Mine eye is a thief that robs my soul.' For this cause David our father with greatest longing prayed to God our Lord, that he would turn away his eyes, that he might not see vanities — for truly everything that has an end is vain. Tell me then: if one had two pence to buy bread, would he spend them to buy smoke? Surely not — for smoke hurts the eyes and gives no sustenance to the body. Even so then let man do: with the outward sight of his eyes and the inward sight of his mind let him seek to know God his creator and the good pleasure of his will, and let him not make the creature his end, which makes him lose the creator."

### Notes — Chapter 118
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on light." (Arabic gloss on *luce*). Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "Whoever does not keep fast his eyes will not escape from the sin of impurity. Inde." (on *libidine*); "God is sovereign." (on *DIO signore nosstro*). The English page running head ("OF GUARDING THE EYE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their footnotes cite Lam. iii. 51 (Vulg. *Oculus meus depraedatus est animam meam*) and Ps. cxix. 37 — recorded as printed. Canonical parallels are asserted: Lam. iii. 51 (Vulg. — 'mine eye is a thief that robs my soul') and Ps. cxix. 37 ('turn away mine eyes from beholding vanity').
2. **"Weeping"** (*inzio piangendo*): the Italian is kept as "weeping"; the check reads "weeping vehemently" — recorded, not followed.
3. **"Two pence"** (*dui soldi*), **"sustenance"** (*sostanza*), **"let man do"** (*fazia* = *faccia*): kept literal, with the check.
4. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle (Italian + Spanish control for ch. 1–120); Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). No CXIX rubric stands between this chapter and the next — see the slice headnote and the ch. 119 notes.

---

## Chapter 119

*[Editorial heading: Prayer — "the hands of our life." No CXIX rubric in the printed Italian column (none recovered in the English column either) — the text runs continuously from chapter 118's close; the number 119 is the edition's editorial division for the prayer discourse, as with XCVI. The heading above is editorial.]*

"For truly, every time that a man sees a thing and forgets God who has made it for man, he has sinned. For if a friend of yours give you a thing to keep in memory of him, and you sell it or forget it — you have offended your friend. Even so does man: seeing the creature and not having the creator in memory — who for love of man has created it — he sins against God his creator, by ingratitude. He therefore who shall look upon women and shall forget God, who for the good of man created woman — he will love her and desire her; and to such a degree will this lust of his break forth, that he will love everything like to the thing loved. Whence was born that sin of which it is a shame to have memory. If then man shall put a bridle upon his eyes, he shall be lord of the sense, which cannot desire that which is not set before it. And so shall the flesh be subject to the spirit — for as the ship cannot move without wind, so the flesh without the sense cannot sin.

"That it is then needful for the penitent to turn idle tales into prayer, reason itself shows, even if it were not a precept of God. For in every idle word man sins, and our God blots out sin by prayer. For prayer is the advocate of the soul; prayer is the medicine of the soul; prayer is the defence of the heart; prayer is the weapon of faith; prayer is the bridle of the sense; prayer is the salt of the flesh, which suffers it not to rot in sin. And I tell you that prayer is the hands of our life — whereby the man that prays shall defend himself in the day of judgment. For he shall heal his soul of sin here on earth, and shall keep his heart, that it be not touched by evil desires — to the vexing of Satan; for he shall keep his sense within the law of God, and his flesh shall walk in righteousness, receiving from God whatsoever he shall ask.

"As God lives, in whose presence we are: a man without prayer can no more be a man of good works than a dumb man can plead his cause to a blind one; than a fistula can be healed without ointment; than a man can defend himself without movement, or make attack without weapons; than one can sail without a rudder, or keep dead flesh without salt. For truly he who has no hand cannot receive. If man could turn dung into gold and clay into sugar, what would he do?"

Then Jesus being silent, the disciples answered: "No one would do anything else but make gold and sugar." Then said Jesus: "Why then does not man turn foolish chatter into prayer? Is time, perchance, given him by God that he may offend God? Surely not. For what prince would give a city to his subject, that he should make war upon him? As God lives: if man knew how the soul is transformed by vain talking, he would sooner bite off his tongue with his teeth than talk. O wretched world! For today men assemble not together to pray, but in the porches of the temple and in the very temple Satan has the sacrifice of vain talk — and, what is worse, of things which I cannot speak of without shame."

### Notes — Chapter 119
1. **The numbering gap is real and recorded**: no CXIX rubric appears in the printed Italian column (nor is one recovered in the English column in the OCR) — the text runs continuously from chapter 118's close (*che il fa smarire dal chreatore*) into the prayer discourse. The number 119 is the edition's editorial division, as with XCVI (slice 20). Recorded exactly as found; no lacuna posited, nothing renumbered.
2. **The *vedere*/*vendere* crux — the famous Ragg misreading**: the printed Italian reads *uedendola tu* ("thou seeing it"); the 1907 English reads "thou shouldest sell it." The Spanish witness (Sydney MS) reads "si la vendiesses o te olvidasses della" ("if thou shouldst sell it or forget it") — an independent witness for *vendere* ("sell") against the print's *vedere*. Per the standing rule the rendering follows the better-supported reading: "sell." The fork is recorded here, not smoothed: Italian print *vedere*, Spanish *vender*, 1907 English "sell" (as misreading, now vindicated by the witness).
3. **"Shall heal his soul of sin"** (*sanera la anima sua da pechato*): the check reads "keep his soul from sin" — recorded, the Italian followed.
4. **"Movement"** (*motto*), **"tales"** (*fabule*), **"chatter"** (*fabullare*), **"rot"** (*putrefare*): kept literal, with the check.
5. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on prayer." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is the creator."; "God is the pardoner." (on *il scancella per la horatione*); "God is the giver." — footnote-letter assignments garbled in the OCR, recorded as legible. The English page running head ("THE EVILS OF VAIN TALKING") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their footnotes cite "See above, 107* (p. 233)" and "Cp. Matt. xii. 36" — recorded as printed. A canonical parallel is asserted: Matt. xii. 36 ('every idle word that men shall speak, they shall give account thereof in the day of judgment'). The editors' remaining citation above ('See above, 107* (p. 233)') stands as printed record only.
6. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "CXX" (printed "CXXc."), is confirmed in the Italian column at folio 129a.

---

## Chapter 120

*[Editorial heading: The fruit of vain talking — words at the price of gold. Manuscript rubric printed "CXXc." (folio 129a); English column "*~~ CXX c." The heading above is editorial.]*

"The fruit of vain talking is this: that it weakens the understanding, so that it is not fit to receive the truth — even as a horse accustomed to carry an ounce of cotton cannot carry a hundred pounds of stone.

"But worse is the man that spends his time in jests. When he shall go to prayer, Satan will put into his memory those same jests — so that, when he ought to weep over his sins, to move God to mercy and receive pardon for his sins, laughing he provokes God to anger, who shall scourge him and reprove him. Woe therefore to them that jest and talk vainly! But if our God holds in abomination them that jest and talk in vain, in what account will he hold them that murmur and slander their neighbour? And in what plight are they that make sinning their trade, as business supremely needful? O unclean world — I cannot conceive how sorely you shall be punished by God! He then who would do penance, I tell you, must give his words at the price of gold."

His disciples answered: "Now who will buy a man's words at the price of gold? Surely no one! And how shall he do penance? It is certain that he will become miserly!"

Jesus answered: "Your heart is so heavy that I cannot lift it up — wherefore in every word I must tell you the meaning. But give thanks to God, who has given you grace to know the mysteries of God. I do not say that the penitent should sell his talk; I say that when he talks, let him reckon that he is throwing away gold. For, doing this — even as gold is spent upon necessary things — so shall he talk when it shall be needful to talk; and even as no one spends gold upon a thing that harms the body, so let him not talk of a thing that would do harm to his soul."

### Notes — Chapter 120
1. **Printed *uendi* followed**: *non dicho che il penitente uendi il suo parllare* — "I do not say that the penitent should sell his talk"; the 1907 English agrees ("should sell his talking"). The rendering follows the printed reading, which answers the disciples' "who will buy?" directly. This is a different word from the ch. 119 *uedendola* crux — the Joosten/Cirillo *vedere* correction applies there, not here. (Ch. 120 is within the Sydney Spanish coverage; collation pending — R6.)
2. **"Shall scourge him and reprove him"** (*li dara flagello he lo riprobera*): the check reads "chastise him, and cast him out" — recorded, the Italian followed.
3. **"A hundred pounds"** (*cento lire* = pounds), **"miserly"** (*hauaro*): kept literal, with the check.
4. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on speech." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "By the living God." (on *Viue DIG*); "God is the giver." (on the grace to know the mysteries); "O wickedness of the world, I cannot know how the great God will punish you. Inde." (on *ho mondo inmondo*). Their footnotes cite "Cp. Qoran xxix. sub fin." and "Cp. Mark iv. 11" — recorded as printed. A canonical parallel is asserted: Mark iv. 11 ('unto you it is given to know the mystery of the kingdom of God'), at 'grace to know the mysteries of God.' A Quran parallel is also asserted: Q 29:45 (the editors' 'Qoran xxix. sub fin.', quoted by them in Sale's wording — 'Prayer preserveth [a man] from filthy crimes and from that which is blameable') — prayer preserving a man from shameful deeds, as in the prayer teaching of chapter 119 carried into this chapter.
5. **Witness coverage**: two-witness in principle; Spanish collation complete (R6: cited at forks only). The next rubric, "CXXI a." (folio 130a), is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 121

*[Editorial heading: The prisoner before the governor — measure your talk. Manuscript rubric "CXXI a." (folio 130a). The heading above is editorial.]*

"When the governor has taken a prisoner whom he examines, and the clerk writes it down — tell me, how does such a man talk?"

The disciples answered: "He talks with fear and to the point, so as not to give suspicion of himself; and he is careful not to say a thing that may displease the governor, but seeketh to say somewhat whereby he may be set free."

Then answered Jesus: "This ought the penitent to do, then, that he lose not his soul. For God has given to every man two angels for recorders — the one writing the good and the other the evil that the man does. If then a man would receive mercy, let him measure his talk more strictly than gold is measured."

### Notes — Chapter 121
1. **Italian-only witness — no Spanish control** (standing flag: the Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only).
2. **"Clerk"** (*cha[n]celiero*, the editors' bracketed restoration); **"recorders"** (*chancelieri* — the check reads "notaries" in both places; recorded, the Italian followed).
3. **"Would receive mercy"** (*uora riceuere misserichordia*; OCR "uolle" read as *uora*).
4. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on listening in silence." Their marginal note glosses the scribe's Arabic mark: "The great God gave to the sons of Adam two angels, and they write what people do, both of good and of wickedness. Inde." (on *dui angioli*).
5. **Witness coverage**: Italian only (see 1). The English column carries the "CXXII" head for the next chapter; no Italian-column rubric for CXXII is recovered in the OCR — see ch. 122 notes.

---

## Chapter 122

*[Editorial heading: Avarice turned into almsgiving — the miser's prayer. No Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR — the text runs continuously from chapter 121's close; the English column carries the head "CXXII b." The heading above is editorial.]*

"As for avarice, let it be turned into almsgiving. Truly I say to you, that even as the plummet has for its end the centre, so the miser has hell for his end — for to the miser it is impossible to possess any good in paradise. Know you wherefore? For I will tell you. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands: the miser, though he be silent with his tongue, says by his works, 'There is no other God but me.' For all that he has he is fain to spend at his own pleasure, not regarding his beginning and his end — that he is born naked, and dying leaves all.

"Now tell me: if Herod should give you a garden to keep, and you would order it as owners — sending no fruit to Herod, and when Herod should send for fruit you drove away his messengers — tell me, would you make yourselves kings over that garden? Surely yes. Now I tell you that even so the miserly man makes himself God over the riches that God has given him.

"Avarice is a thirst of the sense, which, having lost God through sin — because it lives on pleasure — and being unable to delight itself in God, who is hidden from it, goes about gathering temporal things, which it holds as its good; and it grows the stronger, the more it sees itself deprived of God.

"Wherefore the conversion of the sinner is from God, who gives the grace of repentance — as our father David said: 'This change comes from the right hand of God.'

"And I must tell you what man is, if you would know how penance ought to be done. And therefore today let us give thanks to God, who has given us grace to make known his will by my speaking."

Whereupon, lifting up his hands, he prayed, saying: "Lord God almighty and merciful, who in mercy have created us, giving us the rank of men, your servants, with the faith of your true messenger — we thank you for every benefit of yours, and we would worship you alone all the days of our life: weeping over our sins, making prayer and alms, fasting and studying your word, teaching them that are ignorant of your will, suffering from the world for love of you, and laying down our life to death to serve you. Save us, O Lord, from Satan, from the flesh, and from the world — even as you savedst your elect: for love of your own self, and for love of your messenger for whom you didst create us, and for love of all your saints and prophets."

The disciples answered ever: "So be it; so be it, O Lord; so be it, O our merciful God."

### Notes — Chapter 122
1. **Numbering as printed**: no Italian-column rubric for CXXII is recovered in the OCR — the text runs continuously from chapter 121's close (*missura lo horo*) into the avarice discourse; the English column carries the head "CXXII b." Recorded exactly as found; no lacuna posited, nothing renumbered. **Italian-only witness — no Spanish control** (standing flag: the Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only).
2. **The *nontio* passages, translated exactly as written**: *chon la fede del notio tuo ueraze* ("with the faith of thy true messenger" — *notio*, a spelling of *nontio*) and *per ammore del nontio tuo per il qualle ci chreassti* ("for love of thy messenger for whom thou didst create us"). *Nontio* is kept distinct from *messia* throughout — the Italian names no one here, and no name is imported. Footnoted as seam context (echoing the ch. 96–97 "messenger for whom he hath made all things"): Robert's insertion-suspicion and the Quran-contradiction fork are carried here, never decided, never smoothed, never deleted. (R4.)
3. **"There is no other God but me"** (*non uie altro DIG che me*) and **"maketh himself God"** (*si chonstituisse DIG*) — the miser's self-deification, kept exactly as written, never softened.
4. **MS note recorded**: at *chongregare* the printed apparatus notes "MS. chongregaregare (sic)" — the manuscript doubles the syllable; the printed text is followed.
5. **"Plummet"** (*pionbo* = lead-weight), **"thirst"** (*sette* = *sete*), **"laying down our life"** (*metendo la uitta alia morte*): kept literal, with the check.
6. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the repentance of the miserly." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "By the living God." (on *Viue DIG*); "And he is a miser." (on *auaro*); "God guides to repentance." (on *la chonuerssione del pechatore he da DIG*); "There is no power except with God. Inde." (on *nontio tuo*). The English page running head ("THE EVILS OF AVARICE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their footnotes cite "See above, 107* (p. 233)" and "Cf. Job i. 21; 1 Tim. vi. 7" — recorded as printed. Canonical parallels are asserted: Job i. 21 (the miser 'is born naked, and dying leaves all,' against Job's 'Naked came I out of my mother's womb, and naked shall I return thither') and 1 Tim. vi. 7 ('we brought nothing into this world, and it is certain we can carry nothing out'). The editors' remaining citation ('See above, 107* (p. 233)') is an internal cross-reference to their own note, and stands as printed record only.
7. **Witness coverage**: Italian only (see 1). The next rubric, "CXXIII" (folio 131a), is confirmed in the Italian column — the Friday-morning discourse opens the next slice.

## Chapter 123

*[Editorial heading: The composition of man — earth, water, air, and fire. No Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR (folio 131a); English head "CXXIII h." The heading above is editorial.]*

When day came — Friday morning, early — Jesus, after the prayer, called his disciples together and said to them: "Let us sit down. For even as today God created man of the clay of the earth, even so will I tell you what man is, if it please God."

When all were seated, Jesus said again: "Our God, to show his creatures his goodness and mercy, and his omnipotence, with his liberality and his justice, made a composition of four things, each contrary to the other, and united them in one final object — which is man. And these are the earth, the water, the air, and the fire, so that each might temper the extreme of the other. And of these four things he made a vessel, which is the body of man — of flesh, bones, blood, marrow, and skin, with nerves and veins, and with all his inward parts. In it God placed the soul and the sense, as two hands of this life: giving for lodging to the sense every part of the body, wherein it spread itself like oil; and to the soul he gave for lodging the heart, where, united with the sense, it rules the whole life.

"God having thus created man, he put into man a light which is called reason, which was to unite the flesh, the sense, and the soul in one end — to work for the service of God. Whereupon, this work being placed in paradise, and reason being seduced by the sense through the working of Satan, the flesh lost its rest, the sense lost the delight wherein it lives, and the soul lost its beauty.

"Man being left in such a plight, the sense — which finds no rest in labour but seeks delight — not being curbed by reason, follows the light which the eyes show it. Whence, the eyes not being able to see anything but vanity, it deceives itself, and so, choosing earthly things, it sins. Wherefore it is necessary that by the mercy of God the reason of man be enlightened anew, to know good from evil and the true delight; which, when he knows it, the sinner is converted to penitence. Therefore I tell you plainly that if God our Lord does not enlighten the heart of man, the reasonings of men are of no avail."

John answered: "Then to what end does the speech of men serve?"

Jesus answered: "Man as man avails nothing to convert man to penitence; but man as a means which God uses converts man. Wherefore, God working in a secret fashion in man for man's salvation, every man ought to be listened to, so that among all may be received him in whom God speaks to us."

### Notes — Chapter 123
1. **Apparatus as printed**: no Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR (see slice headnote). The English page running head reads "OF THE COMPOSITION OF MAN" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The scribe's Arabic glosses stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated; the 1907 notes render them: "Adam from mud"; "If God will"; "God is beneficent, the gracious, the powerful, good and just" (gloss *l*, printed "1" in the OCR); "God is the creator"; "God created Adam"; "God gives repentance and shows the true way"; "Whom he wishes"; "God is sovereign"; "God works (?) secretly in the sons of Adam." (The prayer's glosses on folio 131a belong to the previous chapter's close per the edition's chaptering and are not listed here.) The check's note at "let us sit" reads "Ps. lxxvii. 10 (cp. Vulg.)" — recorded as printed. No canonical parallel is asserted for this chapter; the check's citation above (Ps. lxxvii. 10, cp. Vulg.) is recorded as printed only — its wording answers to the David saying at the close of ch. 122 ('This change comes from the right hand of God'), not to anything in ch. 123.
2. **MS note recorded as printed**: at "called his disciples together and said" the apparatus notes "MS. suoi dissepoli he disse poll he disseli . sediamo : surely a mere dittography" — the printed text is followed.
3. **The composition teaching is kept whole**: the four contraries (earth, water, air, fire) united in the one final object, man; the vessel of flesh, bones, blood, marrow, skin, nerves, veins, and inward parts; soul and sense as the two hands of this life; the sense lodged through the body like oil, the soul lodged in the heart; reason as the unifying light; the paradise loss — the flesh lost its rest, the sense lost its delight, the soul lost its beauty; the uncurbed sense following the eyes into vanity; reason enlightened anew by God's mercy.
4. **Witness coverage**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only).

## Chapter 124

*[Editorial heading: The true teacher and the false — "everything that conforms to the book of Moses." Manuscript rubric "CXXIV." (folio 132); English head "CXXIV." The heading above is editorial.]*

James answered: "O Master, if by chance there should come a false prophet and a lying teacher, pretending to instruct us, what ought we to do?"

Jesus answered in parable: "A man goes with a net to fish, in which he catches many fishes — but those that are bad he throws away. A man went out to sow, but only the grain that falls on good ground bears fruit. Even so ought you to do: listen to everyone, and receive only the truth — for the truth alone bears fruit of eternal life."

Andrew answered: "Now how shall the truth be known?"

Jesus answered: "Everything that conforms to the book of Moses, that receive for true. For God is one, the truth is one; whence it follows that the doctrine is one, and the meaning of the doctrine is one — and therefore the faith is one. I tell you plainly that if the truth had not been erased from the book of Moses, God would not have given to David our father the second. And if the book of David had not been contaminated, God would not have committed the Gospel to me. For the Lord our God is unchangeable, and has spoken but one message to all men. Wherefore, when the messenger of God shall come, he will come to purify all that the ungodly have contaminated in my book."

Answered he who writes: "O Master, what shall a man do when the law shall be found contaminated and the false prophet shall speak?"

Jesus answered: "Great is your question, O Barnabas. Wherefore I tell you that in such a time few are saved, for men do not consider their end, which is God. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — every doctrine that turns man aside from his end, which is God, is a most evil doctrine. Wherefore you shall consider three things in doctrine — that is, love towards God, pity towards your neighbour, and hatred towards yourself, who have offended God and offend him every day. Wherefore every doctrine contrary to these three heads, flee it — for it is most evil."

### Notes — Chapter 124
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian column carries the rubric "CXXIV." between James's question and Jesus's answer — the edition's division matches the manuscript's here. The English page running head reads "THE TRUE TEACHER AND THE FALSE" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The scribe's Arabic glosses stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated; the 1907 notes render them: "God is one, one knowledge, one religion"; "God will not fail"; "God is holy"; "The apostle of God"; "By the living God." The check cites Matt. xiii. 3–9 at the sower. A canonical parallel is asserted: Matt. xiii. 3–9, the sower — the chapter gives the parable in compressed form ('only the grain that falls on good ground bears fruit'); the net clause beside it is the dragnet of Matt. xiii. 47–48. No other citation is recorded for this chapter.
2. **Seam-adjacent passage, translated exactly as written**: "when the messenger of God shall come, he will come to purify all that the ungodly have contaminated in my book" — Italian *quando uenira il nontio di Dio uera ha puriffichare quanto hauerano li empij chontaminato nel libro mio*. *Nontio* is kept distinct from *messia*; the Italian names no one, and no name is imported. Robert's recorded insertion-suspicion and the Qur'an-contradiction fork are noted here, never decided, smoothed, or deleted. (R4 pending.)
3. **The scribal-falsification sequence is kept whole**: the truth erased from the book of Moses, so God gave David "the second"; the book of David contaminated, so God committed the Gospel to Jesus; the Lord unchangeable, one message to all men.
4. **OCR point flagged, not resolved silently**: the Italian prints "che l se non f usi stato scancellato la uerita dal libro di moisse" (garbled); rendered "if the truth had not been erased from the book of Moses" following the check — debt recorded.
5. **MS note recorded as printed**: "MS. che che (sic)" — the manuscript doubles the word; the printed text is followed (the marker's exact position is not recoverable from the OCR).
6. **"He who writes" is named Barnabas in Jesus's reply** — kept as written (cf. the ch. 83 self-naming).
7. **Witness coverage**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only).

## Chapter 125

*[Editorial heading: Avarice turned into alms — "give of the best." No Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR (folio 133b); English head "cxxv a." The heading above is editorial.]*

"I will return now to avarice. And I tell you that when the sense would gather a thing, or tenaciously keep it, let reason say: 'Such a thing will have an end.' It is certain that if it will have an end, it is madness to love it. Wherefore one must love and keep that which will have no end.

"Let avarice then be turned into alms, giving rightly what was wrongly gathered. And let him see to it that what the right hand gives, the left hand does not know. For the hypocrites, when they give alms, want to be seen and praised by the world. But in truth they are vain — for whom a man works, from him he receives his wages. If then a man would receive anything from God, he must serve God.

"And see to it that when you give alms, you consider that you are giving to God all that you give for love of God. So do not be slow to give, and give of the best of what you have, for love of God.

"Tell me: would you want to receive anything bad from God? No, certainly — O dust and ashes! Then how is there faith in you, if you would give anything bad for love of God?

"It would be better to give nothing than to give a bad thing. For in not giving you will have some excuse according to the world — but in giving a worthless thing and keeping the best for yourselves, what will be the excuse?

"And this is all I have to say to you concerning penitence."

Bartholomew answered: "How long ought penitence to last?"

Jesus answered: "As long as a man is in a state of sin he ought always to repent and do penance for it. So as human life always sins, it ought always to do penance — unless you would make more account of your shoes than of your soul, since every time your shoes burst, you mend them."

### Notes — Chapter 125
1. **Apparatus as printed**: no Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR (see slice headnote). The English page running head reads "OF AVARICE AND ALMSGIVING" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The scribe's Arabic gloss at the head is rendered by the 1907 notes as "The Chapter on almsgiving," with further glosses: "If you wish to give alms, you shall give with the right hand while your left hand hears (sic) it not"; "To whomsoever you have done (it) your reward is incumbent on him"; "If you desire anything from God, you desire the best of things. If you do a deed of charity (almsgiving), give alms of what is good"; "According to what religion should he give alms of what is vile?"; "If you give unto God of what is vile, what can you say before God therewith?" The check cites Matt. vi. 3. A canonical parallel is asserted: Matt. vi. 3 ('let not thy left hand know what thy right hand doeth'), quoted in the chapter's alms teaching and in its setting (Matt. vi. 1–2, the hypocrites who give to be seen). No other citation is recorded for this chapter.
2. **Printed reading recorded, not resolved silently**: the Italian prints "quella * sera la scusa" (asterisk as printed); rendered "what will be the excuse?" following the check, with the editors' note "So MS.: conj. qualle" recorded — debt flagged.
3. **The alms teaching is kept whole**: "what the right hand gives, the left hand does not know"; the hypocrites wanting to be seen and praised by the world; "for whom a man works, from him he receives his wages"; give of the best for love of God; the "dust and ashes" address.
4. **Bartholomew's question and the shoes-and-soul saying are kept entire.**
5. **Witness coverage**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only).

## Chapter 126

*[Editorial heading: The mission of the disciples, two by two — "God has wrought it." Manuscript rubric "CXXVI*." (folio 134b), asterisk as printed; English head "CXXVI a." The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus, having called his disciples together, sent them two by two through the region of Israel, saying: "Go and preach even as you have heard." And they, bowing down, he laid his hand upon their heads, saying: "In the name of God, give health to the sick, cast out the demons, and undeceive Israel concerning me, telling them what I said before the high priest."

So they all departed — except him who writes, with James and John — and went through all Judea, preaching penitence as Jesus had told them, healing every kind of sickness, so that in Israel the words of Jesus were confirmed: that God is one, and Jesus is prophet of God.

When they saw so great a multitude doing what Jesus did in healing the sick — but the sons of the devil found another way to persecute Jesus, and these were the priests and the scribes. So they began to say that Jesus aspired to the kingship over Israel, but feared the common people; so they plotted secretly against Jesus.

When they had gone through Judea, the disciples returned to Jesus, who received them as a father receives his sons, saying: "Tell me, how has the Lord our God worked? Truly I have seen Satan fall under your feet, and you trample him as the vinedresser tramples the grape."

They answered: "O master, we have healed countless sick folk, and have cast out many demons that tormented men."

Jesus said: "God pardon you, O brethren — for you have sinned in saying 'We have healed,' seeing that God has done it all."

Then they said: "We have spoken like fools. Teach us, then, how we ought to speak."

Jesus answered: "In every good work say 'God has wrought it,' and in every bad one say 'I have sinned.'"

"So we will do," said the disciples.

Then Jesus said: "Now what does Israel say, having seen God do by the hands of so many men what God has done by my hands?"

The disciples answered: "They say that God is alone, and that you are prophet of God."

Jesus answered with a joyful face: "Blessed be the holy name of God, who has not despised the desire of me his servant." And when he had said this, they went to take rest.

### Notes — Chapter 126
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian column carries the rubric "CXXVI*." (asterisk exactly as printed; see slice headnote). The English page running head reads "MISSION OF THE DISCIPLES" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The scribe's Arabic glosses stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated; the 1907 notes render them: "The Chapter of [?] associating others with God"; "By the permission of God"; "God is one and Jesus is his prophet"; "God is sovereign"; "God pardons." The check's citations: Mark vi. 7–13; Luke x. 18; Luke x. 17. Canonical parallels are asserted: Mark vi. 7–13 (the sending two by two, to heal the sick and cast out demons, preaching penitence); Luke x. 17 (the disciples' return, reporting the demons cast out); and Luke x. 18 ('I beheld Satan as lightning fall'), alluded to in 'I have seen Satan fall under your feet' — the trampling image is this text's own. The editors' citations stand as printed, as above.
2. **The mission charge is kept whole**: two by two through the region of Israel; "in the name of God give health to the sick, cast out the demons, and undeceive Israel concerning me, telling them what I said before the high priest" (the ch. 93–95 confession). "Him who writes" stays behind with James and John — kept as written.
3. **The priests-and-scribes plot is kept as written**: the charge that Jesus aspired to the kingship over Israel, the note that they feared the common people, and their secret plotting — with the text's own "sons of the devil" naming, not softened.
4. **The "We have healed" correction is kept entire**: rebuked, then taught — "in every good work say 'God has wrought it,' and in every bad one say 'I have sinned'"; Israel's verdict, "God is alone, and you are prophet of God," kept as written.
5. **OCR points recorded as printed, none resolved silently**: "datte la sanita talii inffermi" (rendered "give health to the sick," following the check — "talii" unresolved in the OCR); "predich?.ndo"; "temeua|no" (rendered "feared").
6. **Witness coverage**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only).

## Chapter 127

*[Editorial heading: Jesus preaches at Jerusalem — against self-exaltation. Manuscript rubric "CXXVII*." (folio 135b), asterisk as printed; no English-column head recovered in the OCR. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus departed from the desert and entered into Jerusalem. So all the people ran to the temple to see him. Then, after the reading of the psalms, Jesus went up upon the pinnacle where the scribe used to go up, and, having made a sign of silence with his hand, said: "Blessed be the holy name of God, O brethren, who has created us of the clay of the earth, and not of flaming spirit. For when we sin, we find mercy with God — which Satan will never find, because through his pride he is incorrigible, always saying that he is noble because he is flaming spirit.

"Have you heard, brethren, what our father David says of our God — that he has remembered that we are dust, and that our spirit goes and does not return? That is why he has had mercy on us. Blessed are they that know these words, for they will not sin against their Lord forever — for after the sin they repent, and so their sin does not last. Woe to them that exalt themselves, for they shall be humbled down to the burning coals of hell.

"Tell me, brethren: what is the cause for exalting oneself? Is there any good thing here upon earth? No, certainly — for as Solomon, prophet of God, says: everything that is under the sun is vanity. But if the things of the world give us no cause to exalt ourselves in our heart, much less does our life give us cause — for it is burdened with many miseries, since all the creatures below man fight against us. O how many has the burning heat of summer slain! O how many has the frost and cold of winter slain! How many have been slain by lightning and hail! How many have been drowned in the sea by the fury of the winds! How many have died of pestilence, of famine — or because they were devoured by wild beasts, bitten by serpents, choked by food! O unhappy man, who exalts himself, with so many counterweights, besieged by all the creatures in every place!

"But what shall I say of the flesh and the sense, that desire only iniquity; of the world, that offers nothing but sin; of the reprobates, who, serving Satan, persecute whoever wants to live according to the law of God? Certain it is, brethren, that if man — as our father David says — with his eyes considered eternity, he would not sin.

"To exalt oneself in one's heart is nothing but to shut up the pity and mercy of God, so that he does not pardon him. For our father David says that our God has remembered that we are dust, and that our spirit goes and does not return. Whoever exalts himself, then, denies that he is dust; and so, not knowing his need, he does not ask for help — and that is how he angers God his helper. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands: God would pardon Satan, if Satan knew his own misery and asked mercy of his Creator, who is blessed forever."

### Notes — Chapter 127
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian column carries the rubric "CXXVII*." (asterisk exactly as printed; see slice headnote); no English-column head was recovered in the OCR — the English text runs continuously from ch. 126's close. The English page running head reads "JESUS PREACHETH AT JERUSALEM" — the printer's head, not a manuscript title. The scribe's Arabic glosses stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated; the 1907 notes render them: "God is Lord"; "God is one and Jesus is a prophet"; "In the name of God"; "The Chapter on the sons of Adam"; "God the gracious." The check's citations: Ps. ciii. 14–17; Eccles. i. 2. Canonical parallels are asserted: Ps. ciii. 14 (David's 'he remembereth that we are dust,' quoted in the sermon; the editors' wider range, vv. 14–17, stands as their comparison) and Eccles. i. 2 (Solomon's verdict that all under the sun is vanity). The clause 'our spirit goes and does not return' is this text's own wording; no parallel is asserted for it.
2. **The anti-exaltation sermon is kept whole**: clay, not flaming spirit; Satan incorrigible in pride; David's "we are dust"; the miseries catalogue — summer heat, winter frost, lightning and hail, drowning, pestilence, famine, wild beasts, serpents, choking food; flesh, sense, world, and reprobates; self-exaltation as shutting up God's pity and mercy; the denial of being dust.
3. **"God would pardon Satan, if Satan knew his own misery and asked mercy of his Creator"** — Italian *che DIG perdoneria ha satana se satana chonossesi la sua misseria he dimandassi misserichordia al suo chreatore* — translated exactly as written, never softened.
4. **OCR points flagged, not resolved silently**: "il dessiderio" prints across the gloss as "desside … io"; "cholloro" prints "chollojro"; rendered per sense with the check — debt recorded. At "choked by food" the check's note offers "Or obstructed" — recorded.
5. **Witness coverage**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only).

## Chapter 128

*[Editorial heading: Against the idols of the nations — the Pharisee and the publican. Manuscript rubric (Italian column) "CXXVIII c." at folio 137a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Accordingly, brethren, I, a man, dust and clay, that walk upon the earth, say to you: Do penance and know your sins. I say, brethren, that Satan by means of the Roman soldiery deceived you when you said that I was God. Wherefore, beware that you believe them not, seeing they are fallen under the curse of God, serving the false and lying gods; even as our father David invokes a curse upon them, saying: "The gods of the nations are silver and gold, the work of their hands; that have eyes and see not, have ears and hear not, have noses and smell not, have a mouth and eat not, have a tongue and speak not, have hands and touch not, have feet and walk not." Wherefore said David our father, praying our living God: "Like to them be they that make them and they that trust in them."

O pride unheard-of, this of man, who, being created by God out of earth, forgets his condition and would fain make God at his own pleasure! Wherein he silently mocks God, as though he should say: "There is no use in serving God." For so do their works show. To this did Satan desire to reduce you, O brethren, in making you believe me to be God; because, I not being able to create a fly, and being passible and mortal, I can give you nothing of use, seeing that I myself have need of everything. How, then, could I help you in all things, as it is proper to God to do?

Shall we, then, who have for our God the great God who has created the universe with his word, mock at the Gentiles and their gods?

There were two men who came up here into the temple to pray: the one was a Pharisee and the other a publican. The Pharisee drew nigh to the sanctuary, and praying with his face uplifted said: "I give you thanks, O Lord my God, because I am not as other men, sinners, who do every wickedness, and particularly as this publican; for I fast twice in the week and give tithes of all I possess."

The publican remained afar off, bowed down to the earth, and beating his breast he said with bent head: "Lord, I am not worthy to look upon the heaven nor upon your sanctuary, for I have sinned much; have mercy upon me!" Truly I say to you, the publican went down from the temple in better case than the Pharisee, for that our God justified him, forgiving him all his sin. But the Pharisee went down in worse case than the publican, because our God rejected him, having his works in abomination.

### Notes — Chapter 128
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on Thou shalt not worship idols." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God aids"; "By the living God"; "The curse of God on the polytheists"; "God is living"; "God is great, the creator of all, there is no power save with God"; "God is sovereign"; "God is wise." A line of the scribe's Arabic gloss stands in the Italian column and is recorded, not translated. The English page running heads ("JESUS PREACHETH AT JERUSALEM," "THE PHARISEE AND THE PUBLICAN") are the printer's heads, not manuscript titles. Their footnote cites Ps. cxv. (printed "Ps. cxv. 48.").
2. **"I say" is bracketed in the printed Italian** — *io [ui dicho] ho fratelli* — an editorial insertion by the 1907 editors; it is rendered with the check and recorded as printed, not resolved silently.
3. **The anti-divinity thread is seam context, translated exactly as written**: "I, a man, dust and clay"; "I not being able to create a fly, and being passible and mortal, I can give you nothing of use." Nothing is smoothed. The Italian names no one, and no name is imported. Robert's recorded insertion-suspicion and the Qur'an-contradiction fork stand noted for the named seam at ch. 96–97 (slice 20) — carried here as context, never decided.
4. **"DIOC"** (*io ti ringratio signore DIOC mio*) is rendered "O Lord my God" with the check; the printed form is recorded.
5. **MS note recorded**: at "who hath created the universe" the printed apparatus notes "MS. il il (sic)" — the manuscript doubles the word (*che il il tutto ha chreato*); the printed text is followed.
6. **Single-witness flag**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only). The next manuscript rubric, "CXXIXa." (folio 138a), is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 129

*[Editorial heading: Pride — the axe, the fallen angel, and the woman at Simon's house. Manuscript rubric (Italian column) "CXXIXa." — recorded verbatim as printed — at folio 138a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Shall the axe, perchance, boast itself at having cut down the forest where a man has made a garden? Nay, assuredly, for the man has made all, and the axe, with his hands.

And you, O man, shall you boast thyself of having done aught that is good, seeing our God created you of clay, and works in you all good that is wrought?

And wherefore despise you your neighbour? Know you not that if God had not preserved you from Satan you wouldst be worse than Satan?

Now know you not that one single sin changed the fairest angel into the most repulsive demon? And that the most perfect man that has come into the world, which was Adam, it changed into a wretched being, subjecting him to what we suffer, together with all his offspring? What decree, then, have you, in virtue whereof you may live at your own pleasure without any fear? Woe to you, O clay, for because you have exalted thyself above God who created you, you shall be abased beneath the feet of Satan who lays wait for you.

And having said this, Jesus prayed, lifting up his hands to the Lord, and the people said: "So be it! So be it!" When he had finished the prayer he descended from the pinnacle. Whereupon there were brought to him many sick folk whom he made whole, and he departed from the temple.

Thereupon Simon, a leper whom Jesus had cleansed, invited him to eat bread.

The priests and scribes, who hated Jesus, reported to the Roman soldiers that which Jesus had said against their gods. For indeed they were seeking how to kill him, but found it not, because they feared the people.

Jesus, having entered the house of Simon, sat down to the table. And while he was eating, behold a woman named Mary, a public sinner, entered into the house, and flung herself upon the ground behind Jesus' feet, and washed them with her tears, and anointed them with precious ointment, and wiped them with the hairs of her head.

Simon was scandalized, with all that sat at meat, and they said in their hearts: "If this man were a prophet he would know who and of what sort is this woman, and would not suffer her to touch him."

Then said Jesus: "Simon, I have a thing to say to you." Simon answered: "Speak, O Master, for I desire your word."

### Notes — Chapter 129
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on pride." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God created Adam from mud"; "God keeps"; "God is the creator." A line of the scribe's Arabic gloss stands in the Italian column and is recorded, not translated. The English page running head ("JESUS IN SIMON'S HOUSE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their footnotes cite Isa. x. 15 (the axe); Matt. xxvi. 6; Luke vii. 36–50; John xi. 2 — with the editors' own note that the identification of this Mary with the Mary of John xi. 2 "is traditional, but probably not correct," recorded as printed, not adopted.
2. **The rubric is recorded verbatim**: "CXXIXa." — the trailing "a" stands as printed; it may be the folio-side marker for 138a rather than a gloss letter. The English column's gloss letter is garbled in the OCR ("CXXIX <*.") and is never imported.
3. **"With precious ointment"** renders the Italian *chon onto preciosso*, recorded as printed, with the check.
4. **"Sat down to the table"** renders *si possero ha messa*; the Italian verb is plural as printed — recorded, not emended.
5. **Single-witness flag**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only). The next manuscript rubric, "CXXX«." (folio 139b), is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 130

*[Editorial heading: The two debtors — "Your faith has saved you." Manuscript rubric (Italian column) "CXXX«." — recorded verbatim as printed — at folio 139b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus said: "There was a man who had two debtors. The one owed to his creditor fifty pence, the other five hundred. Whereupon, when neither of them had wherewithal to pay, the owner, moved with compassion, forgave the debt to each. Which of them would love his creditor most?"

Simon answered: "He to whom was forgiven the greater debt."

Said Jesus: "You have well said; I say to you, therefore, behold this woman and thyself; for you were both debtors to God, the one for leprosy of the body, the other for leprosy of the soul, which is sin.

"God our Lord, moved with compassion through my prayers, has willed to heal your body and her soul. You, therefore, love me little, because you have received little as a gift. And so, when I entered your house you didst not kiss me nor anoint my head. But this woman, lo! straightway on entering your house she placed herself at my feet, which she has washed with her tears and anointed with precious ointment. Wherefore truly I say to you, many sins are forgiven her, because she has loved much." And turning to the woman he said: "Go your way in peace, for the Lord our God has pardoned your sins; but see you sin no more. Your faith has saved you."

### Notes — Chapter 130
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on the Giver." — the English-column rubric line for CXXX is missing from the djvu OCR (the English text runs continuously from chapter 129's close into the debtors parable); the gloss survives in the page footnotes, which is how the title is recovered. Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God is merciful, God is sovereign"; "God is sovereign and pardons." The English page running head ("JESUS IN SIMON'S HOUSE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their footnote cites John viii. 11.
2. **The rubric is recorded verbatim**: "CXXX«." — the trailing mark is OCR'd as « and is illegible; flagged, never resolved by importing the English column's letter.
3. **"Fifty pence"** renders the Italian *cinquanta soldi*; the Italian word is recorded, the check's "pence" followed.
4. **"Forgave the debt to each"** renders *ha tutti dui dono il debito qualle* — the printed *quale* is kept as printed, the check's turn followed; recorded, not emended.
5. **Single-witness flag**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only). The next manuscript rubric, "CXXXId." (folio 140a), is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 131

*[Editorial heading: How to escape pride — John at Herod's table. Manuscript rubric (Italian column) "CXXXId." at folio 140a. The heading above is editorial.]*

His disciples drew nigh to Jesus after the nightly prayer, and said: "O Master, how must we do to escape pride?"

Jesus answered: "Have you seen a poor man invited to a prince's house to eat bread?"

John answered: "I have eaten bread in Herod's house. For before I knew you I went to fish, and used to sell the fish to the family of Herod. Whereupon, one day when he was feasting, I having brought thither a fine fish, he made me stay and eat there."

Then said Jesus: "Now how didst you eat bread with infidels? God pardon you, O John! But tell me, how didst you bear thyself at the board? Didst you seek to have the most honourable place? Didst you ask for the most delicate food? Didst you speak when you were not questioned at the table? Didst you account thyself more worthy than the others to sit at table?"

John answered: "As God lives, I did not dare to lift up my eyes, seeing myself, a poor fisherman, ill-clad, sitting among the king's barons. Whereupon, when the king gave me a little piece of flesh, methought that the world had fallen upon my head, for the greatness of the favour that the king did to me. And truly I say that, if the king had been of our Law, I should have been fain to serve him all the days of my life."

Jesus cried out: "Hold your peace, John, for I fear lest God cast us into the abyss, even like Abiram, for our pride!"

The disciples trembled with fear at the words of Jesus; when he said again: "Let us fear God, that He cast us not into the abyss for our pride.

"O brethren, have you heard of John what is done in the house of a prince? Woe to the men that come into the world, for as they live in pride they shall die in contempt and shall go into confusion.

"For this world is a house where God feasts men, wherein have eaten all the holy ones and prophets of God. And truly I say to you, everything that a man receiveth, he receiveth it from God. Wherefore man ought to bear himself with deepest humility; knowing his own vileness and the greatness of God, with the great bounty wherewith he nourishes us. Therefore it is not lawful for man to say: 'Ah, why is this done and this said in the world?' but rather to account himself, as in truth he is, unworthy to stand in the world at God's board. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands, there is nothing so small received here in the world from God, but that in return man ought to spend his life for love of God.

"As God lives, you sinnedst not, O John, in eating with Herod, for it was of God's disposition you didst so, in order that you mightest be our teacher and the teacher of every one that fears God. So do," said Jesus to his disciples, "that you may live in the world as John lived in the house of Herod when he ate bread with him, for so shall you be in truth free from all pride."

### Notes — Chapter 131
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on the mean (lowly)." Their marginal notes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks: "God pardons"; "By the living God"; "God is great and Lord of all." The English page running head ("JOHN EATETH AT HEROD'S TABLE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Their footnote cites Num. xvi (Abiram).
2. **"Abiram"** renders the Italian *habiro*; the Italian spelling is recorded and the check's identification followed.
3. **"Have ye heard of John"** renders *auete uoi intesso ioane* — the Italian can also be read as addressed to John ("have ye heard, John"); the check's turn is followed and the ambiguity flagged.
4. **"For love of God"** renders *pe[r] ammore de DIG* — the *r* is bracketed in the printed Italian; recorded as printed.
5. **Single-witness flag**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only). The next manuscript rubric, "CXXXII." (folio 141b), is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 132

*[Editorial heading: Parables by the sea — the sower, the tares, the figs, the fountain, the apples. Manuscript rubric (Italian column) "CXXXII." at folio 141b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus walking along the sea of Galilee was surrounded by a great multitude of folk, whereupon he went up into a little boat, which drew off from land by itself; and it anchored near land, near enough for the voice of Jesus to be heard. Whereupon they all drew nigh to the sea, and sitting down awaited his word. He then opened his mouth and said: "Behold, the sower went out to sow, whereupon as he sowed some of the seed fell upon the road, and this was trodden under foot of men and eaten up of birds; some fell upon the stones, whereupon when it sprang up, because it had no moisture, it was burnt up by the sun; some fell in the hedges, whereupon when it grew up the thorns choked the seed; and some fell on good ground, whereupon it bare fruit, even to thirtyfold, sixtyfold, and a hundredfold."

Again Jesus said: "Behold, the father of a family sowed good seed in his field: whereupon, as the servants of the good man slept, the enemy of the man, their master, came and sowed tares over the good seed. Whereupon, when the corn sprang up, there was seen sprung up among the corn a great quantity of tares. The servants came to their master and said: 'O sir, didst you not sow good seed in your field? Wherefore then is there sprung up therein a great quantity of tares?' The master answered: 'Good seed did I sow, but while men slept the enemy of man came and sowed tares over the corn.' Said the servants: 'Will you that we go and pull up the tares from among the corn?' The master answered: 'Do not so, for you would pull up the corn therewith; but wait till the time of harvest comes. For then shall you go and pull up the tares from among the corn and cast them into the fire to be burned, but the corn you shall put into my granary.'"

Again Jesus said: "There went forth many men to sell figs. But when they arrived at the market-place, behold, men sought not good figs but fair leaves. Therefore the men were not able to sell their figs. And seeing this, an evil citizen said: 'Surely I may become rich.' Whereupon he called together his two sons and said: 'Go you and gather a great quantity of leaves with bad figs.' And these they sold at the price of gold, for the men were mightily pleased with leaves. Whereupon the men, eating the figs, became sick with a grievous sickness."

Again Jesus said: "Behold, a citizen has a fountain, from which all the neighbouring citizens take water to wash off their uncleanness; but the citizen suffers his own clothes to putrefy."

Again Jesus said: "There went forth two men to sell apples. The one chose to sell the peel of the apple for its weight in gold, caring nought for the substance of the apples. The other desired to give the apples away, receiving only a little bread for his journey. But men bought the peel of the apples for its weight in gold, caring nought for him who was fain to give them, nay even despising him."

And thus on that day Jesus spake to the crowd in parables. Then having dismissed them, he went with his disciples to Nain, where he had raised to life the widow's son; who, with his mother, received him into his house and ministered to him.

### Notes — Chapter 132
1. **Apparatus as printed**: neither column carries a chapter-title gloss for CXXXII. The editors' footnotes gloss the scribe's Arabic marks "God gives" and "By the living God," and cite Matt. xiii. 1–8 (the sower) and Matt. xiii. 24–30 (the tares). The English page running head ("PARABLES OF THE SOWER AND THE TARES") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title.
2. **"Which drew off from land by itself"** renders *la quale si scosto da terra per se stessa* — "by itself" is as printed; the check reads "which lay a little off from the shore by itself." The Italian verb *si ferino* ("they stopped/anchored") is third-person plural with an unexpressed subject; the rendering keeps the check's impersonal turn. Both oddities are recorded, not smoothed.
3. **"Tares"** renders the Italian *holgio* (darnel); the Italian word is recorded, the check's "tares" followed. **"Field"** renders *chapo* (= *campo*); recorded.
4. **"Thirtyfold, sixtyfold, and a hundredfold"** renders the Italian ordinals *trentessimo, sessagessimo, centessimo*; the Italian forms are recorded, the check's turn followed.
5. **Printed readings recorded, none resolved silently**: *n5* in *li homeni che n5 cerchauano* (read "sought not" — the mark stands for *non*); *[dete]* in *he [dete] andete* (the editors' bracketed insertion, rendered "said"); a stray "{" in the printed Italian at the master's reply (*buono seme seminai {*), ignored in rendering.
6. **"Nain"** renders the Italian *naim*; the check's OCR reads "ISTain" at this point — the Italian spelling is followed and the check's garble recorded.
7. **Single-witness flag**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only). The next manuscript rubric, "CXXXIII <*." (folio 143a), is confirmed in the Italian column and opens the next slice.

## Chapter 133

*[Editorial heading: The parables interpreted — the sower and the tares. Manuscript rubric printed "CXXXIII <*." — gloss letter illegible in the OCR of both columns (the English-page occurrence reads "CXXXIII *."); recorded verbatim as printed, no letter imported. The heading above is editorial.]*

His disciples drew near to Jesus, and asked him, saying: "O Master, tell us the meaning of the parables that you spoke to the people." Jesus answered: "The hour of prayer draws near; so when the evening prayer is ended I will tell you the meaning of the parables." When the prayer was ended, the disciples drew near to Jesus, and he said to them: "The man who sows upon the road, upon the stones, upon the thorns, and upon the good ground, is he who teaches the word of God, which falls upon a great number of men. It falls upon the road when it comes to the ears of sailors and merchants, who, by reason of the long journeys they make and the variety of nations with whom they deal, have the word of God taken from their memory by Satan. It falls upon the stones when it comes to the ears of men of the court, for by reason of the great care they have to serve the body of a prince the word of God does not sink into them; so that although they have some memory of it, as soon as they have any trouble the word of God goes out of their memory, for, seeing they serve not God, they cannot hope for help from God. It falls among the thorns when it comes to the ears of those who love their own life, so that, though the word of God grows in them, when carnal desires grow up they choke the good seed of the word of God, for carnal comforts make men forsake the word of God. That which falls upon good ground is when the word of God comes to the ears of him who fears God, so it brings forth fruit of eternal life. Truly I say to you, that in every condition, when man fears God, the word of God will bear fruit in him.

"Of that father of a family I tell you truly that he is God our Lord, father of all things, for he has created all things. But he is not a father by nature, for he is incapable of motion, without which nothing can be generated. Our God, then, whose this world is — his field where he sows is mankind, and the seed is the word of God. So when the teachers are negligent in preaching the word of God through being busy with the business of the world, Satan sows error in the heart of men, from which have come countless sects of wicked doctrine.

"The holy ones and prophets cry: 'O sir, did you not then give good doctrine to men? Why then are there so many errors?' God answers: 'I gave good doctrine to men, but while men have been given up to vanity Satan has sown errors to bring my law to nothing.' The holy ones say: 'O Lord, we will scatter these errors by destroying men.' God answers: 'Do not do this, for the faithful are so closely joined by kinship to the infidels that the faithful would be lost with the infidel; but wait until the judgment, for at that time the infidels shall be gathered by my angels and shall be cast out with Satan into hell. Then the faithful shall come into my kingdom. Certainly many infidel fathers shall beget faithful sons, for whose sake God waits for the world to repent.'"

### Notes — Chapter 133
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter." Their marginal notes: "Who serves not God cannot ask assistance from God" (at the courtiers' failing memory); "God aids"; "God is sovereign" (at "our God, whose this world is"). The English running heads ("FURTHER PARABLES"; "THE PARABLES INTERPRETED") are the printer's heads, not manuscript titles. The check's footnote on "the evening prayer" cites an earlier note (87*). No canonical parallel is asserted; the editors' citation above (87*, their own note on the hours of prayer) is an internal cross-reference and is recorded as printed only. The page's canonical footnotes, Matt. xiii. 10 and Matt. xiii. 18–23, are resolved at ch. 135, note 4.
2. **Folio marker**: the Italian column's first folio figure in this slice OCRs as "143k" — recorded verbatim; the following folios read 144a, 144b, 145a.
3. **"Father of a family"** renders *padre di familgia* (the parable of the householder); the Italian itself keeps the *padre* language for God and then denies natural fatherhood — kept exactly as written, nothing smoothed.
4. **OCR garbles recorded**: *elgie i>io signore* read as "elgie dio signore" ("he is God our Lord"); *quando sono negligen j ti li dottori* read as "negligenti" (negligent). The phrase *pero DIG non uolle* at the courtiers is garbled in OCR but legible in the check's column; the Italian is followed, the garble noted.
5. **DIG / DIO variation**: the Italian uses both *DIG* and *DIO* in this chapter (e.g. *seruendo DIG* beside *parlla DIO*); both are kept as printed, flagged as the scribe's own variation (R-pending territory — not decided).
6. **Witness coverage**: Italian-only witness — no Spanish control (Sydney transcript covers ch. 1–120 only). The next rubric, "CXXXIV" (no gloss letter in either column), is confirmed in the Italian column.
