## Chapter 171

*[Editorial heading: How great is paradise — Herod's gifts and God's. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXId" — i.e. CLXXI with gloss letter d) at folio 186b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"What do you think," Jesus said to his disciples, "of paradise? Is there a mind that can take in such riches and delights? A man would need to have as much knowledge as God has, if he wanted to know how much God will give to His servants.

"Have you seen how Herod gives a present to one of his favourite barons — the way he presents it?"

John answered: "I have seen it twice — and surely a tenth of what he gives would be enough for a poor man."

Jesus said: "But if a poor man were brought before Herod, what would he give him?"

John answered: "One or two mites."

"Now let this be your book to study, to know paradise by. For what God has given to man in this world for the body is like Herod giving one mite to a poor man. But what God will give to soul and body in paradise is like Herod giving all he has — his very life — to one of his servants."

### Notes — Chapter 171
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter on paradise" (gloss key d, shared with the preceding English-column rubric — recorded as printed). Their numbered footnote stands at this chapter: "For the mystic tendency of this chapter, suggesting Suffism, cp. (p. 55) and 159b (p. 339); and see Introd." — recorded, not judged. The English page running head ("GOD THE REWARD OF HIS SERVANTS," p. 393) is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 186b, 187a (printed "(186a)") in the Italian column.
2. **"Herod," "John"** render the Italian *herode*, *ioane* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory. **"Mites"** renders *minuti*, with the check.
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *suoig* (suoi with gloss marker); *dara"* (darà with marker — the OCR's stray quote-mark stands for the marker). None is resolved silently.
4. **The Herod similitude is kept whole**: the favourite baron's present; the tenth part enough for a poor man; the poor man's one or two mites; the book to study; the mite-to-a-poor-man against all-Herod-has-and-his-own-life. Nothing is smoothed between the members.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "CLXXIIb.," is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 172

*[Editorial heading: The sand of the sea — this world beside paradise. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXIIb.", gloss letter b) at folio 187a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"God says this to him who loves Him and serves Him faithfully: 'Go and consider, O My servant, the sand of the sea — see how much it is. If the sea gave you a single grain of sand, would it seem small to you? Surely, yes. As I your Creator live — all that I have given in this world to all the princes and kings of the earth is less than that grain of sand the sea would give you, compared to what I will give you in My paradise.'"

## Chapter 173

*[Editorial heading: The abundance of paradise — shall our body go into paradise? No Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR for this chapter (see Notes). The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Jesus said: "Consider the abundance of paradise. For if God has given man in this world an ounce of good, in paradise he will give him ten hundred thousand loads. Consider the quantity of fruits that are in this world, the quantity of foods, the quantity of flowers, and the quantity of things that serve man. As God lives — in whose presence my soul stands — as the sea still has sand left when one takes a grain from it, so the quality and quantity of figs surpasses the sort of figs we eat here. And so with every other thing in paradise. But more than this I tell you in truth: as a mountain of gold and pearls is more precious than the shadow of an ant, so are the delights of paradise more precious than all the delights of the princes of the world, which they have had and shall have until the judgment of God, when the world shall have an end."

Peter answered: "Shall our body, then, which we now have, go into paradise?"

Jesus answered: "Beware, Peter, lest you become a Sadducee — for the Sadducees say that the flesh shall not rise again, and that there are no angels. So they are deprived of their soul and body going into paradise, and they are deprived of having any service from the angels in this world. Have you perhaps forgotten Job, prophet and friend of God, who says: 'I know that my God lives, and in the last day I shall rise again in my flesh, and with my eyes I shall see God my saviour'? But believe me: this flesh of ours shall be so purified that it shall have none of the properties that it has now — for it shall be purged of every evil desire, and God shall bring it back to the state that Adam was in before he sinned.

"Two servants serve one master in one same work. The one only sees the work and commands the second, and the second works as much as the first commands. Does it seem just to you, I say, that the master should reward only the one who sees and commands, and drive out of the house the one who laboured in the work? Surely not. Now how shall the justice of God bear it, that — the soul and the body serving God with the sense of man — the soul alone should see and command the service? For the soul, eating no bread, does not fast; it does not walk; it does not feel cold and heat; it does not fall sick, nor is it slain — for the soul is immortal, which suffers none of these bodily pains that the body suffers by way of the elements. Is it just, I say, that she alone should go into paradise, and not the body that laboured so much serving God?"

Peter answered: "O master, the body, having made the soul sin, ought not to be placed in paradise."

Jesus answered: "Now how shall the body sin without the soul? Assuredly it is impossible. So in taking away God's mercy from the body, you condemn the soul to hell."

### Notes — Chapter 173
1. **Rubric not recovered — flagged honestly**: no Italian-column rubric for ch. 173 was recovered in the OCR. The Italian text runs continuously from the end of ch. 172 ("parradisso mio.") to "Ora disse iessu chonsiderate la abondantia del parradisso." The English column gives "CLXXIIIt)." (gloss letter t) at the same break — recorded check-only, not imported as base. The break is placed by narrative continuity following the edition's editorial division (the ch. 96/119/123/125 pattern: no Italian-column rubric = the edition's editorial division). The editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter on paradise." Their marginal glosses: "God gives" (a, on *ha datoa*), "God lives, creates, and gives" (c, OCR "ives"), "By the living God" (d, on *Viue Diod*); at the chapter's end, "God is wise" (the "a" not recovered in OCR; probably on *guarda pietro*), "God lives" (b, on *DIG mio uiueb*), "God guards" (c, on *saluatore mioc*). Canonical parallels asserted: Acts xxiii. 8 (the Sadducees say there is no resurrection and no angel), at the warning to Peter; and Job xix. 25–27 ('I know that my redeemer liveth… in my flesh shall I see God… mine eyes shall behold'), at the Job saying as quoted. No Quranic parallel is asserted for this chapter: the check's note 'For the fruits of paradise, cp. Qoran, xiii, xlvii, and lvi' is a thematic cross-reference — paradise fruit appears at Q 13:35 (fruit perpetual), Q 47:15 (all kinds of fruits), and Q 56:20, 28–33 (fruit abundant) — and it stands, with the editors' other citations, as printed record only. The English page running heads ("OF THE ABUNDANCE OF PARADISE"; "OF THE BODY IN PARADISE") are the printers' heads, not manuscript titles.
2. **Folio marks and scribe's gloss**: Vienna MS folio marks (i86b), 187b ("18 7b" in OCR), and 188a (embedded mid-text) fall in the Italian column. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column at the chapter's end ("41)1. b 41)1. C Ji-jU 41)1." in OCR) and are recorded, not translated.
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: "dieci cento millia some" is rendered literally ("ten hundred thousand loads"), not smoothed into a round number. The two-servants similitude and the soul/body justice argument are kept whole, exactly as the Italian sets them.
4. **"Job," "Adam," "Sadducee"** render the Italian *iob, addamo, saduceo* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "188b CLXXIV *.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 174

*[Editorial heading: Who shall eat the foods of paradise — the incorruptible body. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "188b CLXXIV *." — trailing gloss-letter illegible in the OCR; recorded verbatim, not resolved) at folio 188b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"As God lives — in whose presence my soul stands — our God promises his mercy to the sinner, saying: 'In the hour when the sinner grieves over his sin, by myself I will not remember his iniquities forever.'

"Now who would eat the foods of paradise if the body did not go there? The soul? Surely not, seeing that she is spirit."

Peter answered: "Then the blessed shall eat in paradise — but how shall the food come out without uncleanness?"

Jesus answered [uncertain: printed "Bissposse iessu," OCR for "Rissposse" — read with the check]: "Now what blessedness shall the body have, if it neither eats nor drinks? Assuredly it is fitting to give glory in proportion to the one glorified. But you err, Peter, in thinking that such food shall come out in uncleanness — for this body at present eats corruptible foods, and so putrefaction comes forth. But in paradise the body shall be incorruptible, impassible, and immortal, free from every misery; and the foods, which are without any defect, shall generate no putrefaction at all."

### Notes — Chapter 174
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter on paradise." Their marginal glosses: "By the living God" (b, on *Viue Diob*), "God the gracious" (c, on *pechatorec*). The English column's rubric reads "CLXXIVa." (gloss letter a) — divergence from the Italian column's illegible gloss letter, recorded, not reconciled. Canonical parallel asserted: Ezek. xviii. 21, 22 — the wicked who turns from his sins shall live, and 'all his transgressions that he hath committed, they shall not be mentioned unto him' — for the chapter's saying, 'In the hour when the sinner grieves over his sin, by myself I will not remember his iniquities forever.' The editors' other citations stand as printed record only. The English page running head ("OF THE BODY IN PARADISE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 188b (rubric line) and (i87b) in the Italian column.
2. **Printed reading kept as printed**: "Bissposse iessu" is the OCR's reading (B/R confusion for "Rissposse iessu," "Jesus answered"); it is read with the check and flagged here, not resolved silently.
3. **Kept literal**: "by myself" (*per me*) is the oath formula, kept as written; "incorruptible, impassible, and immortal" keeps the Italian's threefold *inchorutibille, impassibille, immortalle*.
4. **"Peter"** renders the Italian *pietro* — the Italian spelling, not canonical memory.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "189a CLXXVa.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 175

*[Editorial heading: God's word by Isaiah against the reprobate. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "189a CLXXVa.", gloss letter a) at folio 189a. The heading above is editorial.]*

God says thus in Isaiah the prophet, scorning the reprobate: "My servants shall sit at table in my house, and they shall feast joyfully with festivity and sounds of harps and organs, and I will let them lack nothing. But you who are my enemies shall be driven away from me, where you shall die of misery, while every servant of mine despises you."

### Notes — Chapter 175
1. **Apparatus as printed**: no chapter-title note is recovered for this chapter in the English column — recorded honestly, not supplied. The English column's rubric reads "CLXXV*. 189*" (gloss letter illegible; the "189*" is the Vienna MS folio) — divergence from the Italian column's gloss letter "a," recorded, not reconciled. Canonical parallel asserted for this chapter: Isa. lxv. 13 (with v. 14) — 'my servants shall eat, but ye shall be hungry… my servants shall rejoice, but ye shall be ashamed' — for the chapter's Isaiah saying of the servants feasting in God's house while his enemies are driven away to die of misery. The Ezek. xviii. 21, 22 citation in the same printed footnote block belongs to the preceding chapter's saying, is asserted there, and stands in this note as printed record only. The English page running head ("OF THE BODY IN PARADISE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 189a (rubric line), "188\")" (as printed in OCR), and (i88a) (English column).
2. **The Isaiah saying is kept whole** — the servants' feast and the enemies' casting-out stand entire, exactly as the Italian sets them, with the "harps and organs" (*citare he organi*) kept literal.
3. **"Isaiah"** renders the Italian *essaia* — the Italian spelling, not canonical memory.

## Chapter 176

*[Editorial heading: Why paradise has foods and rivers — degrees of glory without envy. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXVI*" — no folio mark recovered on the rubric line in the OCR, trailing gloss-letter illegible; recorded verbatim, not resolved). The heading above is editorial.]*

"Of what use is it to say, 'They shall feast'?" Jesus said to his disciples. "Assuredly, God speaks plainly. But of what use are four rivers of precious liquor in paradise, with so many fruits? Assuredly, God does not eat; the angels do not eat; the soul does not eat; the sense does not eat — but the flesh eats, which is our body. So the glory of paradise belongs to the body in the matter of foods, and to the soul and sense in the matter of God and the company of angels and blessed spirits. Which glory shall be better made manifest by the messenger of God [uncertain: the printed column reads "dallo nontio di mo," the trailing "a" being the editors' gloss-letter; the check reads "the messenger of God"] — who, God having created all things for love of him, knows all things better than every other creature."

Bartholomew said: "O master, shall the glory of paradise be equal for every man? If it be equal, it shall not be just; and if it be not equal, the lesser shall envy the greater."

Jesus answered: "It shall not be equal, for God is just, and everyone shall be content, because there is no envy there. Tell me, Bartholomew: there is a master who has many servants, and he clothes all his servants in one same cloth. Do the boys, then, who wear boys' clothes, grieve that they do not have the garments of grown men? Surely, quite the opposite! If the grown ones wanted to put their bigger garments on them, they would grow angry — for the garments not being their size, they would think they were being mocked. Now, Bartholomew, lift your heart to God in paradise, and you shall see that all one glory — though it shall be more to one and less to another — shall bring them no envy at all."

### Notes — Chapter 176
1. **The unnamed *nontio* passage, translated exactly as written**: "la qualle gloria sera melgio maniffesstata dallo nontio di moa il qualle hauendo DIG chreato il tutto per ammore suo chonossce melgio di ogni altra chreatura il tutto" — "Which glory shall be better made manifest by the messenger of God [uncertain] — who, God having created all things for love of him, knows all things better than every other creature." *Nontio* is rendered "messenger," never "messiah"; the Italian names no one here, and no name is imported. Nothing is smoothed, softened, or deleted. The fork is footnoted per the audit: Robert's recorded suspicion that the seam may be a later insertion is carried here rather than decided; the Quran-contradiction fork is noted, not decided.
2. **The [uncertain] on "mo"**: the printed Italian column reads "dallo nontio di moa," where the fused trailing "a" is the editors' gloss-letter (their gloss reads "The prophet of God"). The underlying word "mo" is doubtful in the OCR — it occurs nowhere else in the text. It is taken as "of God" with the check (which reads "the messenger of God"), flagged here rather than resolved silently. Preserved, not decided.
3. **Apparatus as printed**: the English column's rubric reads "CLXXVI*." — matching the Italian column. The editors' marginal glosses: "God and the angels and the spirit and the soul do not eat food. Inde." (d, on *il sensso non manggia d*; "Inde." is the editors' note), "The prophet of God" (a, on the *nontio* passage), "God, Creator" (b, on *hauendo DIG chreato*), "God is just" (c, on *DIO he iusto*). Quranic parallel asserted: Q 47:15 — paradise has rivers of water, of milk, of wine, and of purified honey, and in it all kinds of fruits — for the chapter's question, 'of what use are four rivers of precious liquor in paradise, with so many fruits?' The editors' other citations stand as printed record only. A line of the scribe's Arabic gloss ("41)1." in OCR) stands in the Italian column and is recorded, not translated. Folio mark 189b is embedded mid-text in the Italian column. The English page running heads ("OF THE BODY IN PARADISE"; "DIVERS DEGREES OF GLORY") are the printers' heads, not manuscript titles.
4. **Kept whole**: the four-rivers question, the flesh/body distinction, and the master-and-servants similitude (boys' clothes vs. grown men's garments) are kept entire, exactly as the Italian sets them — including the print's exclamation ("homeni grand!").
5. **"Bartholomew"** renders the Italian *bartolameo* — the Italian spelling, not canonical memory.
6. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXVIia." (Italian column; OCR for CLXXVII), is confirmed.

## Chapter 177

*[Editorial heading: The light of paradise — God the sun, his messenger the moon. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXVIia." — OCR; read as "CLXXVII" with gloss letter "a."; recorded verbatim as printed, not silently corrected) at folio 190a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then he who writes said: "O master, does paradise have sunlight as this world does?"

Jesus answered: "God has said thus to me, O Barnabas: 'The world where you sinful men live has the sun and the moon and the stars that adorn it, for your benefit and gladness — I created it for this. So do you think, then, that the house where my faithful shall dwell would be no better? Assuredly you err in thinking this: for I, your God, am the sun of paradise, and my messenger is the moon, who receives all from me. And the stars are my prophets, who have preached my will to you. So my faithful — as my prophets brought them my word — so in the paradise of my delights they shall receive, for their delight and gladness.'"

### Notes — Chapter 177
1. **The unnamed *nontio* passage, translated exactly as written**: "he il nontio e mio he la luna il qualle da me riceue il tutto" — "and my messenger is the moon, who receives all from me." *Nontio* is rendered "messenger," never "messiah"; the Italian names no one here, and no name is imported. Nothing is smoothed, softened, or deleted. The fork is footnoted per the audit: Robert's recorded suspicion that the seam may be a later insertion is carried here rather than decided; the Quran-contradiction fork is noted, not decided.
2. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter on paradise." Their marginal glosses: "His prophet" (e, on *il nontio e mio*). The English column's rubric reads "CLXXVIId" (gloss letter d) — divergence from the Italian column's gloss letter "a," recorded, not reconciled. The check's footnote "See 41* (p. 91); 57b (p. 131)" is an internal cross-reference, recorded check-only. The English page running head ("DIVERS DEGREES OF GLORY") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio mark 190a is embedded mid-text in the Italian column; (l89*) in the English column.
3. **Printed reading kept as printed**: "Risspojse iessu" (OCR; read as "Rissposse iessu," "Jesus answered") is read with the check and flagged here, not resolved silently. "He who writes" (*cholui che scriue*) is kept literal — the scribe Barnabas speaks in his own voice, and Jesus answers him by name.

Checked 2026-10-11 against the task's rails:

Old orthography handled per the base rule (doubled consonants, intrusive h, DIG = Dio).

Rubrics recorded verbatim including illegible gloss letters ("CLXXIV *.", "CLXXV*.", "CLXXVI*", "CLXXVIia."). Gloss-letter divergences between columns recorded for 174 (*/a), 175 (a/*), and 177 (a/d); 176 matches.
4. **Seam handling** — two unnamed *nontio* passages found (ch. 176 "dallo nontio di moa," ch. 177 "il nontio e mio"); both translated exactly as written, footnoted with Robert's insertion-suspicion and the Quran-contradiction fork — never decided, smoothed, softened, or deleted. No name imported into either. No named-Muhammad passage and no *messia* in this slice. *Nontio* → "messenger" throughout, kept distinct from "messiah."

## Chapter 178

*[Editorial heading: The greatness of paradise — the heavens measured against a grain of sand. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXVIII*." — gloss letter rendered as a star, recorded verbatim) at folio 190a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"And let this suffice you," said Jesus, "for knowing paradise." Then Bartholomew said again: "Master, have patience with me if I ask you one word." Jesus answered: "Say what you desire." Bartholomew said: "Paradise is certainly great, for since such great goods are in it, it must needs be great." Jesus answered: "Paradise is so great that no man can measure it. I tell you in truth that the heavens are nine, among which stand the planets, which are distant one from another five hundred years of human journey; and the earth in like manner is distant from the first heaven five hundred years of journey. But stop at measuring the first heaven, which is as much greater than the whole earth as the whole earth is greater than a grain of sand. And so the second heaven is greater than the first, and the third than the second, and so each one, up to the last heaven, are greater, one than the other. Wherefore in truth I tell you that paradise is greater than all the earth and all the heavens, as the whole earth is greater than a grain of sand." Then Peter said: "Master, paradise must be greater than God, for God is seen inside it." Jesus answered: "Hold your peace, Peter, for you blaspheme and do not realize it."

### Notes — Chapter 178
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "Chapter on paradise" (a) and "Paradise is greater" (b). No canonical parallel is asserted for this chapter. The check's footnote ('above, 111a (p. 241, note 4)') is an internal cross-reference to this gospel's earlier heavens passage and stands as printed record only. The English page running head ("OF THE GREATNESS OF PARADISE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 190a in the Italian column ("19 Ob", "9" OCR).
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *il pavadisso* (OCR; "the paradise") read as *il paradisso*, with the check. Peter's line is kept exactly as written — "paradise must be greater than God, for God is seen inside it" — and Jesus' rebuke is kept whole: "taci pietro che tu bestemij he non te ne hauedi."
3. **"Bartholomew," "Peter"** render the Italian *bartolameo, pietro* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXIX *. " is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 179

*[Editorial heading: Gabriel's shining mirror — God's greatness measured against paradise. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXIX *. " — gloss letter rendered as a star, recorded verbatim) at folio 191a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then the angel Gabriel came to Jesus, and showed him a mirror shining like the sun, in which he saw written these words: "As I live eternally, even as paradise is greater than all the heavens and the earth, as the whole earth is greater than a grain of sand, so am I greater than paradise, and as many times more as the sea has sand, as there are drops of water upon the sea, as there are blades of grass upon the earth, as there are leaves upon the trees, as there are skins upon the beasts — and as many times more as the grains of sand that would fill all the heavens and paradise and more." Then Jesus said: "Let us do reverence to our God, who is blessed eternally." So a hundred times they bowed their heads and prostrated themselves to the ground praying upon their faces. When the prayer was done, Jesus called Peter and told him and all the disciples what he had seen. And to Peter he said: "Your soul, which is greater than all the earth, through one eye sees the sun, which is a thousand times greater than all the earth." "It is true," said Peter. Then Jesus said: "So even now, through paradise you shall see God our Creator." And having said this, Jesus gave thanks to God our Lord, praying for the house of Israel and for the holy city. And everyone answered: "So be it, Lord."

### Notes — Chapter 179
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "Chapter on paradise" (a), "Paradise is greater" (b), "God lives and persists, and is greatest, mighty" (c), "Water" (d, at *aquad*). No canonical parallel is asserted for this chapter. The check's footnote ('9b (p. 15 and note 6 there)') is an internal cross-reference to this gospel's earlier mirror passage and stands as printed record only. The English page running head is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 191a, 191b in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *goza di aquad* ("drops of water"); *quanti pelli sono sopra li animalli* — "skins upon the beasts," kept as printed rather than resolved to "hairs." The list of measures is kept entire, in the Italian's order: sand of the sea, drops of water, grass upon the earth, leaves upon the trees, skins upon the beasts, sand-grains that would fill the heavens and paradise.
3. **"Gabriel," "Israel"** render the Italian *gabrielo, issdraelle* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory. The hundredfold bowing and the prostration "upon their faces" are kept literal.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXXd.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 180

*[Editorial heading: The scribe in Solomon's porch asks how man can merit. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXXd.", gloss letter d) at folio 192a (OCR "102a"). The heading above is editorial.]*

One day, Jesus being in Solomon's porch, a scribe drew near to him, one of those who preached to the people, and said to him: "Master, I have many times preached to this people, and there is in my mind a passage of scripture that I cannot understand." Jesus answered: "And what is it?" The scribe said: "What God said to Abraham our father: 'I will be your great reward.' Now how can man merit?" Then Jesus rejoiced in spirit and said: "Truly you are not far from the kingdom of God. But listen to me, for I will tell you the meaning of such teaching. God being infinite, and man finite, man cannot merit God — and is this your doubt, brother?" The scribe answered, weeping: "Lord, you know my heart; speak then, for my soul desires your voice." Then Jesus said: "As God lives, man cannot merit a little breath which he receives every moment." The scribe was beside himself hearing this, and the disciples likewise marvelled, for they remembered what Jesus said, that what they gave for love of God they would receive a hundredfold. Then he said: "If one should lend you a hundred gold pieces, and you should spend those pieces, could you say to that man: 'I give you a rotten vine-leaf; give me therefore your house, for I merit it'?" The scribe answered: "No, Lord, for he ought first to pay the debt, and then, if he wants anything, he should give him good things; but what use is a rotten leaf!"

### Notes — Chapter 180
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "Chapter on the reward" (d) and "By the living God" (e). Canonical parallels asserted: Gen. xv. 1 ('thy exceeding great reward'), for the scribe's quotation, 'I will be your great reward'; Luke x. 21 ('Jesus rejoiced in spirit'), for the narrative phrase, a verbal parallel only; and Mark xii. 34 ('Thou art not far from the kingdom of God'), for Jesus' word to the scribe. The editors' other citations stand as printed record only. The English page running head ("OF MAN'S MERIT") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio mark 192a (OCR "102a") in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: at *he qualle l he* the check prints a note "1 MS. apparently *quelle*" — the printed text is followed, not silently corrected. The hundred-gold-pieces and rotten-vine-leaf similitude is kept entire, in the Italian's order.
3. **"Solomon," "Abraham"** render the Italian *sallamone, habraha* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXXIa.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 181

*[Editorial heading: Man can merit nothing — his righteousnesses are rotten. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXXIa.", gloss letter a) at folio 192b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus answered: "Well have you said, brother; tell me then: who created man out of nothing? Certainly it was God, to whom God gave the whole world for his benefit. But man by sinning has spent it all, for by sin all the world is contrary to man, and miserable man has nothing to give to God but works rotten with sin. For sinning every day he rots his own works — wherefore Isaiah the prophet says: 'Our righteousnesses are as a menstruous cloth.' Now how can man merit, when he cannot satisfy? And is it perhaps that man does not sin? Certainly it is that our God says by his prophet David: 'Seven times a day the just falls'; how then does the unjust fall? And if our righteousnesses are rotten, how abominable are our unrighteousnesses! As God lives, there is nothing that man ought to shun more than this saying: 'I merit.' Let man know, brother, the works of his hands, and soon he will see his merit. Every good thing that comes out of man — in truth man does it not, but God works it in man; for his being is of God who created him. What man does is to contradict God his creator and to commit sin, for which he merits not reward but torment."

### Notes — Chapter 181
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "Chapter on the miserable" (a), "God gives" (b), "By the living God" (c), "God is the Creator" (d). Their printed note "1 MS.: *he ma*" is recorded as printed; its location is uncertain in the OCR, so it is not silently attached to any clause. Canonical parallels asserted: Matt. xix. 29 ('shall receive an hundredfold'), for the hundredfold saying the disciples remembered; Isa. lxiv. 6 ('all our righteousnesses are as filthy rags'), for the chapter's Isaiah saying, 'Our righteousnesses are as a menstruous cloth'; and Prov. xxiv. 16 ('a just man falleth seven times'), for the seven-times saying — which the chapter attributes to David and phrases 'seven times a day'; the saying itself is Proverbs'. The editors' other citations stand as printed record only. The English page running head ("OF MAN'S MERIT") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 192b, 193a in the Italian column.
2. **Kept literal**: *pano messtruato* — "menstruous cloth"; *quel fanggo*-like frankness is nowhere smoothed. The Isaiah and David quotations are kept whole, exactly as the Italian gives them, including "how then does the unjust fall?"
3. **"Isaiah," "David"** render the Italian *esaia, dauit* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, ".CLXXXIK" [uncertain — see ch. 182 note 1], is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 182

*[Editorial heading: The debt man owes God — the dialogue of the penitent. Manuscript rubric (Ragg ".CLXXXIK" — recorded verbatim; the gloss letter is doubtful in the OCR, flagged [uncertain], not resolved) at folio 193b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Not only has God created man, as I say, but he created him perfect. He gave him the whole world; after the departure from paradise he gave him two angels to guard him; he sent him the prophets; he granted him the law; he granted him the faith; every moment he delivers him from Satan; he willed to give him paradise; and what is more, God willed to give himself to man. Now consider whether the debt is great! To cancel it you would need to have a man created by you out of nothing, to have created as many prophets as God has sent you, with a world and a paradise, and moreover with a God great and good as our God is, and to give it all to God — so would the debt be cancelled, and only the obligation to give thanks to God would remain to you. But since you cannot create a fly, and since there is but one God who is master of all, how can you cancel your debt? Surely, if a man should lend you a hundred gold pieces, you are obliged to restore a hundred gold pieces. The meaning, then, of this, brother, is that God, since he is master of paradise and of everything, can say what pleases him and give what pleases him. Wherefore, when he said to Abraham: 'I will be your great reward,' Abraham could not say: 'God is my reward,' but must say: 'God is my gift and my debt.' So when you, brother, preach to the people, you must explain this passage thus: that God will give man such and such a thing if man works well.

"When God speaks to you, O man, and says: 'My servant, you have worked well for love of me; what reward do you seek from me, your God?' — answer: 'Lord, since I am the work of your hands, it is not fitting that there should be in me sin, which Satan loves. Therefore, Lord, for your own glory, have mercy on the works of your hands.' And if God should say: 'I have forgiven you, and now I wish to reward you,' answer: 'Lord, for what I have done I merit to be punished, and for what you have done you merit to be glorified. Therefore, Lord, punish in me what I have done, and save what you have wrought.' And if God should say: 'What punishment seems fitting to you for your sin?' answer: 'As much as all the reprobate shall suffer, O Lord.' And if God should say: 'For what reason do you seek so great a punishment, my faithful servant?' answer: 'Because each one of them, if they had received from you as much as I have received, would have served you more faithfully than I.' And if God should say: 'When will you receive this punishment, and for how long?' answer: 'Now, and without end.' As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands, such a man would be more pleasing to God than all his holy angels. For God loves true humility and hates pride." Then the scribe thanked Jesus and said to him: "Lord, let us go to the house of your servant, for your servant will give you and your disciples to eat." Jesus answered: "I will come when you promise to call me brother and not Lord, and to say that you are my brother and not my servant." The man promised, and Jesus went to his house.

### Notes — Chapter 182
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the rubric reads ".CLXXXIK" in the OCR — recorded verbatim, the trailing gloss letter flagged **[uncertain]**; the English column's parallel rubric "CLXXXII *." is not imported. The editors' marginal glosses (a–i) read: "Chapter on the verities of penitence (?)" (a), "God is the Creator" (b), "God gives" (c), "God sends" (d), "God bestows" (e), "God is great and good" (f), "God is one and single" (g), "God is the possessor" (h), "God is sovereign" (i) — several stand printed inline as superscripts in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Canonical parallel asserted: Gen. xv. 1 ('thy exceeding great reward'), for the chapter's renewed quotation, 'I will be your great reward.' The printed words 'Or for' are the editors' alternative-rendering note, not a citation, and stand, with the editors' other citations, as printed record only. The English page running head ("THE PENITENT CRAVETH PUNISHMENT") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 193b, 194a, 194b in the Italian column.
2. **The penitent dialogue is kept entire and unsmoothed** — five question-and-answer turns (reward sought; forgiveness; fitting punishment; why so great a punishment; when and for how long), the "punish in me what I have done, and save what you have wrought" answer, and the closing verdict that such a man pleases God more than all his holy angels, kept exactly as written. The scribe's invitation and Jesus' "call me brother and not Lord" condition are kept whole.
3. **"Abraham"** renders the Italian *abraham* — the Italian spelling, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXXIIId.", is confirmed in the Italian column; ch. 183 opens with the scribe's question on true and false humility while they are eating.

## Chapter 183

*[Editorial heading: Become as a little child — the scribe's question on true and false humility. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXXIIId.", gloss letter d) at folio 194b. The heading above is editorial.]*

While they were eating, the scribe said: "O master, you said that God loves true humility — so tell us what humility is, and how it can be true and false."

"In truth I tell you that whoever does not become as a little child shall not enter into the kingdom of heaven."

Everyone was bewildered at hearing this, and they said to one another: "Now how shall a man of thirty or forty years become a little child? Surely this is a hard saying."

Jesus answered: "As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands, my words are true. I have told you that you must become as a little child — for this is true humility. Because if you ask a little child who made the clothes he is wearing, he will answer: My father. If you ask him whose is the house where he lives, he will say: My father's. If you say: Who gives you to eat? he will answer: My father. If you say: Who taught you to walk and to speak? he will answer: My father. But if you say: Who broke your forehead, that your forehead is bound up so? he will answer: I fell down, and so broke my head. If you say: Now why did you fall? he will answer: Do you not see that I am little, so that I have not the strength to walk and run like a grown man? So my father must take me by the hand if I would walk firmly. But so that I might learn to walk well, my father left me for a little while, and I, wanting to run, fell down. If you say: Now what did your father say? he will answer: Why did you not walk quite slowly? See that in the future you do not leave my side."

### Notes — Chapter 183
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter on the child." Canonical parallel asserted: Mark x. 15 and parallels (Matt. xviii. 3; Luke xviii. 17) — the saying that whoever does not receive, or become as, a little child shall not enter the kingdom; the chapter's wording, 'whoever does not become as a little child shall not enter into the kingdom of heaven,' stands closest to Matt. xviii. 3. The editors' other citations stand as printed record only. The English page running head ("THE PENITENT CRAVETH PUNISHMENT," continued from ch. 182) is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Folio marks 194b, 195a in the Italian column.
2. **Check-only divergence**: the 1907 check inserts "[Jesus replied:]" in brackets before "Verily I say unto you" — the Italian names no speaker at that line; the speaker is carried by the following line ("Rissposse iessu"). Nothing was imported from the check.
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: "amma6" (OCR; read with the check as "loves"); the bracket in "mi p[i]lgi per la mano" is the OCR's, recorded, not resolved. "uere/verra/falssa/humillta" spellings kept as printed.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXXIV*.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 184

*[Editorial heading: True humility and false humility defined. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXXIV*." — trailing gloss-letter illegible in the OCR, an asterisk; recorded verbatim, not resolved) at folio 195b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Tell me, is this true?" said Jesus.

The disciples and the scribe answered: "It is most true."

Then Jesus said: "Whoever in truth of heart acknowledges God as the author of every good, and himself as the author of sin, shall be truly humble. But whoever shall speak with the tongue as the child speaks, and shall contradict it in deeds, surely he has false humility and true pride. For pride is then at its height when it makes use of humble things, so as not to be rebuked and trampled upon by men. True humility is a lowliness of soul by which man knows himself in truth; but false humility is a mist from hell which so darkens the understanding of the soul that what a man ought to ascribe to himself he ascribes to God, and what he ought to ascribe to God he ascribes to himself. So the man of false humility will say that he is a grievous sinner, but when someone tells him that he is a sinner he will grow angry against him and will persecute him. The man of false humility will say that God has given him what he has, but that he on his part has not slept, but has done good works.

"And these Pharisees of this present time — brothers, tell me how they walk."

The scribe answered, weeping: "O master, the Pharisees of the present time have the garments and the name of Pharisees, but in their heart and their works they are Canaanites. And would to God they did not usurp such a name, for then they would not deceive the simple. O ancient time, how cruelly you have dealt with us, taking away from us the true Pharisees and leaving us the false!"

### Notes — Chapter 184
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter on the proud." Their marginal glosses: "Account of the completely proud" and "God gives." The English page running head ("OF TRUE HUMILITY") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Lines of the scribe's Arabic gloss stand in the Italian column and are recorded, not translated. Folio marks 195b, 196a in the Italian column.
2. **Check-only divergence**: the 1907 check brackets "[the same]" in "shall contradict [the same] in act" — the Italian (*chontradira in fati*) carries no bracket, so none is added. The English column's rubric is OCR-read "CLXXXIV^" — noted, not used.
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: "autojre" (= autore), "chonchulchata" (= conculcata), "challigine" (= caligine), "Io" for lo throughout (old orthography), "ussurpasseno" (usurp), "chananei" (= Canaanites). The plural verb *chonosserano* with *chi* is kept as printed and rendered naturally.
4. **"Canaanites"** renders the Italian *chananei* — the Italian spelling, not canonical memory.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXXV*.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 185

*[Editorial heading: The world, not time, makes men bad — Gehazi; the scribe begins Haggai's story. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXXV*." — trailing gloss-letter illegible in the OCR, an asterisk; recorded verbatim, not resolved) at folio 196b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus answered: "Brother, it is not time that has done this, but rather the wicked world. For in every time it is possible to serve God in truth, but by drawing near to the world — that is, to the evil customs — in every time men become bad. Now do you not know that Gehazi, servant of Elisha the prophet, with a lie, to the shame of his master, stole the money and the garments of Naaman the Syrian? And yet Elisha had a great number of Pharisees to whom God made him prophesy.

"I tell you in truth that men are so inclined to evil working, and the world so drives them to it, and Satan so entices them to evil, that the Pharisees of the present day flee from every good work and every holy example; and the example of Gehazi is enough for them to be reprobated by God."

The scribe answered: "It is most true." Whereupon Jesus said: "I would that you would narrate to me the example of Haggai and Hosea, both prophets of God, so that we may see the true Pharisee."

The scribe answered: "O master, what shall I say? Surely many do not believe it, although it is written by Daniel the prophet; but to obey you I will narrate the truth. Haggai was fifteen years old when, having sold his patrimony and given it to the poor, he went out from Anathoth to serve Obadiah the prophet. Now the aged Obadiah, who knew the humility of Haggai, used him as a book with which to teach his disciples. So he often presented him with garments and delicate foods, but Haggai always sent back the messenger, saying: 'Go and return home, for you have made a mistake. Would Obadiah send me such things now? Surely not — for he knows that I am good for nothing and only commit sins.' And Obadiah, when he had anything bad, used to give it to the one nearest to Haggai, so that he might see it. Whereupon Haggai, seeing it, would say to himself: 'Now behold, Obadiah has certainly forgotten you — for that thing is suited to me alone, because I am worse than all. And there is nothing so vile but that, receiving it from Obadiah, by whose hands God grants it to me, it is a treasure.'"

### Notes — Chapter 185
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter on the story of Ayou (Haggai?) the prophet." Their marginal gloss: "Setting forth the story of Ayou." Canonical parallel asserted: 2 Kings v. 20 sqq. — Gehazi, servant of Elisha, who with a lie took from Naaman the Syrian money and changes of garments — for the chapter's Gehazi charge, told in the same terms. The editors' other citations stand as printed record only. The English page running heads ("HAGGAI AND HOSE A") are the printers' heads, not manuscript titles. Folio marks 196b, 197a in the Italian column.
2. **Kept entire**: the Gehazi charge is rendered whole — lie, shame of his master, money and garments of Naaman the Syrian — and then condemned whole ("enough for them to be reprobated by God"). The Haggai refusal and the bad-portion sayings are kept literal, including "I am worse than all."
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: "iezi" (= Gehazi), "helliseo" (= Elisha), "aman siro" (= Naaman the Syrian), "abdia" (= Obadiah), "danielle" (= Daniel), "anatot" (= Anathoth), "aggeo/ageo" (= Haggai), "le chui mani" (= le cui mani). The plural verb *uscite* with the singular subject *aggeo* is kept as printed and rendered naturally. The names above render the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXXVI*.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 186

*[Editorial heading: Haggai's prayer. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXXVI*." — trailing gloss-letter illegible in the OCR, an asterisk; recorded verbatim, not resolved) at folio 197a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"When Obadiah wished to teach anyone to pray, he would call Haggai and say: 'Recite here your prayer, so that everyone may hear your words.' Then Haggai would say: 'Lord God of Israel, with mercy look upon your servant, who calls upon you because you have created him. Righteous Lord God, remember your righteousness and punish the sins of your servant, so that they do not pollute your work. Lord my God, I cannot ask you for the delights that you grant to your faithful servants, because I do nothing but sins. So, Lord, when you would give an infirmity to one of your servants, remember me your servant, for your glory.'"

And when Haggai did so, said the scribe, God so loved him that to everyone who in his time stood by him God gave prophecy. And nothing did Haggai ask in prayer that God withheld.

### Notes — Chapter 186
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on the prayer of Ayou." Their marginal glosses: "God is sovereign and just," "God is loving," "God is munificent." The check's footnote reads "On this prayer, see Introduction." Folio mark 197a in the Italian column.
2. **Check-only divergence**: the 1907 check brackets "[the gift of]" before "prophecy" — the Italian (*DIG li dono la profetia*) carries no bracket, so none is added. The English column's rubric is OCR-read "CLXXXVIa." — noted, not used.
3. **The prayer is kept entire**, exactly as the scribe narrates it — the call to remember righteousness and punish the servant's sins, the refusal to ask for delights, the request to be remembered when an infirmity is given — nothing smoothed.
4. **Printed readings kept as printed**: "Diob" (marginal gloss letter b joined to Dio), "nom posso" (= non posso), "arechordati"/"harechordati" (= ricordati, intrusive a), "la riseruassi" (= withhold; read with the check, flagged here rather than resolved silently).
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXXVIIe.", is confirmed in the Italian column at folio 197b.

## Chapter 187

*[Editorial heading: The charity of Hosea. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXXVIIe.", gloss letter e) at folio 197b. The heading above is editorial.]*

The good scribe wept as he said this, as the sailor weeps when he sees his ship broken up. And he said: "Hosea, when he went to serve God, was prince over the tribe of Naphtali, aged fourteen years. And so, having sold his patrimony and given it to the poor, he went to be disciple of Haggai. Hosea was so inflamed with charity that concerning all that was asked of him he would say: 'This God has given me for you, O brother — accept it, therefore!' For which reason he was soon left with only two garments, namely a tunic of sackcloth and a mantle of skins. He sold, I say, his patrimony and gave it to the poor, because otherwise no one would be suffered to be called a Pharisee.

"Hosea had the book of Moses, which he read with greatest earnestness. Now one day Haggai said to him: 'Hosea, who has taken away from you all that you had?' He answered: 'The book of Moses.' It happened that a disciple of a neighbouring prophet was fain to go to Jerusalem, but had not a mantle. Wherefore, having heard of the charity of Hosea, he went to find him and said to him: 'Brother, I would fain go to Jerusalem to perform a sacrifice to our God, but I have not a mantle, wherefore I know not what to do.' When he heard this, Hosea said: 'Pardon me, brother, for I have committed a great sin against you — because God has given me a mantle in order that I might give it to you, and I had forgotten. Now therefore accept it, and pray to God for me.' The man, believing this, accepted Hosea's mantle and departed.

"And when Hosea went to Haggai, Haggai said: 'Who has taken away your mantle?' Hosea replied: 'The book of Moses.' Haggai was much pleased at hearing this, because he perceived the goodness of Hosea. It happened that a poor man was stripped by robbers and left naked. Whereupon Hosea, seeing him, stripped off his own tunic and gave it to him that was naked; himself being left with a little piece of goat-skin over the private parts. Wherefore, as he came not to see Haggai, the good Haggai thought that Hosea was sick. So he went with two disciples to find him, and they found him wrapped in palm-leaves. Then said Haggai: 'Tell me now, wherefore have you not been to visit me?' Hosea answered: 'The book of Moses has taken away my tunic, and I feared to come thither without a tunic.' Whereupon Haggai gave him another tunic.

"It happened that a young man, seeing Hosea read the book of Moses, wept, and said: 'I also would learn to read if I had a book.' Hearing which, Hosea gave him the book, saying: 'Brother, this book is yours, because God has given it to me in order that I might give it to him who weeping desires a book.' The man believed it and received the book."

### Notes — Chapter 187
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter on the story of the Prophet Uza (Hosea?)." Their marginal gloss: "God gives." The English page running heads ("HAGGAI AND HOSE A") are the printers' heads, not manuscript titles. A line of the scribe's Arabic gloss stands in the Italian column and is recorded, not translated. Folio marks 197b, 198a, 198b in the Italian column.
2. **The three givings are kept entire** — the mantle to the traveller, the tunic to the naked man, the book of Moses to the weeping youth — each answered with "The book of Moses," exactly as the Italian sets it. Nothing smoothed.
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: "hossea/ossea/hosea" variants (= Hosea), "nephtalim" (= Naphtali), "di hetta di anni quatordeci" (= aged fourteen years), "ho f ratello" (OCR space; = ho fratello), "dui uesti solle zioe" (= due vesti sole, cioè), "cillicio" (= cilicio), "somo dessiderio" (= sommo desiderio), "ierassale/ierussalem/ierusalem" variants (= Jerusalem), "muolto in folgie di palma" (= wrapped in palm-leaves), "hiuui" (= ivi), "sachrete" (= private parts, kept literal). The names above render the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). This chapter closes the Haggai–Hosea discourse; the next Italian-column rubric opens ch. 188 (next slice).

## Chapter 188

*[Editorial heading: A disciple of Haggai visits Hosea; Hosea sells himself to redeem the widow's son. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXXVIII*.", gloss mark *) at folio 199a. The English column's rubric is OCR'd "CLXXXVIII \" — noted, not used. The heading above is editorial.]*

There was a disciple of Haggai living near Hosea. And wanting to see whether his own book was well written, he went to Hosea and said to him, "Brother, take your book, and let us see whether it is like mine." Hosea answered, "It has been taken from me." "Who took it from you?" said the disciple. Hosea answered, "The book of Moses." Hearing this, the disciple went to Haggai and said to him, "Hosea has gone mad, for he says that the book of Moses has taken the book of Moses from him." Haggai answered, "Would to God, O brother, that I were mad in this way, and that all madmen were like Hosea."

Now the robbers of Syria, having raided the land of Judea, seized the son of a poor widow, who lived near Mount Carmel, where the prophets and Pharisees dwelt. And it so happened that Hosea, having gone to cut wood, met the woman weeping, and at once he began to weep; for when he saw anyone laughing he laughed, and when he saw anyone weeping he wept. Hosea asked the woman the reason for her weeping, and she told him everything. Then Hosea said, "Come, sister, for God wills to give you your son." And the two of them went to Hebron, where Hosea sold himself and gave the money to the widow, who, not knowing how he had gotten that money, received it and bought back her son. He who had bought Hosea took him to Jerusalem, where he kept his house, not knowing Hosea.

Haggai, seeing that Hosea was not to be found, grieved over it. And the angel of God told him how he had been taken as a slave to Jerusalem. The good Haggai, hearing this, wept for the absence of Hosea as a mother weeps for the absence of her son. And he called two disciples and went to Jerusalem. And on entering the city he met Hosea by the will of God, who was laden with bread to carry it to the laborers in his master's vineyard. Recognizing him, Haggai said, "Son, why have you abandoned your old father, who seeks you in grief?" Hosea answered, "Father, I have been sold." Then Haggai said in anger, "Who was that wretch who sold you?" Hosea answered, "God forgive you, my father, for he who sold me is so good that if he were not in the world no one would become holy." "Who then is he?" said Haggai. Hosea answered, "O my father, it was the book of Moses." Then the good Haggai stood as if beside himself, and said, "Would to God, my son, that the book of Moses would sell me too, with all my children, even as it has sold you!" And Haggai went with Hosea to the house of his master, who when he saw Haggai said, "Blessed be our God, who has sent his prophet to my house," and ran to kiss his hands. Then Haggai said, "Brother, kiss the hands of your servant whom you have bought, for he is better than I." And he told him all that had happened; whereupon the master gave Hosea his freedom.

"And this is all that you sought, O master."

### Notes — Chapter 188
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "The Chapter of the account of him that loved God most." Folio marks 199a, 199b, 200a in the Italian column. The English page running head ("HAGGAI AND HOSE A 421") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. MS note at *afflito nestaua*: "1 i. e. ne staua : or possibly we should read restaua." — the printed reading is kept as printed, not resolved.
2. **Kept literal**: *afflito nestaua* is kept; the check renders "remained afflicted thereat" — the MS note is recorded, not imported. The check brackets "[said the scribe]" after the closing line ("And this is all that thou desiredst, O Master") — the Italian has no bracket, so none is added.
3. **"Haggai," "Hosea," "Moses," "Judea," "Carmel," "Hebron," "Jerusalem"** render the Italian *ageo, ossea, moisse, iudea, charmello, hebron, ierusalem* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. The check's footnote cites 2 Kings v. 2 for the Syrian raiders. No canonical parallel is asserted: 2 Kings v. 2 belongs to the Naaman episode (Syrian companies bringing a captive 'little maid' out of Israel), a different episode sharing only the raiding motif; the editors' citation stands as printed record only.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CLXXXIX ».", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 189

*[Editorial heading: The sun stands still twelve hours; Scripture corrupted by human traditions. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CLXXXIX ».", gloss mark ») at folio 200b. The English column's rubric is "CLXXXIX*." — noted, not used. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Jesus said, "This is true, for I am assured of it by God. So that everyone may know that this is the truth, in the name of God let the sun stand still, and not move for the space of twelve hours!" And this was done, to the terror of all Jerusalem and Judea.

And Jesus said to the scribe, "O brother, what do you seek to learn from me, having such knowledge? As God lives, this is enough for the salvation of man — for the humility of Haggai with the charity of Hosea fulfilled the whole law with all the prophets. Tell me, brother: when you came to question me in the temple, did you think, perhaps, that God had sent me to destroy the law and the prophets? Certain it is that God will not do this, for he is unchangeable; and therefore what God ordained as the way of man's salvation, this he caused all the prophets to say.

"As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — if the book of Moses with the book of David our father had not been corrupted by the human traditions of false Pharisees and doctors, God would not have given his word to me. And why speak I of the book of Moses and the book of David? Every prophecy have they corrupted, so that today a thing is not sought because God has commanded it; but men look to see whether the doctors say it and the Pharisees observe it — as though God could be in error and men could not err. Woe then to this faithless generation, for upon them shall come the blood of every prophet and righteous man, with the blood of Zechariah son of Barachiah, whom they slew between the temple and the altar. What prophet have they not persecuted? What righteous man have they let die a natural death? Scarcely one! And therefore now they seek to kill me. They glory in being children of Abraham, and in having the beautiful temple. As God lives — they are children of Satan, and therefore they do his will; and therefore the temple with the holy city shall go to ruin, so that of the temple there shall not remain stone upon stone."

### Notes — Chapter 189
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title note reads "Chapter on 'they falsify.'" Marginal gloss letters in the Italian column: *Viue Dio* (b), *inmutabile* (c), *dotori* (b), *parolla* (° [uncertain, OCR — recorded verbatim]), *lo altare* (d), *Viue DIG* (a, twice). The check's footnote glosses read: a "By the living God"; b "The Jews alter the words after they have been set, and afterwards Christians alter the words in the Gospel. Inde."; c "I am a witness, and this book"; d "Account of the death of Zachariah the prophet" — recorded check-only, not imported. Folio marks 200b, 201a in the Italian column (a "199" mark also interleaves in the OCR between *alia chui* and *presenza* — recorded as an interleaved mark, not Italian-column). The English page running head ("THE SUN STANDETH STILL 423") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title.
2. Canonical parallels asserted: Josh. x. 12, 13 ('Sun, stand thou still upon Gibeon'; 'the sun stood still … about a whole day'; here, 'let the sun stand still, and not move for the space of twelve hours') and Matt. v. 17 ('Think not that I am come to destroy the law, or the prophets'; here, 'did you think … that God had sent me to destroy the law and the prophets?'). The editors' other citation, Matt. xxii. 40, stands as printed record only.
3. **"Jesus," "Jerusalem," "Judea," "David," "Zechariah son of Barachiah," "Abraham," "Satan"** render the Italian *iessu, ierossolima, iudea, dauit, zacharia fiolo di barachia, abraham, satana* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CXCe.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 190

*[Editorial heading: The promise of the Messiah — in Isaac or in Ishmael? Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXCe.", gloss letter e) at folio 201a–201b. The English column's rubric is "CXC e." — noted, not used. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Tell me, brother — you who are a doctor learned in the law: the promise of the Messiah — in whom was it made to our father Abraham? In Isaac, or in Ishmael?" The scribe answered, "O master, I fear to tell you this, because the penalty is death." Then Jesus said, "Brother, I grieve that I came to eat bread in your house, since you love this present life more than God your creator; and for this reason you fear to lose your life, but fear not to lose the faith and the life eternal — which is lost when the tongue speaks contrary to what the heart knows of the law of God."

Then the good scribe, weeping, said, "O master, if I had known how to bear fruit, I would have preached many things which, so as not to raise sedition among the people, I have kept silent." Jesus answered, "Neither the people, nor the whole world, nor all the saints, nor all the angels are to be respected when God's honor is at stake. Let the whole world perish rather than offend God your creator — and do not seek to preserve it by sin, for sin destroys and does not preserve. And God is mighty to create as many worlds as the sea has sand, and more."

### Notes — Chapter 190
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal gloss letters in the Italian column: *messia* (f), *chreatore tuo* (g), *chreatore* (a), *DIG* (b, at *he potente*). The check's footnote glosses read: e "Chapter on 'fear God.'"; f "Apostle"; g "God creates" — recorded check-only, not imported. **Divergence recorded**: the check's gloss f re-reads *il messia* ("the promise of the messiah") as "Apostle" — the Italian says *messia*, and it is rendered "messiah"; nothing is imported from the gloss. MS note "1" at *ma non1 temi*: "MS. no non." as printed (OCR; kept as printed, not resolved) [uncertain]. Folio mark 201b in the Italian column. The English page running head ("SCRIPTURE CORRUPTED BY TRADITION 425") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title.
2. The check's footnote cites John iii. 10 (the doctor learned in the law). No canonical parallel is asserted: John iii. 10 is the rebuke to Nicodemus in the new-birth discourse ('Art thou a master of Israel, and knowest not these things?'), which does not occur in this chapter; the editors' citation, resting on the shared description of a doctor of the law, stands as printed record only.
3. **"Isaac," "Ishmael," "Abraham"** render the Italian *issach, issmaele, abraham* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CXCI.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 191

*[Editorial heading: The scribe's old book of Moses and Joshua — Ishmael father of the Messiah, Isaac father of the messenger of the Messiah. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXCI." — no gloss letter in the Italian column) at folio 202a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then the scribe said, "Pardon me, O master, for I have sinned." Jesus said, "May God pardon you, against whom you have sinned." Then the scribe said, "I have seen an old book written by the hand of Moses and Joshua — he who made the sun stand still, as you have done — servants and prophets of God, which book is the true book of Moses. In it is written that Ishmael is the father of the Messiah, and Isaac the father of the messenger of the Messiah. And thus the book says that Moses said: 'Lord God of Israel, mighty and merciful, manifest to your servant the splendour of your glory.' And God showed him his messenger in the arms of Ishmael, and Ishmael in the arms of Abraham. And near Ishmael stood Isaac, in whose arms was a child, who with his finger pointed to the messenger of God, saying, 'This is he for whom God has created all things.' And Moses cried out with joy: 'O Ishmael, you have in your arms the whole world with paradise! Remember me, servant of God, that I may find grace in God's sight by means of your son, through whom God has made all things.'"

### Notes — Chapter 191
1. **Apparatus as printed**: marginal gloss letters in the Italian column: *DIG* (c, at *perdoniti*), *messia* (d), *nontio del messia* (e), *seruo* (f), *nontio di Diog* (g), *chreato* (* [uncertain — mark recorded verbatim]), *seruo* (e), *il tutto* (h). ## Chapter 192

*[Editorial heading: Mary of Magdala begs Jesus for her brother Lazarus; Jesus sends her to Bethany. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXCIL" — trailing letter L recorded verbatim as the gloss letter [uncertain, OCR]) at folio 202b. The English column's rubric is "CXCII." — noted, not used. The heading above is editorial.]*

"In that book it is not found that God eats the flesh of cattle or sheep. In that book it is not found that God has locked up his mercy in Israel alone; but rather that God has mercy on every man who seeks God his creator in truth. All of this book I could not read, because the high priest, in whose library I was, forbade it to me, saying that an Ishmaelite had written it."

Then Jesus said, "See that you never again keep back the truth, for in the faith of the Messiah God shall give salvation to men, and without it none shall be saved."

And there Jesus ended his discourse. And as they were eating, behold Mary — who wept at the feet of Jesus — entered the house of Nicodemus, for that was the name of the scribe; and weeping she placed herself at the feet of Jesus, saying, "Lord, your servant, who through you has found mercy with God, has a sister and a brother who now lies sick in danger of death."

Jesus answered, "Where is your house? Tell me, for I will come to pray to God for his health." Mary answered, "Bethany is my brother's and my sister's, for my own house is Magdala; so my brother is in Bethany." Jesus said to the woman, "Go quickly to your brother's house, and wait for me there, for I will come to heal him. And fear not, for he shall not die."

The woman departed, and having gone to Bethany found that her brother had died that day; so they laid him in the sepulchre of their fathers.

## Chapter 193

*[Editorial heading: The raising of Lazarus at Bethany. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXCIII." — no gloss letter in either column) at folio 203a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus stayed two days in the house of Nicodemus, and the third day he left for Bethany. And when he was near the city he sent two disciples ahead to announce his coming to Mary. She ran out of the city and found Jesus, and weeping said: "Lord, you said that my brother would not die; and now he has been buried four days. Would to God you had come before I called you, for then he would not have died."

Jesus answered: "Your brother is not dead, but sleeps; therefore I come to wake him."

Mary answered, weeping: "Lord, from such a sleep he shall be awakened on the day of judgment by the angel of God sounding the trumpet."

Jesus said: "Mary, believe me, he shall rise before that day, for God has given me power over his sleep; and I tell you in truth that he is not dead — for only he is dead who dies without finding mercy with God."

Mary returned quickly to announce the coming of Jesus to Martha her sister. There had gathered at the death of Lazarus a great crowd of Jews from Jerusalem, and many scribes and Pharisees. Martha, having heard from Mary her sister of the coming of Jesus, arose in haste and ran outside, and the multitude of Jews, scribes, and Pharisees followed her to comfort her, for they thought she was going to the sepulchre to weep for her brother. And when she arrived where Jesus had spoken with Mary, Martha weeping said: "Lord, would to God you had been here, for then my brother would not have died!"

Mary then came up weeping, whereupon Jesus shed tears, and sighing said: "Where have you laid him?" They answered: "Come and see."

The Pharisees said among themselves: "Now this man, who raised the son of the widow at Nain — why did he let this man die, having said that he would not die?"

Jesus, having come to the sepulchre where everyone was weeping, said: "Do not weep, for Lazarus sleeps, and I have come to wake him."

The Pharisees said among themselves: "Would to God that you slept so!"

Then Jesus said: "My hour is not yet come; but when it shall come, I shall sleep in like manner and shall be speedily awakened."

Again Jesus said: "Take away the stone of the sepulchre."

Martha said: "Lord, he stinks, for he has been dead four days."

Jesus said: "Why then have I come here, Martha? Do you not believe me, that I shall wake him?"

Martha answered: "I know that you are the holy one of God, who has sent you into this world."

Then Jesus, having lifted his hands to heaven, said: "Lord God of Abraham, God of Ishmael and of Isaac, God of our fathers — have mercy upon the affliction of these women, and give glory to your holy name."

And when everyone had answered "Amen," Jesus said with a loud voice: "Lazarus, come forth!"

Whereupon the dead man arose, and Jesus said to his disciples: "Loose him" — for he was bound in the grave-clothes with the napkin over his face, even as our fathers are accustomed to bury.

A great multitude of Jews and some Pharisees believed in Jesus, for the miracle was great. Those who remained in their unbelief departed and went to Jerusalem and reported to the chief of the priests the resurrection of Lazarus, and how many had become Nazarenes — for so they called those who through the word of God which Jesus preached did penance.

### Notes — Chapter 193
1. **Apparatus as printed**: marginal gloss "a" at *DIG de padri nosstri* — the check renders it "The God of Abraham, of Ishmael and of Isaac, and of our fathers"; marginal gloss "b" at *misserichordia apresso DIG* — the check renders it "There is no death except to him who dies without the mercy of God. Inde." The Italian order "God of Ishmael and of Isaac" is kept as written. The English page running heads ("LAZARUS IS SICK"; "JESUS AT THE TOMB OF LAZARUS"; "THE RAISING OF LAZARUS") are the printers' heads, not manuscript titles. Folio marks 203a, 203b, 204a in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *sta[to]* printed with brackets ("would to God you had been here"); *mbrto* for *morto* ("is not dead"); *lie hora* for *e hora* ("and now"); *tia manldato* ("who has sent you"); *nom piangete* for *non piangete* ("do not weep") — none resolved silently.
3. **"My hour is not yet come"** (*la hora mia non he hanchora uenuta*): the check's footnote flags this as inconsistent with the account below at 221b, where Jesus does not die at all — recorded, not decided.
4. **Mary of Magdala**: the Italian has her saying "my house is Magdala" (*cassa mia he magdalo*) while her brother and sister are at Bethany; the check's footnote notes she is here further identified with the Magdalen (cp. 139*) — recorded as the editors' note.
5. **"Nazarenes"** (*nazareni*): kept as written — "for so they called those who through the word of God which Jesus preached did penance"; canonical parallel asserted for the name only: Acts xxiv. 5 ('a ringleader of the sect of the Nazarenes'; here, 'how many had become Nazarenes'). No canonical parallel is asserted for this chapter's definition of the name ('those who through the word of God which Jesus preached did penance'), which is the chapter's own.
6. Canonical parallels asserted: John xi. 6 (he 'abode two days still in the same place'; here, 'Jesus stayed two days in the house of Nicodemus'), John xi. 11 ('Our friend Lazarus sleepeth; but I go, that I may awake him'; here, 'Your brother is not dead, but sleeps; therefore I come to wake him'), and John xi. 21–46 (the tomb scene — the sisters' 'if thou hadst been here,' the weeping, 'Where have ye laid him?', the stone taken away, 'Lazarus, come forth,' and 'Loose him'). The editors' other citation, Matt. xxi. 1, stands as printed record only.
7. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next division, "CXCIV," stands on the English column's head — no Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR at this point; see the slice headnote.

## Chapter 194

*[Editorial heading: Counsel to slay Lazarus — Martha and Mary — the present death is not death. Division per the edition's "CXCIV b. 204t>" head (English column); no Italian-column rubric recovered in the djvu OCR at this point — the Italian runs on from ch. 193 unbroken in the text layer. The heading above is editorial.]*

The scribes and Pharisees took counsel with the high priest to slay Lazarus, for many renounced their traditions and believed in the word of Jesus, because the miracle of Lazarus was great — seeing that Lazarus conversed with men, and ate and drank. But because he was powerful, having a following in Jerusalem, and possessing with his sisters Magdala and Bethany, they knew not what to do.

Jesus entered into Bethany, into the house of Lazarus, and Martha with Mary ministered to him. Mary, sitting one day at the feet of Jesus, was listening to his words — whereupon Martha said to Jesus: "Lord, do you not see that my sister takes no care of you, for she does not provide what you and your disciples must eat?"

Jesus answered: "Martha, Martha, see to what you ought to do, for Mary has chosen a part which shall not be taken from her for ever."

Sitting at table, Jesus with a great multitude that believed in him spoke, saying: "Brothers, I have little time to stay with you, for the time is near when I shall depart from the world. Wherefore remember the words of God spoken to Ezekiel the prophet, saying: 'As I, your God, live for ever — the soul that sins shall die. But if the sinner does penance, he shall not die, but live.'

"Wherefore the present death is not death, but rather the end of a long death — for even as the body, separated from sense, although it has the soul in it, when it is in a swoon has no other advantage over the dead that is buried, except that the buried awaits God to raise him, while he that is in a swoon awaits sense to return. Look, then, upon the present life, which is dead for not having sense of God."

### Notes — Chapter 194
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title key gives "The Chapter on the truths of life" (the "b" gloss belongs to ch. 193's close; the "b" at the "CXCIV b." head is the English column's letter only — never imported). The English page running head ("434 THE GOSPEL OF BARNABAS") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks "204^" (= 204b, OCR), 205a, (204*) in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *si[e]* printed with brackets ("the time is near"); *DIG uosstro\** with asterisk ("As I, your God, live"); *ehe* for *che* ("do you not see that"); *harechordo* ("remember"); *separate* for *separato* ("separated from sense") — none resolved silently.
3. **The counsel against Lazarus is kept whole and unsmoothed**: the reason given is the greatness of the miracle — Lazarus conversing, eating, and drinking — and the reason they hold back is his power, his following in Jerusalem, and his possession of Magdala and Bethany with his sisters. The check's footnote calls this proprietorship of whole villages an obvious anachronism ("we are in mediaeval Europe rather than in the Palestine of the first century") — recorded as the editors' note, the Italian kept as written.
4. **"Ezekiel," "Martha," "Mary"** render the Italian *hezechiele, marta, maria* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
5. Canonical parallels asserted: John xii. 10 ('the chief priests consulted that they might put Lazarus also to death'; here, they 'took counsel … to slay Lazarus'), John xii. 1, 2 (Jesus at Bethany, the supper, 'Martha served,' Lazarus 'one of them that sat at the table with him'; here, Jesus at table in Lazarus's house with Martha and Mary ministering), Luke x. 38–42 (Mary at Jesus' feet hearing his word, Martha's complaint, and 'Mary hath chosen that good part, which shall not be taken away from her'; here, 'Mary has chosen a part which shall not be taken from her for ever'), and Ezek. xviii. 20 — for the quoted clause only ('The soul that sinneth, it shall die'; here, 'the soul that sins shall die').
6. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CXCV b.," is confirmed in the Italian column.
