## Chapter 195

*[Editorial heading: What death is — the bird and the cord — Lazarus offers his house. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXCV b.", gloss letter b) at folio 205b. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Those who shall believe in me shall not die for ever, for through my word they shall perceive God within them, and therefore shall work out their salvation. What is death but an act which nature does by commandment of God? As it would be if one held a bird tied, and held the cord in his hand; when the master wants the bird to fly away, what does he do? Assuredly he commands the hand naturally to open, and so straightway the bird flies away. Our soul, as David the prophet says, is as a sparrow freed from the snare of the fowler, when man stands under the protection of God. And our life is like a cord with which nature holds the soul bound to the body and the human sense. When God wills and commands nature to open, life is broken and the soul escapes into the hands of the angel whom God has appointed to receive souls.

"Friends, do not weep when a friend dies, for our God has so willed it; but weep without end when he sins, for the soul dies, seeing that it separates itself from God, the true life. For if the body without communion of the soul is horrible, much more frightful is the soul without union of God — who with his grace and mercy makes it beautiful and gives it life."

And having said this, Jesus gave thanks to God, whereupon Lazarus said: "Lord, this house belongs to God my creator, with all that he has given me in keeping for the service of the poor; wherefore, since you are poor and have a great number of disciples — when it pleases you, and as much as it pleases you, come to dwell here, for the servant of God will minister to you as much as shall be needful, for love of God."

### Notes — Chapter 195
1. **Apparatus as printed**: marginal gloss "a" at *ha chossi uolluto* — the check renders it "By God the living and persisting"; marginal gloss "b" at *la unione di DIG* — the check renders it "The Chapter on the truths of death"; the "°" mark at *chreatore mio* (the check's p. 437 block gives "c God creates"). The English page running head ("JESUS IN THE HOUSE OF LAZARUS") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 205b, (204b), 206a, (205*) in the Italian column.
2. **Check divergence recorded**: the Italian reads *nelle mani del angello* — "into the hands of the angel" (singular) — while the check's English renders "in the hands of the angels" (plural); the Italian singular is translated as written. The check's footnote on the receiving angel cites 221b and 228* and Quran lxxix (Uriel — the name is the 1907 editors' identification, reached only by their inferential chain: 221b names Uriel among the Four Angels, and 228* gives Uriel the office of 'receiving souls'; the thematic Quran lxxix citation, on angels tearing out and gently drawing forth souls, names no angel; ch. 195 itself names no angel; the identification is recorded in parentheses, not asserted).
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *hato* for *atto* ("an act which nature does"); *il chapo* for *il capo* ("the master"); *nom piangi* for *non piangi* ("do not weep"); *hamicho* for *amico* ("a friend"); *elgie* for *egli è* — none resolved silently.
4. **"David"** renders the Italian *dauit* — the Italian spelling, not canonical memory. The sparrow-and-fowler saying is kept entire.
5. Canonical parallels asserted: Ps. cxxiv. 7 ('Our soul is escaped as a bird out of the snare of the fowlers'; here, 'Our soul, as David the prophet says, is as a sparrow freed from the snare of the fowler'), John xi. 25, 26 ('whosoever liveth and believeth in me shall never die'; here, 'Those who shall believe in me shall not die for ever'), and Phil. ii. 12 ('work out your own salvation'; here, 'shall work out their salvation'). The editors' other citations, John xiii. 33 and Ezek. xviii. 20, stand as printed record only (Ezekiel's clause was already asserted at ch. 194).
6. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CXCVI.," is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 196

*[Editorial heading: How good a thing it is to die — the obligation to teach — John's question. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXCVI." — no gloss letter in either column) at folio 206b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus rejoiced when he heard this, and said: "See now how good a thing it is to die! Lazarus has died only once, and has learned such teaching as the wisest men in the world, who have grown old among books, do not know. Would to God that every man might die only once and return to the world, like Lazarus, so that men might learn to live!"

John answered: "O master, is it permitted to me to speak a word?"

"Speak a thousand," answered Jesus, "for just as man is bound to dispense his goods in the service of God, so is he bound to dispense teaching; and the obligation is so much the greater, inasmuch as the word can raise a soul to penitence, while goods cannot bring back life to the dead. Wherefore he is a murderer who has the means to help a poor man, and, not helping him, the poor man dies of hunger; but greater is the murder of him who by the word of God could convert the sinner to penitence, and converts him not, but stands — as God says — like a dumb dog; against whom God says: 'The soul of the sinner that shall perish because you have hidden my word from him — I will require it at your hands, O unfaithful servant.' In what state, then, are now the scribes and Pharisees, who have the key and will not enter, but rather hinder those who would enter into eternal life!

"You ask me, O John, leave to speak a word, having listened to a hundred thousand of mine. In truth I tell you, I am bound to listen to you ten times for one that you have listened to me. And he who will not listen to others, every time he speaks he shall sin — seeing that we ought to do to others what we would for ourselves, and not do to others what we would not wish to receive."

Then said John: "O master, why has not God granted this to men — that they die once and return like Lazarus, so that they might learn to know themselves and their creator?"

### Notes — Chapter 196
1. **Apparatus as printed**: marginal gloss "b" at *dice Dio* — the check renders it "By God the living, and persistent, and the giver." The check's footnote "*" at "he shall sin" reads "He who does not wish not (sic) to hear others, if he speaks, commits sin each time. Inde." — the Italian has no corresponding marker recovered in the OCR at that point (*he cholui che non uolle asscoltare altri ogni uolta che elgi parllera pecheraa*); the Italian is translated as written. The English page running heads ("JESUS IN THE HOUSE OF LAZARUS"; "438 THE GOSPEL OF BARNABAS") are the printers' heads, not manuscript titles. Folio marks 206b, (205*), 207a, (206*) in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *pecheraa* (doubled a, "he shall sin"); *quen0* for *quello* (OCR-mangled, "what we would not wish to receive"); *ioane / gioane / giouane* for John — none resolved silently.
3. **Kept literal and unsmoothed**: "like a dumb dog" (*chome chane muto*) — canonical parallel asserted: Isa. lvi. 10 ('they are all dumb dogs, they cannot bark' — the watchmen who fail to warn; here, 'stands — as God says — like a dumb dog' for the one who withholds God's word and converts not the sinner), asserted for the dumb-dog image and the watchman context only; "he is a murderer who has the means to help a poor man, and, not helping him, the poor man dies of hunger"; "greater is the murder of him who... could convert the sinner to penitence, and converts him not."
4. **"John"** renders the Italian *ioane / gioane / giouane* throughout — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
5. Canonical parallels asserted: Isa. lvi. 10 (the dumb-dog image and watchman context, as at line 5033), Ezek. xxxiii. 4, 6 (the watchman who fails to warn — 'his blood will I require at the watchman's hand' (v. 6); here, the sinner perishing because the word was hidden, 'I will require it at your hands, O unfaithful servant'), and Luke xi. 52 ('ye have taken away the key of knowledge: ye entered not in yourselves, and them that were entering in ye hindered'; here, 'they have the key and will not enter, but rather hinder those who would enter into eternal life').
6. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CXCVII.," is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 197

*[Editorial heading: The parable of the axe — the sight of burial. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXCVII." — Italian column; the English column's head is OCR-mangled "CXCVIL", noted, not used) at folio 207b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus answered: "Tell me, John: there was a head of a household who gave a perfect axe to one of his servants, to cut down the wood that blocked the view of his house. But the labourer forgot the axe, and said: 'If the master would give me an old axe, I would easily cut down the wood.' Tell me, O John, what said the master? Surely, being angry, he took the old axe and struck him on the head, saying: 'You lazy scoundrel! I gave you an axe with which you could cut down the wood without toil, and you ask for this one, which is used with great toil, and whatever it cuts is wasted, good for nothing. I want you to cut the wood in such a way that your work is good.' Is this true?"

John answered: "It is most true."

"'As I live for ever,' says God, 'I have given a good axe to every man, which is the sight of the burial of a dead man. Those who wield it well remove without pain the wood of sins from their heart. Wherefore they receive my grace and mercy, giving them the reward of eternal life for their good working. But he who forgets that he is mortal, seeing others die at every time, and says: "If I saw the other life, I would do good" — my fury shall be upon him, and I will smite him so with death that he shall never receive any good.'"

"O John," said Jesus, "how great is the advantage of him who from the fall of others learns to stand upright!"

### Notes — Chapter 197
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian-column rubric reads "CXCVII." (no gloss letter); the English column's head is OCR-mangled "CXCVIL" — noted, not used, and no letter imported from it. The English page running heads ("JESUS IN THE HOUSE OF LAZARUS"; "440 THE GOSPEL OF BAENABAS") are the printers' heads, not manuscript titles. Folio mark 207b in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *q3sto* for *questo* ("Is this true?"); *Diob* ("says God" + gloss letter b — the check renders the gloss "By God the living, and persistent, and the giver"); *sconssuma* for *si consuma* ("whatever it cuts is wasted"); *bene uemno* ("he shall never receive any good" — printed form kept, not resolved); *giouani* for *giovanni* (vocative, "O John") — none resolved silently.
3. **The parable is kept entire and unsmoothed**: the perfect axe forgotten, the old axe asked for, the master's anger and the blow on the head, "You lazy scoundrel!" (*poltrone he scellerato*), and the application — the sight of burial as the good axe, the wood of sins, the fate of him who forgets he is mortal.
4. **"John"** renders the Italian *giouane / giouani* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). This chapter closes the slice; the next Italian-column rubric, "CXCVIII.," opens ch. 198 (next slice).

## Chapter 198

*[Editorial heading: Lazarus on dying well — the merit rule, and the confession. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXCVIII. ", no gloss letter) at folio 208a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Then Lazarus said: "Master, truly I tell you, I cannot understand what punishment he deserves who, every time he sees the dead carried to the tomb, does not fear God our creator — and who, for the things of this world, which he must leave behind entirely, offends his creator, who gave him everything." Then Jesus said to his disciples: "You call me master, and you do well, for God teaches you through my mouth. But what will you call Lazarus? Truly, here is the master of all the masters who teach the doctrine of this world. I have taught you how you must live well, but Lazarus will teach you how to die well. As God lives, he has received the gift of prophecy; so listen to his words, which are truth. And you must listen to him all the more, because good living is vain when one dies badly."

Lazarus said: "O master, I thank you for making the truth prized; so God will give you great merit." Then he who writes said: "O master, how does Lazarus speak the truth in telling you 'you will have merit,' when you said to Nicodemus that man merits nothing but punishment? So will you be punished by God?"

Jesus answered: "May it please God that I receive punishment from God in this world, for I have not served him as faithfully as I am bound to. But God has loved me so much in his mercy that he has taken every punishment away from me — so much so that I shall be tormented only in another person. For punishment was fitting for me, since men have called me God. But since I have confessed, not only that I am not God, as is the truth, but I have also confessed that I am not the Messiah — so God has taken the punishment away from me, and he will make a wicked one suffer it in my name, so that only the shame will be mine. So I tell you, my Barnabas: when a man speaks of what God will give to his neighbor, let him say that his neighbor merits it. But let him be careful, when he speaks of what God will give to himself, to say: 'God will give me' — and let him be careful not to say 'I merit,' for God is pleased to grant his mercy to his servants when they confess that they merit hell for their sins."

### Notes — Chapter 198
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian-column rubric reads "CXCVIII. " — recorded verbatim, no gloss letter in the Italian column (the English column's parallel head "CXCVIII. " is likewise without letter; nothing imported). The editors' marginal glosses on the Italian pages are translated by the check as: (a) "God creates"; (b) "By the living God"; (c) "He who lives in good and dies in evil, his good does not profit him" (check adds "Inde."); (d) "God punishes" — recorded as the check renders them, check-only. On the Italian page covering the chapter's close, the check renders the glosses as: (a) "God loves"; (b) "Apostle" [at *il messiab* — recorded exactly as the check prints it]; (c) "God gives"; (d) "God bestows" — check-only, not imported. The check's footnotes cite John xiii. 13 (the 'ye call me Master' saying), and 'Cp. above, 192* (p. 405)' (the Nicodemus passage). Canonical parallel asserted for the opening clause only: John xiii. 13 ('Ye call me Master and Lord: and ye say well'; here, 'You call me master, and you do well'). The internal cross-reference ('192* (p. 405)') stands as printed record only; the rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English running head (page 441, "OF DEATH AND ITS TEACHING") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 208a, 208b, 209a in the Italian column (OCR also shows "(2°7a)" and "(3°7)" — recorded as printed).
2. **Seam material translated exactly as written, never smoothed**: "ho chonfessato che io no son il messia" — "I have confessed that I am not the Messiah"; "saro solamente tormentato in altra perssona imperoche a me se chonueniua pena hauendo li homeni chiamato me DIG... la fara patire ad uno trissto in mio nome talmente che sara sollo mia la uergogna" — "I shall be tormented only in another person... he will make a wicked one suffer it in my name, so that only the shame will be mine." Kept whole, exactly as the Italian gives them. Footnote — undecided, never resolved: (a) the substitution passage sits in the same seam class as the earlier "not the Messiah" denials (ch. 42, 70, 96–97) and carries the standing Quran-contradiction fork (what the Quran says of the crucifixion and of Jesus' messiahship); (b) Robert's recorded insertion-suspicion on the named seam passages stands noted here as context, not as a decision — nothing is deleted or softened. The check's footnote "i. e. Judas Iscariot; cp. 222\* sqq. (pp. 473-481)" is the editors' interpretation — recorded check-only, not imported into the translation. *Messia* and *nontio* are kept distinct throughout the lane; no *nontio* occurs in this chapter.
3. **Kept as written**: "cholui che scriue" ("he who writes") — Barnabas's self-naming; "barnaba mio" ("my Barnabas") — Jesus addresses Barnabas by name; "nichodemo" — Nicodemus; the merit rule is kept entire ("say of his neighbor that he merits it... of himself say 'God will give me'... confess that they merit hell for their sins"), never smoothed against the earlier anti-merit teaching.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CXCIXe. ", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 199

*[Editorial heading: One tear of repentance quenches hell — why God calls good works "merit." Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CXCIXe. ", gloss letter e) at folio 209a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"God is so rich in mercy that, though the water of a thousand seas — if so many could be found — could not put out one spark of the flames of hell, one single tear of him who grieves at having offended God puts out the whole of hell, by the great mercy with which God helps him. So God, to confound Satan and to show his generosity, wanted every good work of his faithful servant to be called 'merit' before his mercy — and wanted it to be spoken this way of his neighbor. But let a man, of himself, be careful not to say 'I merit,' for he would be condemned."

### Notes — Chapter 199
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian-column rubric reads "CXCIXe. " — recorded verbatim, gloss letter e; the English column's parallel head "CXCIX e. " is not imported. The check renders the chapter-title gloss (e) as "The Chapter on kindness (courtesy)" and the marginal glosses as (f) "God is sufficient and the merciful" and (g, OCR "s") "God is munificent" — recorded as the check renders them, check-only. The English running head (page 443, "OF MERCY AND MERIT") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio mark 209a in the Italian column.
2. **Kept literal**: the whole tear-spark measure is kept entire and unsmoothed — "the water of a thousand seas... could not put out one spark... one single tear... puts out the whole of hell." The reason God calls good works "merit" is kept exactly as written: to confound Satan and to show his generosity — paired with the ch. 198 rule that a man must never say "I merit" of himself.
3. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CC. ", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 200

*[Editorial heading: The entry into Jerusalem on the colt — the stones cry out. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CC. " — recorded verbatim; no gloss letter visible in the Italian column) at folio 209b. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus turned to Lazarus and said to him: "Brother, I must stay in the world a short time; so when I am near your house I will never go anywhere else, for you will serve me not for love of me but for love of God." It was near the Passover of the Jews, so Jesus said to his disciples: "Let us go to Jerusalem to eat the Passover lamb." And he sent Peter and John to the city, saying: "You will find a she-ass near the gate of the city, with a colt. Loose her and bring her here, for I need to ride on her into Jerusalem. And if anyone asks you, saying 'Why do you loose her?' tell them: 'The master needs her,' and they will let you bring her." The disciples went and found what Jesus had told them, and so they brought the she-ass with the colt. So the disciples put their mantles on the colt, on which Jesus rode. And it happened that, when the men of Jerusalem heard that Jesus of Nazareth was coming, the men went out with their children, eager to see Jesus, carrying in their hands branches of palm and of olive, singing: "Blessed be he who comes to us in the name of God! Hosanna, son of David!" When Jesus came to the city, the men spread their garments under the feet of the ass, singing: "Blessed be he who comes to us in the name of the Lord God! Hosanna, son of David!" The Pharisees rebuked Jesus, saying: "Do you not see what these say? Make them be silent." Jesus said to them: "As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — if men should be silent, the stones would cry out against the unbelief of wicked sinners." And when Jesus had said this, all the stones of Jerusalem cried out with a great noise: "Blessed be he who comes to us in the name of the Lord God!" Nevertheless the Pharisees remained in their unbelief, and gathering together among themselves they took counsel to catch him in his speech.

### Notes — Chapter 200
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian-column rubric reads "CC. " — recorded verbatim; no gloss letter is visible in the Italian column and none is supplied (the English column's parallel head is OCR'd "CO. ", likewise without letter — not imported). The check renders the marginal glosses as: (a) "By permission of God"; (b) "God is sovereign"; (c) "By the living God" — recorded as the check renders them, check-only; the raw Arabic glosses stand printed inline in the Italian column (OCR "a J^s\* il. b Jj-y. « Jaou. il. d v^j il. f") — recorded as printed. The check renders the ch. 201 chapter-title gloss (d) as "The Chapter on gentleness" — check-only, not imported. Canonical parallels asserted: Matt. xxi. 2–9 and parallels (the entry — the ass and colt loosed and brought, 'The Lord hath need of them,' garments spread, 'Hosanna to the son of David'), Luke xix. 39–40 (the Pharisees' rebuke and 'if these should hold their peace, the stones would immediately cry out'; here the stones cry out with a great noise), and Luke xi. 54 (seeking to catch something out of his mouth; here, 'they took counsel to catch him in his speech'). The editors' other citations, Luke xxii. 8 (Peter and John sent for the ass, not for the Passover) and Luke xx. 26, stand as printed record only; the rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English running head of page 445 was not recovered in the OCR — flagged, not invented. Folio marks 209b, 210a in the Italian column (OCR also shows "(209 )" — recorded as printed).
2. **Kept as written**: the she-ass with the colt — both brought, the mantles laid on the colt, and Jesus rides the colt ("possero li loro mantelli sopra il polledro sopra del qualle chaualcho iessu"); the crowd's two cries kept distinct ("in the name of God" outside the city, "in the name of the Lord God" inside); the two printed forms "fiollo di dauit" and "fiolo di dauit" kept as printed; the stones' cry kept literal and unsmoothed ("all the stones of Jerusalem cried out with a great noise").
3. **"Peter," "John," "David," "Jerusalem"** render the Italian *pietro, giouani, dauit/dauid, ierussalem* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory. "Lazarus" renders *lazzaro*.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCId. ", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 201

*[Editorial heading: The woman caught in adultery — the mirror in the dust. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCId. ", gloss letter d) at folio 210a. The heading above is editorial.]*

When Jesus had entered the temple, the scribes and Pharisees brought to him a woman caught in adultery. They said among themselves: "If he saves her, it is against the law of Moses — so we have him as guilty. And if he condemns her, it is against his own teaching, which preaches mercy." So coming to Jesus they said: "Master, we have found this woman in adultery. Moses commanded that she be stoned; but you, what do you say?" Jesus stooped down and with his finger made a mirror in the earth, in which everyone saw his own iniquities. As they still pressed for the answer, Jesus stood up, and pointing to the mirror with his finger said: "He among you who is without sin, let him be the first to stone her." And again he stooped down, making the mirror. The men, seeing this, went out one by one, beginning with the eldest, for they were ashamed to see their abominations. Jesus stood up, and seeing no one but the woman said: "Woman, where are they who condemned you?" The woman answered, weeping: "Lord, they are gone; and if you will pardon me, as God lives, I will sin no more." Then Jesus said: "Blessed be God! Go in peace and sin no more, for God has not sent me to condemn you."

So Jesus, calling together the scribes and Pharisees, said to them: "Tell me: if one of you had a hundred sheep, and one of them were lost — would you not go seeking it, leaving the ninety-nine? And having found it, would you not put it on your shoulders? And calling your neighbors together, would you not say: 'Rejoice with me, for I have found the sheep I had lost'? Certainly you would do this. Now tell me: will our God love man less — man, for whom he made the world? As God lives — so there is joy before the angels of God over one sinner who repents, for sinners make known the mercy of God."

### Notes — Chapter 201
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian-column rubric reads "CCId. " — recorded verbatim, gloss letter d; the English column's parallel is not imported. The check renders the chapter-title gloss (d) as "The Chapter on gentleness" and the marginal glosses as: (a) "By the living God"; (b) "God loves"; (c) "God created the world for the sons of Adam" (check adds "Inde.") — recorded as the check renders them, check-only; the raw Arabic glosses stand printed inline in the Italian column (OCR "c > «lb.") — recorded as printed. Canonical parallels asserted: John viii. 1–11 (the woman taken in adultery — the same pericope, here with Barnabas's 'mirror in the earth' variant), Luke xv. 3–7 (the lost sheep — the hundred, the ninety-nine left, the seeking, the shoulders, the rejoicing), and Luke xv. 10 ('there is joy in the presence of the angels of God over one sinner that repenteth'; here, 'there is joy before the angels of God over one sinner who repents'). The rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English running head (page 447, "THE WOMAN TAKEN IN ADULTERY") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 210a, 211a in the Italian column (OCR also shows "209 '" — recorded as printed).
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: "fece uno spechio in terra" — "made a mirror in the earth" — kept literal, the mirror detail unsmoothed (the men see their own iniquities in it and leave "one by one, beginning with the eldest"); the closing "[DIG]" stands in the editors' square brackets — kept as printed, not silently expanded. "Viue DIG\* " carries a printed asterisk — recorded as printed. The lost-sheep similitude is kept entire in the Italian's order, including the closing turn: "will our God love man less — man, for whom he made the world?"
3. **"Moses"** renders the Italian *moisse* — the Italian spelling, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCIL" [uncertain — see ch. 202 note 1], is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 202

*[Editorial heading: The physician and the righteous-unrighteous — the lament over Jerusalem. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCIL" — recorded verbatim; the gloss letter is doubtful in the OCR, flagged [uncertain], not resolved) at folio 211a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"Tell me: by whom is the physician more loved — by those who have never had any sickness, or by those whom the physician has healed of a grave sickness?" The Pharisees said to him: "And how will the healthy man love the physician? Certainly he will love him only for not falling sick — and, not knowing sickness, he will love the physician little." Then with force of spirit Jesus spoke, saying: "As God lives — your own tongues condemn your pride, for our God is loved more by the sinner who repents, knowing the great mercy of God upon himself, than by the righteous. For the righteous man does not know the mercy of God. So there is more joy before the angels of God over one sinner who repents than over ninety-nine righteous. Where are the righteous in our time? As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — great is the number of the righteous-unrighteous, for their condition is equal to Satan's." The scribes and Pharisees answered: "We are sinners, so God will have mercy on us." And they said this to tempt him — for the scribes and Pharisees count it the greatest insult to be called sinners. Then Jesus said: "I fear that you are righteous-unrighteous. For if you have sinned and deny your sin, calling yourselves righteous, you are unrighteous. And if in your heart you hold yourselves righteous, and with your tongue you say you are sinners — then you are doubly righteous-unrighteous." So the scribes and Pharisees, hearing this, were confounded and went away, leaving Jesus with his disciples in peace.

And they went into the house of Simon the leper, from whom he had cleansed the leprosy. The citizens gathered the sick to Simon's house and begged Jesus for the healing of the sick. Then Jesus, knowing that his hour was near, said: "Call as many sick as there are, for God is mighty and merciful to heal them." They answered: "We know of no others who are sick here in Jerusalem." Weeping, Jesus answered: "O Jerusalem, O Israel! I weep over you, for you do not know your visitation — for I wanted to gather you to the love of God your creator, as the hen gathers her chicks under her wings, and you would not. So this is what God says to you —"

### Notes — Chapter 202
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the rubric reads "CCIL" in the OCR — recorded verbatim, the trailing gloss letter flagged **[uncertain]**; the English column's parallel head (OCR "ecu. ") is not imported. The check renders the marginal glosses as: (a) "God is living"; (b) "By the living God"; (c) "God is powerful and the Merciful"; (d) "God creates" — recorded as the check renders them, check-only. Canonical parallels asserted: Luke xv. 7, 10 (joy over the repenting sinner — 'joy in the presence of the angels of God over one sinner that repenteth' (v. 10), 'more than over ninety and nine just persons' (v. 7); here, 'more joy before the angels of God over one sinner who repents than over ninety-nine righteous') and Matt. xxvi. 6 (the house of Simon the leper; here the same). The editors' other citation, Luke iv. 38–40 (Simon Peter's house — a different Simon), and their note of the confusion between Simon the Leper and Simon Peter, stand as printed record only; the rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English running head (page 449, "OF THE RIGHTEOUS UNRIGHTEOUS") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 211a, 211b, 212a, 212b in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *iussti iniussti* — "righteous-unrighteous" — kept as the Italian's oxymoron throughout, unsmoothed ("great is the number of the righteous-unrighteous... their condition is equal to Satan's"; "you are doubly righteous-unrighteous"). The editors' MS note at "doue sono¹ li iussti" reads "1 MS. apparently *deue sono* (sic)" — recorded as printed; the printed text is followed. The hen-and-chicks lament is kept whole, ending on the hinge "pero questo dice DIG ha te" ("So this is what God says to you —"), which opens ch. 203's judgment speech — the chapter division is placed by the printed rubric "CCIII*. " as recovered, not by narrative repair.
3. **"Simon," "Israel," "Jerusalem"** render the Italian *simone, issdraelle, ierussalem* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "CCIII*. ", is confirmed at folio 212b — ch. 203 (God's judgment speech on Jerusalem) opens the next slice.

## Chapter 203

*[Editorial heading: God's judgment on Jerusalem — the city of hard heart. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCIII*. " — gloss letter rendered as a star, recorded verbatim) at folio 212b. The chapter opens mid-utterance: God's speech continues the closing saying of ch. 202 (the hen and her chickens). The heading above is editorial.]*

"O city of hard heart and perverse of mind! I have sent you my servant so that you would turn to your heart and do penance. But you, O city of confusion, have forgotten what I did over Egypt and over Pharaoh for love of you, O Israel. Many times you weep so that my servant may heal your body of sickness, and you seek to kill my servant because he seeks to heal your soul of sin. Shall you then alone remain unpunished by me? Shall you then live eternally? And shall your pride deliver you from my hands? Certainly not. For I will bring princes with an army against you, and they will surround you with power, and in such a way will I betray you into their hands that your pride shall fall into hell. I will not pardon the old men nor the widows. I will not pardon the children, but I will give you all to hunger, sword, and derision. And the temple, upon which I looked with mercy, I will desolate together with the city, so that you will be a fable and a derision and a proverb among the nations. Thus is my wrath fixed upon you, and my indignation does not sleep."

### Notes — Chapter 203
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "The Chapter on the anger against Jerusalem" (a) and "God is victorious" (b) — the letter b is printed in the Italian column after *indinatione* at the chapter's close. Canonical parallels asserted: Luke xiii. 34 ('how often would I have gathered thy children together, as a hen doth gather her brood under her wings, and ye would not' — the hinge saying at the close of ch. 202 that this speech continues), Luke xix. 41–44 (the weeping over the city, 'thou knewest not the time of thy visitation,' the surrounding army and the desolation; here in the same words and order), and Isa. xxiv. 10 ('The city of confusion'; here, 'O city of confusion'). The editors' other citation, Luke x. 15 (spoken to Capernaum), stands as printed record only; the rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English page running head ("GOD'S JUDGEMENT ON JERUSALEM") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio mark 212b in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *Viuerai adonque in etterno* — "Shall you then live eternally?" kept as a question, not softened; *vigilla la mia indinatione* — "my indignation does not sleep" — *vigilla* (watching, unsleeping) kept whole. The "city of confusion," "hunger, sword, and derision," and "a fable and a derision and a proverb among the nations" are kept entire, in the Italian's order.
3. **"Israel"** renders the Italian *issdraelle* — the Italian spelling, not canonical memory. The speaker of the speech is *DIG* ("God"), voiced through Jesus ("Wherefore God saith thus unto thee," ch. 202 close).
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCIVc. ", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 204

*[Editorial heading: Jesus heals the sick in Jerusalem — and Jerusalem weeps for her ruin, not her sin. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCIVc.", gloss letter c) at folio 213a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Having said this, Jesus said again: "You do not know that there are other sick folk. As God lives, they are fewer in Jerusalem, those who have a sound soul, than those who are sick in body. And so that you may know the truth I say to you, O sick folk, in the name of God let the sickness depart from you." And having said this, immediately they were healed. The men wept, having heard the wrath of God upon Jerusalem, and asked for mercy. Jesus said: "If Jerusalem shall weep for her sins and do penance, walking in my ways, says God, I will not remember her iniquities any more, and I will not do to her any of the evil which I have said. But Jerusalem weeps for her ruin and not for dishonoring me, with which she has made my name blasphemed among the nations. Therefore my fury is kindled much more. As I live eternally, if Job, Abraham, Samuel, David, and Daniel my servants, with Moses, should pray for this people, my wrath upon Jerusalem will not be appeased." And having said this, Jesus withdrew into the house, everyone remaining in fear.

### Notes — Chapter 204
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "The Chapter on the anger of God against Jerusalem" (c), "By the living God" (d, at *Viue DIG*), and "By God's permission" (e, at *in nome di moe*). Their printed note reads "1 MS. *inffermo*" against the printed *ui dicho inffermi* ("I say to you, O sick folk") — the printed plural is kept, not silently corrected to the singular. Canonical parallel asserted: Jer. xviii. 8 (the nation that turns from its evil — 'I will repent of the evil that I thought to do unto them'; here, 'I will not do to her any of the evil which I have said,' if Jerusalem weeps for her sins and does penance). The editors' other citation, Ezek. xiv. 14, stands as printed record only (the motif of named righteous men who cannot avert judgment; Ezekiel's three are Noah, Daniel, and Job, and Barnabas's six who pray are Job, Abraham, Samuel, David, and Daniel, with Moses); the rest of the note is apparatus as printed. a note on p. 450 of the check cites Luke xv. 7, 10 for the Simon-the-leper/Simon-Peter confusion. Folio marks 213a, 213b in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *dissonor[ar]emi* — the bracketed OCR reading is rendered "dishonoring me" and flagged **[uncertain]** rather than resolved from the check; *Viuo in etterno* — "As I live eternally" — with the gloss letter b printed attached to *etterno* in the Italian column, recorded verbatim. The list of intercessors is kept entire, in the Italian's order: Job, Abraham, Samuel, David, Daniel, with Moses.
3. **"Job, Abraham, Samuel, David, Daniel, Moses, Jerusalem"** render the Italian *iob, abraham, samuele, dauit, danielle, moisse, ierussalem* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCV. ", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 205

*[Editorial heading: Mary anoints Jesus — Judas bargains with the high priest. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCV. ", no gloss letter) at folio 213b. The heading above is editorial.]*

While Jesus was supping with his disciples in the house of Simon the leper, behold Mary the sister of Lazarus entered into the house, and having broken a vessel she poured ointment over the head and garment of Jesus. Seeing this, Judas the traitor wanted to hinder Mary from doing such a work, saying: "Go and sell the ointment and bring the money, that I may give it to the poor." Jesus said: "Why do you hinder her? Let her be, for the poor you shall have always with you, but me you shall not have always." Judas answered: "O master, this ointment could be sold for three hundred pieces of money; now see how many poor would be helped." Jesus answered: "O Judas, I know your heart; have patience therefore, and I will give you everything." Everyone ate with fear, and the disciples grieved, for they knew that Jesus must soon depart from them. But Judas was indignant, because he knew he was losing thirty pieces of money for the ointment not sold, seeing that he stole the tenth of all that was given to Jesus.

He went to find the high priest, who assembled in council priests, scribes, and Pharisees. To them Judas spoke, saying: "What will you give me, and I will betray into your hands Jesus, who wanted to make himself king of Israel?" They answered: "Now how will you give him into our hand?" Judas said: "When I shall know that he goes outside the city to pray, I will tell you, and I will lead you to where he shall be found; for taking him in the city will not be without sedition." The high priest answered: "If you give him into our hand we will give you thirty pieces of gold, and I will do you such good as you shall see."

### Notes — Chapter 205
1. **Apparatus as printed**: no marginal gloss letters in the Italian column for this chapter. Canonical parallels asserted: John xii. 1–8 and Matt. xxvi. 6–13 (the anointing — here conflating the two canonical forms: Simon the leper's house and the pouring over the head from Matthew; Mary the sister of Lazarus, Judas named as the objector, the three hundred pieces, and 'the poor you shall have always with you, but me you shall not have always' from John) and Matt. xxvi. 14 (Judas to the chief priests — 'What will ye give me, and I will deliver him unto you?'; here in the same words, the price thirty pieces). The rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English page running head ("IN THE HOUSE OF SIMON THE LEPER") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 213b, 214a in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *iuda [era] indignato* — the bracketed *era* is kept as printed (supplied by the editors in the Italian column); *elgi robaua la decima di quanto hera dato ha iessu* — "he stole the tenth of all that was given to Jesus" — kept literal, not harmonized; *che si uolle fare Re de issdrael* — "who wanted to make himself king of Israel" — the high priest's charge, kept whole; the thirty pieces of gold kept exactly as written.
3. **"Simon, Mary, Lazarus, Judas, Israel"** render the Italian *simone, maria, lazaro, iuda, issdrael* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory. *iuda traditore* — "Judas the traitor" — is kept as written.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCVI. ", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 206

*[Editorial heading: Jesus confesses in the temple — "I am not God, nor son of God, nor even the Messiah." Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCVI. ", no gloss letter) at folio 214a. The heading above is editorial.]*

When day was come, Jesus went up to the temple with a great multitude of people. Whereupon the high priest drew near to him, saying: "Tell me, O Jesus, have you forgotten what you confessed, that you are not God, nor son of God, nor even the Messiah?" Jesus answered: "Certainly not, for I have not forgotten; for this is my confession, which I shall bear before the tribunal of God on the day of judgment. For all that is written in the book of Moses is most true, because God our Creator is one alone, and I am the servant of God and desire to serve the messenger of God whom you call the messiah."¹ The high priest said: "Then what is the use of coming to the temple with so great a multitude of people? Do you seek perhaps to make yourself king of Israel? Beware lest some danger befall you." Jesus answered: "If I sought my own glory and desired my portion in this world, when the people of Nain wanted to make me their king I would not have fled. Believe me in truth that I seek nothing in this world." The high priest said: "Then we lack one thing to know concerning the messiah." And then the priests, scribes, and Pharisees made a circle around Jesus. Jesus answered: "What is that thing which you seek to know about the messiah? Perhaps it is the lie? Of a truth I will not tell you the lie; for if I had told the lie I would have been adored by you and by the scribes and Pharisees with all Israel. But because I tell you the truth you hate me and seek to kill me." The high priest said: "Now we know that you have the devil upon your back, for you are a Samaritan and have no respect for the priest of God."

¹ **SEAM NOTE.** The Italian reads *he io son seruo di DIG he dessidero di seruire al nontio di DIG e il qualle chiamate messia* — translated exactly as written above: "I am the servant of God and desire to serve the messenger of God whom you call the messiah." Robert's standing suspicion (recorded 2026-10-10) is that the unnamed-*nontio* and named-Muhammad passages in this Gospel may be later insertions rather than the Gospel's own voice — a question never decided, never smoothed, never deleted. The Quran-contradiction fork stays open: the reading is kept as printed and the suspicion is not resolved in either direction.

### Notes — Chapter 206
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian column prints *il mesiab* in the high priest's question — the letter b is recorded verbatim as a printed gloss marker attached to the word (the English column does not supply its reading, and it is not resolved). The editors' marginal glosses (from the check, check-only) render the chapter's Arabic-script glosses: "God our Creator is One, and I am his servant (slave), and I wish to be in the service of his Apostle"; "Apostle"; "God is One, and I am the slave of God"; "God creates"; "The Apostle of God." Their printed note reads "MS. *che he.*" against the printed *che quanto he scrito nel libro di moisse he uerissimo* — the printed reading is kept, not silently corrected. Canonical parallel asserted: John xviii. 36 ('My kingdom is not of this world'; here, 'I seek nothing in this world' — with the refused kingship at Nain, 151b, as the instance). The editors' cross-references 99a (denial of divinity) and 102a (denial of messiahship) are internal to this book and stand as printed record only; the rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The editors' note on *elgi la buggia* reads: 'i.e. that he is Son of David, and not of Ishmael; cp. above, 201b' — recorded as the editors' gloss, not asserted. Ch. 206 does not itself name David or Ishmael at this saying; the gloss reads the saying through the Ishmael-descent passage at ch. 208, and it stands as printed record only; the edition asserts nothing, and readers decide. Folio marks 214a, 214b in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *se io hauessi deto la buggia saria stato addorato da te* — "if I had told the lie I would have been adored by you" — kept whole; the "perhaps it is the lie?" question and the "because I tell you the truth you hate me and seek to kill me" answer kept in the Italian's order. *messia* ("the Messiah," in the high priest's question and the closing messiah question) and *nontio* ("the messenger," in Jesus' confession) are kept DISTINCT, as written.
3. **"Jesus, Moses, Israel, Nain"** render the Italian *iessu, moisse, issdrael, naim* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCVII. ", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 207

*[Editorial heading: Who has the devil on his back — the bridle, the garment, and the head and members. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCVII. ", no gloss letter) at folio 215a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus answered: "As God lives, I have not the devil upon my back, but I seek to cast out the devil. Therefore the devil stirs up the world against me, because I am not of this world. But I seek that God may be glorified, who has sent me into the world. Hear me therefore, for I will tell you who has the devil upon his back. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands, he who works according to the will of the devil — he has the devil upon his back, who has put upon him the bridle of his will and rules him at his pleasure, making him run into every iniquity. Even as a garment changes its name with the change of its owner, though it is all one cloth, so men, though they are all of one matter, are different by the works of him who works in man. If I have — as I know — sinned, why do you not rebuke me as a brother, instead of hating me as an enemy? In truth the members of a body help one another when they are united with the head, and those that are divided from the head do not help it; for the hands of one body do not feel the pain of the feet of another body, but only of the body in which they are united. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands, he who fears and loves God his Creator has the feeling of mercy over those over whom God his head has mercy. And seeing that God does not want the death of the sinner, but awaits every one **[uncertain]** to penance, if you were of that body in which I am incorporated, as God lives you would help me to work according to my head."

### Notes — Chapter 207
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "By the living God" (a, at *Viue Dioa*), "God sends" (b, at *che mi a mandate b*), and "God the merciful: God creates" (c, at *che DIG chapo suo ha misserichordia c*) — check-only. Their printed note reads "So MS. Conj. *hogniuno*" against the printed *he ogniunol.* — the printed reading is kept, the MS form recorded, and the clause flagged **[uncertain]** rather than resolved by the conjecture. Canonical parallels asserted: John viii. 40 ('ye seek to kill me, a man that hath told you the truth'; here, at the close of ch. 206 whose dispute this chapter continues, 'because I tell you the truth you hate me and seek to kill me') and John viii. 49 ('I have not a devil'; here, 'I have not the devil upon my back,' answering the charge of ch. 206). The rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English page running head ("JESUS DISPUTETH WITH THE HIGH PRIEST") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 215a, 215b in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *ha il diauollo ha dosso* — "has the devil upon his back" — kept literal throughout (not modernized to "possessed"); the bridle-and-owner similitudes (bridle of his will; garment changing name with owner) kept entire, in the Italian's order; *chia il diauollo ha dosso* ("who has the devil upon his back") read as *chi ha*; the doubled *che cholui … che cholui* construction kept as written.
3. **Name spellings**: no names occur in the Italian of this chapter besides *Dio/DIG*; nothing imported.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCVIII. ", is confirmed in the Italian column; ch. 208 opens with Jesus' "if I work iniquity, reprove me" saying.

## Chapter 208

*[Editorial heading: The temple confrontation — "If I work iniquity, reprove me"; the stoning; Nicodemus counsels flight beyond Cedron. Manuscript rubric "CCVIII." (no gloss letter) at folio 216a. The heading above is editorial.]*

"If I work iniquity, reprove me, and God will love you, because you will be doing his will. But if no one can reprove me of sin, it is a sign that you are not sons of Abraham as you call yourselves, nor are you incorporated with that head with which Abraham was incorporated. As God lives, so greatly did Abraham love God that he not only broke the false idols and abandoned his father and his mother, but was willing to kill his own son to obey God."

The high priest answered: "This I ask of you, and I do not seek to kill you; but tell me, who was this son of Abraham?"

Jesus answered: "The zeal of your honor, O my God, burns me, and I cannot be silent; therefore I tell you in truth that the son of Abraham was Ishmael, from whom must descend the messiah promised to Abraham, to bless in him all the tribes of the earth."

The high priest, hearing this, grew angry and cried out: "Let us stone this impious man, for he is an Ishmaelite and has blasphemed against Moses and against the law of God." Whereupon every scribe and Pharisee, with the elders of the people, took stones to stone Jesus, who vanished from their eyes and went out of the temple. And through the great desire they had to kill Jesus, blinded by fury and savage hatred, they struck one another, so that a thousand men died there; and they polluted the holy temple.

The disciples and the believers, who saw Jesus go out of the temple — for to them he was not hidden — followed him to the house of Simon. Then Nicodemus came there and advised Jesus to go out of Jerusalem, beyond the brook Cedron, saying: "Lord, I have a garden with a house beyond the brook Cedron; therefore I pray you, go there with some of your disciples to stay there until this hatred of our priests passes. For I will minister to you what is necessary; and the multitude of the disciples, leave them here in the house of Simon and in my house, for God will provide for everything." This Jesus did, and he desired to have with him only the twelve first-called apostles.

### Notes — Chapter 208
1. **SEAM — translated exactly as written.** The Italian reads: *il fiolo di abraha fu issmaelle dal qualle deue disscendere il messia promesso ha habraham di benedire in esso tutte le tribu della terra* ("the son of Abraham was Ishmael, from whom must descend the messiah promised to Abraham, to bless in him all the tribes of the earth"). Rendered word-for-word — never smoothed, softened, deleted, or decided. Robert's insertion-suspicion is recorded; the Quran-contradiction fork stands open and undecided: the passage distinguishes Jesus from "the messiah" and makes the messiah descend from Ishmael, which contradicts the Qur'an's identification of the Messiah with Jesus (Q 3:45; 4:157, 4:171–172). *Messia* is kept distinct from *nontio* — "messiah" here, never "messenger." The editors' rendering of the Arabic marginal gloss at *il messia* — "The prophet of God, the son of Ishmael" — is recorded as the check gives it, not imported into the text.
2. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian column carries marginal gloss letters a (*Viue DIG a*) and b (*il messia b*); the editors render them "By the living God" (a) and "The prophet of God, the son of Ishmael" (b, with "Inde."). The printed *prouedera0* ("God will provide") carries the check's gloss letter c ("God ordains") — kept as printed, the "0" recorded verbatim. The Italian column's Arabic glosses are unrecoverable in the OCR (garbled as "a ^ 4lb. b sJ~. JjU-J ^.1 4)1 / 4)1."). Canonical parallels asserted: John viii. 46 ('Which of you convinceth me of sin?'; here, 'if no one can reprove me of sin') and John ii. 17 ('The zeal of thine house hath eaten me up'; here, 'The zeal of your honor, O my God, burns me, and I cannot be silent'). The editors' folio cross-references ('above, 28'; 'above, 12'; 'above, p. 23, note 6'; '13') are internal to this book and stand as printed record only; the rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The editors also cite Gen. xxii. 18 ('in thy seed shall all the nations of the earth be blessed'). The blessing wording is parallel to this chapter's 'to bless in him all the tribes of the earth,' but Barnabas names the son as Ishmael and makes the messiah descend from him; the citation therefore stands as printed record only, and no application of Gen. xxii. 18 is asserted. The English page running head ("JESUS STOXED BY THE JEWS" — "STOXED" for "STONED" in the OCR) is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 216a, 216b in the Italian column ("215 '" and "15 '" are margin garbles in the OCR).
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *Risspose iessu j* — the "j" after "iessu" is unaccounted for in the check and is recorded verbatim, flagged **[uncertain]** (possibly a gloss or footnote marker in the print). The thousand dead (*mille homeni*) and the temple's pollution are kept entire, unsmoothed.
4. **"Abraham," "Moses," "Ishmael," "Nicodemus," "Simon," "Jerusalem," "Cedron"** render the Italian *habraham/abraham/abraha, moisse, issmaelle, nichodemo, simone, ierussalem, cedron* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). No Italian-column rubric was recovered for the next chapter (see ch. 209 note 1).

## Chapter 209

*[Editorial heading: Gabriel visits Mary at prayer; she seeks Jesus beyond Cedron. No Italian-column rubric recovered in the OCR — the break is placed per the English column's "CCIX *." head and narrative continuity (new scene), flagged honestly; see note 1. The heading above is editorial.]*

At this time, while Mary the virgin, mother of Jesus, was standing in prayer, the angel Gabriel visited her and told her of the persecution of her son, saying: "Fear not, Mary, for God will protect him from the world." Wherefore Mary, weeping, departed from Nazareth and came to Jerusalem, to the house of Mary Salome her sister, seeking her son. But since he had secretly retired beyond the brook of Cedron, she could not see him any more in this world, except after the deed of shame. For the angel Gabriel, with the angel Michael, Raphael, and Uriel, by commission of God, brought him to her.

### Notes — Chapter 209
1. **Numbering**: the djvu OCR recovers no Italian-column rubric for this chapter — the Italian text ("In questo tempo...") begins directly after the English page footer, with no head. The division follows the edition's own chaptering: the English column heads it "CCIX *." (the star is the English column's gloss letter, never imported) and the narrative breaks cleanly to a new scene (Mary at prayer). This is the missing-rubric class of ch. 96/119/123/125/170 — recorded, never repaired; no Italian rubric is invented.
2. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' chapter-title gloss (English column's rendering of the Arabic marginal gloss) reads "Chapter on the descent of Gabriel to Mary." The printed *ehustodirab* ("will protect him") carries the check's gloss letter b, rendered "God guards" — kept as printed. The editors' printed note "1 MS. *uederlo uederlo* (bis)" stands at the printed *uederlo x piu* ("see him any more") — the MS apparently doubles *uederlo*; the printed text is followed, not silently corrected. No canonical parallel is asserted for this chapter. The check's citations Mark xv. 40, xvi. 1 and John xix. 25, with its Salome-tradition note (Epiphanius, Nicephorus), are cross-references for the identity of Mary Salome — named here only as Mary's sister — and stand as printed record only, as does the internal cross-reference 'below, 228' (p. 483) for the angels bringing Jesus to his mother; the rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English page running head ("GABRIEL COMFOETETH MARY" — "COMFOETETH" for "COMFORTETH," long-s OCR) is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. No folio mark recovered in the Italian OCR for this chapter; it falls between 216b (end of 208) and 217a (opening of 210).
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *DID* for DIO in *per chomissione di DID* ("by commission of God") — kept as printed, noted. *lo eccesso dello hobrobio* ("the deed of shame") is kept literal; "crucifixion" is not imported.
4. **"Mary," "Gabriel," "Nazareth," "Jerusalem," "Mary Salome," "Michael," "Raphael," "Uriel," "Cedron"** render the Italian *maria, gabrielo, nazaret, ierussale, maria sallome, michaele, Rafaelo, Vriello, cedron* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.

## Chapter 210

*[Editorial heading: The high priest's speech — the council against Jesus; the two Roman decrees; Herod and the governor united. Manuscript rubric "CCX." (no gloss letter) at folio 217a. The heading above is editorial.]*

When the confusion in the temple ceased with the departure of Jesus, the high priest ascended on high, and having made a sign of silence with his hands, he said: "Brothers, what are we doing? Do you not see that this man has deceived the whole world with his diabolical art? Now, how did he vanish, if he is not a magician? Certainly, if he were holy and a prophet, he would not blaspheme against God and against Moses his servant and against the messiah who is the hope of Israel. And what shall I say? He has blasphemed all our priesthood. Therefore in truth I tell you that if he is not removed from the world, Israel will be polluted, and our God will give us over to the nations. Behold now how through him this holy temple is polluted." And the high priest spoke in such a way that many turned away from Jesus, so that the secret persecution was turned into an open one — so that the high priest went in person to Herod and to the Roman governor, accusing Jesus of wanting to make himself king of Israel; and of this they had false witnesses. Whereupon a general council was held against Jesus, because the Roman decree made them afraid — seeing that twice the senate had sent a decree concerning Jesus. In one decree it was forbidden, on pain of life, that no one should call Jesus of Nazareth, prophet of the Jews, either God or son of God; in the other it was forbidden, under capital sentence, that anyone should contend on account of Jesus of Nazareth, prophet of the Jews. Wherefore on this account there was a great division among them. For some wanted that they should write again to Rome against Jesus; others said that Jesus should be left alone, with no regard for his talk, as that of a fool; others alleged the great miracles that he did. But the high priest spoke, that under pain of anathema no one should say a word in defense of Jesus; and he spoke to Herod and to the governor, saying: "In any case we have a bad bargain in our hands, for if we kill this sinner we have acted against the decree of Caesar, and if we let him live and he makes himself king, how will the matter go?" Then Herod rose up and threatened the governor, saying: "Beware, lest through your favoring this man this nation rebel; for I will accuse you before Caesar as a rebel." Then the governor feared the senate and made peace with Herod, for before they hated one another to the death; and they united as one for the death of Jesus. And they said to the high priest: "Whenever you know where the evildoer is, send to us, for we will give you the soldiers." This was done to fulfill the prophecy of David, who had foretold of Jesus, prophet of Israel, saying: "The princes and kings of the earth are united against the holy one of Israel, because he announces to them the salvation of the world." Whereupon on that day a general search was made for Jesus throughout Jerusalem.

### Notes — Chapter 210
1. **Seam-adjacent *messia* — translated exactly as written.** The high priest's speech reads *chontra il messia il qualle he la speranza de issdraele* ("against the messiah who is the hope of Israel"). This is the priest's word for the expected one — kept distinct from *nontio*, and it stands as the provocation context for the ch. 208 seam saying (the Ishmael-messiah claim is what the priest calls blasphemy). Nothing smoothed, softened, or decided.
2. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian-column rubric reads "CCX." with no gloss letter; the English column's parallel head reads "ccx." (lowercase) — recorded verbatim, the English form never imported. No marginal gloss letters are recovered in the Italian OCR for this chapter. The check's numbered footnotes read "1 See above, 104' (p. 227). 3 See above, I73b (p. 367). 3 Luke xxiii. 8. * Cp. Ps. ii. 2 and Acts iv. 25 sqq.Canonical parallels asserted: Ps. ii. 2 ('The kings of the earth set themselves, and the rulers take counsel together, against the LORD, and against his anointed'; here quoted as David's prophecy — 'The princes and kings of the earth are united against the holy one of Israel'), Acts iv. 25 sqq. (the same psalm applied to Herod, Pilate, and Israel gathered against Jesus — the council of this chapter), and Luke xxiii. 8 (Herod, who 'had desired for a long time to see him' — as ch. 217 states, he 'had long wanted Jesus to come to his house'). The editors' internal cross-references ('above, 104'; 'above, 173b') stand as printed record only; the rest of the note is apparatus as printed.
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *sono uniti li principi he Be della terra* — printed "Be" (for "Re," kings); rendered "kings" with the check, the printed "Be" recorded, not silently corrected. The stray *o* line (*no uedete che chostui ha / o / inganato tutto il monddo*) and the *ill.* garble after *proibito* are recorded as printed. *perjche* ("perche," intrusive j) kept as printed.
4. **"Herod," "Israel," "David," "Caesar," "Rome," "Moses"** render the Italian *herode, issdraele, dauit, cessare, roma, moisse* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "CCXI.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 211

*[Editorial heading: Jesus comforts the disciples in the house of Nicodemus — "your sadness shall be turned into joy." Manuscript rubric "CCXI." (no gloss letter) at folio 218b. The heading above is editorial.]*

While Jesus was in the house of Nicodemus, beyond the brook Cedron, he comforted his disciples, saying: "The hour is near when I shall depart from the world. Therefore console yourselves and do not be sad, seeing that where I go I shall feel no tribulation. Now, will you be my friends if you are sad at my good? No, certainly, but rather enemies. When the world rejoices, be sad, because the rejoicing of the world is turned into weeping; but your sadness shall be turned into joy, and your joy no one shall take from you. For the rejoicing that the heart feels in God its creator, the whole world cannot take it away. See that you do not forget the words which God has spoken to you by my mouth. See to it that you are my witnesses against everyone who shall corrupt the witness that I have witnessed with my gospel against the world, and against the lovers of the world."

### Notes — Chapter 211
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian column carries marginal gloss letters a (*DIG chreatore suo a*) and b (*tesstimonij meib*); the editors' renderings are not recovered in the visible check footnotes and are not supplied. Canonical parallels asserted: John xiv. 1 & 27 ('Let not your heart be troubled'; here, 'console yourselves and do not be sad'), John xiv. 28 (the departure — 'I go away'; here, 'The hour is near when I shall depart from the world'), John xvi. 20 ('the world shall rejoice… your sorrow shall be turned into joy'; here, 'your sadness shall be turned into joy'), and John xvi. 22 ('your joy no man taketh from you'; here, 'your joy no one shall take from you'). The rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English page running head ("CONSPIRACY OF THE RULERS AGAINST JESUS," continued) is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio mark 218b in the Italian column (a stray "217 '" appears in the OCR beside it — recorded as printed).
2. **"Nicodemus," "Cedron"** render the Italian *nichodemo, cedron* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
3. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "CCXIIc.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 212

*[Editorial heading: The prayer of Jesus — "grant your servant to be in the congregation of your messenger." Manuscript rubric "CCXIIc." (gloss letter c) at folio 219a. The heading above is editorial.]*

And lifting his hands to the Lord, he prayed, saying: "Lord our God, God of Abraham, God of Ishmael and Isaac, God of our fathers, have mercy on those whom you have given me, and save them from the world. I do not say, take them from the world, because it is necessary that they bear witness against those who shall corrupt my gospel. But I pray you, keep them from evil, so that on the day of your judgment they may come with me to bear witness against the world and against the house of Israel, which has corrupted your testament. Lord God, strong and jealous, who avenges idolatry against the sons of idolatrous fathers, even to the fourth generation, curse eternally everyone who shall corrupt my gospel which you gave me, by writing that I am your son. For I, clay and dust, am a servant of your servants, and never have I thought myself to be your good servant; for I cannot give you anything for what you have given me, because everything is yours. Lord God, merciful, who shows mercy for a thousand generations upon those who fear you, have mercy on those who believe the words of mine which you have given me. For as you are true God, so the word that I have spoken is true, for it is yours — seeing that I have always spoken as one who reads, who cannot read except what is written in the book that he reads; so have I spoken what you have told me. Lord God, savior, save those whom you have given me, so that Satan may not be able to do anything against them; and save not only them, but everyone who shall believe in them. Lord, generous and rich in mercy, grant to your servant to be in the congregation of your messenger on the day of judgment; and not only me, but everyone whom you have given me, with all those who shall believe in me through their preaching. And do this, Lord, for your own sake, so that Satan may not boast himself against you, Lord. Lord God, who with your providence provided everything necessary for your people of Israel, be mindful of all the tribes of the earth, which you have promised to bless through your messenger, for whom you created the world. Have mercy on the world, and send speedily your messenger, that Satan, your enemy, may lose his empire." And having said this, Jesus said three times: "So be it, Lord God, great and merciful." And everyone answered, weeping: "So be it" — all save Judas, for he believed nothing.

### Notes — Chapter 212
1. **SEAM — three unnamed *nontio* passages, translated exactly as written.** (1) *choncedi al seruo tuo di essere nella chongreggatione de il nontio tuo il giorno del iuditio* ("grant to thy servant to be in the congregation of thy messenger on the day of judgement"); (2) *tutte le tribu della terra le quali hai promesso de benedirlle per il nontio tuo per il qualle chreassti il monddo* ("all the tribes of the earth, which thou hast promised to bless by thy messenger, for whom thou didst create the world"); (3) *manda pressto il nontio tuo azioche perda lo imperio satana innimicho tuo* ("send speedily thy messenger, that Satan thine enemy may lose his empire" — printed *ma|da*, recorded verbatim). The Italian names no one — no name is imported anywhere. Robert's insertion-suspicion is recorded; the Quran-contradiction fork stands open and undecided — never smoothed, softened, deleted, or decided. *Nontio* is rendered "messenger" throughout, kept distinct from *messia*. The prayer's "curse eternally everyone who shall corrupt my gospel... by writing that I am thy son" is kept whole exactly as written — seam context, never softened.
2. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian-column rubric reads "CCXIIc." — the gloss letter c matches the editors' chapter-title gloss "Chapter of the end" ("b Prayer of Jesus" is the other title gloss). Marginal gloss letters in the Italian column: a (*saluatore a*), b (*misserichordia b*), c (*nontio tuo c*, three times), d (*prouidentia tuad*), "do" (*DIO padri nosstri do*), f (*saluali f*), g (*uendichi g*), h (*misserichordiosso h*), i (*uero DIO i*). The editors render them: "* God creates. b Prayer of Jesus. c Chapter of the end. d God is sovereign, the God of Abraham, of Ishmael, of Isaac, and of our fathers. e God is perfect. f God guards. g God is mighty, jealous, and avenging. h God is sovereign and the merciful. 1 God is true." — recorded verbatim ("1" is OCR for the gloss letter i); the Arabic originals are unrecoverable in the OCR (garbled as "4)1." etc.) and no mapping is forced beyond what the letters show. Canonical parallels asserted: John xvii, for the prayer's distinctive petitions (esp. v. 15 — 'I pray not that thou shouldest take them out of the world, but that thou shouldest keep them from the evil'; here, 'I do not say, take them from the world… keep them from evil' — and v. 20, those who shall believe through the disciples' word; here, 'everyone who shall believe in me through their preaching'), John xv. 27 ('ye also shall bear witness'; here, the disciples kept to 'bear witness against the world'), Exod. xx. 4, 5 ('I the LORD thy God am a jealous God, visiting the iniquity of the fathers upon the children unto the… fourth generation'; here, 'strong and jealous, who avenges idolatry against the sons of idolatrous fathers, even to the fourth generation'), Exod. xx. 6 ('shewing mercy unto thousands of them that love me'; here, 'who shows mercy for a thousand generations upon those who fear you'), and Luke xvii. 10 (the servant's no-claim stance — 'we are unprofitable servants'; here, 'never have I thought myself to be your good servant'). The rest of the note is apparatus as printed. The English page running heads ("PRAYER OF JESUS"; "PEAYER OF JESUS" — "PEAYER" for "PRAYER" in the OCR) are the printer's heads, not manuscript titles; "KAOO H U" is print garble recorded as found. Folio marks 219a, 219b, 220a in the Italian column.
3. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *he chontra . alia chassa de issdraele* — the stray stop stands as printed ("against the world and against . the house of Israel"). *misserichordiosso6* — the "6" marker stands as printed (the check shows a gloss marker at "merciful"). *ma|da* for *manda* — recorded, translated "send."
4. **"Abraham," "Ishmael," "Isaac," "Israel," "Satan," "Judas"** render the Italian *habraham, ismaelle, isach, issdraele/issdraelle, satana, iuda* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "CCXIII." (ch. 213, the lamb-day chapter — slice 43's), is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 213

*[Editorial heading: The lamb in the garden — the washing of the feet. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCXIII." — no gloss letter as printed) at folio 220a. The heading above is editorial.]*

When the day came for eating the lamb, Nicodemus secretly sent the lamb to the garden for Jesus and his disciples, telling them all that Herod had decreed with the governor and the high priest. At this Jesus rejoiced in spirit, saying: "Blessed be your holy name, O Lord, because you have not cut me off from the number of your servants who have been persecuted by the world and slain. I thank you, my God, because I have finished your work." And turning to Judas he said to him: "Friend, why do you wait? My time is near — go, then, and do what you must do." The disciples thought that Jesus was sending Judas to buy something for the day of the Passover; but Jesus knew that Judas was betraying him, and, longing to depart from the world, he spoke as follows.

Judas answered: "Lord, let me eat, and I will go."

"Let us eat," said Jesus, "for I have greatly longed to eat this lamb before I am parted from you." And rising, he took a towel and tied it round his waist; and having put water into a basin, he set himself to wash his disciples' feet, beginning with Judas. When Jesus came to Peter, Peter said: "Lord, do you want to wash my feet?"

Jesus answered: "What I am doing you do not know now, but you will know hereafter."

Peter answered: "You will never wash my feet."

Then Jesus stood up and said: "Neither will you come in my company on the day of judgement."

Peter answered: "Lord, wash not my feet only, but my hands and my head."

When the disciples were washed and sat down at table to eat, Jesus said: "I have washed you, yet you are not all clean — for the water of the sea will not wash him who does not believe in me." This Jesus said because he knew who was betraying him. The disciples were saddened at these words, when Jesus said again: "I tell you in truth, one of you will betray me, so that I will be sold like a sheep. But woe to him — for he will fulfil what our father David said of such a man: that he will fall into the pit which he had prepared for another."

At this the disciples looked at one another, saying sorrowfully: "Who will be the traitor?"

Then Judas said: "Will it be I, O Master?"

Jesus answered: "You have said it yourself — who will be the one to betray me." And the eleven apostles did not take his meaning.

When the lamb was eaten, the devil came upon Judas, and he went out of the house; to him Jesus said again: "Do quickly what you must do."

### Notes — Chapter 213
1. **Apparatus as printed**: no gloss letter on the Italian-column rubric "CCXIII."; the English-column head reads "CCXIII." likewise (check-only). Folio marks 220b, 221a in the Italian column. The English page running heads "PRAYER OF JESUS" (p. 467 — OCR "PEAYER") and "THE PASCHAL SUPPER" (p. 469) are the printer's heads, not manuscript titles.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *sciugatogio* (asciugatoio, towel); *chatino* (catino, basin); *hetterno* (in eterno, "never" — the editors' footnote cites Vulg. John xiii. 8, "Non lavabis mihi pedes in aeternum"); *addaltri* (ad altri, "for another"); *partirssi* (partirsi — split across the page break); *madassi* (mandassi, "was sending"). None is resolved silently.
3. **"You have said it yourself"** renders *tu me lo hai deto chi sera cholui che mi tradira* — kept literal; the turn (Judas's own question names the betrayer) is not smoothed.
4. **"And the eleven apostles did not take his meaning"** renders *he no lo intessero li undeci apostoli* (*intessero* for *intesero*, "understood"); the check reads "heard it not" — divergence recorded, the Italian followed.
5. **"This Jesus said because he knew who was betraying him"** renders *Quessto disse iessu perche elgi sapeua che il tradiua* — *che il tradiua* sits between "who was betraying him" and "that he (Judas) was betraying him"; the check takes the former; the fork is noted, the sense kept.
6. **The David pit-saying** (*che elgi chasscera nella fosa cholui che addaltri la haueua preparata*) is kept literal; Canonical parallel asserted: Ps. vii. 15 ('He made a pit, and digged it, and is fallen into the ditch which he made'; here, in Jesus' words, the betrayer 'will fall into the pit which he had prepared for another' — Barnabas itself names David as the speaker).
7. Canonical parallels asserted: Luke xxii. 15 ('With desire I have desired to eat this passover with you before I suffer'; here, 'I have greatly longed to eat this lamb before I am parted from you'), John xiii. 4–11 (the foot-washing — towel, basin, the Peter dialogue, 'ye are clean, but not all'; here in the same sequence), and John xiii. 21–30 (the betrayal foretold, the disciples looking one on another, the devil entering Judas, 'That thou doest, do quickly'; here, 'Do quickly what you must do').
8. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "CCXIV.", is confirmed in the Italian column.
