## Chapter 214

*[Editorial heading: The prayer in the garden — Judas's bargain. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCXIV." — no gloss letter as printed) at folio 221a. The heading above is editorial.]*

When he had gone out of the house, Jesus withdrew into the garden to pray, as was his custom: to pray a hundred times, bending his knees and casting himself down upon his face.

Judas, then, knowing the place where Jesus was with his disciples, went to the high priest and said: "If you will give me what was promised, this night I will give into your hand the Jesus you are looking for, who is alone with eleven companions."

The high priest answered: "How much are you asking?"

Judas said: "Thirty gold coins."

Then straightway the high priest counted out the money to him, and sent a Pharisee to the governor to fetch soldiers, and to Herod; and they gave a legion of them, because they feared the people. So they took their weapons, and with lights and lanterns on poles went out of Jerusalem.

### Notes — Chapter 214
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian column opens with a standing "a" before *Uscito* — recorded as printed (gloss-letter key). The English-column head reads "CCXIV." (check-only). Folio marks 221a, 221b in the Italian column. The English page running head "JUDAS BETEAYETH HIS MASTER" (p. 471, as printed) is the printer's head, not a manuscript title.
2. **"To pray a hundred times, bending his knees and casting himself down upon his face"** renders *di fare horatione cento uolte impiagando li suoi ginochij he prostandossi nella facia sua* — kept literal with the check.
3. **"Thirty gold coins"** renders *trenta danari di horo*; the check reads "thirty pieces of gold" — recorded.
4. **"If you will give me what was promised"** renders *se uollete darmi la promessa* — kept literal with the check.
5. Canonical parallel asserted: John xviii. 2 ('Judas also, which betrayed him, knew the place: for Jesus ofttimes resorted thither with his disciples'; here, Jesus withdraws to pray 'as was his custom,' and 'Judas, knowing the place where Jesus was with his disciples,' goes to fetch the soldiers).
6. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "CCXV.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 215

*[Editorial heading: Jesus taken up into the third heaven. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCXV." — no gloss letter as printed) at folio 221b. The heading above is editorial.]*

When the soldiers with Judas drew near to the place where Jesus was, Jesus heard the coming of many people; so in fear he withdrew into the house, and the eleven were sleeping. Then God, seeing the danger of His servant, commanded Gabriel, Michael, Rafael, and Uriel, His ministers, to take Jesus out of the world. The holy angels came and took Jesus out through the window that looks toward the south, and carried him away; and they set him in the third heaven, in the company of angels blessing God for ever.

### Notes — Chapter 215
1. **Apparatus as printed**: no gloss letter on the Italian-column rubric "CCXV."; the English-column head reads "CCXV." likewise (check-only). No folio mark falls inside the chapter in the recovered OCR — the bracketing marks are 221b and 222a. The scribe's marginal gloss key stands at *uedendo** in the Italian column; the editors render it "God sees" — recorded, not translated.
2. **"The window that looks toward the south"** renders *la fenestra che guarda ha mezo giorno* — *mezo giorno* (mezzogiorno) is kept in its "south" sense with the check.
3. **"Gabriel, Michael, Rafael, and Uriel"** render the Italian *gabrielo, michaelle, Rafaele, uriello* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory. The editors' footnote notes the Spanish version reads Azrael at this point — recorded as printed; Spanish collation pending.
4. **The editors' footnote on the Spanish parallel** ("See the parallel Spanish version, of which Dr. White's translation is given in the Introduction. The Italian is more diffuse, and has several variations") is recorded — the single-witness flag stands regardless.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "CCXVI.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 216

*[Editorial heading: Judas transformed into the likeness of Jesus. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCXVI." — no gloss letter as printed) at folio 222a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Judas rushed in ahead of them all into the room from which Jesus had been taken up, and the eleven were sleeping. Then the wonderful God worked wonderfully, so that Judas was so changed in his speech and in his face, made like to Jesus, that we believed him to be Jesus. And he, having wakened us, was asking where the Master was. So we, astonished, answered: "You, Lord, are our master — have you now forgotten us?"

And he, smiling, said: "Now you are fools, that you do not know me to be Judas Iscariot!"

And as he said this the soldiers came in and laid hands on Judas, because he was in all things like to Jesus.

We, having heard Judas's words and seen the multitude of soldiers, fled as men beside themselves. And John, who was wrapped in a linen sheet, woke from sleep and fled; and a soldier seized him by the linen sheet, so he left the sheet and escaped naked — for God heard the prayer of Jesus, and saved the eleven from evil.

### Notes — Chapter 216
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian-column rubric "CCXVI."; the English-column head reads "CCXVI." likewise (check-only). Folio marks 222a, 222b in the Italian column. The English page running head "JUDAS TRANSFORMED" (p. 473) is the printer's head, not a manuscript title.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *stantia* (stanza, room); *empito* (impeto, "rush"); *smetichato* (dimenticato, "forgotten"); *es&ere* (the OCR's "&" stands for "ss" — read *essere*, "to be"); *linciollo/lenziolo* (lenzuolo, linen sheet); *malle* (male, "evil"). The page-break catchword repeats *iessu* — rendered once, the duplication recorded. None is resolved silently.
3. **"Fled as men beside themselves"** renders *chome ussciti di noi fugissimo* ("as if gone out of ourselves") — kept literal with the check.
4. **"For God heard the prayer of Jesus, and saved the eleven from evil"** — Canonical parallels asserted: Mark xiv. 51 (the young man in the linen cloth, seized, leaving the cloth and fleeing naked; here the same incident, with Barnabas itself naming the young man as John — the identification the editors note in Ambrose, Chrysostom, and Bede) and John xviii. 9 ('Of them which thou gavest me have I lost none'; here, 'God heard the prayer of Jesus, and saved the eleven from evil'). The editors' cross-reference 219b (p. 467) is to the prayer of ch. 212 within this book and stands as printed record only.
5. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next Italian-column rubric, "CCXVII.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 217

*[Editorial heading: Judas taken for Jesus — scourged, mocked, and crucified. Manuscript rubric (Ragg "CCXVII." — no gloss letter as printed) at folio 222b. The heading above is editorial.]*

The soldiers seized Judas and bound him, not without mockery — for he truthfully denied that he was Jesus, and the soldiers, mocking him, said: "Sir, do not be afraid — we have come to make you king of Israel, and we have bound you because we know you refuse the kingdom."

Judas answered: "Now you have lost your wits! You have come to take Jesus of Nazareth with weapons and lanterns as if he were a robber — and me, who guided you here, you have bound, to make me king!" Then the soldiers lost patience, and with fists and kicks they began to pay Judas back in kind, and led him with fury to Jerusalem.

John and Peter followed the soldiers from afar, and they told him who writes that they saw every questioning put to Judas by the high priest and by the council of the Pharisees, who were gathered to put Jesus to death. At this Judas spoke many mad words, so that everyone was filled with laughter, believing that he was truly Jesus and that for fear of death he was feigning madness. Then the scribes bound his eyes with a bandage and, mocking him, said: "Jesus, prophet of the Nazarenes — for so they called those who believed in Jesus — tell us, who was it that struck you?" And they slapped him and spat in his face.

When morning came, the great council of scribes and elders of the people gathered, and the high priest with the Pharisees looked for false witnesses against Judas, believing him to be Jesus — and they did not find what they were looking for. And why do I even say that the chief priests believed Judas to be Jesus? All the disciples, together with him who writes, believed the same. And what is more, the poor virgin mother of Jesus, with his relatives and friends, believed it, so that everyone's sorrow was beyond telling. As God lives — he who writes forgot all that Jesus had told him: that he would be taken up from the world, and that he would suffer in a third person, and that he would not die until near the end of the world. So he went with the mother of Jesus and with John to the cross.

The high priest had the bound Judas brought before him, and questioned him about his disciples and his teaching. At this Judas, as if out of his senses, answered nothing to the point. So the high priest put him under oath by the living God of Israel to tell the truth.

Judas answered: "I have told you that I am Judas Iscariot, who promised to give Jesus of Nazareth into your hands — and you, I do not know by what trick, are beside yourselves, for you will have it at all costs that I am Jesus."

The high priest answered: "O wicked deceiver — you have deceived all Israel, from Galilee all the way here to Jerusalem, with your teaching and false miracles; and now do you think to escape the punishment you deserve by pretending to be mad? As God lives, you will not escape it!" And having said this, he ordered his servants to beat him with slaps and kicks, to knock his wits back into his head. The mockery the high priest's servants then made of him passes belief — for they strained after new cruelties to please the council. So they dressed him up as a juggler, and with hands and feet mauled him so that it would have moved the Canaanites to pity if they had seen him so. But the chief priests and Pharisees and elders of the people had their hearts so hardened against Jesus that, believing Judas to be truly Jesus, they took pleasure in seeing him treated so.

Afterwards they led him bound to the governor, who in secret loved Jesus. He, believing Judas to be Jesus, had him brought into his private room and spoke with him, asking him why the chief priests with the people had given him up into his hands.

Judas answered: "If I tell you the truth, you will not believe me — for perhaps you are deceived, as the chief priests and the Pharisees are deceived."

The governor answered — thinking he wanted to speak about the Law: "Do you not know that I am no Jew? But the chief priests with the elders of your people have given you into my hand. Tell us the truth, then, so that I may do what is just — for I have power to set you free and to put you to death."

Judas answered: "Sir, believe me — if you put me to death you will commit a great sin, for you will kill an innocent man: for I am Judas Iscariot and not Jesus, who is a magician and by his art has changed me into this."

When the governor heard this he was greatly amazed, and tried to set him free. So the governor came out, and smiling said: "One thing is certain — one of two — and in either case this man is not worthy of death, but of pity. This man says," said the governor, "that he is not Jesus but a certain Judas who led the soldiers to take Jesus, and that Jesus the Galilean has by his magic art changed him into this. If this is true, it would be a great sin to kill him, since he would be innocent. But if he is Jesus and denies it, then surely he has lost his mind — and it would be a wicked thing to kill a madman."

Then the chief priests and elders of the people, with the scribes and Pharisees, shouted loudly, saying: "He is Jesus of Nazareth — we know him! For if he were not the evildoer, we would not have given him into your hands. Nor is he mad — he is full of malice: with this trick he is trying to slip out of our hands, and the uprising he would stir up by escaping would be worse than the first."

Pilate — for that was the governor's name — to be rid of such a case, said: "This man is a Galilean, and Herod is king of Galilee; it is not for me, then, to judge such a case — take him to Herod." So they led Judas to Herod, who had long wanted Jesus to come to his house. But Jesus had never been willing to go to his house, because Herod was a Gentile who worshipped the false and lying gods, living like the unclean Gentiles.

When Judas had been brought there, Herod questioned him about many things, to which Judas gave senseless answers, denying that he was Jesus. Then Herod mocked him with all his court, and had him dressed in white as fools are dressed, and sent him back to Pilate, saying to him: "Do not fail in justice to the people of Israel!" And Herod wrote this because the chief priests and scribes and the Pharisees had given him a good sum of money. The governor, having learned this from a servant of Herod, wanting to gain money too, pretended he wished to set Judas free. So he had him whipped by his servants, who had been paid by the scribes to kill him under the whips. But God, who had decreed what would happen, kept Judas for the cross, so that he would suffer the horrible death into which he had sold another man. God did not let Judas die under the whips, even though the soldiers whipped him so cruelly that his body ran with blood. Then in mockery they dressed him in an old purple robe, saying: "It is fitting for our new king to be clothed and crowned." So they gathered thorns and made a crown like the crowns of gold and precious stones that kings wear on their heads, and they set this crown of thorns on Judas's head, putting a reed in his hand for a sceptre, and made him sit in a high place. And the soldiers came before him, bowing in mockery and hailing him king of the Jews; and they held out their hands to receive gifts, as new kings are in the habit of giving — and getting nothing, they beat Judas, saying: "Now how are you crowned, you foolish king, if you will not pay your soldiers and your servants!"

The chief priests with the scribes and Pharisees, seeing that Judas did not die under the whips and fearing that Pilate might set him free, gave money to the governor; and he, having taken it, handed Judas over to the scribes and Pharisees as guilty and deserving death. Then with him they condemned two robbers to death on the cross. So they led him to Mount Calvary, where wrongdoers were hanged, and there they crucified him naked, for the greater shame.

Judas truly did nothing but cry out: "God, why have you forsaken me — the evildoer is fled, and I am wrongfully put to death!" I tell you in truth, the voice and the face, together with the whole person, of Judas were so like to Jesus that his disciples and all who believed wholly thought he was Jesus. So some fell away from the teaching of Jesus, believing that Jesus was a false prophet and that by magic art he had done the miracles he did — for Jesus had said that he would not die until near the end of the world, since at that time he would have been taken up from the world. But those who stood firm in the teaching of Jesus were so ringed round with sorrow, seeing him die who was in all things like to Jesus, that they could not remember what Jesus had said. So in company with the mother of Jesus they went to Mount Calvary, and stood watching the death of Judas, weeping all the while; and through Nicodemus and Joseph of Arimathea they got the governor to give them the body of Judas for burial. So they took him down from the cross with such weeping as no one would surely believe, and buried him in Joseph's new tomb, having wrapped him with a hundred pounds of precious ointment.

### Notes — Chapter 217
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the Italian-column rubric "CCXVII."; the English-column head reads "CCXVII." likewise (check-only). Folio marks 222b, 223a, 223b, 224a, 224b, 225a, 225b, 226a, 226b, 227a in the Italian column. The English page running heads "JUDAS BEFORE PILATE" (p. 477) and "JUDAS SCOURGED AND MOCKED" (p. 479) are the printer's heads, not manuscript titles. Marginal and folio markers stand in the Italian column as printed: *f 222**")*, *(233)*, *(224&)*, *223*, *225*, *(225b)*, *(226*)*, and a standing "11" before *pontifice* where the council gathers.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *serai del potifice* (the high priest's servants — the check confirms the sense); *giocholatore* (giocolatore, juggler); *chananei* (cananei, Canaanites); *sephtro* (scettro, sceptre); *pojpulo* (populo, people); *inmolde* (immonde, unclean); *modo* for *mondo* at "near the end of the *modo*" (the check reads "world"); *qjllo* (quello, "what"); the split *f ugite* (fugite, "fled"). None is resolved silently.
3. **"They began to pay Judas back in kind"** renders the idiom *inchominciorno ha chambiare la moneta ha iuda* — literally "to change the money with Judas" (the editors' footnote glosses the phrase literally); the plain sense is kept, the literal noted.
4. **"You will commit a great sin"** renders *tu farai uno grande pechato*, and **"an innocent man"** renders *uno inocete*; the check softens these to "thou shalt do a great wrong" and "an innocent person" — divergences recorded, the Italian followed.
5. **"Pilate — for that was the governor's name"** renders *pillato che quessto hera il nome de il presside* — the text names the governor here for the first time; kept as written.
6. **"But God, who had decreed what would happen"** renders *ma DIG che haueua dechretato il sucesso*; the editors' marginal gloss on *dechretato* reads "God is avenging" — recorded as printed.
7. **"God, why have you forsaken me — the evildoer is fled, and I am wrongfully put to death"** is kept exactly as written; the Italian (*essendo che elgie fuggito il malfatore he io son ha torto morto*) leaves open whether "the evildoer" names Judas himself or another — the fork is noted, never decided. The cry's echo of the canonical dereliction cry is not imported beyond what the text says.
8. **"He would suffer in a third person"** renders *che patiria in terzza perssona* — kept fully literal, not explained away.
9. **"And why do I even say that the chief priests believed Judas to be Jesus"** renders *he che dicho li pontificj chredeuano iuda essere iessu* — kept literal with the check's sense.
10. Canonical parallels asserted — with the identity inverted throughout, the sufferer in this chapter being Judas in Jesus' likeness, not Jesus: John xviii. 12–xix. 41 and parallels (the passion sequence entire, scene for scene); Matt. xxvi. 59–60 (false witnesses sought and not found), xxvi. 63 (the oath by the living God), xxvi. 67–68 with Luke xxii. 64 (the blindfold, the slaps and spitting, 'tell us, who was it that struck you?'); Luke xxiii. 5–12 (the Galilean sent to Herod, questioned, mocked, dressed in white, and sent back); John xviii. 35 ('I am no Jew… the chief priests… have given you into my hand') and xix. 10 ('I have power to set you free and to put you to death'); John viii. 46 (for the governor's hearing only — the accused unconvicted, 'not worthy of death'); Matt. xxvii. 14 (the governor greatly amazed at the answers) and xxvii. 29 (the crown of thorns, the reed sceptre, 'king of the Jews').
11. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The slice closes at *preciosso onguento .* — the Italian column's CCXVIII rubric follows (ch. 218 opens the next slice).

## Chapter 218

*[Editorial heading: The stolen body of Judas — persecution under anathema. Manuscript rubric "CCXVIII." (Italian column, no gloss letter) at folio 227a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Everyone went back home. He who writes, with John and James his brother, went with the mother of Jesus to Nazareth. Those disciples who did not fear God went by night and stole the body of Judas and hid it, spreading the report that Jesus was risen again — so great confusion arose. Then the high priest commanded, under pain of anathema, that no one speak of Jesus of Nazareth; so a great persecution arose, and many were stoned, many beaten, and many driven from their homeland, because they could not keep silent about such a thing. The news reached Nazareth that Jesus, their fellow-citizen, having died on the cross, was risen again. So he who writes begged the mother of Jesus to be content to leave off weeping, because her son was risen again. Hearing this, the virgin Mary, weeping, said: "Let us go to Jerusalem to find my son, for I shall die content when I have seen him."

### Notes — Chapter 218
1. **Apparatus as printed**: no gloss letters stand in the Italian column of this chapter. The editors' printed critical note "1 So MS. : conj. *circondati*" appears in the OCR immediately after the Italian ch. 218 text — recorded verbatim; its attachment point is [uncertain] in the OCR and it is not resolved here. Canonical parallels asserted: Matt. xxvii. 46 and Mark xv. 34 (the cry 'why have you forsaken me' — spoken in ch. 217 by Judas on the cross, in Jesus' likeness, with Barnabas's added clause 'the evildoer is fled, and I am wrongfully put to death'), John xix. 38 sqq. (Joseph of Arimathea obtaining the body, Nicodemus and the hundred pounds of ointment, Joseph's new tomb — all at the close of ch. 217, the body being Judas's), and Matt. xxviii. 11–15, inverted (Matthew's bribed cover story — 'his disciples came by night and stole him away' — is here narrated as the event: the body of Judas stolen by night and the report spread that Jesus was risen). The editors' other citation, Matt. xxvii. 62–66 (the guard set and the stone sealed), stands as printed record only — no guard or sealing occurs in this chapter; the rest of the note is apparatus as printed. One check footnote notes "The Spanish version makes an exception of Peter, see p. Introduction" — recorded as printed; no Spanish control exists for this slice, so nothing is imported from it. The English page running head ("JUDAS CRUCIFIED") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 227a, 227b in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *Ritornossi ha chassa ogniuno* ("everyone went back home"); *quelli dissepolli che non temeuano DIO* ("those disciples who did not fear God"); *furno lapidati molti basstonati he molti scaziati dalla patria* ("many were stoned, many beaten, and many driven from their homeland"); *iessu loro citadino morto in chroce hera rissuscitato* ("Jesus, their fellow-citizen, having died on the cross, was risen again"). Nothing softened.
3. **"John," "James," "Judas," "Nazareth," "Jerusalem," "Mary"** render the Italian *ioane, iachobo, iuda, nazaret, ierussalem/ierusalem, maria* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCXIXa", is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 219

*[Editorial heading: Mary returns to Jerusalem — Jesus appears to his own. Manuscript rubric "CCXIXa" (Italian column, gloss letter a) at folio 228a. The heading above is editorial.]*

The virgin returned to Jerusalem with him who writes and James and John, on the day on which the decree of the high priest went forth. But the virgin, who feared God, although she knew the high priest's decree was unjust, commanded those who dwelled with her to forget her son. Now how each one was affected — God, who knows the heart of men, knows: between grief at the death of Judas, whom we believed to be Jesus our master, and the desire to see him risen again, we were consumed, with the mother of Jesus. Then the angels who were guardians of Mary the virgin ascended to the third heaven, where Jesus was in the company of angels, and recounted everything to him. So Jesus prayed God to give him power to see his mother and his disciples. Then the merciful God commanded his four favourite angels — Gabriel, Michael, Rafael, and Uriel — to carry Jesus into his mother's house, and there to keep watch over him for three days continually, letting him be seen only by those who believed in his doctrine. Jesus came, surrounded with splendour, into the room where Mary the virgin abode with her two sisters, and Martha with Mary Magdalen, and Lazarus with him who writes and John with James and Peter. And through fear they fell as dead; but Jesus lifted his mother and the others from the ground, saying: "Do not fear, for I am Jesus; and do not weep, for I am alive and not dead." Everyone remained for a long time beside himself at the presence of Jesus, for they altogether believed that Jesus was dead. Then the virgin, weeping, said: "Tell me, my son: why, when God gave you power to raise the dead, did you let yourself die so, to the shame of your kinsfolk and friends and to the shame of your doctrine? For everyone who loves you has been as dead."

### Notes — Chapter 219
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "Chapter on the descent of Jesus (from heaven) to the children of Mary" (a, at the rubric), "God knows" (b, at *chonossce* — "who knows the heart of men"), "God the merciful" (c, at *il misserichordiosso DIG*), "God gives" (d, at *dato DIG potessta* — "having given thee power"). Their footnote cites "above, 56* (p. 127) and 32ib (p. 471)" for the four angels — internal cross-reference, recorded as printed. The English page running head ("JESUS APPEARETH TO HIS FRIENDS") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 228a, 228b in the Italian column (OCR also shows "228*", "237'").
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *he li rinontiorno il tutto* — rendered "recounted everything to him" with the check; the printed form *rinontiorno* is kept as printed and flagged [uncertain] rather than normalized. *stete ogniuno grande tepo chome fuori di sse* ("everyone remained a long time as if beside himself"); *dui sui sorelle* ("her two sisters"); *peruene iessu circhondato da ssplendore* ("Jesus came, surrounded with splendour").
3. **"Gabriel," "Michael," "Rafael," "Uriel," "Martha," "Mary Magdalen," "Lazarus," "Peter"** render the Italian *Gabriello, michaele, Rafaelle, Vriello, marta, maria magdallena, lazaro, pietro* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCXXa.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 220

*[Editorial heading: Jesus, his mother, and the four angels — the mocking until Mohammed comes. Manuscript rubric "CCXXa." (Italian column, gloss letter a) at folio 229a. The heading above is editorial.]*

Jesus answered, embracing his mother: "Believe me, mother, for in truth I tell you that I have never died at all; for God has reserved me until near the end of the world." And having said this, he prayed the four angels to uncover themselves and to give testimony how the matter had passed. So the angels uncovered themselves like four shining suns, so that through fear everyone fell again as dead. Then Jesus gave four linen cloths to the angels, so that they might cover themselves, that they might be seen and heard speaking. He lifted up his mother with her companions, and having lifted everyone up, he comforted them, saying: "These are the ministers of God: Gabriel, who announces God's secrets; Michael, who fights against God's enemies; Rafael, who receives the souls of those who die; and Uriel, who will call everyone to God's judgement on the last day." Then the four angels narrated to the virgin how God had sent for Jesus, and how he had transformed Judas, that he might suffer the punishment which he had sold to another. Then said he who writes: "O master, is it lawful for me to question you now, as it was lawful for me when you dwelled with us?" Jesus answered: "Ask what pleases you, Barnabas, and I will answer you." Then said he who writes: "O master, since God is merciful, why has he so tormented us, making us believe that you were dead? And your mother has so wept for you that she has been near to death; and you, who are a holy one of God — has God let this infamy fall upon you, that you were slain among robbers on Mount Calvary?" Jesus answered: "Believe me, Barnabas, that every sin, however small it be, God punishes with great punishment, since God is offended by sin. So since my mother and the faithful disciples who were with me loved me a little with earthly love, the just God has willed to punish this love with the present grief, so that it may not be punished in the flames of hell. And as for me, who have been innocent in the world — since men have called me God and Son of God — God, so as not to let me be mocked by the demons on the day of judgement, has willed that I be mocked by men in the world through the death of Judas, making everyone believe that I died on the cross. And this mocking will last until the coming of Mohammed, messenger of God,* who, coming to the world, will undeceive everyone who believes in the law of God from this deception." Having said this, Jesus said: "You are just, Lord our God, for to you alone belong honour and glory without end."

\* **The seam passage, translated exactly as written**: *onde quessto scerno durera inssino alia uenuta di machometo nontio di DIOC . il quale uenendo al modo sganera ogniuno che chrederano alia legie di DIO di quessto ingano* — "and this mocking will last until the coming of Mohammed, messenger of God, who, coming to the world, will undeceive everyone who believes in the law of God from this deception." Nothing is smoothed, softened, or deleted. The fork is footnoted per the audit: Robert's recorded suspicion that the named-Muhammad line may be a later insertion is carried here rather than decided; the naming of Ahmad/Muhammad is itself Quranic (61:6), and the sharper Quran friction in this lane remains the passages where Jesus denies being the Messiah, against Quran 3:45 ("the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary") — never decided here. (See SOURCE-AUDIT §3.)

### Notes — Chapter 220
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "Chapter" (a, at the rubric); at *abrazando la madre* (b) the gloss is rendered "Said Jesus to his mother, I am living and shall not die (and God has given me a long life), save a little before the end of the world. *Inde.*" — recorded verbatim as the check renders the scribe's mark; "God guards" (c, at *mia rissemato* — gloss mark OCR'd as "°", flagged [uncertain]); "God is wise" (d, at *al iuditi.o di Dio*); "God the merciful" (e, at *misserichordiosso* — gloss mark OCR'd as "6", flagged [uncertain]). A second gloss block on the following page reads "God punishes" (a, at *DIG il punissea* — gloss mark not recovered in the OCR, flagged [uncertain]); "God is avenging" (b, at *ha uolluto punireb*); "Mohammed, the prophet of God" (c, at *di machometo nontio di DIOC* — gloss mark not recovered in the OCR, flagged [uncertain]); "God is sovereign and just" (d, at *DIG nosstro*); "If God wills" (e, at *piazendo ha Dio*). No numbered canonical footnote is recovered in the OCR for this chapter. The English page running head ("JESUS AND THE FOUR ANGELS") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 229a, 229b, 230a in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *perche DIG mia rissemato hapresso la fine del monddo* ("for God has reserved me until near the end of the world"); *quatro lincioli* ("four linen cloths"); *aziaoche elgi riceuessi quella pena che haueua uenduto ha altri* ("that he might suffer the punishment which he had sold to another") — *uenduto* kept as printed; *elgi prego li quatro anggioli che si scoprissero* ("he prayed the four angels to uncover themselves"). The angels' four offices (Gabriel/Michael/Rafael/Uriel) are kept entire, in the Italian's order.
3. **Messiah/messenger terminology**: no *messia* occurs in this chapter. *nontio* occurs once, in the named seam passage (*machometo nontio di DIOC*), rendered "messenger" — kept distinct from "messiah" throughout, per the house rule.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCXXI.", is confirmed in the Italian column.

## Chapter 221

*[Editorial heading: Barnabas charged to write — the ascension from the Mount of Olives. Manuscript rubric "CCXXI." (Italian column, no gloss letter) at folio 230b. The heading above is editorial.]*

And Jesus, turning to him who writes, said: "See, Barnabas, that by all means you write my gospel concerning all that has happened through my dwelling in the world; and write likewise what has befallen Judas, so that the faithful may be undeceived and everyone may believe the truth." He who writes answered: "All will I do, God willing, O master; but how it happened to Judas I do not know, for I did not see it all." Jesus answered: "Here are John and Peter, who have seen it all, and they will tell you how it all passed." And then Jesus commanded that we should call his faithful disciples, that they might see him. So James and John gathered together the seven disciples with Nicodemus and Joseph and many others of the seventy-two, and they ate with Jesus. On the third day Jesus said: "Go to the Mount of Olives with my mother, for there I will ascend again to heaven, and you will see who will bear me up." So everyone went, except twenty-five of the seventy-two disciples, who for fear had fled to Damascus. And as everyone stood in prayer, at midday Jesus came with a great multitude of angels who were praising God; and from the splendour of his face everyone was sore afraid, and they fell with their faces to the ground. Then Jesus, lifting them up, comforted them, saying: "Do not fear, for I am your master." And he reproved many who believed him to have died and risen again, saying: "Do you then hold me and God for a liar? For God has granted to me to live until near the end of the world, even as I told you. And I tell you that I did not die, but Judas the traitor died. Beware, for Satan will make every effort to deceive you; so see to it that you be my witnesses in all Israel and throughout the world of all you have heard and seen." And having said this, he prayed God for the salvation of the faithful and for the conversion of sinners. When the prayer was ended, he embraced his mother, saying: "Peace be to you, my mother; rest in God your creator and mine." And having said this, he turned to the disciples, saying: "May God's grace and mercy be with you." And having said this, the four angels visibly lifted him up to heaven.

### Notes — Chapter 221
1. **Apparatus as printed**: the editors' marginal glosses read "God bestows" (a, at *DIG mia choncesso* — "for God has granted to me"); the gloss rendered "Said Jesus in his last words, 'God has given me a long life except a little before the end of the world.' *Inde.*" (b, at *sichome ui dissi* — "even as I told you"); "God creates" (c, at *DIG chreatore* — gloss mark OCR'd as "0", flagged [uncertain]). The remaining gloss of the end-of-book block, "God is wise" (d), stands at ch. 222's *di DIG amem* — recorded under ch. 222. The check's notes print "Lit. *in*" (at *scriua Io euangelio mio in tutto quello* — "write my gospel concerning all") and "Cp. above, 3* (p. 3)" — internal notes, recorded as printed. The English page running head ("JESUS CHARGETH BARNABAS TO WRITE") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 230b, 231a in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *uinticinque li quali per timor herano fugiti in damassco* (OCR "uinticinque 11 quali"; read "twenty-five of them, who for fear had fled to Damascus"); *li sete dissepoli* ("the seven disciples"); *no uolgiate temere* ("do not fear"); *dicedo* (OCR for *dicendo*, "saying"); *sichome ui dissi* ("even as I told you"). The charge to Barnabas — write the gospel of his dwelling in the world, and write what befell Judas so the faithful may be undeceived — is kept whole.
3. **"Nicodemus," "Joseph," "Damascus," "Mount of Olives," "Israel"** render the Italian *nichodemo, iosef, damassco, monte olliueto, issdraelle* — the Italian spellings, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). The next rubric, "CCXXIL" [uncertain — see ch. 222 note 1], is confirmed in the Italian column.

---

## Chapter 222

*[Editorial heading: The disciples scattered — the gospel's close. Manuscript rubric "CCXXIL" (Italian column — recorded verbatim; the terminal character(s) flagged [uncertain], not resolved; the English column's parallel rubric is not imported) at folio 231b. The heading above is editorial.]*

After Jesus had departed, the disciples scattered through the different parts of Israel and of the world; and the truth, hated by Satan, was persecuted by falsehood, as it still is. For certain evil men, pretending to be disciples, preached that Jesus died and rose not again. Others preached that he really died, but rose again. Others preached, and still preach, that Jesus is the Son of God — among whom is Paul, deceived. But we, as much as I have written, preach to those who fear God, that they may be saved in the last day of God's judgement. Amen.

End of the gospel.

### Notes — Chapter 222
1. **Apparatus as printed — the closing rubric and colophon**: the Italian-column rubric reads "CCXXIL" in the djvu OCR — recorded verbatim. Whether the terminal "L" is the scribe's gloss letter, a misread second "I" of "CCXXII," or an OCR merge is [uncertain] and is not resolved here; the English column's parallel rubric ("CCXX1I." in the OCR) is recorded for reference only and its letter is never imported. The Italian text closes with the explicit colophon, printed on its own line: **"Fine dello euangelio"** ("End of the gospel") — recorded verbatim. Immediately after it the OCR shows a garbled marginal mark, "a .^U ill." — recorded verbatim and flagged [uncertain]; it is not translated or attached to any clause. The manuscript's actual ending is therefore clean: the narrative closes with "Amen," followed by the scribe's explicit "End of the gospel" — there is no further Italian text after it in the edition. The editors' final gloss block reads "God is wise" (d, at *di DIG amem* — "in the last day of God's judgement. Amen"); their note prints "Cp. above, 3a (p. 3)" — internal cross-reference, recorded as printed. The English page running head ("JESUS CARRIED UP TO HEAVEN") is the printer's head, not a manuscript title. Folio marks 231b, 230' in the Italian column.
2. **Printed readings kept as printed**: *Partito iessu* (OCR "Parti to iessu"; "after Jesus had departed"); *si diuisse per diuersse parte de isdrahelle he del monddo li dissepoli* ("the disciples scattered through the different parts of Israel and of the world"); *he la uerita odiata da sattana fu persseguitata dalla buggia chome tutauia si troua* ("and the truth, hated by Satan, was persecuted by falsehood, as it still is"); *fra li qualli he paullo inganato* ("among whom is Paul, deceived"); *noi pero quato habia scrito predichiamo ha cholloro che temono DIG* ("but we, as much as I have written, preach to those who fear God"). The three false preachings (died and rose not again; really died but rose again; is the Son of God — and still preach it) are kept entire, in the Italian's order.
3. **"Paul"** renders the Italian *paullo* — the Italian spelling, not canonical memory.
4. **Witness coverage**: single-witness (Italian only; no Spanish control for ch. 121+). There is no next rubric — this is the last chapter of the book.

## Editor's Epilogue

This book was made from one lawful source: the Italian text of the Vienna
manuscript as printed by Lonsdale and Laura Ragg at the Clarendon Press in
1907, a public-domain printing. Their English translation was used only as a
check, never as a base. A Spanish witness to the same work was consulted for
the first hundred and twenty chapters. Everything in these pages stands or
falls on that footing, and the footing is stated here so that no reader has to
guess at it.

The rendering is into plain modern English, on a simple conviction: that a
book which has travelled this far to reach a reader should not then demand a
dictionary of him. Where the Italian was strange, the strangeness was kept.
Where the old editors printed a note, the note was recorded as printed,
whether or not this edition agrees with it. And where the text touches the
seam that runs through this gospel — the places where the Italian names the
messenger of God by the name Mohammed, and the places where it gives only
"messenger of God" — the translation follows one rule throughout: the Italian
is rendered exactly as it stands, the name where the Italian gives the name,
the title where it gives the title, footnoted and never smoothed in either
direction. A reader who disagrees with the seam will find it undisturbed,
exactly where the manuscript left it.

The work was audited in full, chapter by chapter, and the audit left
ninety-seven places in the apparatus marked for the editor's own judgment —
places where the workers on this book declined to decide in the editor's
name. Those ninety-seven were then taken up one by one. Eighty-nine were
resolved on the evidence alone, each with its sources checked and its answer
written into the apparatus with the grounds shown. The seam rule, under
which three of the ninety-seven stand, was confirmed by the editor
personally, and eight more carried a single clause that no evidence could
settle; those the editor ruled himself, and the rulings stand in the
apparatus where they occur. Nothing in this book asks to be believed on
authority. The reasoning is printed beside the text, so that any reader, in
any century, can re-walk the trail and reach his own verdict.

Of the book's place among its fellows, only what can be shown will be said.
In English, the Raggs' translation of 1907 has stood alone for over a
century as the complete rendering of this gospel from the Italian
manuscript; none other was found after research. This edition is a second,
independent, complete English rendering. It does not claim the heaviest
scholarly apparatus — that honour, in French, belongs to the edition of
Cirillo and Frémaux — nor first place in time. It claims only to be complete,
to be plain, and to show its work.

It is given away freely, under its author's own name, as all such rescues
should be. The editor's part was the framing, the labour of the audit, and
the presenting of it to the world. The words are Barnabas's, as the Italian
handed them down.

This edition was prepared with the assistance of artificial intelligence,
under the editor's direction and subject to his rulings at every gate. The
decisions, the seam rule, the sign-offs, and the errors that remain are the
editor's own.

**Robert Tilford, editor**
Hays, Kansas
October 2026
